Part I
Quantale Weakness
1    Introduction
The principle of preferring simpler explanations – often termed ”Occam’s razor”
– is a foundational heuristic in learning and reasoning. We here present a re-
thinking and reformulation of what ”simpler” means, by framing it at a higher
level of abstraction than has typically been done. We follow Michael Timo-
thy Bennett’s recent work by construing simplicity as a form of ”weakness” (a
simpler hypothesis is one which restricts less), but we depart from Bennett by
formulating weakness in a more algebraic rather than explicitly semantic way.
    Bennett [Ben25] defines the weakness of an hypothesis as the size of its sat-
isfaction set: the hypothesis that excludes the fewest possible worlds is deemed
the weakest and hence most general. He argues compellingly that this is in some
senses a more foundational way of formalizing Occam’s Razor than Minimum


                                        8
Description Length principles, and shows how these emerge as a consequence of
weakness under appropriate assumptions.
    Building on Bennett’s approach, we formulate a broader notion of weakness.
Philosophically, we ground weakness in the arguably more elementary concepts
of ”relationship” and ”value,” proposing that:
   Given any system for relating and valuing entities, think of an individual
binary relationship as high-prominence to the extent two things it joins are
both valuable. A collection of binary relationships is then weak to the extent it
contains a sufficient aggregate of high-prominence relationships.
     Conceptually: The high-prominence relationships are the obvious ones in
terms of the valuations and relationships given, so if the collection gives you the
high-prominence relationships, it’s not really giving you anything interesting...
it’s giving you the ”obvious” (i.e. less discriminative), which is a weak thing to
do.
     We formalize this broader view of weakness in the category-theoretic setting
using a framework of quantales, which gives a very general notion of relationship
and valuation. We show that our categorial notion of weakness generalizes
Bennett’s possible-worlds notion of weakness, but also goes significantly beyond
it, and can be specialized in a variety of different ways, to yield e.g. probabilistic,
linear (SVM-related), information-theoretic, hypervectorial or quantum notions
of weakness.
     We then explore how these different forms of weakness can be leveraged
to reformulate the particulars of various learning and reasoning models and
methods used in the AI field. The AI methods explored are drawn from the
Hyperon AGI programme [GBD+ 23], however the core theoretical ideas here
are not especially tied to Hyperon, that is just the context in which we have
been working.
     The conceptual picture thickens a bit when one looks at quantum systems,
but one can then reformulate things roughly as,

   Given any system for relating and valuing entities, think of an individual
binary relationship as high-prominence to the extent two things it joins are
both valuable. A collection of binary relationships is then weak to the extent
that, considered through the lens of a variety of complementary perspectives, it
tends to contain a high combined weight of high-prominence relationships in the
various perspectives.

(and one then can make choices regarding how to do the compositing across
different complementary perspectives).
    The breadth of the discussion should not be surprising given what we’re
talking about: Occam’s Razor in all its traditional glory plus more. I.e., the
scope of formal applicability of our categorial weakness is basically the same
as the scope of conceptual applicability of Occam’s Razor as a philosophical
principle. To apply categorial weakness in a certain domain one ”simply” has
to figure out the appropriate way to model that domain as a quantale.


                                          9
    A note on terminology: For simplicity, as the paper proceeds we will mostly
refer to our ”categorial weakness” simply as ”weakness”, using terms like ”set-
theoretic weakness” (for Bennett’s version), ”probabilistic weakness” etc. for
versions obtained from specific quantales.
    And a note on exposition: We would like to apologize to the reader in
advance for the density of some of the material here, and the erratic nature of
the text’s attempts to explain itself intuitively and conceptually. The topics
covered are a wild mix of highly theoretical and close-to-the-practical, and the
text has been put together at a time when the AI field is advancing tremendously
fast... given the time-demands of working at the cutting-edge of the AI field,
we have not had time to put tremendous effort into creating simple and elegant
explanations of new and complex concepts (which is of course a non-trivial thing
to do even with ample time on one’s hands). What we have aimed for instead
is a reasonably clear and accurate presentation of a network of what we think
are some interesting and important concepts, many of which we believe have
significant practical implementation in the near term... and we have faith that
as the ideas here and their applications are fleshed out in the coming years, a
variety of more expository and didactic expositions will unfold.

    The choice of topics here reflects in some measure our own current interests
and pursuits, rather than being an exhaustive or objectively priority-ranked
survey of all applications of the categorial weakness formalism. Numerous fur-
ther applications exist, as one would expect for a concept and construct of such
fundamental importance.
    Beyond the diverse particulars, however, what we aim to demonstrate here is
that our categorial framing of ”weakness” not only unifies a spectrum of simplic-
ity measures in AI and information sciences, but also yields practical algorithms
and architectures for learning, inference, communication, and decision-making.
Many steps remain to turn the algorithms, structures and ideas presented here
into practical systems; what we aim for here is to articulate a significant body
of highly promising and interconnected directions.



                                       14
1.2     PRIMUS and Hyperon
A number of the weakness applications pursued here are closely related to the
Hyperon AGI framework or associated PRIMUS cognitive architecture ... so
a brief review of these constructs will be useful at this point, before launching
into technical particulars.
    In the ongoing pursuit of human-level Artificial General Intelligence (AGI)
and ultimately Artificial Superintelligence (ASI), a variety of approaches are cur-
rently being pursued, ranging from end-to-end deep learning to purely symbolic
systems and hybrid cognitive architectures. PRIMUS/Hyperon distinguishes it-
self as a broadly integrative, metagraph-centered framework that brings together
symbolic reasoning (e.g. PLN), probabilistic inference, sub-symbolic learning
(e.g. MOSES), attention dynamics (ECAN), planning and motivational con-
trol (MetaMo) under a single shared knowledge store. Its Hyperon runtime–
built on the MeTTa language and Metta-IL with GSLT semantics–provides the
high-performance substrate for incremental, asynchronous module interaction
and approximate stochastic dynamic programming orchestration. By unifying
these diverse AI algorithms through a common cognitive-synergy paradigm and
(based on the work given here) now a common weakness-based biasing frame-
work, PRIMUS/Hyperon offers a scalable pathway for combining the strengths
of today’s neural, statistical, and symbolic methods, positioning it as a ex-
tremely promising approach (in our admittedly biased view) in the roadmap
toward robust AGI and the longer-term goal of ASI.

1.2.1   Weakness as a Unifying Bias in PRIMUS
PRIMUS interleaves two cognitive meta-dynamics over a shared collection of
Atomspaces–stored in in-RAM MORK instances and distributed via DAS–both
driven by MeTTa scripts: goal-driven cognition, in which the MetaMo motiva-
tional framework maintains and prioritizes goals to invoke planning, inference,
learning and decision routines; and background cognition, where unsupervised
pattern mining, concept formation and evolutionary processes continuously en-
rich the Atomspaces involved.
    Within both of these meta-dynamics, PRIMUS leverages a suite of AI algo-
rithms, designed for close and synergetic operation. One of the key purposes
and themes of this paper is to explain how PRIMUS’s various AI algorithms can
be guided by weakness as a principle, thus bringing them even closer together
than has been the case in historical PRIMUS prototypes.
    As we will see here, for instance,

   • Probabilistic Logical Networks (PLN) – PRIMUS’s core method for han-
     dling declarative knowledge – can use weakness or SVM-guided inference
     to rank and select only the simplest, highest-confidence derivations for
     both goal-driven proofs and background proof-mining, keeping the belief
     graph lean and reusable.

   • Evolutionary program learning, via the MOSES framework – PRIMUS’s


                                        15
        core method for handling procedural knowledge – extends classical genetic
        algorithms with Kraft-type weakness priors and adaptive schema theorems
        to evolve compact, high-utility codelets for control and synthesis tasks
   • Concept blending – a core PRIMUS tool for creativity – can apply cate-
     gorical blending routines parameterized by chosen weakness semirings to
     invent novel concepts, merging existing ontologies in a controlled manner
   • Economic attention allocation (ECAN) – PRIMUS’s key method for man-
     aging high level resource distribution – can diffuse Short-Term and Long-
     Term Importance values via a commutative quantale ODE system, yield-
     ing chaotic attractor dynamics that model sustained, structured atten-
     tional focus under resource constraints.
   • The MetaMo motivational framework – which guided PRIMUS’s goal-
     directed meta-dynamic – orchestrates these subsystems by integrating lo-
     cal weakness gradients into short-term importance and confidence signals,
     focusing effort on under-explained subgoals and flagging high-weakness re-
     gions for future objectives, thus closing the loop between inference, learn-
     ing and decision-making
   Across these and other cognitive algorithms, PRIMUS’s hallmark cognitive
synergy emerges from embedding these diverse algorithms within an approxi-
mate stochastic dynamic programming template. As detailed in the Patterns of
Cognition paper [Goe21d] , each algorithm in the PRIMUS palette –from for-
ward/backward PLN chaining to MOSES evolution, ECAN attention dynamics
and concept formation–can be formalized as a discrete decision system on typed
metagraphs, optimized via greedy or stochastic DP processes and efficiently im-
plemented through hylomorphisms and chronomorphisms. The weakness frame-
work provides the quantale-based Occam simplicity measures that ground these
DP formulations, yielding pragmatic second- and third-order approximations to
higher-order probabilistic reasoning suitable for real-time AGI under resource
limitations.

1.2.2     Cognitive Synergy and the Structuring Role of Weakness
A central organizing principle in PRIMUS is cognitive synergy: the notion that
diverse reasoning and learning processes–each with its own inductive biases and
computational trade-offs–can compensate for one another’s blind spots, yield-
ing emergent problem-solving capabilities greater than the sum of individual
modules. Realizing such synergy in practice requires coordinating when and
how modules share partial results, hypotheses, and critiques. Here the con-
cept of weakness, defined as quantitative measures of under-determination or
under-support for a candidate hypothesis, provides a unifying scaffold. Weak-
ness scores guide attention selection (focusing resources on the most uncertain
regions of the graph), priority scheduling (interleaving fast heuristic scans with
deeper logical proofs), and belief revision (deciding when to retract or rein-
force inferences). Because weakness is computed uniformly across modules, it


                                        16
becomes straightforward to tune synergy pathways, compare alternative orches-
tration strategies, and formally analyze convergence properties.

1.2.3    The Hyperon Implementation Infrastructure
To bring PRIMUS from design to scalable implementation, we developed Hy-
peron, a high-performance metagraph runtime and toolkit engineered for cog-
nitive architectures. Hyperon encapsulates the shared knowledge graph in a
memory-mapped, versioned store supporting atomic micro-transactions, fine-
grained subscriptions, and lock-free concurrent updates. Its event-driven ex-
ecution engine dispatches updates to registered cognitive operators–each run-
ning in its own lightweight coroutine–with prioritization driven by weakness
and salience metrics. Hyperon also provides a rich set of built-in utilities for
graph querying, incremental consistency checking, and distributed deployment,
enabling researchers to experiment with large-scale PRIMUS networks on multi-
core and cluster-based infrastructures with minimal boilerplate.

1.2.4    MeTTa, Metta-IL, and Exploration of Weak-Model Languages
At the heart of Hyperon lies the MeTTa meta-programming language and its
underlying Metta-IL, whose formal semantics are grounded in Graph-Structured
Lambda Theory (GSLT). MeTTa’s homoiconic syntax treats graph nodes, hy-
peredges, and patterns as first-class values, allowing concise expression of PRIMUS
algorithms–whether defining a weakness propagation operator or writing a cus-
tom attention policy–in a uniform language. The flexibility of MeTTa’s parsing
and macro facilities further permits rapid prototyping of domain-specific dialects
tailored to particular forms of uncertainty. For example, in this work we intro-
duce the Constructive Duality-Driven Domain (CoDD) language atop MeTTa,
enabling seamless experimentation with weak-model learning formalisms by
defining new truth-value constructors, evidence-aggregation rules, and inference
schemata without modifying the core runtime. This extensibility makes MeTTa
an ideal substrate both for implementing PRIMUS’s cognitive synergy mecha-
nisms and for theoretical exploration of alternative weak reasoning paradigms.



2       A General Categorial Notion of Weakness
We now review Bennett’s notion of weakness, and explain how it extend to a
more general notion of weakness formulated via quantales. We will save the
quantum-theoretic extensions for Chapter 11, as these involve some additional
nuances.

2.1     Bennett’s Notion of Weakness
Bennett’s notion of “weakness” (which in our categorial perspective becomes
”set-theoretic weakness”) is a formalized way of choosing, among several expla-


                                       17
nations that all fit observed data, the one that makes the fewest assumptions.
Imagine you have some observed facts, and you want to pick a rule or a hy-
pothesis that explains those facts. You could choose a very specific rule that
only applies in a tiny number of situations, or you could choose a more general
rule that applies in many situations. Bennett observes that the most “weak”
rule is the one that rules out the fewest possible worlds; equivalently, it is the
one that applies in the largest set of possible scenarios consistent with the data.
By picking the “weakest” explanation, you are following what we would call an
Occam-style principle: prefer the explanation that generalizes best because it
makes the fewest extra commitments beyond the observed evidence.1
    The key insight here is that via formulating weakness in an axiomatic way
focused on semantics, one comes up with a more fundamental depiction of ”Oc-
cam’s razor style heuristics” than the ”minimum description length” approach
more commonly taken. MDL approaches emerge as a way of achieving or ap-
proximating weakness under appropriate assumptions.

Formal Definition. Let U be a finite universe of possible worlds. A hypothesis
(or explanation) is a subset H ⊆ U of worlds in which the hypothesis is true.
Equivalently, we view a hypothesis as a condition that rules out all worlds not
in H.

   1. Define the weakness of a hypothesis H by

                                             w(H) = |H|

       where |H| is the number of worlds in H. A larger w(H) means the hy-
       pothesis is weaker, since it excludes fewer possible worlds.
   2. Suppose we have a collection of hypotheses {Hi } i∈I that are all consistent
      with some observed data D; that is, each Hi contains at least those worlds
      in which D holds. Then Bennett’s criterion is to choose the hypothesis
      H ∗ satisfying
                               H ∗ = arg max w(Hi ).
                                                 Hi ⊇D

       In other words, among all hypotheses that explain D, pick the one that
       has the largest cardinality w(Hi ).

Properties.
    • Monotonicity under Refinement. If a hypothesis H1 is a refinement
      of H2 (meaning H1 ⊆ H2 ), then

                              w(H1 ) = |H1 | ≤ |H2 | = w(H2 ).
    1 As we will note in section 2.6 below, this is our own take on the relation between Bennett’s

weakness and the classical ”Occam’s Razor” idea, and Bennett’s own perspective is slightly
different.



                                               18
      Thus more specific explanations (which exclude more worlds) are strictly
      stronger and hence have smaller weakness.
   • Lattice Bounds. The collection of all subsets of U forms a Boolean
     algebra (and in particular a lattice) under intersection and union. For any
     two hypotheses H1 , H2 ⊆ U ,
                                                            
         H1 ∩ H2 ⊆ H1 , H2 =⇒ w(H1 ∩ H2 ) ≤ min w(H1 ), w(H2 ) ,

      and
                                                        
         H1 ∪ H2 ⊇ H1 , H2      =⇒     w(H1 ∪ H2 ) ≥ max w(H1 ), w(H2 ) .

      Hence w(·) is order-preserving (isotone) on this lattice.
   • Connection to MDL instantiations of Occam’s Razor. Bennett
     shows that selecting the maximum-weakness hypothesis is equivalent to
     choosing the hypothesis of minimum description length when one places a
     constraint on the “vocabulary size” of descriptions. In particular, if there
     are only finitely many distinct hypotheses whose descriptions have length
     at most L, then the one of greatest weakness among those corresponds to
     the one of minimal description length. This is how Bennett’s weakness cri-
     terion recovers the standard MDL Occam-style principle [Ris78]: shorter
     descriptions (weaker hypotheses) are preferred. We will elaborate on this
     aspect in Chapter 5 below.
   • Statistical Generalization. Suppose there is an underlying (unknown)
     probability distribution Pr on U . Then the probability mass of a hypoth-
     esis H is                            X
                               Pr(H) =        Pr(u).
                                            u∈H

      If we require that each hypothesis Hi covering the data D also satisfies

                                     Pr(Hi ) ≥ p0 ,

      for some threshold p0 , then among those Hi , the one of maximal weakness
      w(Hi ) will also tend to have larger probability Pr(Hi ), assuming worlds
      in U are roughly equally likely. In Bennett’s original work, this is made
      precise via bounds showing that with high probability, a sample-derived
      hypothesis whose weakness is large will generalize better to unseen data.
      We will explore the application of weakness to probability via a closely
      related but slightly different route below.

2.2    Probabilistic Weakness as a Generalization of Ben-
       nett’s Measure
We will be generalizing Bennett’s weakness quite broadly below, but let us
begin with the most straightforward extension, to a natively probabilistic form
of weakness.


                                       19
    To generalize this to a setting where worlds have different likelihoods or
importances, we have to switch from looking at individual worlds to looking at
pairs of worlds.
    To see why, consider that the weakest hypothesis is supposed to be the
one that ”restricts the least” – and when we say ”restrict the least,” what
we really mean is ”collapse as few important distinctions as possible.” Under
a uniform distribution, counting single worlds left ”possible” captures that:
you simply count how many worlds survive your filter. But once worlds carry
different probabilities, a single-world count no longer tells you how much overall
possibility you’re throwing away–because throwing out a high-probability world
is far more restrictive than throwing out a low-probability one.
    What we can do instead is view each pair (u, v) of worlds as occurring with
joint probability p(u) p(v) under independent draws. Summing those probabil-
ities over H yields                      X
                             wp (H) =          p(u) p(v),
                                      (u,v)∈H

which reduces to |H| when p is uniform.
    What we are asking here is: ”If I draw two independent samples from my
belief distribution, what is the chance that they end up in the same collapsed
equivalence class?” That probability directly measures how many and how sig-
nificant the collapsed distinctions are, in exactly the sense of ”restricting the
least.” Minimizing that pair-collision probability guarantees you’re preserving as
much of the total probability mass of distinctions as possible. In other words,
you’re keeping your model from merging two independently likely scenarios,
which is exactly what you want if your goal is to restrict your hypothesis space
as little as you can.
    This provides a smooth, interpretable simplicity bias that can be estimated
and optimized in statistical settings. We will revisit this concept below as a
specialization of a broader notion of quantale-weakness, but it also has a simple
meaning and definition on its own.

2.3     Weakness, Logical Entropy and Graphtropy
.
    Next we clarify and explore some relationships between weakness and a few
other measures of the informativeness of mappings. This will be interesting and
useful in itself, and will also help us find the right way to generalize weakness
into a category-theoretic setting.
    The core relationship we want to look at here relates to logical entropy [Ell13]
– and then a generalization of this notion called graphtropy [Goe19].

2.3.1   Weakness and Logical Entropy
Logical entropy is a different concept from the more familiar Shannon entropy;
it quantifies the probability that two independent draws from a distribution
yield different outcomes. more formally:


                                        20
Definition. Let Π = {B1 , . . . , Bm } be a finite partition of a sample space and
write p(Bj ) for the probability of block Bj . Then the logical entropy of Π is
                                                m
                                                X
                            h(Π) = 1 −                p(Bj )2 .
                                                j=1

    The connection with weakness should be conceptually somewhat clear –
weakness measures, in a rough sense, how general or indistinct a rule or condi-
tion is ... and one can view this as related to, specifically, the probability that
two draws fall into the same block of a partition or the same satisfaction set of
a rule.
    More precisely, we may say that:
   • If a partition (or rule) is maximally weak, then everything is in one block –
     so two draws surely lie in the same block, and logical entropy (the chance
     they differ) is nzero.
   • If a partition is maximally strong, then each element is isolated, so two
     draws surely differ, and logical entropy is near its maximum.
   Formally, it is not hard to see that for any finite partition or rule,
                       weakness + logical entropy = 1.
Thus, in the context of partitions, these are basically two ways of looking at the
same thing.

From Probabilistic Weakness to Partition-Weakness To understand
why this identity makes sense, we first elaborate the notion of ”partition weak-
ness” as a special case of the weakness of probability distributions.
    In the continuous or distributional setting, the general notion of weakness
measures how large an event H is under a probability distribution. Concretely,
if (Ω, p) is a probability space and H ⊆ Ω is any measurable event, then
                                                 
                              w(H) = Pr ω ∈ H .
                                           ω∼p

When we draw two independent samples ω, ω 0 ∼ p, the product space (Ω ×
Ω, p ⊗ p) inherits the same definition:
                                          (ω, ω 0 ) ∈ H .
                                                      
                        w(H) =        Pr
                                      0
                                   (ω,ω )∼p⊗p

Theorem 1. Let Ω be a finite set with probability measure p, and let Π =
{B1 , . . . , Bm } be a partition of Ω. Define the diagonal event
                HΠ = { (ω, ω 0 ) ∈ Ω × Ω : ∃ j, ω ∈ Bj ∧ ω 0 ∈ Bj }.
Then the weakness of HΠ under the product measure equals the partition-weakness:
                                                          m

                                           21
Proof. By independence,
               Pr ω ∈ Bj ∧ ω 0 ∈ Bj = p(Bj ) × p(Bj ) = p(Bj )2 .

Since the blocks Bj × Bj are disjoint and cover HΠ , summing over j gives

which is exactly the definition of partition-weakness.
Thus partition-weakness is nothing more than the general probabilistic weak-
ness of the single event ”two independent draws fall in the same block,” under
the IID assumption. It does not rely on the finite-universe counting approach
of Bennett’s original weakness, but emerges naturally from the distributional
setting.
    It then follows immediately that
Theorem 2. For any finite partition Π,
                                w(HΠ ) + h(Π) = 1.
Proof. By definition,
                                   m

                     h(Π) = 1 −          p(Bj )2 = 1 − w(HΠ ).
                                   j=1

Rearranging gives w(HΠ ) + h(Π) = 1, as claimed.

2.3.2   Graphtropy and Weakness
The relation between weakness and logical entropy extends naturally beyond
the case of partitions, to the situation of ”distinction graphs” indicating a given
perceiving system’s capability to distinguish between two entities asymmetri-
cally [Goe19]. We review the notions of distinction graphs and graphtropy, then
show that graphtropy is exactly the weakness of the non-distinction event in the
product sample space.

2.3.3   Distinction Graphs
A distinction graph is a simple graph that encodes which pairs of states an
observer cannot distinguish.
Definition 1 (Distinction Graph). Let U be a finite set of states. A distinction
graph is an undirected graph
                                   G = ( U, E ),
where each edge (u, v) ∈ E indicates that the observer cannot distinguish state
u from state v. We assume reflexivity, so (u, u) ∈ E for all u ∈ U .
    Thus G divides states into indistinguishability classes, but need not be tran-
sitive (so the relation may not form a true partition).


                                           22
2.3.4   Graphtropy
Graphtropy measures how coarse (weak) the observer’s distinctions are by com-
puting the probability that two independent draws from U land in an indistin-
guishable pair.

Definition 2 (Graphtropy). Let G = (U, E) be a distinction graph on a finite
set U . The graphtropy of G is

                                               |E|
                                 h(G) =             ,
                                              |U |2

where |E| counts each reflexive edge (u, u) and each undirected edge (u, v) once.
   Equivalently, if ω, ω 0 are drawn uniformly and independently from U , then

                             h(G) = Pr (ω, ω 0 ) ∈ E .
                                                   


Graphtropy as Weakness We now relate graphtropy to the general notion
of weakness under a product distribution.
Theorem 3. Let G = (U, E) be a distinction graph on finite U and equip U × U
with the uniform distribution. Define the non-distinction event

                    HG = { (ω, ω 0 ) ∈ U × U | (ω, ω 0 ) ∈ E}.

Then the general weakness of HG under the product measure equals the graph-
tropy of G:

                                       (ω, ω 0 ) ∈ HG = h(G).
                                                    
              w(HG ) =     0
                              Pr
                         (ω,ω )∼Unif(U ×U )

Proof. By definition of weakness in a probability space,
                                                 |HG |
                      w(HG ) = Pr (ω, ω 0 ) ∈ HG =       .
                                                   |U |2

But HG = E, so |HG | = |E|, and hence

                                        |E|
                            w(HG ) =         = h(G).
                                       |U |2



    This result shows that graphtropy is simply the probability that two inde-
pendent samples are not distinguished by the observer, i.e. the weakness of the
non-distinction hypothesis. A high graphtropy indicates a very weak (coarse)
distinction graph, while a low graphtropy reflects a strong (fine) set of distinc-
tions.



                                        23
2.4     Lifting Weakness to a Categorical Setting
The notion of weakness is already very broad conceptually, however its specific
mathematical formalization is tied to elementary set-theoretic constructs, which
makes the elaboration of some conceptually natural applications unnecessarily
laborious on a technical level.
    With this in mind, we ask here: Can the notion of weakness be lifted to
category theory, where objects and morphisms are abstracted beyond sets and
functions? That is: We want to keep the intuitive concept the same, but formal-
ize it in a more abstract way that will make it easier for us to see the applications
in different sorts of domains and to different sorts of AI systems.
    We will start with ordinary categories and then move on to enriched cate-
gories and quantales, which turn out to give the most useful version of weakness
we have found so far.
    In a categorical framework, conceptually we replace ”rules that match pat-
terns” by ”objects representing propositions or inference rules”, and we say that
one object is weaker than another if there exists a morphism from the weaker
object to the stronger one. Intuitively:
   • Objects in our category are contexts or conditions under which a rule
     applies.
   • A morphism f : A − B means ”whenever condition A holds, condition B
     also holds.” Hence A is weaker (less restrictive) than B because B implies
     A in reverse, or equivalently, A factors through B.
   • In this way, weakness becomes the existence of a morphism. The weaker
     object can feed into the stronger object via a morphism.
    Thus ”universal weakness” corresponds to an initial object (which has a
unique arrow to every other object), and ”absolute strength” corresponds to
a terminal object (to which every object admits a unique arrow). By framing
weakness in categorical terms, we obtain a powerful, abstract language to talk
about generality and specificity that applies uniformly across many domains:
sets, topological spaces, logical theories, and so on.

2.4.1   Defining Weakness via Morphisms
Definition 3 (Weakness Ordering). Let C and D be objects in W. We say C
is weaker than or equal to D, denoted

                                     C  D,

if and only if there exists a morphism f : C → D in W. Dually, D is stronger
than or equal to C if C → D.
Remark. Since morphisms compose and identities exist,  is a preorder on
Ob(W). We may pass to the associated partial order by quotienting out isomor-
phisms if desired.


                                         24
2.4.2   Initial Object as Maximally Weak
Definition 4 (Universal Weakness). An object 0 ∈ Ob(W) is universally weak
if it is initial; that is, for every C ∈ Ob(W), there is a unique ! : 0toC. In
symbols:
                                  ∀ C, ∃! (! : 0 → C).
    Universally weak means ”applies to every situation” – it is the least restric-
tive condition. Dually, a terminal object is ”universally strong”, matching no
more than all other objects do.

2.4.3   Simple Examples
Example 1 (Sets and Subsets). Let P(X) be the power-set of some fixed set
X. Define a category WX whose objects are subsets A ⊆ X, and a morphism
f : A → B exists iff A ⊆ B (choose exactly one arrow when that condition
holds). Then weakness A  B means A ⊆ B. The initial object is ∅, since
∅ ⊆ B for every B ⊆ X. The terminal object is X.
Example 2 (Types and Coercions). In a programming-language category, ob-
jects are types, and morphisms are implicit coercions or subtyping relations. A
type T1 is weaker (more general) than T2 if there is a coercion T1 → T2 . The
void type (which has no inhabitants) is often initial, and a universal top type
(e.g. Object) is terminal.

2.4.4   Characterizing Weakness in a Category with Subobjects
In a more general category C with subobjects, we can interpret conditions as
subobjects of a fixed universe object U . Then:
Definition 5 (Category of Subobjects). Let U be a fixed object in a category
C. The subobject category SubC (U ) has:
   • Objects: Monomorphisms m : A ,→ U (each A is a subobject of U ).

   • Morphisms: Given m1 : A1 ,→ U and m2 : A2 ,→ U , a morphism f : (A1 ,→
     U ) − (A2 ,→ U ) is any arrow f : A1 − A2 in C such that m2 ◦ f = m1 . In
     effect, A1 factors through A2 .
Composition and identities come from C.

Proposition 1. In SubC (U ), we have (A1 ,→ U )  (A2 ,→ U ) if and only if
A1 is a subobject of A2 (i.e. there exists a monomorphism A1 ,→ A2 such that
the triangle commutes).
Proof. By definition of morphisms in SubC (U ), A1 → A2 must satisfy A2 ,→
U ◦ f = A1 ,→ U . Since both A1 ,→ U and A2 ,→ U are monos, this forces f
itself to be a monomorphism A1 ,→ A2 . Conversely, any such monic f yields a
valid arrow in the subobject category.


                                       25
2.4.5   Weakness as Factorization
The following observation is elementary but worth calling out:
Theorem 4. Let C be any category with a chosen object U , and consider
SubC (U ) with objects mA : A ,→ U , mB : B ,→ U . Then A is weaker than
B (in the sense of the subobject category) if and only if A factors through B,
i.e. there is a morphism f : A − B in C such that mB ◦ f = mA . Moreover, the
initial object is the zero subobject 0 ,→ U (if it exists), and the terminal object
is U ,→ U itself.
Proof. By the definition of morphisms in SubC (U ), A  B precisely means there
exists f : A → B with mB ◦ f = mA . Such an f is necessarily a monomorphism
(since mA is mono and mB is mono), so A is literally included in B as a sub-
object. The initial object 0 ,→ U factors through every A ,→ U via the unique
arrow 0 − A, and U ,→ U is terminal since every subobject A ,→ U factors
uniquely through the identity on U .
Remark. In many familiar categories (e.g. Set or any topos), 0 is the empty
set or initial object, and U is the whole universe. This matches the intuition
that the empty condition is the weakest (it holds nowhere), and the universal
condition holds everywhere and is strongest.
    Overall, by formulating weakness as the existence of a morphism in a cat-
egory of conditions or subobjects, we find that that the abstract categorical
framework naturally captures the ordering of rules or contexts by generality.
An initial object is universally weak (applies everywhere), and a terminal ob-
ject is universally strong. This port of weakness into category theory is both
conceptually meaningful – because it matches our intuitive notion of ”applies
to more cases” – and useful, because it applies uniformly across diverse fields:
logic, type theory, toposes, and beyond.

2.5     Enriched-Category Weakness.
Next we may articulate the generalized notion of weakness in the context of
enriched categories, which have a notion of (e.g. quantitative) measure or val-
uation associated with them. This turns out to open up a host of practical
applications in a very natural way.
    The formulation of weakness in this setting looks a bit more like the logical
entropy or probabilistic weakness than like the traditional weakness, in that it
involves pairs of objects. We will reiterate why it comes out this way below,
after going through the formal details.
    Let C be a category enriched over a commutative quantale (V, ⊗, e, ≤), with
object set U . Suppose we have a measure µ : U − V . Then the weakness of any
subobject (subgraph) H ⊆ U × U is defined by
                                    M                
                         w(H) =           µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) ,
                                   (u,v)∈H

where ⊕ is the quantale join in V .


                                        26
Quantale Weakness and Pairwise Distinctions An intuitive justification
for this definition would be as follows (following the justification of the definition
of probabilistic weakness given above). In the quantale formulation, weakness
measures how many–and how serious–binary distinctions a relation fails to make.
This naturally leads to summing over pairs of objects rather than singletons:
                                      M                 
                          w(H) =              µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) ,
                                      (u,v)∈H

where H ⊆ U × U is the relation of undistinguished pairs, µ : U → V is an
object valuation, and ⊕, ⊗ are the join and multiplication in the commutative
quantale V .

   • Distinctions are pairwise. Each time the relation collapses two objects
     u and v, it removes exactly that distinction. Counting singletons cannot
     track which objects are conflated.
   • Joint importance. Weighting each pair by µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) ensures that
     confusing two high-value objects contributes more to overall weakness than
     confusing two low-value ones.

     Thus, whenever we need to know which distinctions are lost and how impor-
tant they are, we work over pairs. The quantale framework then gives a flexible,
uniform way to assign and aggregate those pairwise weights.
     This brings us back to the rough verbal gloss given at the start of the paper,
i.e.
     Given any system for relating and valuing entities, think of an individual
binary relationship as high-prominence to the extent two things it joins are
both valuable. A collection of binary relationships is then weak to the extent it
contains a sufficient aggregate of high-prominence relationships.

Formal Reconciliation with Bennett’s Original Weakness To articulate
the reduction of quantale weakness to the original Bennett weakness: Basically
we are looking at the special case where we choose the real-addition quantale

               V = (R≥0 , ×, 1, ≤),    ⊗ = ×,       ⊕ = +,   µ(u) ≡ 1,

Here we obtain                            X
                             w(H) =               1 = |H|,
                                        (u,v)∈H

and we exactly reproduce Bennett’s “count the remaining worlds” measure of
weakness. Thus our enriched-category formulation subsumes the classical set-
theoretic notion as the special case of unweighted counting, while extending
seamlessly to arbitrary measures of weight and join in any commutative quan-
tale.




                                          27
2.6    Relation Between Bennett’s and Occam’s Razors
How these various mathematical notions relate to the broader philosophical no-
tion of ”Occam’s Razor” is an interesting question, verging more into philosophy
and history rather than mathematics.
    What William of Occam originally wrote (in Latin) was ”plurality should
not be posited without necessity” – which is generally interpreted conceptually
as the principle that, among explanations that equally fit the data, one should
prefer the hypothesis that introduces the fewest unnecessary assumptions.
    Bennett contrasts the three approaches:

   • Simplicity-maxing (simp-maxing) refers to selecting the hypothesis with
     the shortest descriptive length, measured for example by bit-count or pro-
     gram size. This strategy penalizes every additional symbol equally, with-
     out regard for which worlds it rules out.
   • Weakness-maxing (w-maxing) refers to selecting the hypothesis that im-
     poses the weakest constraints, i.e. that admits the largest set of possible
     worlds. We might call this set-theoretic weakness criterion ”Bennett’s
     Razor” – it is a semantic measure of parsimony: two hypotheses of equal
     length can differ in how many worlds they exclude.
   • Scale-maxing refers to preferring methods that leverage increased compu-
     tational resources, data, or model capacity. It is a brute-force approach,
     orthogonal to any simplicity measure.

   One could also identify other such principles e.g.

   • Lulz-maxing (aka ”Zarathustra’s Razor”) refers to preferring methods
     that are maximally entertaining to some particular entity, for instance
     Zarathustra

    Although all four meta-approaches guide hypothesis selection, one may say
that simp-maxing and w-maxing instantiate distinct senses of Occam-ish ”sim-
plicity”: one over symbols, the other over possibilities, while scale-maxing relies
on sheer scale rather than parsimony .. and lulz-maxing does something else
different.
    Each of these four meta-approaches can be encompassed within quantale
weakness if one sets up the quantale appropriately. Let (Q, ≤, ⊗) be a com-
mutative quantale of constraint costs, and let each constraint c carry a weight
w(c) ∈ Q. For any hypothesis H, its weakness is defined by
                                            _
                            weakness(H) =       w(c),
                                             c∈H
      W
where is the quantale join. By selecting different quantales and weight func-
tions, one recovers each of the following meta-approaches:



                                        28
   • Simplicity-maxing. Take Qsimp = (N ∪ {∞}, ≤) with monoid operation +,
     and set w(c) = 1 for each symbol or token in the description. Then
                                              X
                             weakness(H) =           1
                                                   symbol∈H

      is exactly the description length, so minimizing weakness selects the short-
      est hypothesis.
   • Weakness-maxing. Let Qweak = (2W , ⊆) be the lattice of sets of possible
     worlds, and set w(c) = {worlds satisfying c}. Then
                                              \
                             weakness(H) =        w(c)
                                                   c∈H

      is the set of worlds admitted by H, and choosing H to minimize the size of
      this intersection (or equivalently maximize its cardinality) yields the least
      constraining hypothesis.
   • Scale-maxing. Use Qscale = (R, ≤) and define w(M ) = −resource(M ) for
     each model or mechanism M . Then

                          weakness(M ) = max(−resource(c))
                                             c∈M

      encodes negative resource usage, so minimizing weakness picks the model
      with maximal resource allocation.
   • Lulz-maxing. Choose Qlulz = (R, ≤) and set w(c) = −fun(c) for an enter-
     tainment value function fun. Then minimizing
                                          _
                           weakness(H) =     (−fun(c))
                                              c∈H

      selects the hypothesis that maximizes entertainment value.

    However, as we will show in Section ??, there is a quantale isomorphism be-
tween Qweak and Qsimp . The set-theoretic weakness measure and the description-
length measure coincide, exhibiting a formal isomorphism between weakness-
maxing and simplicity-maxing, which is conceptually because these are two dif-
ferent ways of manifesting the conceptual notion of Occam’s Razor. The general
notion of quantale weakness, on the other hand, maintains something of the gen-
eral structure of Occam’s Razor, but can be used much more broadly than any
reasonable interpretation of William of Occam’s original (appropriately terse)
formulation.

2.7    Syntactic versus Semantic Weakness: Intuitive Recap
While we will wait till Section ?? to formally articulate the relationship between
Bennett’s weakness (w-max) and MDL-style weakness (simp-max), it may be
worth zooming out a bit and giving a brief conceptual re-cap of this point.


                                        29
  The MDL/ simp-max approach interprets Occam’s Razor syntactically, using
measures like:

   • The number of symbols required for description (Minimum Description
     Length)
   • The parameter count in a model
   • The perceived mathematical complexity

   These measures are fundamentally syntactic—they depend on our choice of
representation rather than the actual content of the explanation.
   Bennett’s concept of “weakness” (w-max) represents a fundamental shift in
perspective. Rather than asking “how concisely can we describe this?”, weakness
asks: “how many possibilities does this explanation leave open?”
   Going back to intuitive basics, consider two explanations for why a light
turned on:
   Explanation A: “Someone flipped the switch”

   • Excludes millions of alternative scenarios
   • Highly specific ⇒ STRONG
   Explanation B: “Something happened involving electricity”

   • Preserves many possibilities (switch, timer, sensor, surge, etc.)
   • Highly general ⇒ WEAK
    The crucial insight: Explanation B is “weaker” precisely because it commits
to less about the actual state of affairs.
    That is: Bennett’s weakness is committment-based rather than symbol-
based.
    Consider these equivalent propositions:
   • “The switch was flipped”
   • “El interruptor fue accionado”

   • “Der Schalter wurde umgelegt”
   • “01010011 01110111 01101001 01110100 01100011 01101000”
   Traditional MDL assigns different complexity values based on encoding length.
Weakness recognizes that these make identical commitments about the world—
they eliminate the same set of alternative possibilities.
   To see intuitively why these come out to basically the same thing – presaging
the math we’ll go through later – consider what happens when we describe
something:



                                       30
  1. Short descriptions must be vague: With only n bits available, we can
     distinguish at most 2n possibilities. Any description must therefore leave
     many options open.
  2. Precise descriptions must be long: To uniquely identify one option
     among N possibilities requires at least log2 (N ) bits.

Principle 1 (The Fundamental Trade-off). Specificity costs bits.

   So the proportionality between simp-max and w-max emerges naturally, and
the following crude argument (to be run through later in more detail) is elemen-
tary:

   • A hypothesis with weakness W (leaving W possibilities open)
   • Can be uniquely identified using log2 (N/W ) bits, where N is the total
     number of possibilities
   • Therefore: high weakness ⇔ few bits needed ⇔ short description

   More formally:

               weakness(H) = W                                                (1)
                                           
                                        N
                    MDL(H) ≈ log2               = log2 (N ) − log2 (W )       (2)
                                        W

    Thus MDL and log(weakness) differ only by a constant.
    One of Bennett’s key contributions in his thesis was showing this relationship
is not approximate but exact under reasonable assumptions:

  1. Finite description space: With symbols from a finite alphabet, there
     are exactly V L programs of length L

  2. Unique assignment: The weakest hypothesis among those expressible
     with ≤ L symbols necessarily claims the shortest available description
  3. Information-theoretic necessity: You literally cannot encode a stronger
     (more specific) hypothesis using fewer bits—the information theory bound
     prevents it

Remark (The Parking Lot Analogy). Think of descriptions as parking spots
and hypotheses as vehicles of different sizes. The largest vehicles (weakest hy-
potheses) necessarily occupy the most accessible spots (shortest descriptions) by
geometric necessity.
   This equivalence is more than a mathematical curiosity:

   • Algorithmic flexibility: Searching for simple descriptions (MDL) auto-
     matically yields weak hypotheses


                                        31
   • Computational choice: Searching for weak hypotheses automatically
     yields simple descriptions
   • Implementation freedom: One can optimize whichever is computation-
     ally more tractable

   In a way the universe appears to have this connection built into its information-
theoretic fabric: entities that are easiest to describe are precisely those that
make the fewest assumptions about the world. Occam’s Razor succeeds not
by philosophical decree but because information theory enforces it–you cannot
have a short description without high weakness, nor high weakness without a
short description.

2.8    Natural Compositionality and Quantale Weakness
Continuing the intuitive recap of the formal constructs we’ve presented, one
may also think about weakness combinatorially. For systems with multiple
components:
   • Traditional approach: Requires ad hoc methods to aggregate compo-
     nent complexities
   • Weakness approach: The weakest explanation of the whole naturally
     emerges from combining the weakest explanations of the parts
   This compositionality is elegantly captured by the quantale formulation –
which we have expressed intuitively as
   Given any system for relating and valuing entities, think of an individual
binary relationship as high-prominence to the extent two things it joins are
both valuable. A collection of binary relationships is then weak to the extent it
contains a sufficient aggregate of high-prominence relationships.
   The initially opaque notion of “high-prominence relationships” becomes per-
haps clearer when reformulated:

Remark (Prominent vs. Obscure Relationships). “Prominent” relationships
are connections between elements that both have high measure values—they stand
out or are prominent given the base measure µ. A hypothesis is weak when it
primarily consists of such prominent relationships—it’s highlighting connections
between already-significant elements rather than revealing hidden structure.

   This perspective directly connects to:
   • Probability: Weak explanations have high probability (many instantia-
     tions exist)
   • Information: Weak explanations convey little information (minimal sur-
     prise)




                                       32
   The central equation:
                                     M
                           w(H) =             [µ(u) ⊗ µ(v)]
                                    (u,v)∈H

   translates to a straightforward algorithm:
  1. Enumerate all pairs (u, v) related by hypothesis H

  2. Measure the “prominence” of each element of the pair-set: µ(u), µ(v)
  3. Combine these measures: µ(u) ⊗ µ(v)
                                                   L
  4. Aggregate via the supremum operation:
   Intuitive interpretation: A hypothesis is weak if it primarily connects
elements that were already prominent (had high measure values) individually.
   Weakness-based approaches to AI, which will consume most of this book,
are then interesting because they optimize for:
  1. Minimal assumptions: Avoiding unnecessary commitments

  2. Broad applicability: Maintaining generality
  3. Robustness: Reducing overspecification
   These properties directly benefit:
   • Generalization: Weak explanations transfer to more situations

   • Fault tolerance: Fewer specific commitments mean fewer failure modes
   • Efficiency: Computational resources aren’t wasted on irrelevant details
   Overall, weakness represents the “right” formulation of Occam’s Razor be-
cause:

  1. Semantic focus: It measures assumption content, not representational
     artifacts

  2. Natural composition: Weak components naturally yield weak systems
  3. Theoretical unity: Probability, information, and complexity emerge as
     facets of a single framework
  4. Practical guidance: Optimizing for weakness produces more general,
     robust solutions

    Traditional “simplicity” was always gesturing toward this deeper principle—
we prefer explanations that assume as little as necessary. Weakness makes this
intuition mathematically precise and practically actionable.



                                        33
2.9     Quantale Weakness and Bennett’s Layers
It’s also worth explicating how our notion of quantale weakness relates to the
notion of ”layered” weakness Bennett introduces in his thesis.
    In Bennett’s approach, the notion of an abstraction layer is made explicit
by indexing the cost structure with a layer L – which in our approach could
be formulated as a corresponding quantale QL . Hypotheses and constraints are
interpreted relative to L, and the weakness of a hypothesis H is defined as
                                            _
                           weakness L (H) =     ωL (c),
                                                   c∈H

where each constraint weight ωL (c) lies in the quantale QL . This explicit rela-
tivization clarifies how specificity and granularity depend on the chosen layer.
    In the present framework, different representational granularities are handled
by selecting different quantales (for example, Qbits versus Qsentences ) rather than
by introducing a separate layer parameter. Concretely, one simply writes
                                              _
                             weakness(H) =        ω(c)
                                                   c∈H

with the understanding that the chosen quantale already encodes the desired
level of abstraction. In this view, an explicit layer index is a useful notational
convention but not a mathematical necessity, since the choice of quantale im-
plicitly determines the layer of analysis.

2.10      Weakness and Hoare-Style Preconditions
2

    Next, let us explore a different twist on the conceptual foundation of weak-
ness in the theory of possible worlds.
    The notion of ”possible worlds” involved in Bennett’s original notion of weak-
ness treats as the elements of a truth-set and measures simplicity by counting
(or weighting) how many of those worlds remain undistinguished. This is com-
patible with the notion of ”possible worlds” used in Kripke semantics, where
”possible worlds” serve as states of a modal frame, but truth is evaluated via an
accessibility relation between worlds rather than by membership alone. Hoare
logic then treats program states as worlds and uses preconditions and postcon-
ditions to describe which states lead to which, effectively defining a one-step
accessibility relation under program execution.
    Integrating the categorial version of weakness into these Kripke-semantics
possible worlds turns out to be straightforward and fruitful. By viewing Hoare
preconditions as subspaces of Kripke worlds equipped with a quantale-based
weakness measure, we bring all three ideas together: Bennett”s Occam-style
world counting becomes a graded measure of state-set permissiveness, Kripke
   2 The observation in this section was made by Greg Meredith after hearing a talk presenting

aspects of an earlier version of this paper


                                             34
accessibility defines ”reachability” under program steps, and Hoare pre/post
triples emerge as constraints on which weighted world-sets guarantee correct exe-
cution. This unification lets us speak in a single formalism about how many–and
how important–initial states are collapsed or preserved by a program, yielding
a precise Occam-biased weakest-precondition framework.
    In traditional Hoare logic, a precondition is simply the set of states from
which running a program is guaranteed to achieve a desired postcondition. We
propose to view that precondition set not merely as a yes-or-no predicate, but as
a ”weakness-weighted” subspace of all possible states. By assigning each state a
numerical valuation and then aggregating those valuations over the precondition
set, we obtain a single scalar that tells us how permissive–or ”weak”–that pre-
condition really is. A larger value means the program will work correctly from
more initial states, but it also signals that the precondition is less restrictive.
    Formally, let W be the set of all states and let P ⊆ W be a precondition. In
our quantale-weakness framework we assign each state w ∈ W a valuation µ(w)
in a commutative quantale (V, ⊗, e, ≤), and define
                                           M
                                w(P ) =        µ(w),
                                         w∈P

where ⊕ is the quantale join. The quantity w(P ) measures how permissive P
is: higher values correspond to weaker (more inclusive) preconditions.
    When we combine this idea with a Kripke-style accessibility relation R ⊆
W ×W (the one-step reachability of states under program execution), we recover
the classical weakest precondition. Given a postcondition Q ⊆ W , the weakest
precondition in the usual sense is

                  Pre(Q) = { w ∈ W | ∃w0 ∈ Q, (w, w0 ) ∈ R}.

In our framework we also get a weakness score for this set:
                                       M
                       w Pre(Q) =               µ(w),
                                        w∈W, w0 ∈Q
                                         (w,w0 )∈R

which quantifies how many and how important are the states from which Q is
reachable in one step. This extra information lets us articulate statements such
as ”the program is correct for all but a low-weight subset of initial states” in a
precise way.
    Framing preconditions as weighted subobjects aligns cleanly with both modal-
logic and Hoare-logic semantics, while introducing an Occam-style simplicity
bias: we can prefer the minimally restrictive precondition P whose aggregated
weakness stays below a chosen threshold yet still guarantees correctness. In
practice, this means we can automatically search for the largest P such that
w(P ) ≤  for a given tolerance , offering a principled trade-off between gener-
ality and safety.
    Overall, embedding weakness into program logic provides a unified frame-
work for talking about preconditions, reachability, and robustness. We no longer


                                        35
ask merely ”does the program work?” but also ”how widely and how robustly
does it work across different initial scenarios?”

2.11     Quantale Weakness in a Probabilistic and Logical-
         Entropy Context
The quantale definition of weakness has an extremely clear and obvious relation-
ship to logical entropy (which is essentially equivalent to probabilistic weakness).
    To draw this out: Recall that logical entropy of a partition measures the
probability that two independent draws fall into different blocks. If W is a
finite set of outcomes with probability distribution p : W → [0, 1], and π is a
partition of W , then the logical entropy of π is
                                        X
                      h(π) =                         p(u) p(v)
                                      u,v∈W
                             u and v in different blocks

    Equivalently, as we have seen above, if we let Hπ ⊆ W × W be the indis-
tinction relation
           P      (pairs in the same block), then the same-block probability is
wp (Hπ ) = (u,v)∈Hπ p(u) p(v), and logical entropy satisfies h(π) = 1 − wp (Hπ ).
    These latter two formulas fit exactly into our quantale-weakness framework
by choosing:
   • The probability quantale (V, ⊗, ⊕, ≤) = ([0, 1], ×, +, ≤).
   • The valuation µ(u) = p(u).
   • The subobject (relation) Hπ ⊆ W × W of same-block pairs.
Then the quantale definition of weakness
                         M                               X
            w(Hπ ) =            µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) =                    p(u) p(v)
                         (u,v)∈Hπ                     (u,v)∈Hπ


is precisely the same-block probability, and its complement 1 − w(Hπ ) is the
logical entropy h(π).
    We see here once again that probabilistic weakness (as a special case of
quantale weakness) and logical entropy are two sides of the same coin: both
arise by summing over pairs, weighted by joint probability, but one measures
indistinction (same-block weakness) while the other measures distinction (log-
ical entropy). This connection provides an intuitive interpretation of quantale
weakness as ”the probability that two random draws fail to be distinguished by
the given relation.’

2.11.1   Mapping Between Mutual-Information and Probabilistic Weak-
         ness
As a related example of the flexibility of the enriched-category framework for
managing weakness, we can use it trivially to tweak the probabilistic weakness


                                          36
into an informational weakness. In Chapter 14 below we will have reason to
define the weakness of a causal explanation E via its mutual information I(E),
and will set
                             Winfo (E) = 2− I(E) ,
so that
                             I(E) = − log2 Winfo (E).
It’s easy to see that Winfo (E) ∈ [0, 1] and is ⊗-monoidal under subadditivity of
I(·).
    While meaningfully different in a quantitative sense, this is basically the
same story as the probabilistic weakness described above. Both the mutual-
information-based and probabilistic notions of weakness take values in the same
commutative quantale
                                       
                 Q = [0, 1], ≤, ⊗, e , x ⊗ y = x × y, e = 1.

Thus there is an identity quantale-homomorphism

                             φ : Q −→ Q,     φ(x) = x,

respecting ⊗ and the order.
    Explicitly, the mapping that identifies an explanation E with the correspond-
ing event HE whose probability encodes its informational content is as follows.
If                                                  
                            I(E) = − log2 Pr HE ,
then
                 Winfo (E) = 2−I(E) = Pr HE = Wprob HE .
                                                     

Thus
                                         Winfo = φ−1 Wprob ,
                                                         
                 Wprob = φ Winfo ,
and the relation I(E) = − log2 Wprob (HE ) provides the simple mapping between
the two weakness structures.

2.12      Fuzzy Weakness
Just as we have used quantales to extend weakness to probability measures, we
can equally well extend it to fuzzy sets.
   Let X be a universe and let ϕ : X → [0, 1] be a valuation on elements. We
construct a quantale

               V = [0, 1],    a ⊕ b = max(a, b),   a ⊗ b = T (a, b),

where T is any commutative associative t-norm (for example T (a, b) = min(a, b)
or T (a, b) = a b). The units are 0 for ⊕ and 1 for ⊗.

Fuzzy Subobject A fuzzy subset H of X is given by its membership function

              H : X → [0, 1],     H(x) = degree to which x ∈ H.


                                        37
Weakness of a Fuzzy Set We define the weakness of H by ”joining” over
all elements:
                     M                              
              w(H) =    ϕ(x) ⊗ H(x) = max T ϕ(x), H(x) .
                                                    x∈X
                        x∈X

If one prefers a bounded-sum join, use
                                                     X               
       a ⊕ b = min(1, a + b)     =⇒       w(H) = min 1, T (ϕ(x), H(x)) .
                                                               x∈X

   Thus fuzzy-set weakness is obtained by enriching subobjects in the [0, 1]
quantale and ”summing” membership via ⊕ and ⊗ .

2.13     Hypervector Weakness
In a similar vein we show how one can set up a quantale to model weakness in
hypervector space, choosing in particular the Modular Composite Representa-
tion of Snaider and Franklin from [SF14a] (though something similar could be
done for other hypervector frameworks).
    Let r ≥ 2 be even and n ∈ N. Define the hypervector space

                                       V = Znr

with two binary operations (the Modular Composite Representation)

   • Binding (monoidal product)

                     a ⊗ b = ( ai + bi mod r )ni=1 ,       e = (0, 0, . . . , 0).

   • Grouping (join) For any finite multiset {v (1) , . . . , v (k) } ⊂ V , let

Models whose outputs are close to the origin in V (i.e. simple, low-complexity

models under this quantale-weakness schema.

2.14     Linear Weakness and SVMs
Another interesting variation of quantale weakness to look at is ”linear weak-
ness” constructed over a Hilbert space. We will show here that the margin-based
notion of the quality of a classifier used in SVMs [CV95] arises as special case
of quantale weakness, via appropriate choice of quantale.

2.14.1   Linear-Kernel (SVM) Weakness
Let Φ : U − H embed data into a Hilbert space, and let k(u, v) = hΦ(u), Φ(v)i
be its positive-definite kernel. We use the quantale V = [0, ∞] with order ≥,
product ⊗ = min, identity e = ∞, and join ⊕ = sup. Define
                                                kwk
                                   µ(u) =
                                            yu (wT Φ(u))

                                            39
for labels yu ∈ {±1} and normal vector w of a separating hyperplane; this
assigns to each point its signed distance to the decision boundary. Then for the
subobject
                         H = { u ∈ U : yu (wT Φ(u)) ≥ 0}
we compute

                                                     kwk           yu (wT Φ(u))
     w(H) = sup min µ(u), µ(u) = sup                    T
                                                               = inf              ,
              u∈H                              u∈H yu (w Φ(u))    u      kwk

which is exactly the margin of the SVM classifier. Maximizing this w(H) is
therefore equivalent to solving the hard-margin SVM optimization.

2.14.2    Extension to Nonlinear-Kernel SVM
For practical application to SVMs, it is important to note that this linear-
weakness construct also applies to the nonlinear kernel case. The previous con-
struction relies only on an implicit feature map Φ and inner products hΦ(u), Φ(v)i.
Hence it carries over unchanged to nonlinear-kernel SVMs via the kernel trick.
This is a fairly obvious point once one works through all the formalism, but to
avoid confusion we will elaborate it briefly.

Setup.    Let X be our input space, and let

                                          k :X ×X →R

be a positive-definite kernel. By Mercer’s theorem there is a (possibly infinite-
dimensional) feature map Φ : X → H into a Hilbert space H so that hΦ(x), Φ(x0 )i =
k(x, x0 ). An SVM classifier takes the form
                                    n

                                    i=1

with dual coefficients αi and training points {(xi , yi )}.

   Define
                    X                                              kwk

which is exactly the (hard-margin) SVM margin. All inner products are com-

So working things through, it’s clear that nonlinear-kernel SVMs fit directly into
the enriched-category weakness framework: by choosing the same quantale and
measure construction, the margin maximization problem appears as maximizing
categorical weakness of the classifier’s acceptance region.

2.15     Weakness in a Finance Context
As an example of more specialized application of the quantale-weakness setup,
consider the financial modeling domain. We will now explore a couple different
ways of applying this formalism in the finance context – an application we will
take up further in Chapters ?? and 34.1 where we will explore its value in the
AGI attention allocation setting and also the more obvious domain of financial
time series modeling.

2.15.1   A Financial Payoff Quantale and Its Weakness
We can model financial operations–aggregation of payoffs and compounding of
returns–via the commutative quantale
                           
          V = [0, ∞], ⊗, ⊕ with a ⊗ b = a × b, a ⊕ b = a + b,

unit for ⊗ equal to 1 (no gain) and neutral for ⊕ equal to 0 (no payoff). Note
that                            X  X
                          a×       bi =     (a × bi ),
                                  i         i

so ⊗ distributes over arbitrary ⊕.

Interpretation of Quantale Operations
   • Monoidal product ⊗ (multiplication) models compounding of returns: if a
     position yields factor a in one period and b in the next, the total factor is
     a × b.
   • Join ⊕ (addition) models aggregation of independent payoffs: if two dis-
     joint positions yield payoffs a and b, the total payoff is a + b.




                                       41
Weakness as Expected Payoff or Exposure Let X be a set of financial
instruments or scenarios, and let
                               m : X → [0, ∞]
assign each instrument its payoff (or scenario payoff). Fix a valuation (weight-
ing)
                               ϕ : X → [0, ∞],
for instance portfolio weights summing to one or scenario-probabilities. Then
the weakness of m is
                          M               X
                 w(m) =        ϕ(x) ⊗ m(x) =      ϕ(x) m(x).
                         x∈X                      x∈X

In finance this is exactly the expected payoff (or exposure) under weights ϕ.

Examples
  1. Portfolio return: X is a set of assets, m(x) is the return factor of asset x,
     and ϕ(x) is the fraction of capital invested. Then w(m) is the portfolio’s
     expected return factor.
  2. Scenario loss: X is a set of stress-test scenarios, m(x) is the loss in sce-
     nario x, and ϕ(x) is the probability of x. Then w(m) is the expected
     loss.
   Thus the quantale ( [0, ∞], ×, + ) neatly captures the algebra of financial
returns and payoffs, and its induced weakness w(m) corresponds to the funda-
mental financial concept of expectation (of return or loss).

2.15.2   Risk-Normalized Expected Payoff as Weakness
We continue to work in the commutative quantale
                                  
                  V = [0, ∞), ×, + , ⊗ = ×, ⊕ = +,
with unit 1 for ⊗ and zero 0 for ⊕.

Modified Valuation with Risk Discounting Let
   • X be a set of financial instruments or scenarios,
   • m : X → [0, ∞) assign each x ∈ X its payoff,
   • p : X → [0, 1] be a probability or weight distribution,
   • r : X → (0, ∞) be a risk measure (e.g. volatility, Value-at-Risk).
Fix a risk-aversion parameter γ ≥ 0 and define a risk-discount function
                                  f (r) = r−γ .
Then set the risk-normalized valuation
                                                        p(x)
                     ϕ(x) = p(x) × f r(x)         =          γ .
                                                        r(x)

                                       42
Weakness as Risk-Normalized Expected Payoff                  The weakness of the
payoff predicate m is
                        M            X              p(x)
               w(m) =     ϕ(x) ⊗ m(x) =                  γ m(x).
                        x∈X                   x∈X
                                                     r(x)

This quantity is the expected payoff discounted by risk to the power γ. For
γ = 0 it reduces to the usual expected payoff; for γ > 0 it penalizes high-risk
instruments.

Alternative: Prospect-Theory Distortions One may also incorporate
prospect-theory style value and probability weighting:

                    v : [0, ∞) → [0, ∞),   π : [0, 1] → [0, 1],

where v is a concave value function (e.g. v(x) = xα , 0 < α < 1) and π a
                                                   pδ
probability-weighting function (e.g. π(p) = (pδ +(1−p)δ )1/δ ). Then define


                                                   X                
          ϕ(x) = π p(x) × f r(x) ,         w(m) =          ϕ(x) v m(x) ,
                                                     x∈X

yielding a risk- and prospect-adjusted expected value as the weakness.

Interpretation
    • If r(x) is asset volatility and γ > 0, then w(m) favors high-return, low-
      volatility assets.
    • In scenario analysis, if r(x) is scenario severity, w(m) down-weights ex-
      treme but low-probability losses.
    • Prospect-theory distortions further capture observed human risk-attitudes
      via v and π.
   Thus by modifying the valuation ϕ(x) to include a geometric risk discount
and/or prospect-theory distortions, the quantale weakness w(m) becomes a nat-
ural measure of payoff normalized by risk.


3     A (Fairly) General Theory of Generalization
There is a fairly obvious and straightforward sense in which, among multiple
models explaining the same data, the model with the smallest Bennett set-
theoretic weakness will generalize the best. Here we explain how to generalize
this connection between weakness and generalization to quantale weakness. This
will also lead us to generalize our notion of quantale weakness a bit, guided by
the observation that there are more general forms of quantale weakness (than the
ones presented above) that both have some philosophical and scientific meat to


                                      43
them, and also support robust conclusions of the form ”weaker models generalize
better.”
   The theoretical analysis of generalization runs deep and we will address it
from various angles throughout these pages, however what we argue here is that
when the matter is viewed at an appropriate level of abstraction, one can explain
a variety of different notions of generalization in a very simple and basic way.

3.1    Quantale Weakness and Expected Link-Prediction Ac-
       curacy
We start by presenting a very general and fairly trivial-looking set of assump-
tions under which quantale-weak models (in the sense described above) will
generalize better. This turns out to yield classical results about the implica-
tions of set-theoretic and probabilistic weakness as immediate corollaries.
    We start by modeling generalization as a ”link prediction” task. In many
settings one evaluates a model’s ability to ”generalize” by withholding some
known links (u, v) from a relation H ⊆ U × U and then trying to predict
them. We now show that, under a simple generative assumption, a higher sum-
of-products weakness w(H) directly yields better expected accuracy in such
link-prediction tasks.
Assumption (Habitual Link Model). There is a valuation µ : U → R≥0 . For
any pair (u, v), the true probability of a link is
                            µ(u) µ(v)                    X
              Pr (u, v) ∈ H =          ,         Z=               µ(x) µ(y).
                                Z
                                                          x,y∈U

This captures the idea that nodes with large µ-values tend to form many links
together.
   We now withhold a random subset Htrain ⊂ H and attempt to predict the
removed edges in Htest = H \ Htrain . We use the following predictor:

                                            µ(u) µ(v)
                               p̂(u, v) =             ,
                                               Z
identical to the true generative model.

Theorem 5 (Weakness Bounds Expected Accuracy). Under the Habitual Link
Model, the expected accuracy (fraction of correctly predicted test?links, in expec-
tation over random withholding) is

                                w(H)                  X
               E[Accuracy] =         ,      w(H) =            µ(u) µ(v),
                                 Z
                                                     u,v∈H
                                                      u<v

so it increases monotonically with the sum-of-products weakness w(H).

is
