Discussion This theorem formalizes the intuition that when links are gen-
erated proportionally to µ(u)µ(v), a relation H with larger sum-of-products
weakness w(H) contains more ”habitual” link-patterns for the predictor to ex-
ploit, thereby boosting its expected accuracy. In this sense, greater quantale
weakness under the habitual-link prior indeed leads to better generalization in
link-prediction tasks.
    One could certainly argue there’s nothing surprising here. If you assume
the world is based on a certain structural distribution of data, then models
reflecting this structural distribution will generalized better. Umm, yeah. How-
ever, what’s interesting is how this extremely obvious and simplistic principle
of generalization comprises the essential meat of the generalization guarantees
underlying so many sophisticated learning and reasoning approaches.

3.2    Application to Partition-Weakness
Specializing the above analysis to the case of partition-weakness leads to useful
but unsurprising conclusions about logical entropy and generalization.
   A partition Π on a finite set U induces an equivalence relation, and its logical
entropy (partition?weakness) is
                                      X                     |B|
                       HL (Π) = 1 −         p2B ,    pB =        .
                                                            |U |
                                      B∈Π

This equals the probability that two independent uniform draws from U fall
into different blocks?i.e. the fraction of distinguished pairs.

Habitual-Distinction Model Assume the true relation H consists of all
pairs within the same block of Π, and we withhold a random subset Htest ⊂ H.
We predict (u, v) ∈ H by guessing ”same-block” whenever u and v land in the
same block of Π. Then the expected accuracy on Htest is exactly
                                   X 2
                   Pr same block =        pB = 1 − HL (Π).
                                       B∈Π


                                        45
Equivalently, HL (Π) measures expected distinction accuracy on the complement
of H. Thus a partition with larger logical entropy (weaker, more distinctions)
yields better generalization for predicting which pairs do not belong together,
and conversely more cohesion ( p2B large) generalizes better for predicting
                                  P
which pairs do belong together.

3.3    Application to Bennett’s Set-Theoretic Weakness
Similarly, specializing our link prediction analysis to set-theoretic weakness pro-
vides a new route to the familiar connection between Bennett’s weakness and
generalization.
    For a propositional theory Φ over a finite universe of possible worlds W ,
define its weakness as the fraction of worlds satisfying it:

                                   |{w ∈ W : w |= Φ}|
                          w(Φ) =                      .
                                          |W |

Under the uniform-model assumption – i.e. that withheld propositions are drawn
uniformly from those true in Φ – we predict truth by assuming Φ holds. Then
the expected accuracy on the withheld set is precisely w(Φ). Hence a theory with
greater set?theoretic weakness (more models) generalizes better in the sense of
correctly predicting more withheld truths.

Unified Perspective Thus we see both the partition and set case fit the
general quantale-weakness → generalization mapping:

   • In the partition quantale, weakness HL (Π) quantifies distinctions and gov-
     erns expected prediction accuracy of non-membership.

   • In the Boolean-algebra quantale, weakness w(Φ) quantifies model-set size
     and governs expected prediction accuracy of truth.

    Under simple generative priors (uniform-model or uniform-block assump-
tions), higher quantale weakness directly yields higher expected link – or truth
– prediction accuracy, making ”weaker” models provably better at generaliza-
tion in their respective domains.

3.4    Generalized Quantale Weakness and Expected Link-
       Prediction Accuracy
The above analysis of the generalization properties of quantale weakness sug-
gests a generalization of the notion of quantale weakness itself. Because it is
simple to see that the analysis we’e given works perfectly well if one tweaks the
definition of weakness to involve a joint measure on pairs, rather than assuming
a product of marginals. All the conclusions about weakness and generalization
carry over mutatis mutandis.

is larger will, under this generative model, admit higher expected link-prediction
accuracy. Thus quantale weakness in its most abstract form continues to cap-
ture the essential connection between structural sparsity (or ”weakness”) and
generalization performance.


                                               47
    I.e., once again: If you assume the world is based on a certain structural
distribution of data, then models reflecting this structural distribution will gen-
eralized better. Umm, yeah. And what’s interesting is how this extremely
obvious and simplistic principle of generalization can be seen to underlie so
many useful and sophisticated things.

3.5     Philosophical Principles of Weakness and Generaliza-
        tion
Philosophy and physics provide numerous examples where instantiation of this
notion of generalized weakness may be interesting. In most of the present work
we will stick with the ”traditional” form of weakness involving a sum of products,
but a sequel digging deeper into potential physics applications will leverage some
of these variations centrally.
    Here we briefly review here three conceptual notions from the literature –
Peirce’s tendency-to-take-habits, Smolin’s Precedence Principle and Sheldrake’s
Morphic Resonance – that fit cleanly into a weakness framework where the
product in the sum-of-products is replaced with a product plus an additional
correlation term – encompassed by the generalized weakness from the previous
section. In a way these three concepts are all close variants on the same idea,
but they are habitually associated with slightly different mathematics.

3.5.1   Regularized Weakness and Philosophical Variants
We begin by observing that a regularized product?measure prior is a special
case of the generalized?weakness framework:
   Let
                      µcomb (u, v) = µ(u) µ(v) + λ M (u, v),
where

   • µ : U → R≥0 is a marginal ?activity? measure,
   • M : U × U → R is a correction term (e.g. mutual information, rarity
     penalty, semantic distance),
   • λ ≥ 0 trades off baseline product?measure and regularizer.

   Define the regularized weakness of H ⊆ U × U by
                                      X
                       wcomb (H) =        µcomb (u, v).
                                      (u,v)∈H


All the expected?accuracy bounds for an arbitrary joint µ(u, v) hold verbatim
upon replacing µ(u, v) with µcomb (u, v) and w(H) with wcomb (H).




                                        48
3.5.2     Peircean Habit Variant.
Peirce argued that repeated co?occurrence of ideas or actions gives rise to habits
which then bias future inference and behavior [Pei92]. In his words, ?habit-
taking? is the process by which patterns of association become entrenched
through repetition. We capture this by setting

          MP (u, v) = frequency with which u and v co?occur,       λ = λP ,

so that
                       µP (u, v) = µ(u) µ(v) + λP MP (u, v).
Here the regularizer boosts links with strong historical co?occurrence, aligning
weakness with Peircean habit?strength.

3.5.3     Smolin?s Precedence Variant.
Smolin?s Precedence Principle holds that the probabilities of future events are
shaped by the relative frequency of analogous events in the past, so that ?what
has happened before will influence what happens again? [Smo13]. Intriguingly,
Smolin uses this as an alternative way to derive the basic equations of quantum
mechanics. We model Smolin’s Principle by

         MS (u, v) = count of past instances where u preceded v,     λ = λS ,

giving
                        µS (u, v) = µ(u) µ(v) + λS MS (u, v).
The regularizer thus favors links that mirror established precedents.

3.5.4     Sheldrake?s Resonance Variant.
Sheldrake proposed that form and behavior are influenced by a ?morphic field?
which accumulates a collective memory of past similar patterns, a process he
calls morphic resonance [She81]. We let

           MM (u, v) = resonance strength between u and v,      λ = λM ,

so that
                       µM (u, v) = µ(u) µ(v) + λM MM (u, v).
Here the correction term encodes collective memory effects as in Sheldrake?s
hypothesis.

3.5.5     Implications for Generalization.
In each variant, weakness
                                         X
                              w(H) =             µ? (u, v)
                                       (u,v)∈H



                                         49
blends independent activity with a domain?specific correction. Under the corre-
sponding generative model, larger w(H) still yields higher expected generaliza-
tion, while M encodes the extra inductive bias (habits, precedents, resonance)
in a unified framework.

3.6    Weakest?Hypothesis?Driven Meta?Learning: A Con-
       ceptual Template
Finally, we leverage these ideas about generalized weakness to describe a weak-
est?hypothesis?driven learner that simultaneously discovers its own inductive
bias and uses it to choose actions (including internal ones) so as to maximize
cumulative reward. This is emphatically not a practical proposal for an AGI
algorithm, but rather a conceptual template for thinking about learning under
uncertainty.

Core Idea.
   • Maintain a finite history H of past observations, actions and rewards.
   • Fix a finite family {µθ }θ∈Θ of candidate joint valuations on (state, action)
     pairs. These might include product?measure priors, Peircean habit priors,
     Smolinian precedence priors, etc.
   • On each step, meta?learn the best measure µθ∗ by subsampling H (cross?validation):
     ask, ?Which µθ would have led me?via choosing the weakest useful hypoth-
     esis?to best predict my past rewards??
   • Given µθ∗ , enumerate a finite set of candidate hypotheses h (e.g. simple
     decision rules) consistent with H. Compute each hypothesis?s weakness
                                         X
                             wθ∗ (h) =        µθ∗ (u, v),
                                         (u,v)∈h

      and select the weakest h that still promises high reward. Execute the
      action it prescribes.
   • Treat internal operations?memory pruning, hypothesis?space refinement,
     update of Θ, etc.?as actions in the same framework.

Why This Matters.
   • By searching over measures µθ , the learner automatically adapts its induc-
     tive bias to match the structure of its environment.
   • Choosing the weakest hypothesis ensures that the agent only commits to
     as much structure as is needed to explain and predict reward, guarding
     against overfitting.
   • Viewing internal algorithm updates and memory management as actions
     unifies learning, planning, and self ?modification under one decision?theoretic
     roof.


                                       50
Relation to Universal AI Frameworks.           This template echoes ideas from:
    • Solomonoff induction, which weights all computable hypotheses by their
      algorithmic complexity.
    • The Gd̈el machine, which self?modifies by proving that a change will im-
      prove expected utility.
    • AIXI, which selects actions to maximize expected reward under a universal
      prior over environments.
Like these frameworks, our approach searches over hypotheses (and even over
the prior itself), but it does so by privileging the weakest useful explanation
rather than shortest program or universal mixture.

A Conceptual, Not Practical, Algorithm. Because the history H, hy-
pothesis space and measure family Θ must all be finite, the template is in
principle computable?but typically astronomically expensive. Its value lies in
illustrating how an agent might bootstrap both its inductive bias and its be-
havior, always favoring the minimal structure needed to predict reward. This
perspective can guide the design of more efficient, approximate algorithms, and
we will revisit these conceptual ideas as appropriate in the following pages.


4     Weakness in Logical Quantales
The quantale weakness framework encompasses various logic systems just as
easily as it does quantitative operations. We will here present a simple ap-
proach to constructing ”logical weakness” and then explore connecting this to
different forms of truth values, including Heyting algebra based (intuitionist),
paraconsistent (Constructible Duality Logic) and uncertain (Probabilistic Logic
Networks).

4.1    The Logic Quantale: Definitions of Weakness and Mu-
       tual Information
Let W be a nonempty set of possible worlds. We form the power-set quantale
                                                        
                 Qlogic = P(W ), ⊗logic , ⊕logic , ∅, W

where for A, B ⊆ W :

                A ⊗logic B = A ∩ B,         A ⊕logic B = A ∪ B.

This makes Clogic , the one-object monoidal category with Hom(ast, ast) = Qlogic ,
into a commutative quantale.




                                       51
Formulas as Predicates            A logical model or predicate on a domain X is a
function
                                   m : X → Qlogic ,
sending each x ∈ X to the set of worlds m(x) ⊆ W in which the formula holds.

Weakness in the Logic Quantale Given a background valuation ϕ : X →
Qlogic , define the weakness of m by
                         M                       [           
          wlogic (m) =          ϕ(x) ⊗logic m(x) =   ϕ(x) ∩ m(x) .
                      logic x∈X                         x∈X

Intuitively, wlogic (m) is the set of worlds consistent with both background knowl-
edge ϕ and the candidate predicate m–a larger set means a weaker (more per-
missive) model.

Residuation and Implication           The quantale residuation (implication) (logic
is given by
           A (logic C = { w ∈ W : A ∩ {w} ⊆ C} = (W \ A) ∪ C.

4.1.1   Connection to Hoare-Style Preconditions
We mentioned above how Hoare preconditions connect with the notion of possible-
worlds used in the foundation of the weakness concept. Hoare preconditions can
also be worked into the logical quantale setup we are now exploring.
    Hoare logic treats a precondition P ⊆ W as the set of states from which
running a program guarantees a postcondition Q. In the quantale framework,
one defines the classical weakest precondition
                  Pre(Q) = { w ∈ W | ∃ w0 ∈ Q, (w, w0 ) ∈ R},
where R ⊆ W × W is the one-step reachability relation. The weakness score of
this set is                    M                  [
            wlogic Pre(Q) =            µ(w) =           µ(w),
                                    w∈W, w0 ∈Q          w,w0
                                     (w,w0 )∈R        (w,w0 )∈R

quantifying how many–and how important–initial states lead into Q in one step
   Thus the same power-set quantale (P(W ), ∩, ∪) supports:
   • Logical weakness of any predicate P ⊆ W as ∪w∈P µ(w).
   • Hoare preconditions as particular subobjects defined by reachability R.
   • Quantified robustness of a program via the scalar weakness wlogic (Pre(Q)),
     giving a precise Occam-biased measure of how permissive the precondition
     is.
   In this way, just as alluded earlier, our categorical logic quantale seamlessly
recovers and extends the Hoare-style weakest-precondition framework by en-
dowing it with a graded Occam-style simplicity bias.


                                          52
4.2    Quantale for Heyting Algebra
One can make a similar development for an intuitionistic logic based on Heyting
algebras.
    Let H be any complete Heyting algebra, with order ≤, meet ∧, join ∨, top
element > and bottom element ⊥. Then
                                                  
                          QH = H, ≤, ∧, >, ∨

is a commutative quantale, since finite meets distribute over arbitrary joins in
a frame.
    A valuation µ : U → H on a universe U assigns each case u ∈ U a
truth?degree µ(u) ∈ H. For any relation R ⊆ U ×U , define its Heyting?weakness
by                               _                 
                    wH (R) =            µ(u) ∧ µ(v) ∈ H.
                                (u,v)∈R

   By contrast, the logic quantale from Chapter 2 is
                                                  
                        Qlogic = P(W ), ⊆, ∩, W, ∪ ,

and for a predicate m : X → P(W ) one has
                                     [            
                        wlogic (m) =   φ(x) ∩ m(x) ,
                                      x∈X

i.e. the union of all surviving worlds :contentReference[oaicite:1]index=1.

Concrete example.
   • U = {u, v}, with µ(u) = 0.4, µ(v) = 0.7 in H = [0, 1] (meet min, join
     max).
   • Relation R = {(u, v), (v, v)}.

Then

       wH (R) = max{min(0.4, 0.7), min(0.7, 0.7)} = max{0.4, 0.7} = 0.7,

whereas interpreting the same data in the logic quantale yields

                        wlogic (R) = {u} ∪ {v} = {u, v},

a non?graded set. Thus the Heyting?quantale weakness is a graded value in
[0, 1], while the classical logic quantale gives only a Boolean set of worlds.




                                          53
4.3     Quantale for Constructible Duality Logic (CDL)
Constructible Duality Logic (CDL), introduced by Patterson [Pat98], is a non-
classical logical system designed to model reasoning in the presence of construc-
tive knowledge and its dual—constructive refutation. CDL is a bivalent logic in
which both proof and disproof are treated as primitive and independent forms
of epistemic justification.
    Rather than taking truth as primitive, CDL is based on the notion of con-
structibility: a proposition is considered accepted (“true”) if there exists a con-
struction (e.g., a proof), and rejected (“false”) if there exists a disproof. Im-
portantly, the absence of either does not force a classical determination of the
truth value. This allows CDL to formally accommodate situations where neither
a proposition nor its negation is currently justified—capturing a key aspect of
reasoning under uncertainty and partial knowledge.
    CDL supports a richer set of modal and epistemic distinctions than classical
logic, while avoiding some of the paradoxes and explosion principles of more
rigid systems.

4.3.1   Simple Quantitative Extension of CDL
In prior work [Goe21b], we have extended CDL by introducing a quantitative
generalization suitable for application in AI systems, particularly in the context
of reasoning with uncertain and partial evidence. In this extension, rather than
simply treating proof and disproof as binary conditions, each proposition is
associated with counts of supporting and refuting evidence instances—analogous
to constructive arguments for and against the proposition.
    These counts can be interpreted in Bayesian terms or aggregated using other
probabilistic or evidential frameworks, leading to the assignment of degrees
of belief or support. This results in a logic system that preserves the dual
constructive character of CDL, while enabling graded inference.
    This quantitative CDL framework can be seen as a special case of, or com-
plementary to, frameworks such as Probabilistic Logic Networks (PLN, to be
reviewed in Chapter ?? below), where truth values are explicitly probabilistic. In
both systems, inference is based not on binary deduction, but on propagation of
uncertain, context-dependent evidence. Goertzel’s formulation allows for the ex-
plicit integration of logical dualities (e.g., proof/disproof, affirmation/negation)
with practical AI inference under bounded computational resources.

4.3.2   A Quantale for CDL Truth Values
We will present a general categorial framing for CDL, then afterwards explain
how the simple quantitative extension mentioned above fits into this framework.
    Formally, CDL can be constructed using two Heyting algebras, one for posi-
tive and one for negative evidence. Specifically, let H be a Heyting algebra with
order ≤, meet ∧, join ∨, top 1 and bottom 0. Define

                                VCD = H × H op ,


                                        54
ordered by
                  (x, x0 ) ≤ (y, y 0 )     ⇐⇒         x≤y       and y 0 ≤ x0 .
Give VCD the commutative quantale structure

   (x, x0 ) ⊕ (y, y 0 ) = x ∨ y, x0 ∧ y 0 , (x, x0 ) ⊗ (y, y 0 ) = x ∧ y, x0 ∨ y 0 ,
                                                                                 


with unit I = (1, 1) and zero 0 = (0, 0). These operations recover the CD logic
conjunction and disjunction rules:

    (x, x0 ) ∧ (y, y 0 ) = (x ∧ y, x0 ∨ y 0 ),        (x, x0 ) ∨ (y, y 0 ) = (x ∨ y, x0 ∧ y 0 ).

4.3.3    Weakness and Information Theory on the CD Quantale
Let (H, ∧, ∨, →, 0, 1) be a Heyting algebra, and define the CD quantale

VCD = H×H op ,          (x, x0 )⊕(y, y 0 ) = (x∨y, x0 ∧y 0 ),      (x, x0 )⊗(y, y 0 ) = (x∧y, x0 ∨y 0 ),

with unit ICD = (1, 1) and zero 0CD = (0, 0).

Weakness on VCD           A VCD -valued predicate (subobject) is a function

                                       m : X → VCD .

Its weakness is the quantale-join over all elements:

                                                 x∈X

Algebraic Properties By the axioms of a commutative quantale and Heyting

4.3.4   The Simple Quantitative Extension of PLN as a Special Case
        of the Quantale Framework
By instantiating the Heyting algebra H underlying the CDL quantale

                                          VCD = H × H op

to a complete lattice of raw evidence counts–for example

                        H = (N ∪ {∞}, ≤, ∧ = min, ∨ = max)

or more generally
                                H = ([0, ∞], ≤, min, sup),
and interpreting each proposition P as the pair (n+ , n− ) ∈ H × H op of its con-
structive ”proof” and ”disproof” counts, one recovers exactly the simple quan-
titative CDL reviewed above. Under this specialization the quantale operations

   (x, x0 ) ⊕ (y, y 0 ) = (x ∨ y, x0 ∧ y 0 ),          (x, x0 ) ⊗ (y, y 0 ) = (x ∧ y, x0 ∨ y 0 ),


                                                  56
become the appropriate evidence-aggregation rules–combining positive counts
by their maximum (or by addition in an additive quantale variant) and negative
counts dually–while the residuated implication

                      (x, x0 ) ( (y, y 0 ) = (x → y, y 0 → x0 )

encodes conditional inference on these counts. Thus his pragmatic scheme of
tracking and propagating explicit support and refutation tallies is realized as
the special case of the general CDL quantale semantics when H is chosen to
model raw quantitative evidence.

4.4    Quantale for PLN Simple Truth Values
Probabilistic Logic Networks (PLN) is a framework for uncertain inference that
integrates probabilistic reasoning with symbolic logic, designed to serve as a
foundation for Artificial General Intelligence (AGI). It extends traditional logic
systems by incorporating probabilistic truth values and enabling inference under
uncertainty, while maintaining computational tractability.
    PLN operates within a typed metagraph formalism, where knowledge is rep-
resented as nodes and links in a graph structure. Each logical statement (or
atom) is annotated with a Simple Truth Value (STV), which quantifies both
the estimated strength (probability) and confidence (certainty about the es-
timate) of the statement.

Simple Truth Values       A PLN Simple Truth Value is a pair (s, c), where:

   • s ∈ [0, 1] is the strength, representing an estimate of the probability that
     the statement is true.
   • c ∈ [0, 1] is the confidence, indicating the degree of support behind the
     strength value.

    The confidence can be interpreted in Bayesian terms as related to the equiv-
alent sample size used to compute the strength. For instance, a strength of 0.8
with a confidence of 0.9 suggests strong evidence supporting a high probability
of truth.

Inference in PLN PLN defines probabilistic inference rules (e.g., deduction,
induction, abduction) that operate directly on STVs. These inference rules are
derived from Bayesian principles and uncertainty propagation, ensuring that
resulting truth values preserve meaningful probabilistic interpretations. For
example, in a simple deductive rule A → B, if A and A → B are known with
associated STVs, then the STV for B can be computed via an appropriate prop-
agation formula that reflects both the strength and confidence of the premises.




                                         57
Semantic Foundations and Practical Approximations As will be artic-
ulated further in Section 7.4, the semantics of PLN can be formally grounded
using Graph-Structured Lambda Theory (GSLT), a general formalism for repre-
senting knowledge, computation, and logic in a unified higher-order framework.
Within GSLT, it is possible to represent and manipulate arbitrary higher-order
probabilities, including infinite-order belief structures [Goe10] [War22]. In this
context, the STVs used in PLN can be understood as pragmatic approximations
to second- or third-order probability distributions–simplified summaries of un-
certainty about uncertainty–that have proved effective for practical reasoning
under resource constraints. Other PLN truth value structures, such as indefi-
nite probabilities, serve a similar role as computationally feasible projections of
deeper probabilistic semantics.

4.4.1     A Quantale for PLN Simple Truth Values
Let
                               VPLN = R≥0 × R≥0 ,
ordered componentwise. For any two STVs (n+ , n− ), (m+ , m− ) ∈ VPLN define
(n+ , n− )⊕(m+ , m− ) = (n+ +m+ , n− +m− ),           (n+ , n− )⊗(m+ , m− ) = (n+ m+ , n− m− ),
with unit 1 = (1, 1) and zero 0 = (0, 0). Here ⊕ models the union of indepen-
dent micro-situations by adding evidence counts, and ⊗ models independent
conjunction by multiplying proportions.

4.4.2     Homomorphism Between VCD and VPLN
Fix an ensemble size N > 0. Define
                  Φ : VCD → VPLN ,         Φ(x, x0 ) = (N x, N x0 ).
Then for any (x, x0 ), (y, y 0 ) ∈ VCD :
Φ (x, x0 )⊕(y, y 0 ) = N (x∨y), N (x0 ∧y 0 ) = (N x, N x0 )⊕(N y, N y 0 ) = Φ(x, x0 )⊕Φ(y, y 0 ),
                                           

Φ (x, x0 )⊗(y, y 0 ) = N (x∧y), N (x0 ∨y 0 ) = (N x, N x0 )⊗(N y, N y 0 ) = Φ(x, x0 )⊗Φ(y, y 0 ).
                                           

Thus Φ is a commutative-quantale homomorphism, mapping CD p-bits to PLN
evidence counts by renormalizing paraconsistent truth values into positive and
negative evidence.

4.4.3     Exemplifying Where CDL and PLN May Differ: Conflicting
          Evidence in Sensor Diagnosis
Consider a simple fault-diagnosis task with two binary sensors S1 , S2 ∈ {0, 1}
and a hidden fault variable F ∈ {OK , Fail }. Each sensor gives both positive
and negative evidence:
      • Sensor S1 : reports ”1” usually when F = Fail , but sometimes falsely.
      • Sensor S2 : reports ”0” usually when F = OK , but occasionally misses a
        fault.


                                          58
 PLN Probabilistic View We collect counts of observations and learn a
standard PLN simple-truth-value model:

           P (F = Fail | S1 = 1) = 0.9,                  P (F = OK | S1 = 1) = 0.1,

and similarly for S2 . From these we build a joint probability distribution
PPLN (S1 , S2 , F ), compute marginals and then the Shannon mutual information
                                X                                      PPLN (s1 , s2 , f )
       IPLN (F ; S1 , S2 ) =               PPLN (s1 , s2 , f ) log                             .
                                                                     PPLN (f ) PPLN (s1 , s2 )
                               s1 ,s2 ,f

This quantifies, in bits, how much observing both sensors reduces average un-
certainty about the fault.

CD-Logic View Instead of crisp probabilities, represent each (s1 , s2 , f ) by a
paraconsistent truth-pair (p, n) ∈ H × H op where

p = degree of evidence for(F = f | S1 , S2 ),                 n = degree of evidence against(F = f | S1 , S2 ).

These live in the CD quantale VCD = H × H op . We form a VCD -valued distri-
bution                                   M
              PCD (s1 , s2 , f ) ∈ VCD ,   PCD (s1 , s2 , f ) = (1, 1).
                                                  s1 ,s2 ,f

Then the CD-mutual information is
                          M h                                                  i
     ICD (F ; S1 , S2 ) =     PCD (s1 , s2 , f ) ( PF (f ) ⊗ PS1 ,S2 (s1 , s2 ) .
                          s1 ,s2 ,f

This measures how evidence for and against ”flows” between sensors and fault,
explicitly accounting for contradictory readings.

Different Insights
   • PLN mutual information IPLN gives a single scalar (in bits) of expected
     uncertainty reduction, but it averages over all cases and cannot distinguish
     conflicting vs. supporting evidence patterns.

   • CD mutual information ICD lives in H × H op and can separately quan-
     tify accumulated positive vs. negative evidence flow. A high ICD compo-
     nent in the ”against” coordinate reveals systematic sensor conflicts, even
     if the probabilistic IPLN remains moderate.
    Thus for the same data, IPLN (F ; S1 , S2 ) tells you how much uncertainty
is reduced on average, whereas ICD (F ; S1 , S2 ) tells you which way and how
strongly evidence supports or contradicts a diagnosis–crucial in safety-critical
or adversarial settings.



                                                    59
4.5    Connecting the Logic Quantale to the CDL and PLN
       Quantales

Define mCDL = ECDL ◦ m, ϕCDL = ECDL ◦ ϕ. Then
                     M                                          
      wCDL (mCDL ) =    ϕCDL (x) ⊗CDL mCDL (x) = ECDL wlogic (m) .
                          CDL x

Similarly for wPLN , using EPLN = Ψ ◦ ECDL .


                                            60
Preservation of Mutual Information Given a joint predicate P : X ×Y →

                M h                                                 i                    

               CDL x,y

And again IPLN (X; Y ) = Ψ(ICDL (X; Y )) = EPLN (Ilogic (X; Y )).
    Hence the logic quantale of possible-world sets embeds functorially into the
CDL and PLN quantales, carrying over both Occam-style weakness and mutual-
information measures intact.

4.5.1    CDL and PLN as Truth-Value Attachments to the Logic Quan-
         tale
Informally, the above formalism is basically viewing CDL and PLN as different
ways to attach truth value objects to expressions in the categorial logic quantale.
    Let                                                      
                    Qlogic = P(W ), ⊗logic = ∩, ⊕logic = ∪
be the ”logic quantale” of possible-world predicates, viewed as the single-object
monoidal category

                  Clogic : Ob = {ast}, Hom(ast, ast) = Qlogic .

CDL Embedding            Define the Constructible-Duality-Logic quantale

                           QCDL = (LCDL , ⊗CDL , ⊕CDL )

and a strict monoidal functor

                              ECDL : Clogic −→ CCDL

by                                M

Then

ECDL (A∩B) = ECDL (A)⊗CDL ECDL (B),             ECDL (A∪B) = ECDL (A)⊕CDL ECDL (B).

Thus each logic-formula A ⊆ W is assigned a CDL truth-value object ECDL (A).

PLN Embedding Let π = (πw )w∈W be a probability distribution and define

              EPLN : Clogic −→ CPLN ,            EPLN (A) =         πw .

                                          61
One checks

EPLN (A∩B) = EPLN (A)⊗PLN EPLN (B),            EPLN (A∪B) = EPLN (A)⊕PLN EPLN (B).

Thus each logic-formula A is assigned a PLN truth-value EPLN (A).

Interpretation In this view, CDL and PLN are simply two different choices
of ”truth-value objects” attached to the same underlying logic quantale via strict
monoidal functors. Logical operations ∩, ∪ are preserved, and both the Occam-
style weakness and quantale mutual information defined on Qlogic transport
directly into QCDL and QPLN by functoriality.


5    Relationships Between Set-Theoretic Weak-
     ness and Program Size
The concept of weakness arose as an attempt to formalize the intuitive notion
of ”Occam’s Razor”– roughly ”when in doubt between multiple hypotheses, all
else equal, prefer the simpler.” The intuition being that if A and B both explain
the same thing, but A is simpler, B probably has some extra cruft on it. This
intuition needs some adjustment to deal with quantum mechanics and other
sorts of observer-dependence (as we’ll argue in Chapter 11 below), but it does
have some solid foundation as has been shown definitively by various related
threads of work in learning theory.
    The general convention in modern times has been to formalize Occam’s Ra-
zor using minimum description length, and this does basically work, but it
gets messy. Weakness takes a semantic more than syntactic approach, and as
Bennett has shown this makes many things a fair bit cleaner. However, there
are very clear and very close connections between the weakness and MDL ap-
proaches. Bennett gives some basic results in this regard in his thesis, showing
(in essence) that weakness plus a finite-vocabulary assumption or similar on
one’s description language, gives a sort of equivalence between weakness and
MDL. Here we pursue this sort of equivalence a little further, looking closely at
what happens without the finite-vocabulary assumption, and expressing Ben-
nett’s weakness/MDL correspondence in a novel (and in some ways more re-
vealing) form. Finally, we explore a special programming language (CoDD) in
which there is an even stronger correlation between weakness and MDL and
consider whether this language could be useful in the practical search for weak
patterns within AI systems.
    This section deals basically with the original Bennett-style set-theoretic
weakness. In Chapter 6 we explore how the weakness/MDL parallel may be
extended to general quantale weakness, via appropriately extending the notion
of what constitutes a ”language.”




                                       62
5.1    General Weakness/Description-Length Relation
Let U be a finite universe of N equally likely worlds. A hypothesis h is any
subset H ⊆ U , with weakness

                                    w(h) = |H|.

Suppose we fix any prefix-free description language mapping binary strings p to
hypotheses h = Dec(p). Let

            `(p) = length of p,         L(h) = min{`(p) : Dec(p) = h}

be the description length of h.

Kraft Bound.        Prefix-freeness implies the Kraft inequality
                                  X
                                      2−`(p) ≤ 1.
                                    p

Grouping by decoded hypothesis,
             X      X                   X
                          2−`(p) ≤ 1 =⇒   2−L(h) ≤ 1.
                h    p:Dec(p)=h                       h


Weakness vs. Length. Since each hypothesis h covers exactly w(h) worlds,
its uniform probability is
                                         w(h)
                               P (h) =        .
                                           N
We also have a universal probability lower bound

                             m(h) = 2−L(h) ≤ P (h),
                         −L(h)
                    P
because otherwise      h2      would exceed one. Rearranging,

                                  w(h) ≥ N 2−L(h) .

Equivalently,
                           L(h) ≥ log2 N − log2 w(h).

Interpretation. This inequality holds for any prefix-free description scheme.
It shows that shorter descriptions can never rule out too many worlds: a hy-
pothesis of length L must leave at least N/2L worlds possible. Conversely, very
weak hypotheses (large w) admit short descriptions. So basically, description-
length and weakness are inversely coupled in the broadest class of languages via
the Kraft bound.




                                           63
5.2    Bennett’s Finite-Vocabulary Extension of the Kraft
       Bound
Bennett’s approach to connecting weakness with MDL adds to the Kraft argu-
ment the key assumption of a finite description vocabulary of size V . Then:

  1. There are at most V L distinct codewords of length exactly L, and at most
                 V L+1
     PL     `
       `=0 V < V −1 codewords of length ≤ L.

  2. Consequently, among all hypotheses expressible with descriptions of length
                              L+1
     ≤ L, there are at most VV −1 distinct hypotheses. Let

                                WL = max{ w(h) : L(h) ≤ L}.

  3. By the Kraft bound,
                                             L
                      X                      X                 V L+1 −L
                                2−L(h) ≤           V ` 2−` <        2 .
                                                               V −1
                    h:L(h)≤L                 `=0


      Since each term 2−L(h) ≤ w(h)/N , summing gives

        WL       X                 X        w(h)        X                   V L+1 −L
           ·              1 ≥                    ≥               2−L(h) >        2 .
        N                                    N                              V −1
               h:L(h)≤L          h:L(h)≤L             h:L(h)≤L

      Hence
                                             V L+1 −L
                                    WL >          2 N.
                                             V −1
  4. As L increases, the right-hand side grows exponentially in L, forcing WL
     – the maximum weakness among all length-≤ L programs – to grow in
     lockstep. Thus the unique hypothesis of maximal weakness among those
     of length ≤ L is also the unique hypothesis of minimal description length
     achieving that weakness.

    In this way Bennett’s finite-vocabulary assumption strengthens the one-sided
Kraft bound into a true equivalence: within the finite set of hypotheses of de-
scriptionlength at most L, the weakest hypothesis is precisely the shortest. This
underlies his Occam’s razor principle that ”maximal weakness” and ”minimal
description length” coincide in any realistic, finite coding scheme.

5.3    ENF for Arbitrary Languages and Normalization Cost
While Bennett’s argument connecting MDL and weakness is quite powerful, its
assumptions are still fairly special. To re-express the above math in slightly
plainer language, Bennett’s finite-vocabulary argument shows a tight equiva-
lence between weakness and description length only under two strong assump-
tions:


                                            64
   • Prefix-free, semantics-preserving code: every syntactic extension
     must change the decoded hypothesis.
   • No dead code or unused definitions: all symbols in the program
     contribute to its meaning.

ENF Guarantees Prefix-Free, Dead-Code-Free Representation . Now
one might wonder: if you’re starting with some arbitrary language, then why
not just remove the redundancies and dead code from your language to get a
weakness-friendly version? This idea brings us directly to Holman’s notion of
an Elegant Normal Form [Hol90], which my colleagues and I have been using
in automated program learning for a couple decades [Loo06]. Indeed, in princi-
ple, any programming language or formalism can be equipped with an Elegant
Normal Form (ENF) that enforces:

   • Prefix-free codes: each program maps to a unique tree/DAG, so no
     encoding is a prefix of another.
   • Non-dead code: maximal sharing and canonical merging remove any
     subexpressions that do not contribute to the program’s output.

Under these conditions, Bennett’s finite-vocabulary argument raises the one-
sided Kraft bound into a full equivalence: the weakest hypothesis of a given
ENF size is also the shortest description.
    However, the cost of performing this sort of elegant normalization may be
highly language-dependent ... and sometimes highly high...

Normalization Cost Depends on Language Semantics
CMMC or MeTTa-calculus In our AI work on the Hyperon system, we have
   made use of simple formal language such as CMMC ([Goe20b], and see
   Appendix A.1) or the MeTTa Calculus [Mer25], which are basically combi-
   natory/logical DSLs consisting of tree-structured terms and fixed rewrite
   rules. In the case of these languages (or others like them), transforming
   an arbitrary term into ENF requires

        • a single bottom-up DAG-merging pass, and
        • child-list sorting for commutative operators,
     all running in time polynomial in the tree size.
General Languages More broadly, for general programming languages, achiev-
    ing full ENF entails:
       1. Dead-code elimination, which is equivalent to deciding reachability
          and equivalence in a Turing-complete setting (undecidable in gen-
          eral).



                                      65
          2. Canonical serialization, requiring normalization of macros, inlining,
             alias analysis, etc., with worst-case exponential blow-up.
        Thus normalization to ENF in an arbitrary practical programming lan-
        guage can be super-expensive; it can even be outright pragmatically im-
        possible in the general case.
Overall, while ENF can in theory make any language satisfy Bennett’s prefix-
free, dead-code-free requirements, in practice DSLs like CMMC or MeTTa tend
to admit efficient ENF normalization, whereas full GPLs like most common
imperative programming languages tend not to.

5.3.1     Why Bennett’s Argument Fails in Practice for General MDL
          (e.g. C Programs)
To drive the latter point home more fully, consider that in a language like C,
for instance:

  1. You can add arbitrary comments, whitespace, unused functions or global
     variables without affecting the program’s behavior; such extensions in-
     crease length but do not change the set of worlds the program admits.
  2. C is not a prefix-free encoding of its semantics: two different source texts
     can compile to the same binary, and long strings of dead code compile
     away.

   Hence in plain C:

                       L(h1 ) > L(h2 ) 6=⇒ w(h1 ) < w(h2 ).
    A longer C program can be no stronger (it may admit exactly the same
behaviors) or even weaker if it accidentally changes some constraint. Thus
minimum-length C programs need additional normalization (dead-code elim-
ination, canonical ordering, etc.) before one can recover a monotonic length-
weakness relation. And the normalization needed to make an arbitrary language
like C into a prefix-free, dead-code-free formalism requires powerful rewrite
passes that can approach the difficulty of full program equivalence – an un-
decidable problem in general. In particular:

   • Dead-code elimination demands whole-program analysis of control-flow
     and data-flow to decide which functions, globals or branches are never
     reachable. In Turing-complete languages, reachability is undecidable, so
     practical compilers use conservative approximations that can still leave
     substantial dead code.
   • Prefix-free encoding requires a canonical serialization of abstract syn-
     tax (removing comments, whitespace, renaming identifiers, reordering com-
     mutative operations, inlining macros, etc.). Achieving a unique represen-
     tation up to semantics often entails deep normalization – constant folding,


                                        66
       loop unrolling, syntactic sugar elimination – which can blow up program
       size and compilation time.
   • Semantic preservation must be guaranteed: every normalization step
     must preserve the program’s observable behaviors. Verifying that two
     large programs are semantically equivalent is itself at least as hard as the
     halting problem, so real- world toolchains rely on heuristics and may fail
     to detect some semantically redundant code.
   • Scalability issues arise because every small optimization or canonical
     rewrite may interact with others. A single combinator- like elimination can
     trigger further inlining, constant propagation, or alias analyses, leading to
     an exponential cascade in worst- case compile time or intermediate code
     size.

5.4     Monotonicity of Weakness in the CoDD Model
A particularly elegant framework for exploring relationships between weakness
and program size is the Combinational Decision DAG (CoDD) framework lan-
guage, described in [Goe20a] and reviewed here in Appendix A.2.
   In CoDD, each program h defines a set of possible “worlds” in which h holds
true. We measure the weakness of h by

                     w(h) = {worlds consistent with h} .

A larger w(h) means h rules out fewer worlds and is therefore weaker.

Extension implies strength. A program A extends a program B if A can
be obtained by composing B with additional combinators or subprograms. Con-
cretely, if                                  
                             A = asti B, C
for some combinator asti and subprogram C, then the set of worlds satisfying
A is a subset of those satisfying B. Hence

      w(A) = { worlds satisfying A} ≤ { worlds satisfying B} = w(B).

Thus any extension strictly strengthens the hypothesis and reduces its weakness.

Stronger length- weakness correlation. In CoDD, program length (the
number of combinator applications) and weakness are tied by the Compositional
Simplicity Measure:
                                                        
                   σ(h) =      min      σ(y) + σ(z) + ci ,
                             y,z,i: h=yasti z

where each asti carries cost ci . Because each extension increases σ(h) by exactly
the cost of one combinator, length and cost track one another precisely, and cost
inversely tracks weakness.


                                         67
   By contrast, in most programming languages adding lines or imports does
not guarantee strictly greater strength or lower generality, since libraries may
bundle large amounts of code without changing the set of inputs accepted.
CoDD’s free-algebra construction ensures that every syntactic extension nar-
rows the hypothesis, yielding a direct, monotonic correlation between program
length and weakness that is far stronger than in conventional languages.

5.5    Relation of CoDD to Bennett’s Argument
As we’ve reviewed above, Bennett’s thesis shows that in any finite-vocabulary
prefix-free code, the hypothesis that rules out the fewest worlds among all de-
scriptions of length at most L is exactly the one of minimal description length.
He derives this by combining the Kraft inequality with an upper bound on the
number of codewords of each length, yielding a tight correspondence between
maximum weakness and minimum length under the finite-vocabulary assump-
tion.
    Our CoDD argument is a concrete instantiation – and a substantial strength-
ening – of Bennett’s general result. In CoDD:

   • The vocabulary is the finite set of combinators {asti }.
   • Programs are built as trees (or DAGs) of these combinators in Elegant
     Normal Form, giving a unique, prefix-free encoding.
   • The CoDD cost σ(h) counts exactly the number of combinator applications
     (each with fixed cost ci ), so program length and cost coincide.

   • Weakness w(h) is the exact number of worlds consistent with h, and each
     single-combinator extension both increases cost by ci and strictly reduces
     weakness.

    This yields not merely the one-sided Kraft bound w(h) ≥ N 2−σ(h) , but a
bijective monotonicity:

                     σ(h0 ) = σ(h) + ci =⇒ w(h0 ) < w(h),

and when all ci = 1 exactly

                               w(h) = N 2−σ(h) .

Thus CoDD realizes Bennett’s Occam-weakness equivalence in its purest form:
every additional combinator shrinks the hypothesis by a provable factor, and
minimal-length hypotheses are exactly maximal-weakness hypotheses without
any slack or constant–factor losses.




                                       68
5.6    Program Search with ENF and CoDD Weakness
So what. might be the use of this elegant , weakness-friendly CoDD represen-
tation of programs? Not for humans to program in, obviously. But there could
be advantages for automated program learning.
    We suggest that combining the Elegant Normal Form (ENF) representation
of Combinational Decision DAGs (CoDDs) with the tight correspondence be-
tween CoDD cost and hypothesis weakness yields a principled framework for
searching weak models that fit a dataset.

  1. Representation. Each candidate hypothesis is encoded as a CoDD in
     ENF. ENF guarantees a unique, canonical DAG per hypothesis, maximal
     sharing of common subexpressions, and a direct node count equal to the
     CoDD cost σ(h).
  2. Fitness Function. For each hypothesis h compute:

       Loss(h)   (data fit), QQuadn = #{nodes in ENF of h}    (CoDD cost).

      Combine by a regularized score,

                                F (h) = Loss(h) + λ n,

      where λ > 0 trades off generalization bias.
  3. Search Algorithm. Employ evolutionary or stochastic search over ENF
     trees:
        • Mutations add, delete or swap combinator nodes, then re-merge ENF
          subgraphs.
        • Crossovers splice parent DAGs at shared subtrees, preserving ENF.
        • ENF recomputation after each edit runs in time proportional to the
          edit size.
  4. Generalization Guarantee. In CoDD, adding a single combinator in-
     creases cost by a fixed positive amount and strictly decreases weakness.
     Penalizing node count therefore enforces an exact discrete Occam bias,
     ensuring that higher F (h) correlates with hypotheses that both fit data
     and generalize.
  5. Practical Benefits.
        • Deduplication: ENF collapses identical subprograms, focusing search
          effort.
        • Smooth landscape: each node edit yields a controlled change in both
          Loss and cost.
        • Scalability: DAG sharing avoids exponential blow-up when combin-
          ing good substructures.


                                        69
         • Theory-backed bias: the cost-weakness theorem guarantees sound
           Occam-style regularization without ad-hoc heuristics.

    This framework thus seems to have potential to provide an efficient, seman-
tically grounded method for evolving the weakest models that explain observed
data.


6       Quantale-based Weakness as ”Generalized MDL”
Having worked through various aspects of the relation between set-theoretic
weakness and standard MDL, we now show that that general quantale weakness
can be understood as a form of Minimum Description Length – however, not
in the usual sense of bit-strings, but in a generalized ”language” built from the
objects themselves. By viewing each object as a symbol whose code-word lives
in the free commutative quantale on our set of generators, we obtain a natural
”length” function valued in the same quantale. The weakness of any relation
then emerges as the maximal ”description length” of the two-symbol code-words
it contains. In the special case where our quantale is the familiar natural-number
length algebra, this construction reduces exactly to classical MDL.

6.1     Generalized MDL Interpretation of Quantale-based
        Weakness
Let (V, ⊗, e, ≤) be any commutative quantale, and let

                                  µ : U −→ V

be a given object-weighting (measure) on the underlying set U . We now show
how the usual ”weakness” measure given above can be viewed as a kind of
Minimum Description Length in a free quantale ”language.”


6.1.1    The free commutative quantale on U .
Form the free commutative quantale
                 X
        N[U ] =      nu u nu ∈ N, all but finitely many nu = 0 ,
                    u∈U

where
 X       X      X                          X        X      X
    nu u ⊗  mu u =  (nu +mu ) u,                 nu u ⊕  mu u =  max(nu , mu ) u,

and the unit e is the empty sum.




                                       70
6.1.2    Induced length map.
There is a unique surjective quantale homomorphism

                                  Φ : N[U ]  V

sending each generator u ∈ U to its weight µ(u) ∈ V . We define the length of
any multiset w ∈ N[U ] by

                                `(w) = Φ(w) ∈ V.

Concretely, if w = u1 + · · · + un , then

                      `(w) = µ(u1 ) ⊗ µ(u2 ) ⊗ · · · ⊗ µ(un ),

independent of ordering by commutativity.

Generalized Length-like Properties It is perhaps worth clarifying why
this is a reasonable way to generalize the ordinary notion of ”expression length”?
    The map
                                                         
` : N[U ] −→ V , ` u1 + · · · + un = Φ u1 + · · · + un = µ(u1 ) ⊗ · · · ⊗ µ(un )

satisfies the following axioms, mirroring the usual properties of a length function:

   1. Null length. The empty multiset is the unit of the free quantale, and its
      length is the unit of V :

                                   `(0) = Φ(0) = e.

   2. Additivity (monoid homomorphism). Concatenation (quantale mul-
      tiplication) adds lengths:

                             `(w1 ⊗ w2 ) = `(w1 ) ⊗ `(w2 ).

   3. Commutativity. Since N[U ] is commutative, any reordering of genera-
      tors leaves the length unchanged.
   4. Monotonicity. If w1 ≤ w2 in the free quantale order (pointwise inclusion
      of multiplicities), then
                                `(w1 ) ≤ `(w2 ).

   5. Join-preservation. The quantale join corresponds to taking the more
      expensive of two code-words:

                             `(w1 ⊕ w2 ) = `(w1 ) ⊕ `(w2 ).




                                            71
6.1.3    Weakness as MDL-style supremal length.
Each pair (u, v) ∈ H corresponds to the two-letter multiset u + v ∈ N[U ], whose
length is
                           `(u + v) = µ(u) ⊗ µ(v).
Hence the quantale-valued weakness
                         M                             M
             w(H) =           µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) =                      `(u + v)
                        (u,v)∈H                        (u,v)∈H

is exactly the ”supremum of description-lengths” of all two-letter code-words
drawn from H. In this sense, the native quantale-valued weakness is a gener-
alized MDL: the free algebra N[U ] plays the role of the language, `( · ) is the
code-length function, and w(H) the maximal codelength over H.

6.2     Recovery of classical MDL.
It’s not hard to see how this general sort of MDL reduces to standard ”MDL
over one dimensional languages” when the quantale is appropriately defined.
    Suppose we take the special case where the quantale
                 (V, ⊗, e, ≤) = (N, +, 0, ≤)    and µ : U → N
assigns each generator an integer weight. Then the canonical map Φ : N[U ] → V
is in fact an isomorphism, and the length
                                  `(w) = Φ(w)
of any multiset w coincides with the usual sum-of-letter-weights code length.
Hence                    M                                  
               w(H) =         `(u + v) = max µ(u) + µ(v) ,
                                             (u,v)∈H
                        (u,v)∈H

which exactly recovers the standard MDL principle of taking the maximal com-
bined code length over the candidate code-words in H. In this way, classical
MDL appears as the special case of our generalized framework when the under-
lying quantale is the natural-number length quantale.

Relation to Bennett-Style Reduction via Kraft’s Inequality The ar-
gument just given is, in fact, just a rearrangement of a ”standard” reduction of
Bennett’s set-theoretic weakness to standard MDL (under the assumption of a
prefix-free language with a finite vocabulary, etc.)
   When U is finite and each µ(u) ∈ N, the five axioms for
                                   ` : N[U ] → N
(null length, additivity, commutativity, monotonicity, join-preservation) guar-
antee that the lengths {`(u) | u ∈ U } satisfy the Kraft-McMillan inequality
                                 X
                                    2−`(u) ≤ 1.
                                  u∈U


                                        72
By Kraft’s theorem, there thus exists a prefix code on U whose codeword-lengths
are exactly `(u), and the induced extension
                                                X
                         `(u1 + · · · + un ) =     `(ui )
                                                     i

becomes the ordinary MDL length of the string u1 · · · un . Hence the supremal
description length
                       M                                      
                w(H) =      `(u + v) = max `(u) + `(v)
                                                  (u,v)∈H
                             (u,v)∈H

coincides with Bennett’s classical reduction of quantale-weakness to the usual
MDL principle, under the finiteness and integer-length assumptions.

6.3    Morphisms between sequential and multiset languages
The general relation between multi-set based languages and ordinary sequence-
based languages is fairly simple but probably worth explicitly calling out.
   Obviously, in ordinary human or programming-language communications we
use sequences of symbols (a free monoid U ∗ ) rather than unordered multisets.
However, there is a canonical “forgetful” quantale homomorphism
                    q : (P (U ∗ ), ∪, concat)  (P (NN[U ]), ∪, ⊗)
induced by the map q : U ∗ → NN[U ] that sends each word to its commutative
multiset of letters. Concretely,
                q # (L) = { q(w) | w ∈ L},    q(w1 · w2 ) = q(w1 ) ⊗ q(w2 ).
            #
Because q       preserves both unions and concatenation, it is a morphism of quan-
tales.

6.4    Refinement of weakness to MDL in ordinary languages
Given a weight map ` : NN[U ] → V , one obtains a V -valued length on sequences
by                                             
                             `seq (w) = ` q(w) .
For any relation H ⊆ U × U , the multiset-weakness
                                    M             
                         w(H) =           ` {u, v}
                                        (u,v)∈H

coincides with the supremal length of all two-letter words under `seq . Thus
the abstract quantale-weakness exactly lifts to a Minimum Description Length
on ordinary sequential codes. In the special case V = P (S) with union and
intersection, this construction gives a faithful embedding of multiset messages
into set-theoretic descriptions, showing that – as we have already concluded
above in a more specialized way – any multiset-based agent protocol can be
implemented in a standard symbolic language without loss of the weakness
(expressivity) metric.


                                             73
6.5     Extensions to Other Quantales
The same pattern of ”forgetting order” and passing from sequential codes to
multiset codes carries over to richer quantales. For the probability quantale
V = ([0, 1], ×, +), one defines

                q : U ∗ → NN[U ],         q(w1 · w2 ) = q(w1 ) ⊗ q(w2 ),

and then sets                     Y                   O
                    `prob (w) =          p(u) nu =            µ(u)nu ,
                                  u∈U                u∈q(w)
                                  L
so that the multiset-weakness (u,v)∈H µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) agrees with the supremum
of product-lengths of two-letter words under `prob . Likewise for the quantum-
operator quantale (End(H), ◦, ⊕), one treats each unitary or projection symbol
as a generator, sends a sequence of gates to its multiset of gate-types, and then
reconstructs the overall operator by multiplying (composing) the generators
in any order. In each case the forgetful map q : U ∗  NN[U ] is a quantale
homomorphism, and the induced length on sequences `quantum (w) = `(q(w))
or `prob (w) = `(q(w)) reproduces exactly the multiset-based weakness measure
when maximized over the relevant pairs.

6.6     Example: Quantum Operator Multiset Language
To illustrate the breadth of this framework, let’s consider a totally different sort
of communication – between two quantum systems, assuming their states as
”uncollapsed”, communicating about aspects of their states using the multiset-
language corresponding to the quantum-operator quantale.

Vocabulary of primitive rotations. Two uncollapsed quantum agents agree
on a single basic rotation about the z-axis:

                                 µ(r) = Rz π4 ∈ End(C2 ),
                                             
                    U = {r},

where
                        Rz (φ) = e−iφZ/2
                                                                
                                                 Z = Pauli Z
is the unitary rotation through angle φ. The free commutative quantale on U
is
                            N[U ] = { n · r | n ∈ N},
with

 (n · r) ⊗ (m · r) = (n + m) · r,       (n · r) ⊕ (m · r) = max(n, m) · r,   e = 0 · r.

The induced length map is
                                                  n
                   `(n · r) = Φ(n · r) = Rz ( π4 ) = Rz n π4 .
                                                           




                                            74
Communicating an internal phase. If an agent’s internal state is the uni-
tary Rz (θ), it chooses the smallest n ∈ N such that

              n π4 = θ   (or the nearest multiple if θ is not exact),

and sends the multiset
                                 wmin (θ) = n · r.
Upon receipt, the other agent composes n copies of Rz ( π4 ) to reconstruct Rz (θ)
exactly (or approximately).

Distinctive features.

    • Messages are finite multisets of identical primitives, not sequences of sym-
      bols.
    • The rotations commute, so only the count n matters.
    • The “length” `(n · r) = Rz (nπ/4) is an operator in End(C2 ), not a bit-
      count.
    • This lets uncollapsed quantum agents communicate continuous internal
      parameters (rotation angles) via discrete multiset codes in the operator
      quantale.


7     Weakness-Guided Probabilistic Logical Infer-
      ence
In this section we show how the abstract notion of weakness can be integrated
into the Probabilistic Logic Networks framework for uncertain AI inference
[Goe08] by casting PLN semantics as a Probabilistic Graph-Structured Lambda
Theory (P-GSLT). This provides a unified, graph-based foundation for both
hypothesis finding and inference control, ensuring that more general (weaker)
explanations are explored first, subject to probabilistic plausibility.

7.1     Graph-Structured Lambda Theory
Graph-Structured Lambda Theory (GSLT) [F1R25] represents lambda- or combinator-
style terms as a directed labeled graph G = (V, E). Each node v ∈ V is labeled
by a symbol (variable, constant, combinator, or predicate), and each directed
edge e = (u → v) ∈ E carries a label indicating its logical or computational
role (e.g. application, argument, or annotation). Reduction rules – such as
beta-reduction or combinator rewrites – are implemented by local graph trans-
formations: whenever a pattern subgraph L is found, it can be replaced by
another subgraph R according to the rewrite rule. This approach unifies term
syntax and operational semantics in a single combinatorial structure.



                                        75
7.2    Probabilistic Enrichment
To model uncertainty in this setting, we enrich GSLT into a Probabilistic GSLT
(P-GSLT) by assigning probabilities to nodes and edges. Concretely:
   • A prior probability PP(v) ∈ [0, 1] is attached to each node v ∈ V , reflecting
     the plausibility of the corresponding atomic proposition or combinator.
   • A conditional probability QQ(e) ∈ [0, 1] is attached to each edge e ∈ E,
     expressing the likelihood that the edge’s relation holds given its source
     node.

Under a standard Bayesian factorization, the prior probability of any subgraph
H ⊆ G is                       Y              Y
                   Pr(H) =         PP(v) ×        QQ(e).
                                 v∈H            e∈H

Observed data D then refines these priors into posteriors Pr(H | D) via Bayes’
rule. This construction integrates logical structure and probabilistic reasoning
in a single semantic framework.

7.3    Probabilistic Weakness in a GSLT Setting
We define a partial order among hypotheses (subgraphs) by saying H1 is weaker
(more general) than H2 , written H1  H2 , if for every probability threshold
p ∈ [0, 1],
                    Pr(H2 | D) ≥ p =⇒ Pr(H1 | D) ≥ p.
In words, any data instance that makes H2 sufficiently probable also makes H1
at least that probable. Equivalently, H1 must cover all the explanatory power
of H2 and possibly more. This formalizes the intuitive notion that a weaker
hypothesis is one that applies to a broader range of situations without dropping
below any probability threshold.

Derivation from Categorical Weakness To see formally why this is the
same as the probabilistic weakness we discussed above, recall that in the enriched-
category framework we set

                            w(H | D) = Pr(H | D),

the posterior probability of hypothesis H given data D. It is straightforward to
show that the partial order


      H1  H2     ⇐⇒     ∀ p ∈ [0, 1], Pr(H2 | D) ≥ p =⇒ Pr(H1 | D) ≥ p

is equivalent to comparing these weakness values.




                                       76
7.4    Semantic Foundation of PLN via Graph-Structured
       Lambda Theory
Graph-Structured Lambda Theory (GSLT) provides a unified, higher-order, and
graph-based semantics in which both terms (logical assertions) and rewrites
(inference steps) are first-class citizens. We now outline how PLN’s uncertain-
inference machinery embeds naturally into a GSLT enriched over the PLN quan-
tale.

Terms and Rewrites as a Reduction Graph                     A GSLT is specified by

   • two sorts, T (terms) and R (rewrites),
   • constructors
           – s, t : R → T assigning source/target terms to each rewrite,
           – term-constructors such as application ( ) : T × T → T and abstrac-
             tion [] : (T → T ) → T ,
           – rewrite-constructors ρ(·) for each inference rule (e.g. deduction, in-
             duction, abduction),
   • equations of the form
                                                         
                               ρ(~x) : s ρ(~x) 7−→ t ρ(~x)

      declaring that whenever a term matches s(ρ), it may be rewritten to t(ρ).
In PLN, each inference rule is exactly such a rewrite: matching premise-terms
annotated with STVs and producing a conclusion-term with its propagated
STV. Thus the space of all PLN proofs is realized as the reduction graph of the
GSLT.

Quantale Enrichment = Truth-Value Arithmetic                        We enrich the GSLT
reduction graph in the commutative quantale

VPLN = [0, 1]×[0, 1],     (s, c)⊕(s0 , c0 ) = (s+s0 , c+c0 ),   (s, c)⊗(s0 , c0 ) = (s s0 , c c0 ),

so that:

   • parallel derivations of the same conclusion aggregate via ⊕,
   • sequential chaining of inference steps multiplies via ⊗.
Concretely, the hom-object from premises to conclusion in the enriched category
is the ”sum” (over all paths) of ”products” (along each path) of edge-weights –
exactly reproducing PLN’s STV propagation formulas.




                                           77
Higher-Order and Modal Interpretations Because GSLT admits higher-
order term-constructors (e.g. abstraction on T → T ), it inherently supports
higher-order probabilities (probabilities about probabilities). PLN’s simple truth
values then appear as pragmatic projections (second- or third-order marginals)
of this fully higher-order semantics. Moreover, identifying suitable rewrite-
constructors yields modal types

                                  hAiB,      [A]B

that correspond to PLN’s conditional and guaranteed-entailment modalities.

Change-of-Base and Practical Approximation Different truth-value struc-
tures arise by changing the enrichment quantale. For example, the homomor-
phism
                Φ : VCD −→ VPLN ,      Φ(x, x0 ) = (N x, N x0 )
is precisely a change-of-base functor that renormalizes paraconsistent truth val-
ues into PLN evidence counts, preserving both ⊕ and ⊗. In implementation, one
works with a coarse-grained GSLT for tractable proof search and a fine-grained
GSLT with indexing and normal-form optimizations for efficiency.

5. Summary By viewing PLN’s formulas as T -terms and its inference rules
as R-rewrites in a GSLT enriched over (VPLN , ⊕, ⊗), we obtain a fully composi-
tional, higher-order semantics in which:
     • proof-theoretic derivations correspond to paths in the reduction graph,
     • probabilistic strength-and-confidence calculations arise from the quantale
       enrichment,
     • higher-order and modal aspects of uncertainty are captured by the theory’s
       native support for function-type constructors and modal rewrite-symbols.
This categorical-enriched perspective thus elegantly unifies the symbolic and
numeric facets of PLN.

7.5      Inference Control with Weakness Ordering
??
   To leverage probabilistic weakness in PLN proof search, one simplistic but
potentially helpful strategy is to use the following algorithm:

Basic idea: Maintain a priority queue of candidate subgraphs (hypotheses),
always expanding the weakest ones first, subject to a minimum posterior prob-
ability. In more detail:

     1. Initialization. Generate a set of initial hypotheses by matching observed
        facts D to small subgraphs of G. Compute each hypothesis’s posterior
        Pr(H | D).


                                        78
    2. Priority Queue. Insert all hypotheses with Pr(H | D) ≥ τ into a queue
       ordered by the weakness relation: if H1  H2 , then H1 precedes H2 .
    3. Search Loop.
         • Pop the most general (weakest) hypothesis H.
         • If H meets the success criterion (e.g. predicts D with high confi-
           dence), return H.
         • Otherwise, apply applicable rewrite rules to H to produce refined
           hypotheses H 0 , compute Pr(H 0 | D), and insert those with Pr ≥ τ
           into the queue.
    4. Termination. Halt when the queue is empty or a satisfactory hypothesis
       is found.

    Because weaker hypotheses cover more of the data space, this strategy prior-
itizes broad, high-entropy explanations before drilling down into special cases,
improving both efficiency and generalization.


8     Factor Graphs over Commutative Quantales
Factor graphs were originally introduced in the context of Bayesian networks as
a generalized graphical model that makes both the factorisation of a joint dis-
tribution and the flow of messages in belief-propagation explicit. In particular,
any Bayesian network can be converted into a semantically equivalent factor
graph, and standard inference algorithms (sum-product, max-product) admit a
uniform description in this framework.
    Paul Rosenbloom and colleagues have advocated (Bayesian) factor graphs
as a broadly applicable, ”universal” representation for AGI architectures, argu-
ing that many forms of knowledge – probabilistic, logical, relational – can be
encoded as factors and variables in a single graph [Ros10]. While we wouldn’t
go quite that far – in our humble view factor graphs are not literally the one
grand unified representation for every aspect of intelligence – they do provide a
remarkably versatile substrate for combining diverse inference rules and knowl-
edge modalities in a uniform, parallelizable way.
    In this section we extend the factor-graph paradigm by replacing real-valued
weights with elements of an arbitrary commutative quantale, thereby capturing
not only probabilities but also fully-quantified logical expressions and graded-
weakness or confidence measures la PLN, CDL and related formalisms. We
show how the same message-passing machinery carries over verbatim to the
quantale setting, and how this enriched view naturally supports multiresolution
inference, structured priors, and incremental updates.
    We also discuss the practical realization of quantale factor graphs in the
Hyperon framework using the MOR AtomSpace. MORKs efficient storage of
atoms, hyperedges and attributes aligns perfectly with the factor-graph data
model, enabling rapid insertion, retraction and indexed lookup of factors and


                                       79
variables. This makes quantale factor graphs not only theoretically unified but
also immediately usable as a background inference engine in a Hyperon-based
AGI system.

8.1       Factor Graphs in Commutative Quantales
  1. Quantale recap. A commutative quantale is a tuple
                                        _
                            (Q, ≤, ⊗, e, )

        where
          • (Q, ≤) is a complete lattice,
          • ⊗ is an associative, commutative product distributing over arbitrary
            joins,
          • e ∈ Q is the unit for ⊗,
            W
          •    denotes the join (least upper bound).
  2. Variables and factors.
          • Variable nodes v ∈ V , each ranging over a domain Dv .
          • Factor nodes f ∈ F , each equipped with a local function
                                        Y
                                  φf :       Dv −→ Q.
                                       v∈Ne(f )


  3. Global weight. A full assignment x = (xv )v∈V is given weight
                                   O              
                          W (x) =       φf xNe(f ) .
                                            f ∈F


  4. Marginalization. To marginalize out V \ S one computes
                                 _ hO               i
                      W (xS ) =           φf xNe(f ) .
