8.1.1     Cheap Weakness Computation via Message Passing
Because the factor graph is typically sparse, one can run a generalized belief-
propagation algorithm:
                                                      W
   • Each message update involves only a few ⊗ and operations.
   • Complexity per iteration is O(|V | + |F |).
              W
   • If ⊗ and are chosen to be cheap (e.g. min-plus, truncated sum), updates
     are very fast.


                                        80
8.1.2   Storing Factor Graphs in Mork
Factor graphs, due to their intrinsic hierarchical nature, lend themselves very
naturally to implementation in MORK as an Atomspace substrate.
   In a Mork AtomSpace:

   • VariableAtoms represent variable nodes.
   • FactorAtoms represent factor nodes, tagged by rule type.
   • HyperEdgeLinks connect each FactorAtom to its neighboring VariableAtoms.
   • AttributeAtoms store the local quantale functions or weights.

Mork supports
   • O(log N ) insertion/retraction of atoms and links,
   • indexing and subsumption lookups for lattice-structured quantales,

   • efficient bulk and incremental updates.

8.2     Factor Graphs over a Quantale of Logical Expressions
One very interesting application of quantale factor graphs is to the domain of
logical expressions. This allows a much richer use of factor graphs for probabilis-
tic logic than is achieved in conventional Bayes Net and related applications.

8.2.1   The Quantale of Logical Expressions
   • Carrier Q: the set of all well-formed formulas (WFFs) in a given logic,
     modulo logical equivalence.
   • Order ≤: logical entailment,

                                 p≤q      ⇐⇒     p |= q.

   • Monoid product ⊗: conjunction,

                                     p ⊗ q = (p ∧ q).

   • Unit e: the tautology (True).
          W
   • Join : disjunction (least upper bound under ≤),
                             _
                                pi = p1 ∨ p2 ∨ · · · .
                                 i




                                         81
8.2.2      Variables, Factors, and Global Formula
   • Variables v: each ranges over a domain Dv (e.g. Boolean or finite).
   • Factors f : come with a local constraint formula

                                           φf (v) ∈ Q,

        where v = {v1 , . . . , vk } are the free variables of φf .
   • Global conjunction:
                                         O                 ^
                             Φ(V) =             φf (V) =          φf (V),
                                         f ∈F              f ∈F

        a single formula in Q over all variables V.

8.2.3     Marginalization as Existential Quantification
To focus on a subset S ⊆ V, we compute
                         _ h^          i           ^
             ΦS (vS ) =          φf (v) = ∃ vV\S .   φf (v).
                              vV\S f ∈F                            f ∈F


8.2.4      Message-Passing of Formulas
   • Variable→Factor: each variable sends to f the join of all incoming
     factor-messages.
   • Factor→Variable: to send from factor f to variable v:
          1. Conjoin φf with all incoming messages from other neighbors.
          2. Existentially quantify out all variables except v.
          3. The result is the new message mf →v (v).

8.2.5     Efficiency Considerations
   • Formula representation: compile to a shared canonical form (AST,
     BDD, SDD) for subformula sharing.
   • Incremental updates: adding or changing one φf only triggers local
     message updates.
   • Shared subformulas: identical subexpressions are stored once and reused
     across factors.




                                                82
8.2.6     Interpretation
This construction yields a distributed constraint network:
                                                     V
   • Global consistency is checking satisfiability of f φf .
   • Marginals are logical projections via ∃.
   • Message-passing is constraint propagation via ∧ and ∃.

8.3      Combinatory Decision DAGs as a Special Case of Quan-
         tale Factor Graphs
In this section we show how any Combinatory Decision DAG (CoDD) can be
viewed as a factor graph over a suitable commutative quantale Q. The usual
CoDD inference algorithm then becomes an instance of quantale-valued message
passing.

8.3.1     Quantale and Factor Graph Definitions
   • Quantale Q. A commutative quantale is a tuple
                                         _
                             (Q, ≤, ⊗, e, )

        where ≤ is a complete lattice order, ⊗ is an associative, W commutative
        product distributing over joins, e is the unit for ⊗, and   is the lattice
        join.
   • Factor graph. A factor graph is a bipartite graph (V, F, E) where
          – V is a set of variable nodes, each ranging over some domain Dv .
                                                                        Q
          – F is a set of factor nodes, each with a local potential φf : v∈Ne(f ) Dv →
            Q.
          – E ⊆ V × F are edges connecting variables to factors.

8.3.2     Encoding a CoDD as a Factor Graph
Given a CoDD with decision nodes implementing various PLN rules and edges
carrying ”weakness” values:
  1. Variables. For each edge in the CoDD (i.e. each intermediate proposition
     or pattern), introduce a variable node v ∈ V whose domain is the set of
     possible ”truth” or ”pattern” values.
  2. Factors. For each CoDD decision node d, introduce a factor node f ∈ F
     with local potential
                          φf (xv1 , . . . , xvk ) = xv1 ⊗f · · · ⊗f xvk ,
        where ⊗f is the quantale operation corresponding to the PLN rule encoded
        by d. The neighbors {v1 , . . . , vk } are precisely the edges feeding into d.


                                            83
  3. Global inference. A complete assignment x to all variables has weight
                                   O              
                          W (x) =       φf xNe(f ) .
                                        f ∈F

        Computing the marginal weight of any variable v corresponds to summing
        out (joining over) all other variables:
                                           _ hO         i
                                W (xv ) =         φf (x) .
                                       xV \{v} f ∈F


8.3.3     Message-Passing Implements CoDD Inference
By running a generalized belief-propagation algorithm over this factor graph:
                                                   W
   • Variable→Factor messages collect the join ( ) of incoming factor mes-
     sages.
   • Factor →Variable messages combine (via ⊗) the local potential φf Wwith
     incoming variable messages, then eliminate other variables by join ( ).

   These updates exactly mirror CoDD’s downstream propagation of weakness
values through combinatory decision nodes, yielding the same graded inference
results but in a unified, parallelizable framework.

8.3.4     Advantages of the Factor-Graph View
   • Structural sharing. Repeated sub-DAGs in the CoDD become shared
     factor nodes, avoiding redundant recomputation.
   • Incremental updates. Adding or modifying a CoDD node corresponds
     to inserting or updating a single factor, with O(log N ) cost in Mork.
   • Multiresolution. By choosing Q to be a chain of sub-quantales, one can
     perform coarse-to-fine weakness inference akin to multigrid methods.

   Thus every CoDD is naturally a special case of a quantale-valued factor
graph, enabling efficient, incremental, and parallel graded inference.

8.4      Efficient PLN Background-Inference via Quantale-Annotated
         Logical Factor Graphs
In order to support wide-ranging ”background inference” over a large Atom-
Space, we maintain an ongoing Factor Graph whose variable nodes correspond
to logical expressions and whose factor nodes encode the combination rules of
PLN. Each logical expression carries, as a separate annotation, its current uncer-
tainty (truth-value) living in a distinct commutative quantale Qtruth . Inference
then proceeds by lightweight message-passing over this graph rather than by
expensive rule-search, enabling rapid discovery of implicit knowledge.


                                       84
8.4.1     Quantale-Annotated Logical Factor Graph
   • Variable nodes. Each free logical expression φ in the AtomSpace is a
     variable node v with domain {true, false}.
   • Annotations. On each v we store an attribute α(v) ∈ Qtruth representing
     its current PLN truth-value or confidence.
   • Factor nodes. For each PLN inference rule application r deriving ψ from
     premises φ1 , . . . , φk , we create a factor node fr with local potential

                   φfr (α(φ1 ), . . . , α(φk )) = α(φ1 ) ⊗r · · · ⊗r α(φk )

        where ⊗r is the quantale operation associated with rule r.
   • Global inference. The combined confidence of any expression φ is ob-
     tained by marginalizing over all pathsW in the graph, i.e. by join-summing
     over all factor contributions via the   operation of Qtruth .

8.4.2     Background Inference Heuristic in MeTTa-Style Pseudocode
Below is a sketch of a MeTTa-like module that continually ingests new facts, up-
dates the factor graph, and propagates uncertainties to reveal all high-confidence
conclusions:

(module PLN.BackgroundInference

  ;;--- Initialize global factor graph over quantale Q_truth
  (def fg (fg-create :quantale Q_truth))

  ;;--- Add or update a fact phi with truth-value val
  (defn update-fact! (phi val)
    ;; ensure var-node exists
    (when-not (fg-has-var? fg phi)
      (fg-add-var fg phi Boolean))
    ;; annotate its current truth
    (fg-set-annotation fg phi val)
    ;; for each applicable PLN rule r deriving psi from phi:
    (for r in (find-pln-rules phi)
      (let ((psi (apply-rule-head r phi)))
        ;; ensure factor-node exists
        (when-not (fg-has-fctr? fg r phi psi)
          (fg-add-fctr fg r (list phi) psi))
        ;; set rule-specific tensor on factor
        (fg-set-factor-op! fg r :tensor (tensor-op r)))))

  ;;--- Main background loop
  (defn background-loop ()


                                          85
      ;; ingest newly asserted or retracted facts
      (let ((deltas (observe-atomspace-deltas)))
        (for (phi,val) in deltas
          (update-fact! phi val)))
      ;; run one round of message-passing
      (fg-infer fg (all-vars fg))
      ;; extract and act on new high-confidence conclusions
      (for phi in (vars-with-annotation-above fg threshold)
        (assert-to-atomspace phi (fg-get-annotation fg phi)))
      ;; schedule next iteration in 1 second
      (sleep 1) (background-loop))

  ;; start the background inference
  (background-loop))

   This design leverages:
   • Sparse updates, since only the local subgraph around changed facts is
     revisited;
   • Parallel message-passing, enabling multi-core scaling over millions of nodes;

   • Quantale joins and tensors, giving consistent graded aggregation of all
     inference paths;
   • Anytime behavior, as partial results are always available during continuous
     background processing.

8.5    Peircean Weakness in PLN Factor?Graph Message Pass-
       ing
In standard PLN (Probabilistic Logic Networks) inference, factor?graph mes-
sage?passing exploits a weakness measure to schedule and prune messages, typ-
ically based on product?measure assumptions. Here we show how to replace
that traditional weakness with a Peircean weakness derived from a habit?based
prior, thereby biasing inference toward the most ?entrenched?? links. This
modification is interesting because it aligns the propagation order with Peirce?s
notion of habit formation, potentially yielding more human?like generalizations
in noisy or sparse data settings.

Peircean Habit Prior.       Introduce a joint ?habit?strength?? valuation
                                                      µP (u, v)
            µP : U × U −→ R≥0 ,         Pr (u, v) ∈ H =          ,
                                                          Z
capturing Peirce?s tendency to form habits :contentReference[oaicite:2]index=2.




                                       86
Peircean Weakness Function. For any message carrying formula φ whose
atomic links are Links(φ) ⊆ U × U , define
                                          X
                    WeaknessP (φ) =         µP (u, v).
                                      (u,v)∈Links(φ)


Modified Message Scheduling. In the standard Weakness?Driven Belief?Propagation
algorithm, each message mf →v is assigned

                             w = Weakness(mf →v ).

We replace this by
                          wP = WeaknessP (mf →v ),
                           
and enqueue wP , mf →v , v in the priority queue ordered by ascending wP .
Pruning and update steps?discarding messages with wP < τ , combining via ⊗,
and propagation to neighbors?remain as in the original algorithm :contentRef-
erence[oaicite:3]index=3.

Conceptual Impact. By prioritizing messages with lower Peircean weakness,
the inference process focuses first on the strongest habitual associations. This
yields a propagation order that mirrors human tendencies to rely on well?worn
patterns, potentially improving generalization when data are scarce or noisy.


Part II
Universal & Quantum AI
9    Weakness-Flavored Universal AI
Practical intelligence, biological or artificial, is largely about coping with re-
source restrictions of various sorts – temporal, spatial, energetic and so forth.
Nonetheless, it has proved valuable from an AI system and algorithm design
perspective to also look at what happens when these resource restrictions are
substantially relaxed and explore what AI systems may look like under highly
(even very unrealistically) abundant resource scenarios. This research direction
has led to systems like Marcus Hutter’s AIXI [Hut05] and Juergen Schmid-
huber’s Godel Machine [Sch07], among others. We develop here versions of
these theoretical AI systems founded on the weakness concept, showing some
interesting theoretical properties that in some ways simplify things compared
to classical approaches based on other concepts such as minimum description
length.




                                       87
9.1     WAIXI – AIXItl via CoDD Weakness
We sketch here an ”WAIXI” analogue of Hutter’s resource-bounded AIXItl
agent, replacing Solomonoff/MML priors with a weakness prior over CoDD pro-
grams in Elegant Normal Form.

9.1.1   Agent-Environment Interaction
At each time step t = 1, 2, . . . the agent has history
                        ht−1 = a1 o1 r1 . . . at−1 ot−1 rt−1 ,
where ai ∈ Act are actions, oi ∈ Obs observations, and ri ∈ R rewards. Upon
choosing at , the environment returns (ot , rt ).

9.1.2   Model Class: CoDD Predictors
Let Ht,` be the set of all CoDD programs h in ENF such that:
   • h takes as input a history ht−1 and candidate action a and outputs a
     distribution Pbh (o, r | ht−1 , a).
   • The description cost σ(h) ≤ ` (CoDD cost bound).
   • The runtime of h on any input of length ≤ t is at most t (time bound).

9.1.3   Weakness Prior
Define a prior weight on each h ∈ Ht,` by
                     w(h)α
      π(h) = P               0 α
                                 ,   w(h) = CoDD-weakness of h,       α > 0.
                 h0 ∈Ht,` w(h )

This favours weaker (more general) predictors within the resource bounds.

For any h ∈ Ht,` and continuation policy π, define the expected m’step return
                       X                      h                                 i
       h                                                 h                  0
     Vt,m (ht−1 , a) =   Pbh (o, r | ht−1 , a) r + max
                                                    0
                                                       Vt,m−1 (ht−1 oa, r, a  )  ,
                                                     a
                       o,r
       h
with Vt,0 ≡ 0 and m chosen so that t+m does not exceed the global time bound.

9.1.5   Action Selection
We then define an AIXItl -CoDD agent (a species of WAIXI, weakness-driven
AIXI, agent) that picks

                   at = arg max      π(h) Vt,m  ht−1 , a .

                                     h∈Ht,`

This maximizes the posterior-weighted expected return, where the posterior is
just the prior (no updating) restricted to predictors that run within (t, `).


                                         88
9.1.6   Discussion
   • Weakness prior vs. MDL prior: We replace 2−`(h) by w(h)α , biasing to-
     ward CoDD programs that carve out the largest set of possible environ-
     ments.

   • Resource bounds: By limiting both σ(h) and runtime, we recover the
     tractability of AIXItl .
   • Algebraic synergy: Under CoDD’s monoidal ENF, crossover-like opera-
     tions preserve weakness additively. Thus if one were to evolve the model
     class Ht,` via genetic operators, schematic theorems guarantee convergence
     toward maximal-weakness predictors.
   • Updatelessness: No posterior update is required–π(h) is fixed at decision
     time–so the agent is weakly updateless within its resource envelope.

   This construction unites the Occam-weakness principle, resource-bounded
computation, and AIXI’s Bayesian maximization in a single CoDD-based frame-
work.

9.1.7   Performance Guarantees via Weakness-Prior Mixtures
We have described a CoDD-based AIXItl agent that uses a fixed ”weakness
prior” π(h) ∝ w(h)α over a resource-bounded model class HCalt,` and selects
actions to maximize the mixture value
                                      X
                                                 h
               Vmixture (ht−1 , a) =      π(h) Vt,m (ht−1 , a).
                                    h∈HCalt,`

    To begin fleshing out how this sort of agent works, we now give two illus-
trative theorems showing how the algebraic properties of weakness yield clean
bounds on this mixture’s performance relative to any single model in HCalt,` .

Theorem 7 (Mixture-Performance Lower Bound). For every h ∈ HCalt,` , the
mixture value satisfies
                                   h
                                                              h
     Vmixture (ht−1 , a) ≥ π(h) Vt,m (ht−1 , a) = exp ln π(h) Vt,m  (ht−1 , a).

Since π(h) = w(h)α /Z with Z = h0 w(h0 )α , we get
                                   P

                                                     
                                                          h
            Vmixture (ht−1 , a) ≥ exp α ln w(h) − ln Z Vt,m (ht−1 , a).

In particular, if w(h) is close to 1 (a very weak, general model) then π(h) is
large and the mixture value is within a moderate constant factor of V h .
Sketch. Because π is a probability distribution, h0 π(h0 ) = 1, hence each non-
                                                P
                            0
negative summand π(h0 )V h cannot exceed the total. Rearranging gives the first
display.


                                      89
Theorem 8 (Regret Bound for True-Environment Model). Suppose the true
environment’s predictor h∗ ∈ HCalt,` has weakness w(h∗ ) > 0. Then at each
decision step,

            h ∗                                         1      1
      max Vt,m (ht−1 , a) − max Vmixture (ht−1 , a) ≤     ln        + ln Z,
       a                     a                          α    w(h∗ )
                      α                                                 ∗
             P
where Z =       h w(h) . As α − ∞, the regret tends to ln Z, and if w(h ) is
maximal then the regret vanishes.
Sketch. Using the log-sum-exp bound maxi xi ≤ α1 ln i eP
                                                    P αxi
                                                            , set xh = ln V h
                                                    1
(viewing values as positive). One shows maxh V ≤ α ln h w(h)α V h , which
                                               h
                         ∗
in turn bounds max V h by a mixture-logarithm. Converting back from logs
yields the stated regret bound.

Discussion.
   • The mixture-performance lower bound shows that any single model’s weak-
     ness directly boosts its contribution to the agent’s decision value.
   • The regret bound quantifies how much worse the mixture can do compared
     to the best resource-bounded predictor h∗ ; it grows only logarithmically
     in 1/w(h∗ ) and in the normalizer Z.
   • Because under CoDD’s ENF every syntactic extension strictly lowers weak-
     ness, one can control Z by limiting program size, and ensure w(h∗ ) remains
     substantial for highly general predictors.

   These results illustrate how the monotonicity and additivity properties of
CoDD weakness lead to transparent performance guarantees for the AIXItl -style
agent built on a weakness prior.

9.2    Bellman Dynamic Programming as a Special Case of
       WAIXI
It is interesting to observe how, under appropriate assumptions, a concrete
algorithm like Dijkstra’s shortest- path algorithm emerges as a special case of
weakness-AIXI. This is also a reasonably AGI-pertinent observation since a
number of important practical AGI algorithms can be viewed as approximate
stochastic dynamic programming [Goe21d].
    Let (Q, ⊗, ⊕) be a commutative quantale. View a directed graph C as a
Qenriched category with

                      Ob(C) = S,     C(s, s0 ) = qs→s0 ∈ Q,

the ”weight” of the arrow s → s0 .




                                       90
Hypotheses as Paths and Weakness Prior A hypothesis h is any finite
path
                            e1     e2        e
                h : s = s0 −−→ s1 −−→ · · · −−n→ sn = g.
Its weakness is the monoidal product of edge-weights:
                                       n
                                       O
                           w(h) =             C(si−1 , si ).
                                        i=1

WAIXI places a prior over paths via

                                       w(h)α
                        π(h) = P             0 α
                                                 ,     α > 0,
                                       h0 w(h )

biasing toward weakest (i.e. highest-w) paths .

Mixture Value as Soft Bellman Backup Define the WAIXI value of start-
ing in state s by mixing over all paths from s to goal g:
                                     M            
                           V (s) =        π(h) r(h) ,
                                   h:s→g

where r(h) is the reward of path h. If we further identify r(h) = w(h), then in
the limit α → ∞ the mixture concentrates on the single path maximizing w(h),
giving
                              V (s) = max w(h).
                                         h:s→g


Bellman Fixpoint Emergence The optimal-weakness value V (s) so defined
satisfies the Bellman equation in the quantale:
                                M                      
                        V (s) =      C(s, s0 ) ⊗ V (s0 ) .
                               s0 ∈S

This is exactly the shortest-path (or max-convolution) dynamic-programming
update, recovered as the α → ∞ limit of WAIXI planning under a weakness
prior.

Greedy Policy Extraction       A corresponding optimal action (edge) from s is

                        s∗ = arg max
                                  0
                                     C(s, s0 ) ⊗ V (s0 ),
                                   s

yielding the usual greedy policy for shortest-path planning.
    Thus shortest-path dynamic programming on a Q-enriched graph is exactly
WAIXI over the hypothesis class of all paths, with path-weakness as the Occam
prior, and its Bellman backup emerges in the α → ∞ limit of the WAIXI
mixture.


                                         91
9.3     Universality and Asymptotic Optimality of the Weakness-
        CoDD AIXItl Analogue
We now give universality and asymptotic optimality theorems for the Weakness-
CoDD AIXItl agent introduced above.

9.3.1   CoDD-ENF Universality
Let Act, Obs, R be finite sets of actions, observations and rewards, and let
HCalt,` be the class of CoDD-ENF programs of cost ≤ ` and runtime ≤ t that
map histories and actions to distributions over Obs × R.

Theorem 9 (Polynomial-size Simulator). For any deterministic (or probabilis-
tic) classical Turing machine M running in time T (n) and space S(n) on inputs
of length n, there exists a CoDD-ENF program
                                                                
            h ∈ HCalt,` with ` = O T (n) + S(n) , t = O T (n) ,

which simulates M step-by-step to inverse-polynomial accuracy in its output
distribution. Hence the CoDD-ENF class is Turing-universal up to polynomial
overhead.
Sketch. Encode each Turing-machine transition as a small CoDD combinator;
chaining T (n) transitions yields cost O(T (n)). Space bound S(n) limits the
size of the tape-encoding subgraph to O(S(n)). ENF merging prevents blow-up
beyond these overheads.

9.3.2   Asymptotic Optimality under Weakness Prior
                                                                  w(h)α
Recall the agent maintains a fixed ”weakness prior” π(h) = P           0 α
                                                                            over
                                          X                      h0 w(h )
                                                   h                       h
HCalt,` , and selects action at = arg max   π(h) Vt,m (ht−1 , a), where Vt,m  is
                                       a
                                               h
the m-step Bellman return under model h.
Theorem 10 (Regret Vanishing). Suppose the true environment predictor h∗ is
computable in time T (n) and thus lies in HCalt,` for all sufficiently large (t, `).
Then for any ε > 0 there exist T0 , L0 such that for all t ≥ T0 and ` ≥ L0 ,
                          ∗
                        h
                  max Vt,m (ht−1 , a) − max Vmix (ht−1 , a) < ε.
                   a                       a

In other words, the agent’s regret relative to the optimal computable predictor
vanishes as its resource bounds grow.
Sketch. By Theorem 9, h∗ can be encoded in HCalt,` for large (t, `). Since
π(h∗ ) > 0 and does not vanish, standard mixture-regret bounds (e.g. log-sum-
exp or mutual-information arguments) imply the weighted combination of V h
approaches maxh V h within ε as π(h∗ ) − 1 when ` − ∞ and α > 0.

9.3.3   Mixture-Performance Lower Bound

9.3.4   Conclusion
Together, Theorems 9 and 10 show that the Weakness-CoDD AIXItl analogue
is both computationally universal (simulating any effective environment predic-
tor) and asymptotically optimal (regret vanishes as resource bounds grow). The
mixture-value guarantee further quantifies how the Occam-weakness prior en-
sures robust contributions from general models throughout the decision process.

9.4     Sampling-Based Weakness Estimation and Robust Guar-
        antees
In practical situations we would basically always need to approximate the ideal
weakness prior π(h) ∝ w(h)α by Monte Carlo sampling from a posterior over
possible worlds. We now restate the definitions and theoretical results–including
error terms due to finite sampling or bootstrap noise.

9.4.1   Monte Carlo Weakness Estimator
Let W be the (unknown) distribution over worlds ω consistent with historical
data. We draw K independent samples ω1 , . . . , ωK ∼ W. For each CoDD
program h, define the Monte Carlo estimate
                      K
                    1 X                                 ŵ(h)α
            ŵ(h) =     1 h(ωk ) holds ,        π̂(h) = P        0 α
                                                                     .
                    K                                     h0 ŵ(h )
                       k=1


9.4.2   Bootstrap for Robustness
To guard against faulty observations or biased samples, we repeat the above B
times with data-resampling (bootstrap) and obtain ŵb (h) for b = 1, . . . , B. A
conservative estimate is w̃(h) = minb ŵb (h), or the lower (100−p)% percentile
of {ŵb (h)}. We then set π̃(h) ∝ w̃(h)α .




                                       93
9.4.3   Uniform Error Bound
By Hoeffding’s inequality, for any δ > 0 and finite program class HCal, with
probability at least 1 − η over sampling,

                                                          1      2|HCal|
           sup ŵ(h) − w(h) ≤ δ       provided    K ≥         ln         .
         h∈HCal                                          2δ 2       η

9.4.4   Effect on Universality
Theorem 11 (Support Preservation). If the true environment model h∗ ∈ HCal
has w(h∗ ) > 0, then for any δ < w(h∗ ), with high probability ŵ(h∗ ) > 0 and
hence π̂(h∗ ) > 0. Thus the Monte Carlo-approximate agent still assigns nonzero
weight to h∗ and retains universality.

9.4.5   Regret Bound with Prior Error
Let Vmix andPV̂mix be the exact and approximate mixture values respectively,
and let Z = h w(h)α . If suph |ŵ(h) − w(h)| ≤ δ then kπ̂ − πk1 ≤ O(δ), and
we have:
Theorem 12 (Regret Degradation). For any history ht−1 and decision step,
                                                              ∗
max Vmix (ht−1 , a) − max V̂mix (ht−1 , a) ≤ O(δ) max ln V h (ht−1 , a) + O(δ) ln Z.
  a                    a                             a

In particular, as δ − 0, the approximate regret converges to the ideal regret.

9.4.6   Sample Complexity
Combining the above, to ensure that with probability ≥ 1 − η the regret degra-
dation is ≤ ε, it suffices to choose
                            ε            1    |HCal| 
                   δ=O           ,    K = O 2 ln          .
                            ln Z            δ      η

9.4.7   Bootstrap Error Robustness
Bootstrap sampling further reduces bias: if each ŵb (h) estimates w(h) with error
δ and the bootstrap distribution has failure probability η/B, then the aggregated
estimate w̃(h) satisfies the same δ-error bound with probability 1−η, preserving
the above universality and regret bounds.

These results show that all earlier universality and regret-vanishing theorems
for the Weakness-CoDD AIXItl analogue continue to hold up to small additive
terms O(δ) due to Monte Carlo or bootstrap estimation error.




                                       94
9.5     Strengthened Regret Bounds via CoDD Weakness Struc-
        ture
The generic regret-degradation bounds of the previous section apply to any
prior. However, because the CoDD weakness prior obeys a Kraft-type exponen-
tial tail, we can obtain substantially tighter bounds that depend only on the
description length of the true model rather than the total hypothesis-space size.

9.5.1    Exponential Tail of the Weakness Prior
Recall that under CoDD ENF, each program h has weakness

                                 w(h) = N 2−σ(h) ,

and we set
                      w(h)α       2−α σ(h)           X           0
          π(h) = P          0 α
                                =          ,    Z=         2−α σ(h ) ≤ 1.
                      h0 w(h )      Z
                                                      h0

Hence
                       1                      1
                 ln        = α σ(h) ln 2 + ln   ≤ α σ(h) ln 2.
                      π(h)                    Z

9.5.2    Improved Mixture Regret Bound
Let V ∗ be the optimal m-step return under the true model h∗ , and let Vmix be
the mixture value under π. Then for any α > 0,
                                                                        ∗
 Vmix ≥ π(h∗ ) V ∗ =⇒ V ∗ − Vmix ≤ 1 − π(h∗ ) V ∗ ≤ V ∗ − 2−α σ(h ) V ∗ .
                                                 

Equivalently, using a log-sum-exp argument,
                                     1    1
                      V ∗ − Vmix ≤     ln     ≤ σ(h∗ ) ln 2.
                                     α π(h∗ )
Thus the regret is bounded purely in terms of the description length σ(h∗ ) of
the true model, independent of the total number of programs.

9.5.3    Effect of Monte Carlo / Bootstrap Error
Suppose our estimated prior π̂ arises from a weakness estimate ŵ(h) satisfying
suph |ŵ(h)−w(h)| ≤ δ. Then one shows kπ̂ −πk1 = O(δ), and the mixture-value
degradation satisfies

                        Vmix − V̂mix ≤ O(δ) maxa V ∗ (a),

so that the total regret under the approximate prior obeys

                 V ∗ − V̂mix ≤ σ(h∗ ) ln 2 + O(δ) max V ∗ (a).
                                                      a

In particular, for sufficiently small δ this remains dominated by σ(h∗ ) ln 2.


                                        95
9.5.4   Sample Complexity Improvement
Because the effective support of π concentrates on programs of length σ ≤ L
with total mass at least 1 − 2−αL , one needs only
                              1              1 
                         K = O 2 αL ln 2 + ln
                                             η
Monte Carlo samples to guarantee δ-uniform error with probability 1 − η. Thus
sample complexity scales with the true model’s description length L = σ(h∗ )
rather than ln |HCal|.

Summary By exploiting the exponential decay of the CoDD weakness prior,
we replace generic ln |HCal|-type regret and sampling-complexity bounds with
much tighter, length-based bounds that depend only on the description length
σ(h∗ ) of the true environment model.

9.6     When Description Length Dominates Sampling Error
What then can we say about the conditions under which the description length
rather than the MC/sampling error would be dominant?
   Recall our regret bound for the approximate-prior agent:

                      Regret ≤ σ(h∗ ) ln 2 + C δ Vmax ,

where

   • σ(h∗ ) ln 2 is the description-length term,
                                       √
   • δ = suph |ŵ(h) − w(h)| = O(1/ Keff ) is the Monte Carlo error,
                     ∗
   • Vmax = maxa V h (ht−1 , a),
   • C is a constant absorbing the log-sum-exp factors.

9.6.1   Dominance Condition
The description-length term dominates whenever

                                                            σ(h∗ ) ln 2
              σ(h∗ ) ln 2  C δ Vmax     ⇐⇒        δ                   .
                                                             C Vmax
              √
Since δ = O(1/ Keff ), this becomes

                                        C 2 Vmax
                                             2
                           Keff                     2 .
                                       σ(h∗ ) ln 2

Here Keff is the effective number of independent samples, which in practice is
K/τ if the sampler has mixing time τ . Thus in real-world systems:


                                       96
   • If the true model’s description length σ(h∗ ) is moderate and Vmax is
     bounded, then a polynomially large sample size K suffices to make the
     sampling term negligible.
   • If the generative sampler mixes rapidly (small τ ), Keff ≈ K and one
     achieves δ − 0 quickly, so the description-length term σ(h∗ ) ln 2 sets the
     regret floor.
   • Conversely, in systems with very complex generative models (large mixing
     time τ or high effective dimension), one may need impractically large K
     before δ falls below σ(h∗ ) ln 2/(C Vmax ). In that regime the MC error
     dominates until sufficient samples are drawn.

9.6.2   Scaling and Convergence of the Generative Model
More precisely, if the posterior sampler satisfies
                                               p τaspectral gap γ > 0, then by
standard Markov-chain concentration δ = O         K with τ = 1/γ. Substituting
gives                                          q         
                    Regret ≤ σ(h∗ ) ln 2 + O        τ
                                                    K Vmax     .
                                                                 √
Hence for fixed τ and Vmax , the sampling error term decays as 1/ K and the
                                                                  2
description-length term remains the dominant factor once K = Ω(τ Vmax /(σ(h∗ ) ln 2)2 ).

In summary, in realistic settings with well-behaved, moderately sized generative
models, one quickly reaches a regime where σ(h∗ ) ln 2 dominates the regret, and
further sampling yields diminishing returns. Only in the presence of very slow
mixing or high-dimensional posteriors does the Monte Carlo error term remain
significant for large numbers of samples.

9.6.3   Concrete Numerical Illustration
To get a more concrete feel for when the description-length term σ(h∗ ) ln 2
dominates the sampling error C δ Vmax , recall
                        r                                 2
                           τ                CV
                                                   max
                    δ≈       ,    Kτ                        .
                          K                   σ(h∗ ) ln 2

Here τ is the mixing-time of your world-sampler, Vmax the maximum achievable
return, and C ≈ 1 a modest constant from the log-sum-exp bound. We illustrate
two stylized real-world regimes:
Case A: Fast Mixing, Concise Model.

                      τ = 102 ,    σ(h∗ ) = 20,   Vmax = 10.

Then                             1 × 10 2
                K  102                     = 102 (0.721)2 ≈ 52.
                                20 × 0.693


                                         97
So K ∼ 102 -103 samples suffice, and the description-length term 20 ln 2 ≈ 13.9
dominates.

                     τ = 106 ,   σ(h∗ ) = 10,   Vmax = 100.

Here one needs a few million samples before δ Vmax < σ ln 2 ≈ 6.93. Only after
K & 2 × 106 does the description-length term begin to dominate.
Interpretation.
   • In well-behaved domains with small mixing-time (τ . 103 ) and moderately
     concise true models (σ & 10), σ ln 2 dominates after only a few hundred
     samples.
   • In challenging settings with slow samplers (τ & 106 ) or large returns
     (Vmax & 102 ), you may need millions of samples before the Occam-weakness
     term is the primary source of regret.
   Thus in practical systems one should benchmark the sampler’s mixing-time
τ and the true model’s description length σ(h∗ ) to choose a sample size K that
ensures the Occam-bound σ(h∗ ) ln 2 is indeed the dominant regret term.

9.7    Practical Viability of the Weakness-CoDD AIXItl Ana-
       logue
AIXItl is intended more of a theoretical construct than a practical AI approach.
However, it is still interesting to explore how far one could go toward making
it practically usable. There are some cases where this might actually be feasi-
ble, but more importantly, this can also be a way of framing the utilization of
other more practical techniques. Here we will make some back of the envelope
calculations regarding the practical use of both Weakness-CODD AIXItl and,
more scalably for most real-world uses, the combination of evolutionary learning
and EDAs [nL02] for learning control strategies. The latter approach has been
explored theoretical and in prototype experiments as part of the Hyperon AGI
design, and here
    We compare two implementation strategies for a Weakness-CoDD AIXItl -
style planner:
  1. Monte Carlo sampling of possible worlds to estimate the weakness prior.
  2. Asynchronous EDA (Estimation-of-Distribution Algorithm) seeded from
     a continually learned knowledge graph.
    In the following sections we will give stylized numerical estimates for the
cost of these two approaches, and explore these in the context of two concrete
use-cases: a mobile humanoid robot and a persistent, curious chatbot research
assistant.


                                       98

   NRmpop = 100, G = 20, K = 50, tRmeval = 0.5Rm s, f = 0.8, M = 100.

9.11     Use Case: Chatbot Research Assistant
9.11.1    Monte Carlo Planner

9.11.2    Asynchronous EDA Planner

  NRmpop = 100, G = 20, K = 500, tRmeval = 0.5Rm s, f = 0.8, M = 500.

reduce to minutes.

9.12     Conclusions
Wrapping up our crude back-of-the-envelope calculations:
   • Monte Carlo sampling attains Occam-dominated regret with K = 102 -103
     samples.
   • Asynchronous EDA seeded by a knowledge graph converts exponential
     search into O(G NRmpop σ 2 ) work, feasible with M ≥ 106 cores.
   • Humanoid robot high-level planning in seconds, mid-level in minutes; low-
     level on dedicated loops.
   • Chatbot planning in seconds; EDA variants in minutes.
    Both strategies, with moderate parallelism and hierarchical control, render
the evolutionary/EDA Weakness-CoDD AIXItl analogue viable for real-world
systems. This sort of approach could be pursued on its own as an AGI architec-
ture, or (more promising in our view) it can be coupled with other structures
and methods in an integrative design like Hyperon. Our emphasis here has been
on showing a fairly direct conceptual and formal route from abstract consider-
ations regarding weakness and AIXItl to practical components of real-world AI
systems.


                                      101
9.13     Weakness-AIXI, Quantum Evolution, and Wu Wei
         Geodesics
Next, taking a different view on the behavior of weakness-AIXI systems, we en-
deavor to prove that a Bayesian agent endowed with a weakness prior (“weakness-
AIXI”) updates its belief distribution along geodesics of an entropically-regularized
optimal-transport cost in a quantale of representational effort. We then show
that quantum evolution, in its hydrodynamic (Madelung) form, follows the same
geodesic principle under a Schrodinger-bridge cost. This allows an interesting
interpretation of the universal wave function as a belief distribution over clas-
sical configurations, whose dynamics coincide with the agent’s minimal-effort
inference.

9.13.1     Schrödinger bridges
A Schrödinger bridge interpolates between two probability distributions µ0 and
µ1 by finding the most likely evolution under a reference stochastic process
p(x, t), subject to endpoint constraints. The variational principle underlying
this process minimizes the entropic transport cost:
                    Z t1 Z                                           
                                       1                       ρ(x, t)
       WSB [ρ, v] =          ρ(x, t)     mkv(x, t)k2 +  log              dx dt, (3)
                     t0   Rn           2                       p(x, t)

subject to the continuity equation constraint

                                ∂t ρ + ∇ · (ρv) = 0.

   Here:

   • The first term quantifies the kinetic effort required to deform the distri-
     bution ρ(x, t) through velocity field v(x, t).
   • The second term penalizes divergence from the reference evolution p(x, t),
     which could represent free diffusion or another background process.
   • The parameter  controls the trade-off between kinetic and entropic cost;
     in the limit  → 0, the functional reduces to Wasserstein-2 transport.
    Minimizing WSB yields the Schrödinger bridge between the boundary distri-
butions. In our framework, we interpret this minimal entropic-kinetic action as a
measure of total representational effort. This represents a refined quantification
of ”weakness” in evolving belief states or quantum configurations

9.13.2     Weakness-AIXI and Optimal Transport
Let H be a countable hypothesis class and let
                                            X
                  ∆(H) = {ρ : H → [0, 1] |    ρ(h) = 1}
                                                h∈H



                                        102
be the belief simplex. Fix a commutative quantale (Q, ≤, ⊗, e) and a weakness
prior w : H → Q. At each discrete time t, the agent holds ρt ∈ ∆(H), selects
an action policy πt , receives an observation ot , and updates by

                  ρt+1 (h) ∝ ρt (h) P (ot | h, πt ) exp − 1 w(h) ,
                                                                 

where  > 0 is an entropy-regularization parameter.
   Under the continuum limit t 7→ t∆t, small ∆t, and large-horizon, the discrete
update induces a flow ρ(t) satisfying
                     n                                                o
        ∂t ρ = −∇W2 Eρ [ w(h) ] − Eρ [log P (o | h, π(t))] +  KL(ρkp) ,

where p is the prior predictive. By the Jordan-Kinderlehrer-Otto theory, this
is the gradient flow of a free-energy in the Wasserstein-2 metric with entropic
regularization. Equivalently:
Theorem 13. The continuum-time belief dynamics ρ(t) minimize the Schrodinger-
bridge cost
                       Z TZ                               ρ(h, t) 
          WSB [ρ, v] =       ρ(h, t) 21 kv(h, t)k2 +  log           dh dt
                        0  H                               p(h, t)
subject to ∂t ρ + ∇· (ρv) = 0. Thus ρ(t) is a geodesic in the quantale of weakness
costs.
Sketch. Discretize time and apply the minimizing-movement (JKO) scheme:
                                  n                       o
                    ρk+1 = arg min 2∆t
                                     1
                                       W22 (ρ, ρk ) + F[ρ] ,
                                  ρ

with F[ρ] = Eρ [w] − Eρ [log P (o)] +  KL(ρkp). As ∆t → 0, this converges to
the W2 -gradient flow of F, which is equivalently the Schrodinger-bridge varia-
tional solution. Standard quantale-enriched-category arguments then yield the
geodesic property.

9.13.3   Parallel with Quantum Evolution
                                   p
In quantum mechanics, let ψ(x, t) = ρ(x, t) eiS(x,t)/~ satisfy the Schrodinger
equation. The Madelung transform yields

                        ∂t ρ + ∇ · (ρv) = 0,   v = ∇S/m,

and a quantum Hamilton-Jacobi equation with quantum potential. One shows
that (ρ, v) minimize exactly the same Schrodinger-bridge cost
                           Z TZ                           ρ
              WSB [ρ, v] =        ρ(x, t) 21 mkvk2 + ~ log    dx dt,
                            0  Rn                          p
with p the free-diffusion prior and  = ~. Thus quantum evolution is also a
geodesic in the same quantale of representational effort.


                                       103
9.13.4   Quantum Wave Function as Belief Distribution
Under this unified picture:
   • The magnitude ρ(x, t) = |ψ(x, t)|2 is the belief weight on classical config-
     uration x.

   • The phase S(x, t)/~ encodes the direction of belief update (velocity v).
   • Planck’s constant ~ is the entropic budget controlling the trade-off between
     deviating from free evolution and kinetic belief transport.
    Hence the universal wavefunction ψ can be viewed as a universal belief func-
tion, whose dynamics coincide with the minimal-effort inference of a weakness-
AIXI agent. Wú wéi – effortless action – becomes the common principle govern-
ing both rational belief updates and quantum motion in a single information-
geometric framework.

9.14     A Weakness-Based Goedel-Machine Analogue
Having made a weakness/CoDD analogue of AIXItl and explored its properties
a bit, we now create an analogous analogue of a (computable) Godel Machine
along the rough lines of [Sch07]. That is, we sketch a Goedel-Machine-style self-
modifying agent whose proof system and code are both expressed in CoDD’s
ENF, and whose self-improvement criterion is based on weakness (Occam bias)
and expected utility.
   We will not explore this Goedel Machine construct as thoroughly as we have
the AIXI analogues above, but further theoretical development could certainly
be fruitfully pursued.

9.14.1   Godel Machine Concept
A Goedel Machine continually searches for proofs that rewriting a part of its
own code will increase its expected performance. In our analogue, the machine’s
code is a CoDD program in Elegant Normal Form (ENF), and its proofs are also
CoDD terms via the Curry-Howard correspondence in a simple dependently
typed logic. The machine uses a weakness prior to bias towards more general
(higher-weakness) rewrites, and a utility model to evaluate the expected reward
of each candidate program. When it finds a proof that both
   • the replacement code runs within its resource bounds, and
   • the expected utility gain exceeds the cost of rewriting,
it performs the rewrite. This ensures it only self-modifies in ways that provably
improve its weakness-prior-weighted performance.




                                      104
9.14.2   Formal Specification
 Code and Proofs as CoDD ENF Terms. Let CoDDENF be the set of
all ENF-normalized CoDD programs. We fix a simple typed lambda-calculus L
with:
                    Types: Code, Proof, Bool, R, . . .
and with Curry-Howard:

                Proof(P ) Cong { p ∈ CoDDENF | p has type P }.

Utility and Weakness Predicates. We assume a fixed model M ∈ CoDDENF
of the environment that, given a history h and candidate code c, returns a dis-
tribution over future rewards. Define:

                   EU(c) = Er∼M (c) [ r ],    Cost(c) = σ(c),

and the weakness-prior-weighted utility

                         WU(c) = EU(c) + λ ln w(c),

where w(c) is the CoDD weakness of c and λ > 0 trades off Occam bias.

Self-Improvement Criterion. At each step t, let its current code be ct . The
machine searches for a proof term

                       π : Proof WU(c0 ) − WU(ct ) > δ
                                                      

in the logic L, where δ > 0 exceeds the cost of self-modification. That is, it
proves
                           WU(c0 ) > WU(ct ) + δ.

Rewrite Rule.     Once such a proof π is found, the machine executes the CoDD
program
                             ct+1 = Replace(ct , c0 )
which replaces the designated subgraph of ct with c0 . By proof soundness,
WU(ct+1 ) is guaranteed to exceed WU(ct ).

9.14.3   Theoretical Properties
Theorem 14 (Safe Self-Modification). If L is consistent and all proofs are
found within the machine’s resource bounds, then every self-rewrite strictly in-
creases WU(c). Furthermore, because weakness ln w(c) is monotonic in ENF
size, the machine avoids pathological overfitting rewrites that reduce Occam-bias.
Sketch. Soundness of L ensures π implies WU(c0 ) > WU(ct ) + δ. The ENF-
monotonicity of w(c) prevents arbitrary code bloat from increasing weakness,
so the Occam term λ ln w(c) penalizes overly specific rewrites.


                                       105
Theorem 15 (Eventual Optimality). Assume the set of candidate rewrites and
proof search are fair (every true improvement can eventually be proven). Then
the sequence (ct ) converges–modulo resource bounds–to a (local) maximum of
WU(c).
Sketch. Each rewrite increases WU by at least δ. Since the code size and run-
time are bounded, there are only finitely many programs with WU below the
global maximum. Fair proof search ensures eventual discovery of each beneficial
rewrite.

9.14.4    Discussion
This construction implements a Weakness Goedel Machine:
   • Self-modification is provably safe and weakness-aware.

   • The Curry-Howard embedding into CoDD ENF unifies code, proofs, and
     weakness measurement.
   • Occam bias is baked in via the ln w(c) term, preventing over-specialized
     rewrites.

   • Resource bounds on proof search and program size guarantee computabil-
     ity.
Thus we obtain a computable, weakness-driven analogue of Schmidhuber’s Goedel
Machine, firmly grounded in the algebraic theory of CoDD.


10       Mind-World Correspondence Principles
In [Goe13] it is proposed that one ideal form for a theory of general intelligence
to take would be: You feed it a description of an environment, a set of goals
and a set of resource limitations.. and it tells you a cognitive architecture that
would effectively work toward the given goals in the given environment under
the given resource limitations (when this is feasible at all).
    This ideal theoretical ”Mind-World Correspondence” framework has not yet
been achieved, but we can take some steps in this direction using the theoretical
concepts developed above.

10.1     From Weakest Model to Smallest CoDD Controller
We start by exploring the question: Suppose we have a characterization of the
weakest model M explaining which actions will lead to which results for a certain
system in a certain environment, based on a volume of historical data. What
can we say about the nature of the smallest CoDD program P that will allow
a system to perform optimally in the environment based on model M? Can we
generate P from M in some direct way using the relevant symmetries?



                                       106
    It seems this is actually relatively straightforward given the formalisms we’ve
set up. (Of course, this relative simplicity is the result of operating fairly oblivi-
ously to practical resource restrictions, so it needs to be taken with some grains
of salt in terms of practical applications. In real life, outside very simple sit-
uations one can’t really aim at ”performing optimally.” But the theory is still
interesting, and potentially of some use.)
    Of course this is not really the idealized general theory of general intelligence
fantasized above, because knowing this sort of ”weakest model” is different than
just knowing ”a description of an environment, a set of goals and a set of resource
limitations” – there is a lot of computational work inbetween. But it is still a
somewhat interesting step forward.
    Suppose we have learned a weakest CoDD model

                        M : S × A −→ Dist(O × R × S)

in Elegant Normal Form (ENF), where S is the state-space, A the action-space,
O observations, R rewards, and Dist denotes a distribution object. We now ask:
Q. What is the smallest CoDD program

                                   P : S −→ A

that, when run in the environment, achieves optimal expected return accord-
ing to M ? And can we construct P directly from M , perhaps exploiting any
symmetries of M ?


 Optimal-Control as Program Composition. By dynamic-programming
over a finite horizon H, the optimal policy P is given by
                                                 h               i
                                                              0
               P (s) = arg max E(o,r,s0 )∼M (s,a) r + max
                                                        0
                                                          V (s  )  ,
                           a∈A                            a


where V (s) is the optimal value-function. In CoDD this becomes the composi-
tion of two subprograms:

                          P = ArgMax ◦ Expect ◦ M,

where

   • M is the given model S × A − Dist(O × R × S),
   • Expect computes the Bellman-update Dist(O × R × S) − Dist(R + V (S)),
     and
   • ArgMax picks the action a maximizing the expectation.




                                         107
 Cost and Weakness Accounting. Because M is in ENF with cost σ(M ),
and Expect and ArgMax can each be implemented by a fixed finite CoDD
fragment of cost cRmE and cRmA , the total cost is

                        σ(P ) = σ(M ) + cRmE + cRmA .

No smaller program can realize the same semantics, since any program com-
puting the Bellman expectation or the argmax must contain those combinators.
Thus P is minimal in ENF.


 Exploiting Symmetries. If M satisfies a group-equivariance ∀g ∈ G :
M (g · s, g · a) = g · M (s, a), then Expect ◦ M and hence P are also G-equivariant.
By passing to the quotient S/G, one can reduce the effective state space and
synthesize a smaller ENF for M as M̄ : S/G × A/G − Dist(. . . ). The corre-
sponding controller P̄ on S/G then lifts back to P on S, yielding a program
whose cost is reduced by the size of the group orbits:

                    σ(P̄ ) = σ(M̄ ) + cRmE + cRmA  σ(P ).



Direct Generation Procedure.

  1. Normalize the learned M into ENF, merging shared subgraphs.
  2. Detect symmetries of M (automorphism group G) and construct the quo-
     tient model M̄ .
  3. Compose P̄ = ArgMax ◦ Expect ◦ M̄ .

  4. Lift P̄ to P on the full state space by acting through the group G.
    This yields the smallest ENF program P implementing the optimal policy,
directly synthesized from M and its symmetries.

10.1.1   Computational Complexity of CoDD-ENF Program Synthe-
         sis
We analyze each step of this synthesis pipeline in terms of the size N of the
CoDD graph for M (number of nodes+edges) and the size |G| of its automor-
phism group.

ENF Normalization. Merging identical subgraphs and re-ID-ing nodes can
be done in O(N log N ) time via a bottom-up hash-consing pass. In practice this
is linear-ithmic in the number of combinator applications.




                                        108
Symmetry Detection. Computing the automorphism group of a general
graph is graph-isomorphism-complete. State-of-the-art algorithms (e.g. nauty/Traces)
run in quasipolynomial or heuristically near-linear time on typical structured
graphs, but worst-case cost can be superpolynomial (quasipolynomial in N ).

Quotient Construction. Once generators for G are known, building the
quotient model M̄ by collapsing each orbit takes O(N · log|G|) to identify orbits
and O(N ) to rebuild edges.

Controller Composition. Forming P̄ = ArgMax ◦ Expect ◦ M̄ is a fixed-
size CoDD composition whose cost is O(1) in N (up to the cost of copying the
quotient graph).

 Lifting via Group Action. Lifting P̄ back to P requires applying each
group element in G to P̄ ’s subgraph, at cost O(|G| · |P̄ |).

Overall Cost.
                                                             
            O N log N       + GI(N ) + O N log|G| + O |G| · NP̄ ,

where GI(N ) is the cost of graph-automorphism on N nodes and NP̄ is the
size of the quotient controller. In most practical cases the ENF normalization
and quotient-construction terms dominate, with symmetry detection handled
by efficient heuristics and lifting linear in the group size.

Worst-Case. In √ the worst case, GI(N ) can be quasipolynomial, for example
GI(N ) = exp O( N log N ) . Hence the overall worst-case complexity is

                         p                                       
     Tworst (N ) = exp O( N log N ) + O N log N + N log|G| + |G| N ,

which is dominated by the graph-isomorphism term as N − ∞.

Typical-Case (Heuristic). Empirically, state-of-the-art graph-automorphism
algorithms (e.g. nauty, Traces) run in near-linear or low-degree polynomial
time on structured graphs arising from CoDD programs, and often produce
small automorphism groups |G| = O(1) or O(log N ). In such cases the pipeline
cost reduces to

                                                        
Ttypical (N ) = O N log N + N log|G| + |G| N = O N log N   (|G| = O(log N )).

    Thus, while the theoretical worst-case is quasipolynomial, the practical com-
plexity of synthesizing the minimal CoDD controller from a learned model is
effectively O(N log N ) for most real-world applications.



                                       109
10.2    From Weaker Models to Synthesized Controllers
The discussion in the previous section assumed we have the weakest model M
explaining which actions will lead to which results for a certain system in a
certain environment, based on a volume of historical data; it then used this
model M to synthesize a program P generating the actions explained by the
model. We now explore the situation where we have two different such models
M and M1 , and look at the relations between the corresponding synthesized
programs P and P1 .
    Conceptually, this partially addresses the issue that assuming we have a
weakest explanatory model is a big assumption, involving a lot of computational
work beyond just having a description of an environment and goals.
    Specifically, suppose we have two learned CoDD-ENF models
                    M, M1 : S × A −→ Dist(O × R × S)
that both explain the same historical data, but satisfy
                                  M  M1
in the weakness ordering (i.e. M is strictly more general, and hence weaker,
than M1 ). Let the corresponding minimal controllers be
           P = arg max ◦Expect ◦ M,     P1 = arg max ◦Expect ◦ M1 .

Weakness Prior on Programs (aside). To be sure it’s understood, what
we mean by weakness of programs here is as follows, in line with our above
discussions. We assign each CoDD program Q a prior weight
                                 2−α σ(Q)
                     w(Q) = P        −α σ(Q0 )
                                               ∝ 2−α σ(Q) ,
                                 Q0 2
where σ(Q) is the ENF description length of Q and α > 0 is a fixed constant.
Thus w(Q1 ) ≤ w(Q2 ) ⇐⇒ σ(Q1 ) ≥ σ(Q2 ).

Literal Weakening. If M1 is obtained by a specialization (graph-refinement)
M1 = Spec(M ), then the same rewrite lifts through expectation and argmax:
                                                               
P1 = arg max ◦Expect ◦ Spec(M ) = Spec arg max ◦Expect ◦ M = Spec(P ).
Hence P1 is literally an extension of P : every combinator in P appears in P1 ,
plus additional structure.

Strict Weakness without Literal Weakening. More generally, if M  M1
but M1 is not a direct subgraph-specialization of M , the Bellmanfusion pipeline
still preserves weakness ordering:
                  w(M ) ≥ w(M1 )      =⇒     w(P ) ≥ w(P1 ).
In particular σ(P ) ≤ σ(P1 ), so P is no more specific than P1 , even though
neither program may be a literal extension of the other.


                                      110
Incomparable Models. If neither M  M1 nor M1  M , then the con-
trollers P, P1 likewise need not be comparable under . They share the common
Bellmanfusion core but differ in handling corner cases. One can form the join
model M ∨ M1 , whose minimal controller P ∨ P1 is the least upper-bound of P
and P1 in the weakness lattice.

Takeaways.
   • Specialization lifts to programs. A literal weakening M 7→ M1 yields a
     direct program refinement P 7→ P1 = Spec(P ).
   • Weakness is monotonic. M  M1 implies w(P ) ≥ w(P1 ), i.e. σ(P ) ≤
     σ(P1 ).
   • Controller lattice. Joins and meets of models map to the corresponding
     joins and meets of their minimal controllers.
    Thus weakening at the model level always respects weakening at the controller
level, and when that weakening is a true refinement, the synthesized programs
refine in lock-step.


11     Quantum Weakness
So far we have been elaborating different applications of our ”basic” quantale-
based formulation of weakness. Now we will deviate from this mode slightly and
give a special sort of elaboration or tweak of this formulation. It turns out that
porting the categorical-weakness framework to the quantum domain requires
more than just choosing a quantale based on quantum algebra, it involves re-
rethinking Occam’s Razor a little bit. The issue is that in noncommutative
settings no single scalar captures simplicity (or weakness or whatever) across
all complementary measurements. We need to go a bit more postmodern, and
move from a classical ”one-score” view to a fully multiperspectival – and, it will
be argued, tunably aggregated – quantum weakness.

11.1    Quantum Weakness as Categorical Weakness
Let (C, ⊗, I) be a monoidal category of system+context (e.g. finite-dimensional
Hilbert spaces and CP maps) and let

                             W : C op −→ (P, ≤, )

be a weakness functor landing in a (possibly noncommutative) quantale (P, ).
In the quantum state category, a subobject (effect) E has

                               W (E) = Tr(ρ E),

the Born-rule expectation under prior ρ. This is exactly categorical weakness
specialized to the quantum case.


                                       111
    While this math set-up is clear, the actual evaluation of weakness in this
set-up needs careful attention because of the observer-dependence implicit in
quantum mechanics [BLM96]. To elaborate we need some conceptual setup:

Accessible measurement contexts (POVMs). A positive operator-valued
measure (POVM) on a Hilbert space H is a finite collection of effects
                                               m
                                               X
                      {Ej }m
                           j=1 ,     Ej ≥ 0,         Ej = I.
                                               j=1

Each Ej corresponds to one measurement outcome, and given a quantum state
ρ, the Born-rule assigns
                            Pr(j) = Tr(ρ Ej ).
In our framework, each context α ∈ A is such a POVM, and for simplicity we
focus on a particular effect Th,α ∈ {Ej } that ”tests” hypothesis h.
   Hence, instead of a single scalar W , we obtain a family of weakness scores
                                           
                      Wα (h) = Tr ρ Th,α ,        α ∈ A,

one for each accessible POVM context α. Aggregation then collapses this family
{Wα (h)} into the scalar Occam prior used by the agent.
   Things get a little subtler when you connect this with the subtleties of quan-
tum measurement, which we will pursue in Section 11.5 below. First however
we will pause to explain how this notion of weakness relates to the Schrodinger
bridge weakness explored earlier.

11.2     Relationship between the quantum-operator quan-
         tale and the Schrödinger-bridge quantale
Conceptually, both quantales arise as Occam-style weakness measures on differ-
ent domains of quantum ?effort.? The quantum-operator quantale measures the
size or complexity of static operators on a Hilbert space, while the Schrödinger-
bridge quantale measures the minimal entropic transport cost of evolving quan-
tum (or classical) densities. Despite these different interpretations, they share
the same abstract structure and are linked by a precise Galois connection.


11.2.1    The two quantales
   • Quantum-operator quantale

         VQ = { A : H → H | A ≥ 0},      A ⊕Q B = A + B,       A ⊗Q B = A B.

       Order is the Loewner order A ≤ B ⇐⇒ B − A ≥ 0.




                                       112
   • Schrödinger-bridge quantale

     QSB = { W : (ρ, v) → R≥0 },            (W1 ⊕SB W2 )[ρ, v] = max{W1 [ρ, v], W2 [ρ, v]},

         (W1 ⊗SB W2 )[ρ, v] = W1 [ρ|[t0 ,t1 ] , v] + W2 [ρ|[t1 ,t2 ] , v],   eSB [ρ, v] = 0,
     with pointwise ordering W1 ≤ W2 ⇐⇒ W1 [ρ, v] ≤ W2 [ρ, v].



11.2.2     The embedding map F : VQ → QSB
For each positive operator A ∈ VQ , define
                                             Z t2
                                
                           F (A) [ρ, v] =        hv(t), A v(t)i dt.
                                              t0

   • F is monotone: A ≤ B =⇒ F (A) ≤ F (B).
   • F sends sums to concatenations:

                       F (A ⊕Q B) = F (A + B) = F (A) ⊗SB F (B).

   • F (0) = eSB .


11.2.3     The ?Hessian? map U : QSB → VQ
For any cost functional W ∈ QSB , let

                                             δ 2 W [ρ, v]
                               U (W ) =                       ,
                                                 δv 2     v=0

the operator whose quadratic form matches the second variation of W at zero
velocity. Then
   • U is monotone: W1 ≤ W2 =⇒ U (W1 ) ≤ U (W2 ).
   • F a U is a Galois connection:

                               F (A) ≤ W        ⇐⇒       A ≤ U (W ).



11.2.4     Take-away
     The maps F and U exhibit a quantale-adjunction between the static
     operator picture and the dynamic transport-cost picture. In this
     way, the quantum-operator quantale and the Schrödinger-bridge quan-
     tale are two facets of the same abstract weakness framework, bridged
     by a precise Galois connection.


                                             113
11.3     Implications of the Galois connection for geodesics
Conceptually, the adjunction F a U not only links the two quantales, it also
**identifies their intrinsic Lawvere metrics** and shows that geodesics in one
picture correspond exactly to geodesics in the other.


11.3.1     . Lawvere metrics on the two quantales
Each commutative quantale (Q, ⊕, ⊗) induces a generalized distance (a Lawvere
metric)
                   dQ (x, y) = inf{ q ∈ Q | x ⊗ q ≥ y},
where ?≥? is the quantale order. Concretely:

   • Operator?quantale metric

                       dVQ (A, B) = inf{ C ≥ 0 | A + C ≥ B},

       i.e. the smallest positive operator C needed so that A + C dominates B.
   • Schrdinger?bridge quantale metric

                    dSB (W1 , W2 ) = inf{ W | W1 ⊗SB W ≥ W2 },

       i.e. the minimal additional ?cost?functional? W so that concatenating W
       onto W1 covers W2 .



11.3.2     Distance adjunction
Because F and U satisfy

                          F (A) ≤ W ⇐⇒ A ≤ U (W ),

one deduces for all A ∈ VQ and W ∈ QSB :
                                                                                      
dSB F (A), W = inf{ Ω | F (A)⊗SB Ω ≥ W } = inf{ Ω | F (A⊕Q U (Ω)) ≥ W } = dVQ A, U (W ) .

In particular, putting W = F (B) gives the exact equality of intrinsic distances:
                                        
                        dSB F (A), F (B) = dVQ (A, B).




                                      114
11.3.3    Correspondence of geodesics
   • Operator?space geodesics: A ?straight?line? path A(t) = (1 − t) A0 +
     t A1 , t ∈ [0, 1] attains the infimum in dVQ (A0 , A1 ).
   • Bridge?cost geodesics: The Schrdinger bridge W ∗ (t) solving the mini-
     mal?cost interpolation between F (A0 ) and F (A1 ) attains the infimum in
     dSB (F (A0 ), F (A1 )).

    By the distance adjunction, F carries the linear operator geodesic A(t) onto
the unique cost?geodesic W ∗ (t) = F (A(t)), and U carries W ∗ (t) back to the
linear segment A(t). Thus the two pictures share exactly the same geodesic
structure, viewed through the lens of the Galois connection.

11.4     Mapping reward functionals to optimal quantum cir-
         cuits
One can use this adjunction to interestingly map reward functions to the quan-
tum circuits optimally realizing said reward functions – a form of ”Mind-World
Correspondence Principle.”
   Given a control-theoretic reward functional R[ρ, v], define the corresponding
cost
                               W [ρ, v] = − R[ρ, v],
so that minimizing W is equivalent to maximizing R. By the Galois connection
F a U between the Schrödinger-bridge quantale and the quantum-operator
quantale, compute the positive operator

                                         δ 2 W [ρ, v]
                         A = U (W ) =                     ,
                                             δv 2     v=0

which encodes the quadratic penalty of the cost functional.
   In the operator quantale (VQ , ⊕Q , ⊗Q ), the ?wu wei? geodesic from the
identity I to the target operator e−A is given by the straight-line interpolation

                     A(t) = (1 − t) I + t e−A ,     t ∈ [0, 1].

By functional calculus this yields a one-parameter family of unitaries
                                                 
                              U (t) = exp −i A(t) ,

whose time-slicing or Trotterization produces a quantum circuit that is optimal,
in the Occam-weakness sense, for the original reward R.
    Optionally, pushing A(t) back through F recovers the explicit cost along the
path:                                  Z t2
                             
                     F (A(t)) [ρ, v] =     hv(t), A(t) v(t)i dt,
                                        t0

confirming that this circuit indeed minimizes W [ρ, v] (and thus maximizes R[ρ, v]).


                                       115
   Hence one obtains a principled pipeline
                               U
              R 7→ W = −R −
                          → A 7→ { U (t) = exp(−i A(t))},

linking reward design to optimal quantum-circuit synthesis via the quantale
Galois connection.

11.5     Multiperspectival Quantum Weakness
So, let A be the finite set of accessible measurement contexts (e.g. POVMs or
observables) on an ensemble of quantum systems. For each hypothesis H and
context α ∈ A,                               
                        wα (H) = Tr ρ TH,α      (∈ [0, 1])
is the ”weakness in context” under prior state ρ.

Aggregation Schemes Rather than a single w, we obtain a vector {wα (H)}α∈A .
Occam’s Razor then becomes: choose the hypothesis whose vector is ”largest”
under some aggregation rule, e.g. one could choose:

  1. Worst-case (infimum):

                               Winf (H) = min wα (H).
                                             α∈A


  2. Weighted average:
                                    X                  X
                      Wavg (H) =          pα wα (H),       pα = 1.
                                    α∈A                α


  3. Weighted-multiplicative:
                          Y        ηα                      X
              Wmult (H) =     wα (H) ,             ηα ≥ 0,       ηα > 0.
                              α∈A                            α


  4. Lexicographic limit: Choose exponents ηα1  ηα2  · · ·. Then Wmult
     effectively orders hypotheses by the smallest w(1) first, then w(2) , etc.,
     exactly enforcing a ”weakest-context first” rule.

11.5.1   Unified Occam’s Razor
Whatever the aggregation scheme in any case we can set a Bayesian prior
                                      η0
                       π(H) ∝ W (H) , η0 > 0,

and prefer the hypothesis maximizing W (H). Conceptually then our quantum
Occam’s razor becomes something like:
Among all hypotheses that fit your observations, choose the one that strikes the
best balance of simplicity (weakness) across every relevant perspective– where


                                      116
this balance gives more emphasis to the perspectives you care about most. One
extreme way to assess this ”best balance” is to demand that your chosen hy-
pothesis be the simplest (weakest) even in the perspective comprising the single
hardest or most critical test. Another way is to look at a weighted average across
perspectives, perhaps weighting each one by some meta-level simplicity measure.
    Or even more loosely, as we phrased it in the introduction,
    Given any system for relating and valuing entities, think of an individual
binary relationship as high-prominence to the extent two things it joins are
both valuable. A collection of binary relationships is then weak to the extent
that, considered through the lens of a variety of complementary perspectives, it
tends to contain a high combined weight of high-prominence relationships in the
various perspectives.
(and one then can make choices regarding how to do the compositing across
different complementary perspectives).

11.6    Multiperspectival Weakness: Identical Ensemble Case
Let A be the finite set of all accessible measurement contexts (POVMs) on
an ensemble of identically prepared systems {Si }. For each hypothesis H and
context α ∈ A, define the per-context weakness
                                                     
                       wα (H) = Pr ”accept” under α ,

estimated empirically by applying α to a subset of the ensemble.

Aggregation by Infimum or Average            A simple multiperspectival Occam’s
Razor compares
                                                       X

                       α∈A

                                     Y          ηα
                         Wmult (H) =     wα (H) ,

Lexicographic Limit By choosing the ηα so that ηα1  ηα2  · · · (for a
suitable ordering α1 , α2 , . . .), the product Wmult (H) effectively compares hy-

and so on–recovering the lexicographic ”worst-context first” rule as a special
case.


                                       117
11.6.1    Potential Generalization to Other Noncommutative Algebras
It’s a side note we will not follow up here in details, but for the purpose of
framing future work, it’s worth noting that exactly the same multiperspectival
construction applies in any noncommutative setting–quaternionic, octonionic,
or beyond–by replacing POVM contexts with the appropriate family of auto-
morphisms or measurement tests and aggregating via infimum, average, multi-
plicative weights, or the lexicographic limit.

11.7     Multiperspectival Weakness for Heterogeneous En-
         sembles
In realistic settings systems S1 , . . . , Sn may differ in nuisance parameters but
share a common causal preparation structure. We therefore align contexts via
a meta-model and aggregate weakness both over contexts and over systems,
using either infimum, average, or weighted-multiplicative–and recovering lexi-
cographic ordering as an extreme limit.

Two-Tiered Simplicity Principle.
  1. Use a classical causal meta-model to align heterogeneous systems and

  2. Apply a multiperspectival weakness calculus–infimum, average, Weighted-

This preserves a robust, observer- and system-dependent Occam’s Razor in non-
