                                                120
11.9     Mapping Between the CD-Logic Quantale and the
         Quantum-Operator Quantale
In [Goe21c] it is shown that fuzzy paraconsistent logic tends toward quantum
logic as one takes the limiting value of certain parameters. Here we reconstruct
that argument in the context of CDL and quantum-operator quantales – and
then show how the quantale approach can express the argument much more
nicely than its original form.

11.9.1   Reconstructing the Original Mapping from CDL to Quantum
In what follows we show how the fuzzy paraconsistent (”CD-logic”) quantale

VCD = H×H op ,    (x, x0 )⊕CD (y, y 0 ) = (x∨y, x0 ∧y 0 ),   (x, x0 )⊗CD (y, y 0 ) = (x∧y, x0 ∨y 0 ),

relates to the quantum-operator quantale

     VQ = { A : cH → cH | A ≥ 0},        A ⊕Q B = A + B,         A ⊗Q B = A B,

via an approximate quantale homomorphism .

From p-Bits to Density Operators Given a fuzzy p-bit (w+ , w− ) ∈ [0, 1]2 ∼    =
VCD , choose an additive-generator pair (f, f ∗ ) of a Schweizer-Sklar t-norm so
that f, f ∗ : [0, 1] → R≥0 . Define a pure-state amplitude

                           ψw = f (w+ ), f ∗ (w− ) ∈ C2 ,
                                                  


normalized so that kψw k = 1. Then set
                              
                    Φ w+ , w− = ρw = |ψw ihψw | ∈ VQ .

Approximate Preservation of Join and Tensor One verifies that for any
two p-bits u, v ∈ VCD :
                                           
     Φ u ⊕CD v ≈ Φ(u) ⊕Q Φ(v),     Φ u ⊗CD v ≈ Φ(u) ⊗Q Φ(v),

where the approximation error can be made arbitrarily small by choosing the
t-norm parameter p → −∞.

Negation and Involution CD-logic negation CD (w+ , w− ) = (w− , w+ ) is car-
ried by Φ into the quantum involution

                              Φ CD (w) = ρ∗w ,
                                      


up to a unitary phase, matching the complex-conjugation symmetry in quantum
mechanics




                                         121
Insight and Consequences This construction shows that:
   • Fuzzy p-bits in CD logic embed approximately as qubit density operators.
   • CD-logic disjunction and conjunction map into operator addition and
     multiplication, explaining the ”complex-amplitude” algebra of quantum
     probabilities as an emergent approximation to paraconsistent truth-value
     algebra.
   • The approximation error reflects assumed ”evidential-error uncertainty,”
     tying the mapping to probabilistic-logic-networks semantics.
    Thus the paper’s core argument–that quantum probability arithmetic arises
from fuzzy paraconsistent logic under small evidential-error assumptions–can be
restated succinctly as an approximate quantale homomorphism Φ : VCD → VQ .

11.9.2   A Categorical Refinement of the Mapping via Lax Monoidal
         Functors
We have exhibited an approximate homomorphism Φ : VCD → VQ between
the CD logic quantale (VCD , ⊕CD , ⊗CD ) and the quantum-operator quantale
(VQ , ⊕Q , ⊗Q ). We now show how to restate this neatly in category-theoretic
terms:

Quantales as One-Object Monoidal Categories Recall that any com-
mutative quantale (V, ⊗, ⊕) may be viewed as a one-object monoidal category
CV :

   Ob(CV ) = {ast},   Hom(ast, ast) = V,      composition = ⊗,    tensor = ⊕.

In this picture a quantale homomorphism is exactly a strict monoidal functor
between the corresponding one-object categories.

The Approximation as a Lax Monoidal Functor Our map Φ fails to
preserve ⊕ and ⊗ on the nose, but only up to small error. This is precisely a
lax monoidal functor
                            Φ : CVCD → CVQ ,
equipped with structure morphisms

 φ2 : Φ(u) ⊕Q Φ(v) −→ Φ(u ⊕CD v),        ψ2 : Φ(u) ⊗Q Φ(v) −→ Φ(u ⊗CD v),

and unit maps

                  φ0 : 0Q → Φ(0CD ),         ψ0 : IQ → Φ(ICD ),

satisfying the usual coherence diagrams for a lax monoidal functor (Mac-Lanes
pentagon and triangle axioms).


                                       122
Strictification in the Limit By choosing the t-norm parameter p → −∞,
one shows that the laxators φ2 , ψ2 become arbitrarily close to invertible. In
the limit they yield an (ordinary) monoidal functor, i.e. a genuine quantale
homomorphism. This passage from lax to strict is an instance of strictification
in monoidal category theory.

Enrichment and Profunctors Alternatively, one can regard both CVCD and
CVQ as enriched over a ”metric” quantale [0, ∞], measuring the operator-norm
distance on VQ and an analogous distance on VCD . Then Φ is simply a short
(Lipschitz) enriched functor, whose laxator norms shrink to zero as p → −∞.
This yields a fully enriched explanation of the approximation error.

Conceptual Benefit      Framing Φ as a lax monoidal functor (or enriched func-
tor) makes clear:
   • The ”approximation” arises from non-invertible lax-structure maps.
   • Standard coherence and strictification theorems guarantee that, in an ap-
     propriate limit, the approximation collapses to an exact quantale homo-
     morphism.
   • One may track the error uniformly by measuring the deviation of the lax-
     ators from isomorphism, using either 2-categorical coherence or enriched-
     category metrics.
    Thus the approximation result becomes an instance of a general pattern
in category theory–lax monoidal functors and their strictification–providing an
elegant, conceptual account of why fuzzy paraconsistent logic embeds (approx-
imately) into quantum operator arithmetic.

11.10     Quantum Universality and Asymptotic Optimality
We now establish two central results for the multiperspectival quantum AIXItl
agent–equipped with any of our aggregation-based Occam priors (infimum,
weighted-average, weighted-multiplicative or its lexicographic limit)–namely:
(1) the hypothesis class is universal for quantum computation, and (2) the
agent’s regret vanishes as its resource bounds grow, regardless of which full-
support prior is used.

11.10.1   Universality of the Quantum Hypothesis Class
Recall that for resource bounds t (max runtime) and ` (max description length),
the class

       HCalQ
                   
             t,` = h = (Hh , {Th,α }α∈A , Eh ) | σ(h) ≤ `, runtime(h) ≤ t

contains hypotheses encoding a Hamiltonian Hh , multiperspectival tests {Th,α },
and a reward/observation effect Eh .


                                      123
Theorem 16 (Polynomial-size Quantum Circuit Simulation). Let U be any n-
qubit unitary implementable by a quantum circuit of size s. Then for sufficiently
large (t, `) with
                          ` = O(s), t = poly(n, s),
there exists h ∈ HCalQ
                     t,` whose Hh and Eh simulate U to inverse-polynomial
                     Q
accuracy. Hence HCalt,` is universal for quantum computation.

Sketch. Encode each elementary gate as a constant-cost CoDD subgraph; a
circuit of s gates becomes a CoDD program of cost O(s). ENF merging bounds
the runtime to poly(n, s), and Eh projects onto the circuit’s output with inverse-
polynomial error.

11.10.3   Discussion
   • Robust Occam Bias. Infimum, weighted-average, weighted-multiplicative,
     and lexicographic priors all implement multiperspectival Occam’s Razor,
     from strict worst-case to softer context-weighted simplicity.
                                                              √
   • Convergence Constants. While all priors yield O( T ) regret scal-
     ing, the weighted-multiplicative and lexicographic schemes can shrink con-
     stants by magnifying per-context simplicity gaps.
   • Generality. These results extend to any noncommutative/observer-dependent
     hypothesis category (quaternionic, octonionic, etc.) using the same ensemble-
     based aggregation machinery.

11.11     Comparison of Lexicographic vs. Weighted-Multiplicative
          Priors
In both lexicographic and weighted-multiplicative formulations, the mixture-
regret of AIXItl satisfies the standard bound
                                    q                    
                   Regret(T ) ≤ O       T ln M − ln π(h∗ ) ,

where M = |H| and π(h∗ ) is the prior mass on the true hypothesis. The two
approaches differ only in how large π(h∗ ) becomes:

  1. Weighted-Multiplicative Prior.
                                            L
                                            Y  (j) ηj
                              πmult (h) ∝      w (h) ,
                                            j=1


     with moderate exponents {ηj }. If ∆j = w(j) (h∗ ) − maxh6=h∗ w(j) (h), then
                                               L

                                      125
Implications for Convergence
                                                        √
   • Both approaches achieve the minimax-optimal O( T ) scaling.
                                                    P
   • The constant under the square-root is ln M − j ηj ∆j . Because the lexi-
     cographic prior concentrates nearly all weight
                                              P     on the largest gap ∆1 (and
     subsequently on ∆2 , etc.), it maximizes j ηj ∆j and thus minimizes the
     regret constant.
   • A finite weighted-multiplicative prior trades off between worst-case robust-
     ness (η1 large) and average-case stability (ηj moderate), yielding slightly
     larger regret constant but greater resilience to noise in any single context.
   • In practice, one chooses {ηj } to balance convergence speed (larger expo-
     nents) against sampling-noise robustness (smaller exponents).

11.12     Practical Robustness of Weighted-Multiplicative Pri-
          ors in Approximate AIXItl
In ideal (exhaustive) AIXItl , a lexicographic prior can minimize the regret
constant by concentrating almost all weight on the worst-case context. How-
ever, real-world approximations–e.g. Monte-Carlo-Tree-search or particle-based
AIXI–cannot enumerate every hypothesis or context pair exactly, and suffer
from sampling noise, limited budget, and heuristic proposals. In that setting, a
finite-exponent weighted-multiplicative prior enjoys several practical advantages:

  1. Smoothness vs. brittleness.
        • Lexicographic: A tiny sampling error in the estimated smallest weak-
          ness w(1) can completely reorder the prior, discarding otherwise good
          hypotheses.
        • Weighted-multiplicative: Finite exponents ηj < ∞ ensure each con-
          text contributes
                  P        continuously. A small misestimate shifts ln π(h) by
          at most j ηj δj , avoiding catastrophic model-switching.
  2. Computational tractability.
        • Lexicographic: Requires sorting every candidate’s full L-vector of
          weaknesses to identify the minimal entries–prohibitive for large, dy-
          namic particle sets.
        • Weighted-multiplicative: Only needs to compute a scalar j [w(j) (h)]ηj
                                                                    Q
          per particle, trivially parallelizable and compatible with incremental
          updates.
  3. Adaptivity to heuristics.
        • Lexicographic: Hard to combine with domain-specific proposal dis-
          tributions or value-guided sampling, since any context rank tie can
          overturn all prior structure.


                                       126
        • Weighted-multiplicative: One can smoothly incorporate heuristic qual-
          ity scores or importance’sampling weights into the exponents ηj or
          into the context weights pj , preserving the Occam bias while respect-
          ing sampler design.
  4. Balanced exploration-exploitation.
        • Lexicographic: Over-biases exploration toward contexts deemed worst
          by minimal samples, risking neglect of high-value regions.
        • Weighted-multiplicative: Tunable {ηj } allow a spectrum from worst-
          case emphasis (η1 large) to average-case exploration (ηj moderate),
          supporting more stable search dynamics.
Conclusion. While lexicographic priors attain the smallest regret constant in
theory, a finite-exponent weighted-multiplicative prior offers a more robust, com-
putationally feasible, and sampler-friendly Occam bias for practical, sampling-
based AIXItl implementations.

   • Writing Ŵmult (h) = [ŵα (h)]ηα , one shows
                            Q

                                         X                         X 
         ln Ŵmult (h) − ln Wmult (h) ≤      ηα ln(ŵα /wα ) = O δ  ηα ,
                                            α                         α

     so relative error in Wmult is O(δ) for moderate η.
   Thus the measured prior π̂(h) ∝ [Ŵ∗ (h)]η0 differs only by O(δ) (additive or
multiplicative) from the true π(h).

Regret Bound with Sampling Error. Let σ(h∗ ) be the description length
of the true model and Vmax the maximum reward. Then, for any full-support
Occam prior π,
                       Regret ≤ σ(h∗ ) ln 2 + C δeff Vmax ,
                                                   P
where δeff = δ for infimum/average and δeff = O(δ ηα ) for the multiplicative
case. To make the sampling-error term negligible compared to the Occam term,
choose M so that δeff  σ(h∗ ) ln 2/Vmax .

Lexicographic Robustness. In the lexicographic limit (ηα1  ηα2  · · ·),
one must ensure δ is smaller than the minimal gap between successive w(j) .
Otherwise, orderings can flip. In practice, one uses finite but large {ηα } to
approximate lexicographic behavior while controlling robustness to sampling
noise.

Illustration. For |HCalQ | = 106 , |A| = 10, δ = 0.01, ηα moderate, and
η0 > 0, one still needs M ∼ 105 shots per context to achieve δeff . 0.01. The
Occam term σ(h∗ ) ln 2 (e.g. 50) thus dominates the sampling penalty (≈ 0.2).

Quantum Parallelism. Amplitude-estimation can reduce the required M
from O(1/δ 2 ) to O(1/δ) in the weighted-average or multiplicative cases, further
improving practical feasibility.

11.14     Illustrative Examples and Use Cases
To very roughly gauge the potential feasibility of this sort of approach in any
future practical scenario, we estimate the number of quantum measurement
shots and resulting decision-latencies for two representative applications. These
estimates depend only on the size of the hypothesis class, the number of mea-
surement contexts, and the desired uniform estimation error δ, and are indepen-
dent of whether one uses infimum, weighted-average, weighted-multiplicative, or
lexicographic Occam priors.



                                      128
Uniform-Error Shot Count Let HCalQ be the number of candidate mod-
els and A the number of distinct measurement contexts. To ensure ŵα (h) −
wα (h) ≤ δ simultaneously for all h and α with confidence 1 − η, Hoeffding’s
inequality plus a union bound requires

                      1      2 HCalQ A 
             M ≥          ln                  shots per context.
                     2 δ2          η

Humanoid Robot Control
   • HCalQ = 106 , A = 3 (vision, touch, proprioception).
   • State-space dimension d = 1024, typical effect rank k ≈ 150, so wα ≈
     150/1024 ≈ 0.15.
   • With δ = 0.01, η = 0.01:

                          M & 5 × 104      shots/context,

     yielding unoptimized latency 3 × 5 × 104 × 1 ms ≈ 150 s at 1-ms/shot.
   • The Occam-bias term σ(h∗ ) ln 2 ≈ 50 dominates any O(δ) sampling penalty.

Persistent Chatbot Assistant
   • HCalQ = 106 , A = 8 (syntactic, semantic, pragmatic, etc.).
   • Embedding dimension d = 216 , effect rank k ≈ 103 , so wα ≈ 103 /216 ≈
     0.015.

   • For δ = 0.005, η = 0.01:

                          M & 2 × 104      shots/context,

     yielding latency 8 × 2 × 104 × 0.5 ms ≈ 80 s at 0.5-ms/shot over 8 parallel
     contexts.
   • The Occam-bias term σ(h∗ ) ln 2 ≈ 138 again dominates sampling error.

Key Takeaways
   • The required shot counts and latencies depend only on HCalQ , A , and
     δ, not on the choice of multiperspectival prior.
   • Aggregation schemes differ in how the residual estimation error δ affects
     the sharpness
             P      of the Occam prior (e.g. an O(δ) shift for infimum/average
     vs. O(δ ηα ) in the multiplicative case, or the need to resolve minimal
     gaps for lexicographic ordering).



                                     129
   • Even with moderate target error (δ ∼ 10−2 ), the sampling penalty is
     negligible compared to classical Occam terms in both robot and chatbot
     settings.
   • Quantum-parallel amplitude estimation can further reduce shot require-
     ments from O(1/δ 2 ) to O(1/δ).

11.15     Graphtropic Formulation of the Multiperspectival
          Quantum Razor
We now show how to recast our ”many-perspective” quantum Occam’s Razor
in the language of graphtropy, by viewing each measurement context as a dis-
tinction graph and then aggregating their graphtropies.

 Distinction graphs per context. Let H be the finite set of pure states of
your quantum system. For each hypothesis h and POVM context α ∈ A, define
the distinction graph
                      
Gα (h) = V = H, Eα (h) , (ψ, φ) ∈ Eα (h) ⇐⇒ Pr (”accept” | ψ) = Pr (”accept” | φ),
                                                       h,α                 h,α

i.e. two states are linked if Th,α cannot distinguish them.

Graphtropy per context.        The graphtropy of Gα (h) is the normalized edge-
density
                                      |Eα (h)|
                           Hα (h) =     |H|
                                            ∈ [0, 1].
                                         2
Equivalently, one may define entropy of the partition induced by connected
components, but edge-density suffices to measure ”fraction collapsed.”
                                                                 
Multiperspectival aggregation. We obtain a vector of graphtropies Hα (h) α∈A .
Aggregate into a single scalar by one of:

   • Worst-case (infimum): Hinf (h) = min Hα (h).
                                              α
                                        X                    X
   • Weighted average: Havg (h) =            pα Hα (h),          pα = 1.
                                         α                   α
                                                  Y         ηα               X
   • Weighted-multiplicative: Hmult (h) =              Hα (h) ,      ηα ≥ 0,       ηα >
                                                  α                            α
     0.
   • Lexicographic limit: Choose exponents so ηα1  ηα2  · · ·. Then
     maximizing Hmult orders hypotheses by their minimal Hα , then second-
     minimal, etc., enforcing ”simplest in the hardest context first.”




                                       130
Quantum Razor via Graphtropy. Finally, set a Bayesian prior
                               η0
                   π(h) ∝ H∗ (h) , η0 > 0,

where H∗ is one of the above aggregations. The agent then mixes over hypothe-
ses according to π(h) and proceeds with value updates and action selection as
usual.
In this graphtropic picture, the multiperspectival quantum Razor says:

       ”Construct a graph of collapsed distinctions for each measurement
       context; compute its graphtropy (fraction of indistinguishable pairs);
       then aggregate those graphtropies–by minimum, average, product,
       or lexicographic product–to obtain a single simplicity score. Prefer
       the hypothesis that maximizes this aggregated graphtropy.”

   In particular, the weighted-multiplicative and lexicographic cases arise as
special choices of the exponents {ηα }, yielding a unified graphtropic formulation
of Occam’s Razor in fully observer-dependent, noncommutative settings.


12      Quantale Information Theory
In [BS12], it is shown that algorithmic information can be cast as a particu-
lar case of Shannon information via appropriate choice of assumed information
source. We show here that this argument works also for mutual information
based weakness – and that it can be extended beyond this case and applied to
weakness over an arbitrary quantale? This yields an elegant algebra of informa-
tional quantities in a quite general and flexible setting.

12.1     Baez & Stay’s Argument: Algorithmic Information
         as a Special Case of Shannon Entropy
We begin by recounting Baez and Stay’s demonstration show that algorithmic
entropy (Kolmogorov complexity) can be viewed as a special case of the entropy
of a probability measure – i.e. Shannon information – by choosing an appropriate
Gibbs distribution over the set of programs. The same argument ports neatly
to our set-up here.

 Programs as Microstates Let U be a fixed universal, prefix-free Turing
machine and let
                  X = x ∈ {0, 1}∗ | U (x) halts
                       

be the set of its halting programs. We regard each program x ∈ X as a ”mi-
crostate” of a statistical-mechanical system.




                                        131
Gibbs Ensemble over Programs Fix a real parameter γ > 0. The Gibbs
distribution p on X that maximizes Shannon entropy subject to a constraint on
the expected code-length |x| is
                                    e−γ |x|               X
                        pγ (x) =            ,    Z(γ) =         e−γ |x| .
                                     Z(γ)
                                                          x∈X


  Self-Information and Code-Length                    Under this distribution, the self-
information of a program x is
                           − log pγ (x) = γ |x| + log Z(γ).
In particular, choosing γ = ln 2 yields
                         2−|x|
          pln 2 (x) =           ,        − log2 pln 2 (x) = |x| + log2 Z(ln 2),
                        Z(ln 2)
so up to the additive constant log2 Z(ln 2), the self-information equals the pro-
gram length (Kolmogorov complexity).

Algorithmic Entropy via Relative Entropy For any natural number n,
let δn be the point-mass distribution on outputs equal to n. The relative entropy
of δn with respect to pln 2 is
                     X                              X        
   S(δn |pln 2 ) = −   δn (i) log pln 2 (i) = − log      2−|x| + log Z(ln 2),
                    i                                     x:U (x)=n

which for γ = ln 2 recovers the algorithmic entropy of n up to the constant
log Z(ln 2) .
    Thus Baez and Stay establish that Kolmogorov complexity and related ”al-
gorithmic entropy” arise naturally as special cases of Shannon’s entropy and
relative entropy, when the prior over programs is taken to be the appropri-
ate Gibbs ensemble. This bridges the foundations of statistical mechanics and
algorithmic information theory in a unified framework.

12.2    Porting Baez and Stay’s Argument to Weakness
Now we extend Baez and Stay’s argument from Shannon information to weak-
ness. We work in the common quantale
                                       
                    Q = [0, 1], ≤, ⊗, 1 , x ⊗ y = x y,
and compare two weakness functors on a category of explanations C.

Shannon (Mutual-Information) Weakness For any explanation E, let
I(E) be its mutual information (e.g. between past and future states). Define
                                    Winfo (E) = 2− I(E) .
By subadditivity of mutual information, Winfo is a lax monoidal functor into Q.


                                                132
Bennett’s (MDL) Weakness Fix a finite, prefix-free ”description language”
L (e.g. a dead-code-eliminated MDL grammar or CoDD). For any explanation
E, let
                   CL (E) = min{|p| : p ∈ L∗ , p encodes E}
be its minimal code-length. Then Bennett’s weakness is
                               WL (E) = 2− CL (E) ,
also lax monoidal in Q.

Finite-Vocabulary Mapping Assume the ”true” source over explanations
is the MDL-Gibbs ensemble
                   2− CL (E)
        pL (E) = X           0 (normalization = 1 if the sum is 1),
                    2− CL (E )
                   E0

so that each explanation’s Shannon self-information equals its code-length:
                            − log2 pL (E) = CL (E).
Under this assumption,
       I(E) = CL (E),     =⇒     Winfo (E) = 2−I(E) = 2−CL (E) = WL (E).
Hence Shannon-weakness and Bennett’s discrete set-theoretic weakness coincide
on Q.

Summary This shows that when one fixes a finite, prefix-free description
language L and adopts the corresponding MDL-Gibbs distribution as the source,
the Shannon-based weakness functor Winfo reduces exactly to Bennett’s original
weakness WL . Thus algorithmic information and Shannon information both
basically equate to classic set-theoretic weakness under the finite-vocabulary
(MDL) assumption.

12.3    Generalizing Baez and Stay’s Argument to an Arbi-
        trary Quantale
Baez and Stay’s original derivation of Shannon entropy and mutual information
uses only a commutative product and a commutative sum. We can therefore
port their argument to any commutative quantale (V, ⊗, ⊕) endowed with a
weakness measure w : X → V .

 Quantale-Gibbs Distribution          Define the Gibbs weight of each outcome
x ∈ X by
                                 
                   exp⊗ − w(x)/T               M                   
           P (x) =                 ,      Z=          exp⊗ − w(x)/T ,
                         Z
                                               x∈X

where exp⊗ : V → V is the quantale-exponential and T > 0 is a temperature
parameter.


                                       133
Entropy via a Quantale Logarithm Choose a logarithm map
                                  log⊕ : V → R
that transforms ⊕ into real addition. Then the entropy of x is
                                                
                            H(x) = − log⊕ P (x) .
By the associativity and commutativity of ⊗ and ⊕, H obeys the usual additivity
and chain-rule identities.

Mutual Information in the Quantale Setting           For a joint weight P (x, y) ∈
V , define
                                P (x, y)
            I(x; y) = log⊕                 = H(x) + H(y) − H(x, y),
                             P (x) ⊗ P (y)
where division uses the quantale’s residuation. Standard properties (nonnega-
tivity, symmetry, data-processing inequalities) follow from the quantale axioms.

Categorical Port of Baez-Stay Baez and Stay express Shannon information
as a natural transformation in a symmetric monoidal category of stochastic
maps. Their proof requires only:
   • A commutative monoidal product (⊗) for system combination,
   • A commutative join (⊕) for mixing or marginalization,
   • A logarithm/residuation to relate ⊗ and ⊕.
Since any commutative quantale (V, ⊗, ⊕) provides these structures, their entire
argument carries over unchanged. We thus obtain a full Shannon-style informa-
tion theory over an arbitrary quantale-weakness measure.

12.4    Algebraic Properties of Quantale-Entropy and Mu-
        tual Information
Let (V, ⊗, ⊕) be a commutative quantale, P : X → V a Gibbs-quantale distri-
bution with normalizer Z, and log⊕ : V → R a homomorphism of ⊕ into real
addition. Define
                                                            P (X, Y )
         H(X) = − log⊕ P (X),          I(X; Y ) = log⊕                  .
                                                         P (X) ⊗ P (Y )
Then the following properties hold:

Nonnegativity
                         H(X) ≥ 0,       I(X; Y ) ≥ 0,
since 0 ≤ P (x) ≤ 1 in the quantale implies − log⊕ P (x) ≥ 0, and by residuation
P (X, Y ) ≤ P (X) ⊗ P (Y ) implies I(X; Y ) ≥ 0.


                                       134
Additivity for Independent Systems            If P (X, Y ) = P (X) ⊗ P (Y ), then
                                                 
                 H(X, Y ) = − log⊕ P (X) ⊗ P (Y ) = H(X) + H(Y ),

and hence
                                     I(X; Y ) = 0.

Chain Rule
                                                      M                             
H(X, Y ) = H(X) + H(Y | X),              H(Y | X) =         P (x) ⊗ − log⊕ P (Y | x) .
                                                      x∈X


Mutual Information Identities

           I(X; Y ) = H(X) + H(Y ) − H(X, Y ),         I(X; Y ) = I(Y ; X).

Data-Processing Inequality For any Markov chain X → Y → Z,

                                 I(X; Z) ≤ I(X; Y ),

since marginalization and the quantale join ⊕ are monotone.

Relative Entropy Define
                  M                                  
           D P kQ =     P (x) ⊗ log⊕ P (x) − log⊕ Q(x) .
                              x∈X

Then
                      D(P kQ) ≥ 0,     D(P kQ) = 0 ⇐⇒ P = Q.
    These laws follow directly from the associativity, commutativity, monotonic-
ity, and residuation properties of the quantale (V, ⊗, ⊕) together with the ho-
momorphism log⊕ .

12.5       Recovering Quantum Information Theory from the
           Quantum-Operator Quantale
It is straightforward and unsurprising to see that this sort of quantale informa-
tion, applied to a quantale of quantum operators, yields the standard quantum
information theory.
    Let cH be a finite-dimensional Hilbert space and let V be the quantale of
positive semidefinite operators on cH, ordered by the Löwner order (A ≤ B iff
B − A is positive), with

                          A ⊕ B = A + B,        A ⊗ B = AB
       L         N
(and       and       extended to joins and products over families).


                                          135
Quantum-Gibbs State Given a Hamiltonian H as a self-adjoint operator,
define the Gibbs state at temperature T > 0,
                                  
                       exp⊗ − H/T                     
                  ρ=                , Z = Tr exp(−H/T ) ,
                             Z
where exp⊗ (−H/T ) is the usual operator exponential.

Quantale-Logarithm via Functional Calculus           Use the operator-logarithm

                               log⊕ (A) = log(A)

defined on positive operators. Then the von Neumann entropy arises as
                                                      
                   S(ρ) = − Tr ρ log⊕ (ρ) = − Tr ρ log(ρ) .

Quantum Relative Entropy For two states ρ, σ ∈ V , define
                 h                     i                    
      D(ρkσ) = Tr ρ log⊕ (ρ) − log⊕ (σ) = Tr ρ (log ρ − log σ) .

This satisfies D(ρkσ) ≥ 0 with equality iff ρ = σ.

Quantum Mutual Information For a bipartite state ρAB on cHA ⊗ cHB ,
define
                          ρAB
        I(A : B) = log⊕         = S(ρA ) + S(ρB ) − S(ρAB ),
                        ρA ⊗ ρB
where ρA = TrB [ρAB ] and similarly for ρB .

Algebraic Properties All standard identities–nonnegativity, additivity on
tensor-product states, chain rules, data-processing inequality–follow from the
quantale axioms for (V, ⊗, ⊕) together with properties of the operator trace Tr
and the functional calculus log.
    Thus by instantiating the general quantale-based entropy and divergence
constructions on the quantale of positive operators, one exactly recovers the
familiar von Neumann entropy, quantum relative entropy, and quantum mutual
information of standard quantum information theory.

12.6.1   A Theorem Comparing Margin-Mutual-Information and Stan-
         dard Mutual Information in Feature Selection
One interesting potential application of this sort of SVM-margin information is
to feature selection. Often one does feature selection via mutual information,
before feeding data to an SVM – but it seems probably a better approach to use
the mutual information from the SVM-margin quantale here, than the standard
version of MI.


                                            137
Setup Let X be the input space partitioned into two disjoint subsets of fea-
tures, XS (relevant) and XU (irrelevant). Let Y be the binary class label.
Suppose a classifier is a linear SVM that computes the margin
                                           wT ΦS (XS )
                                  µS =                 ,
                                              kwk
where ΦS (XS ) is the feature map on XS only. We assume XU does not influence
the true decision boundary but may be spuriously correlated with Y .

Definitions
   • Standard MI criterion: select subset S of size k that maximizes I(XS ; Y ).
   • Margin-MI criterion: select S that maximizes I(µS ; Y ), where µS =
     wT ΦS (XS )/kwk.

Theorem       Under the assumptions
  1. Y → XS → µS is a Markov chain,
  2. XU ⊥ µS | XS ,
the following hold for any superset S 0 = S ∪ U :
                I(µS ; Y ) = I(µS 0 ; Y ),     I(XS ; Y ) < I(XS 0 ; Y ).
Hence the margin-MI criterion ranks the true relevant set S above any superset
including irrelevant features, whereas the standard MI criterion may be misled
by spurious correlations in XU .

Proof First, since µS is a deterministic function of XS , by the data-processing
inequality
                            I(µS ; Y ) ≤ I(XS ; Y ).
Next, for the superset S 0 = S ∪ U , the margin remains
                                       wT ΦS (XS )
                             µS 0 =                = µS ,
                                          kwk
because XU does not enter the SVM decision function. Therefore
                                 I(µS 0 ; Y ) = I(µS ; Y ).
On the other hand, by the chain-rule for mutual information,
    I(XS 0 ; Y ) = I(XS , XU ; Y ) = I(XS ; Y ) + I(XU ; Y | XS ) > I(XS ; Y ),
whenever XU carries any residual information about Y given XS .
  Combining these inequalities yields
               I(µS ; Y ) = I(µS 0 ; Y )   and I(XS ; Y ) < I(XS 0 ; Y ),
which proves that margin-MI correctly prefers S over S 0 , while standard MI
does not.


                                             138
12.6.2   Using SVM Channel Capacity as a Deep-Kernel Learning
         Objective
It may also be interesting to use quantities derived from SVM-margin informa-
tion theory as part of the objective function for learning an SVM kernel, e.g.
via deep kernel learning.
    Let {(xn , yn )}N
                    n=1 be a labeled training set, with yn ∈ {±1}. We have a deep
kernel Φθ (x) with parameters θ, and an SVM solve producing decision weights
wθ and bias bθ .

Define the Margin and Gibbs Prior For each input x, define the signed

                                    wθ · Φθ (x) + bθ
                         µθ (x) = y                   .
                                         kwθ k

                                                    

                    Pθ (x) = PN                         .

                                               1
                 Pθ (y | x) =                                    ,

where α  1 controls the steepness.

                              Pθ (x, y) = Pθ (x) Pθ (y | x),

                                     N

                              N

                                           139
   • ϕ(i, j) m((θK )ij ) is the quantale-weakness regularizer on kernel parame-

   • C > 0 trades off hinge loss and capacity,
   • λ > 0 trades off weakness regularization and capacity.

                               θ ← θ − η ∇θ L(θ),

backpropagating through the SVM solve (e.g. via implicit differentiation or a

    This objective encourages the deep kernel to produce representations that
both separate classes (hinge loss), respect simplicity (weakness regularizer), and
maximize the information capacity of the induced SVM channel.

12.6.3   A Margin-Entropy Generalization Bound
SVM-margin information theory seems also to have applications to analyzing
the generalization behavior of SVMs.
   Let X be a finite input set and f : X → {±1} an SVM classifier with (signed)
margin µ(x). For temperature T > 0 define the Gibbs margin distribution
                                                     
                                      exp −|µ(x)|/T
                       P (x) = P                   0
                                                         .
                                    x0 ∈X exp −|µ(x )|/T

Let E = {x ∈ X : f (x) 6= y(x)} be the error region under the true labels
y(x) ∈ {±1}, and write
                    X                    X
             perr =    P (x),  H(P ) = −     P (x) log P (x).
                     x∈E                        x∈X

Theorem 18 (Margin-Entropy Bound). Under the above definitions,

                                          H(P )
                                 perr ≤         .
                                           ln 2
Proof. Denote p = perr and q = 1 − p. The binary entropy of the event E
satisfies
                    h(p) = − p ln p − q ln q ≤ H(P ),
because partitioning X into E and its complement can only decrease entropy.
But for 0 ≤ p ≤ 12 the binary entropy obeys

                 h(p) = − p ln p − (1 − p) ln(1 − p) ≥ p ln 2,

since −(1 − p) ln(1 − p) ≥ 0 and − ln p ≥ ln 2 when p ≤ 21 . Combining gives

                            H(P ) ≥ h(p) ≥ p ln 2,


                                       140
hence
                                                H(P )
                                   perr = p ≤         .
                                                 ln 2


In words, if the Gibbs margin distribution has low entropy, then the proba-
bility mass of misclassified inputs under that distribution is small. Since low
margin-entropy implies that most probability concentrates on high-margin (well-
classified) points, this bound captures how margin-separation drives generaliza-
tion.

12.7     Fuzzy Information Theory
Let (V, ⊗, ⊕) = ([0, 1], T, max) where T is a continuous t-norm. Given a fuzzy
subset H : X → [0, 1], one may normalize memberships to define a fuzzy prob-
ability distribution
                                          H(x)
                              PH (x) = P           ,
                                         u∈X H(u)

or work directly on the unnormalized H(x) using ⊕ = max. Choose a logarithm
map
                      log⊕ : [0, 1] → R, log⊕ (a) = ln(a).

                                           142
12.7.2   Connections into the Fuzzy Literature
The constructions of fuzzy entropy and mutual information we have given can
be viewed as a unifying algebraic reformulation of several classical approaches
in the fuzzy-information literature:

   • De Luca & Termini [LT72] introduced a measure of entropy for a single
     fuzzy set                     X
                     EDT (H) = −      H(x) ln H(x),
                                             x∈X

     and showed it satisfies analogues of nonnegativity and maximality at the
     uniform fuzzy set.
   • Yager (1980s) proposed alternative fuzzy-entropy functionals using dif-
     ferent aggregation operators (e.g. max-min, ordered weighted averaging),
     reflecting various attitudes toward uncertainty in a fuzzy set [Yag79].
   • Kaufmann & Tsaur developed fuzzy cross-entropy and divergence mea-
     sures, extending Kullback-Leibler divergence to fuzzy membership func-
     tions [KT98].
   • Pal & Mitra (1990s) defined fuzzy mutual information for a fuzzy joint
     relation HXY : X × Y → [0, 1] by

                  If (X; Y ) = EDT (HX ) + EDT (HY ) − EDT (HXY ),

     and proved properties such as nonnegativity and a fuzzy data-processing
     inequality [MP94].

Quantale-Enriched Reformulation In our approach we take the unit in-
terval [0, 1] with join ⊕ = max (or bounded sum) and a chosen t-norm ⊗ = T as
a commutative quantale (V, ⊗, ⊕). Fuzzy sets H : X → [0, 1] become V -valued
subobjects, and the canonical quantale entropy
                                     M                
                         Hq (H) = −      H(x) ⊗ ln H(x)
                                       x∈X

and mutual information
                                M
                                      HXY (x, y) ⊗ ln HXH(x)⊗H
                                                          XY (x,y)
                                                                        
                 Iq (X; Y ) =                                   Y (y)
                                x,y

recover the De Luca-Termini, Yager, Kaufmann-Tsaur, and Pal-Mitra formu-
lae as special cases when one chooses ⊕ and ⊗ appropriately. This quantale
perspective makes explicit the algebraic laws (associativity, commutativity, dis-
tributivity) underlying all these fuzzy-information measures and places them
within a single, compositional framework.

12.8.1   Episodic Memory Retrieval via Hypervector Mutual Infor-
         mation
One promising application of hypervectors in an AGI context is to episodic
memory. Let’s flesh out how information on the hypervector quantale might
help with this.
   Let r ≥ 2, n ∈ N , and work in the modular hypervector space V = Zrn .
Suppose we have a set of N episodic memory vectors
                              {v (1) , v (2) , . . . , v (N ) } ⊂ V,
and a memory cue represented by a hypervector
                                           c ∈ Zrn .

Define the Component-wise Joint Distribution Index vector compo-
nents by i = 1, . . . , n. Define a joint distribution over pairs of symbols in
Zr × Zr by
                              1
              Pc,v (a, b) =     { i : ci = a and vi = b} ,                a, b ∈ Zr .
                              n
Its marginals are
                           X                                   X
                Pc (a) =          Pc,v (a, b),     Pv (b) =            Pc,v (a, b).
                              b                                  a


Hypervector Mutual Information The mutual information between the
cue c and a candidate memory v = v (k) is
                             X                  Pc,v (a, b)
                   I(c; v) =    Pc,v (a, b) ln               .
                                               Pc (a) Pv (b)
                                  a,b∈Zr

Higher I(c; v) indicates that v shares more co-occurrence structure with the cue.


                                              145
3. Retrieval by Maximal Mutual Information                       To retrieve the episode
best matching the cue, compute
                          k ∗ = arg max I c; v (k) .
                                                  
                                         1≤k≤N
           ∗
Return v (k ) (or the associated memory) as the retrieved episode.

Pattern Clusters and Emergence More generally, given a subset of episodes
S ⊂ {1, . . . , N }, form the aggregate vector
                                           M
                                    vS =       v (k)
                                             k∈S

(using the modular grouping operator). Define I(c; vS ) as above. One can then:
   • Pattern intensity:                                
                                       IS (c) = I c; vS .
   • Emergent synergy: for two clusters S, T , compute
                                                I(c; vS∪T )
                                σS,T (c) =                       .
                                             I(c; vS ) I(c; vT )
     A value σS,T (c) > 1 indicates that the joint cluster S ∪ T has more shared
     structure with the cue than expected from S and T separately.

Discussion
   • This method treats each hypervector component as a random variable
     taking values in Zr , and uses standard Shannon mutual information to
     measure semantic overlap.
   • The retrieval rule arg max I(c; v) generalizes simple cosine or Hamming-
     distance matching to a full information-theoretic criterion.
   • Cluster-based retrieval and synergy detection identify emergent multi-
     episode patterns that align most strongly with a given cue.

                                      P : X × Y → Qlogic ,

13       Efficient Implementation of WAIXI and Re-
         lated Methods via Galois Connections
In most realistic problems, certainly most cases related to AGI one uses approx-
imate stochastic DP –e.g. Monte-Carlo, TD, or prioritized sweeping–instead of
exact Bellman backups. As argued in [Goe21d], one can also view a great va-
riety of AGI-related algorithms as specialized forms of approximate stochastic
dynamic programing. Toward that end, and leveraging ideas from [DH16, ?]
behind the scenes, we now show how the same Galois-fusion idea carries over:
by writing the DP update as a single MeTTa-IL rule that samples transitions in
MORK, weights them by Occam-weakness, and folds them into an approximate
value or Q-function. We also indicate how a similar strategy may be possible
for quantum implementations.

13.1      Approximate Stochastic DP via MeTTa-IL, MORK
          and Galois Fusion
13.1.1     Stochastic DP Heuristic
Let Q : S × A − R be an action-value function. A generic one-step TD update
is                                                                    
                                                     0 0
          Qt+1 (s, a) = Qt (s, a) + η r + γ max
                                             0
                                                Qt (s , a ) − Qt (s, a)  ,
                                                     a
                 0
where (s, a, r, s ) is sampled from M , η a learning rate, and γ a discount.

13.1.2     Galois Connection
We again view V = {Q : S × A − R} ordered pointwise, and P = CoDDENF
ordered by weakness w(h). Define

              α(Q) = PolicyFromQ (arg max Q(s, a)),              γ(h) = Qh ,
                                              a

where Qh is the Q-function induced by running policy h. One shows α a γ as
before, so that stochastic updates in V lift to a fuseable operator in P .




                                              148
13.1.3      Parallel Efficiency
      • Sampling, lookup and TD-error computation for each (s, a) is fully parallel
        as Rholang actors.
      • No global Q-table is materialized: updates stream into the ENF-CoDD
        structure.
      • The reducer need only perform in-place graph rewrites of the affected
        subgraphs.

13.1.4      Weakness Through the Adjunction
By construction the fused rule implements Pt+1 = (α ◦ T ◦ γ)(Pt ) in one pass,
where T is the expected TD-operator. Since α preserves suprema, the Occam-
weakness bias encoded in α carries through every update, ensuring the evolving
controller remains as general as possible subject to the stochastic learning ob-
jective.

This demonstrates that even approximate, sampling-based DP methods admit
a one-pass, Galois-fused implementation in MeTTa-IL + MORK + Rholang,
with Occam-weakness seamlessly interwoven.

13.2       Galois-Fusion for Reinforcement Learning in MeTTa-
           IL and MORK
We show how common RL algorithms–viewed as approximate stochastic DP–fit
into the same Galois-adjoint α a γ pattern and can be implemented as a single
fused MeTTa-IL rewrite over a MORK hypergraph.

13.3       Category-Enriched Setup
Let
                      V = {Q : S × A − R},       P = CoDDENF ,
with orders Q1 ≤V Q2 ⇔ ∀(s, a), Q1 (s, a) ≤ Q2 (s, a) and h1 ≤P h2 ⇔
w(h1 ) ≥ w(h2 ).
   Define the Galois connection
                                           
             α(Q) = PolicyFromQ -greedy(Q) ,    γ(h) = Qh ,
so that α(V ) ≤P h ⇐⇒ V ≤V γ(h).

13.4       Q-Learning as Fused MeTTa-IL Rule
BellmanTD Operator
                                                                    
                                                     0 0
             TQ (Q)(s, a) = Q(s, a) + η r + γ max
                                               0
                                                  Q(s , a ) − Q(s, a)  ,
                                                  a
                                       0
for each sampled transition (s, a, r, s ) ∼ M .


                                           149
13.5     SARSA and Eligibility Traces
SARSA Operator
                           δ = r + γ Q(s0 , a0 ) − Q(s, a),
with (s, a, r, s0 , a0 ) sampled. The fused rule is identical, replacing maxa0 Q by the
sampled Q(s0 , a0 ), and updates propagate along eligibility traces in the reducer.

13.6     Actor-Critic / Policy-Gradient
   • Critic update: same as Q-Learning or SARSA to estimate V (s).
   • Actor adjunction: α(V ) = SoftmaxPolicy(V ), γ(π) = Vπ .
   • Policy-gradient step:

                       ∆θ = η ∇θ ln πθ (a | s) r + γV (s0 ) − V (s) .
                                                                  


13.7     General Recipe
   1. Identify the RL update operator T (TD, SARSA, PG, etc.) on V -space.
   2. Choose α : V − P (policy extractor) and γ : P − V (value evaluator) so
      α a γ.

   3. Write one MeTTa-IL rule that:
         γ reads the current program P to get Q or V .
        T samples transitions from MORK, computes errors or gradients.
        α emits and then reduces all updates in-place to yield P 0 .

Benefits

   • No explicit Q or π tables–updates stream through the hypergraph.
   • Massive parallelism: each sampled transition is a separate concurrent ac-
     tor.
   • Low memory overhead: only CoDD programs and MORK edges are stored.

   • Uniform implementation pattern for all major RL algorithms.

13.8     Quantum-Fused Bellman via QRAM and Quantum
         Circuits
We next outline how the same Galois-fusion idea–fusing ”unfold” (γ), Bellman
update (B), and ”fold” (α)–can potentially be implemented on a quantum com-
puter to solve large MDPs with quadratic speedups.



                                         150
13.8.1   QRAM Encoding of the MDP
   • Store the transition model M : S × A − Dist(R × S) in quantum RAM
     (QRAM) as amplitude-encoded arrays.
                                                                     P p
   • Each basis state |s, ai addresses a superposition of transitions r,s0 M (s, a; r, s0 ) |r, s0 i.

   • The current value-function V : S − R is likewise stored as amplitudes in a
     QRAM block.

13.8.2   Superposed Bellman Fold
We prepare the uniform superposition over all (s, a):
                                    1     XX
                          |ψ0 i = p               |s, ai .
                                   |S||A| s∈S a∈A

Then one coherent quantum circuit performs:

  1. Expectation: Apply a controlled-unitary that, for each |s, ai, loads the
     transition amplitudes and the value amplitudes of successor states |s0 i,
     then computes
                                X
                                  M (s, a; r, s0 ) r + γ V (s0 )
                                                                
                      E(s, a) =
                                   r,s0

     into a target register (using standard reversible arithmetic).
                                                                 p
  2. Weakness Prior : Multiply the amplitude of |s, a, Ei by π(P ), where
     π(P ) ∝ w(P )α is computed once and stored in a small ancilla.

  3. Maximization: Perform a Grover-style ”quantum argmax” subroutine to
     mark and amplify the |s, ai with maximal weighted E(s, a).

   Crucially, all three steps–lookup in QRAM (γ), compute expectation (B),
and perform Grover maximization (α)–occur in one coherent circuit of depth
O(poly log |S||A|).

13.8.3   Galois Adjunction in the Quantum Domain
We again view V = {V : S − R} ordered pointwise and P = CoDDENF ordered
by weakness. The quantum circuit above implements

                             |P 0 i = (α⊗B ⊗γ) |P i

in superposition, and by linearity and amplitude-preservation it respects the
Galois connection α a γ. The resulting |P 0 i is the amplitude-encoded optimal
controller.



                                          151
13.8.4    Complexity and Speedup
   • QRAM queries: each superposition lookup is O(1) quantum-time.
   • Expectation update: reversible arithmetic on r+V (s0 ) takes O(poly log |S|).
                                                          p
   • ArgMax via Grover: finds the maximal (s, a) in O( |S||A|) steps.
                                           p
   • Overall: one fused circuit of depth O( |S||A| poly log |S||A|), yielding a
     quadratic speedup over the classical O(|S||A|) pass.

13.8.5    Summary
This quantum analogue implements the Bellman fusion α ◦ B ◦ γ in a single
coherent QRAM-based circuit, carrying the Occam-weakness prior as amplitude
weights and using Grover amplification for the α-fold. By exploiting quantum
parallelism and Grover’s speedup, it achieves the same one-pass, low-memory
DP fusion with a quadratic runtime improvement.


Part III
Decision , Motivation &
Consciousness
14       Weakness-Based Measurement of Causality
         and Clarity
Causality is a folk concept with a great number of different interpretations. In
the effort to construct rigorous theories that capture significant aspects of the
intuitive notion of causality in a manner useful for AI systems, the concept of
weakness can be significantly valuable.
    Among other applications, we will use the theory of causality here to develop
a theory of ”clarity of representation” in neural models and other AI networks.
Roughly speaking we will say a network has a clear internal representation if
there tend to be simple causal stories regarding why it gave the output it did
in various situations.

14.1     From Conceptual to Formal Notions of Causality
But what shall we take causality to mean here? – Intuitively, we often say
that ”A causes B” if the occurrence of event A makes event B much more
likely than it would be without A. In everyday language, we look for a set of
mechanisms M – for instance, physical or biological processes – such that when
A happens, those mechanisms explain why B follows. Formally, we can express


                                      152
this by comparing the probability of B given A versus the probability of B given
not-A:
                          P (B | A)  P (B | ¬A).
Here P (·) is a ”simple” probability model, meaning that we measure simplicity
by assuming the mechanisms in M have low descriptive complexity. In other
words, we prefer explanations where the rules or equations in M are not overly
complicated. Typically we also require that A precedes B in time, so that
causality flows forward. When A and B each stand for a category of events,
we say ”A causes B” if almost every time we see an event a in category A and
a nearby event b in category B, the same causal relationship applies. Thus,
a causal explanation of why A leads to B is simply a proof-using the low-
complexity mechanisms in M -that P (B | A) is much larger than P (B | ¬A).
    One can elaborate these basic ideas into a formal framework combining the
algebra of weakness (measuring descriptive simplicity) with an algebra of prob-
abilistic counterfactual strength. We begin by defining key concepts.
  1. Events and Mechanisms. Let A and B be events or event-categories
     in some sample space Ω. We write
                              M = {m1 , m2 , . . . , mk }
     for a finite set of hypothesized mechanisms. Each mi is a rule or function
     that, when combined with initial data, yields predictions about future
     outcomes.
  2. Weakness Algebra (W, ≤). We assume there is an ordered set W of
     weakness values (also called ’simplicity scores–), with a partial order -
     ≤– such that lower elements represent weaker (i.e. simpler) descriptions.
     Concretely, if w ∈ W measures the combined complexity of mechanisms
     M , then a smaller w(M ) implies a simpler explanatory model.
  3. Probabilistic Algebra (P, ). Let P be the set of real-valued condi-
     tional probabilities on events, ordered by -– meaning ’significantly larger
     than.– For two probabilities p1 , p2 , we write
                           p1  p2    ⇐⇒      p1 > p 2 + 
     for some fixed significance margin  > 0.
  4. Causal Explanation as a Theorem. We say that
                                          M
                                      A −→ B
     is a valid causal explanation under mechanisms M if and only if
               P (B | A; M )  P (B | ¬A; M )     and A precedes B.
     Here P (B | A; M ) denotes the probability of B given A when we use
     exactly the mechanisms in M as our model. Intuitively, A causes B via
     M exactly when knowing A and M makes B much more likely than not
     having A (still using the same mechanisms).


                                      153
  5. Simple Probability Function. Define a simple probability function
     P as one whose description-encoded in bits-has weak complexity relative
     to W. Formally, if w(P ) denotes the weakness value of this probability
     model, then we require that for all comparisons we make, w(P ) ≤ β for
     some threshold β. Thus P is ’simple– if its specification does not exceed
     a fixed complexity budget.
  6. Combined Algebra via Fibring. We now define a fibring (or -product-
     type–) algebra (C, Prec) that combines weakness and probabilistic strength.
     An element of C is a pair

                           (w, δ)   with    w ∈ W, δ ∈ R,

     where δ = P (B | A; M ) − P (B | ¬A; M ) measures the counterfactual
     strength of A on B, and w = w(M ) + w(P ) measures the combined
     simplicity cost of specifying mechanisms M and the probability function
     P . We define the order -Prec– on C as:
                                                                     
        (w1 , δ1 ) Prec (w2 , δ2 ) ⇐⇒    δ1 > δ2 ∨ δ1 = δ2 and w1 ≤ w2 .

     In words, a pair with larger counterfactual strength δ is considered -
     greater– (more convincing as a causal explanation); if two pairs have iden-
     tical δ, we prefer the one with lower complexity w.
  7. Interpretation. To assert -A causes B– we pick the pair

                   (w(M ) + w(P ), P (B | A; M ) − P (B | ¬A; M ))

     that is maximal under -Prec–. In effect, we look for the smallest (weakest)
     mechanisms M and simplest probability P that yield the largest increase
     in B’s probability when A occurs. This formalizes the intuition that a -
     good– causal explanation is both simple (low complexity) and strong (high
     counterfactual effect).

   To measure ”how much A causes B”, we perform two competing evaluations:
 (a) We measure how much seeing A boosts the probability of B (that is δ =
     P (B | A) − P (B | ¬A)).
 (b) We measure how simple our explanation is (that is w, the combined com-
     plexity of our mechanisms and probability model).
We then compare different explanations (M, P ) by first looking at which one
gives the largest probability boost δ. If two explanations give the same boost,
we prefer the one that is simpler. In other words, a cause A of B is best
characterized by the fewest, simplest mechanisms that produce the greatest
increase in B’s likelihood when A occurs. This aligns with our intuition: a good
causal explanation should explain a lot (large δ) without invoking an overly
complex mechanism (small w).


                                      154
14.2     Weakness-Weighted Counterfactual Causality (WWCC)
         vs. Bennett-style Organism Causality
Bennett, in his thesis on weakness, articulates a theory of causality grounded in
organisms and their quest for survival. We can articulate the theory on causality
presented above – which we my call Weakness-Weighted Counterfactual Causal-
ity (WWCC) – as a generalization of Bennett’s notion.
    To summarize the WWCC framework in a simple way, we identify causal
relations by combining a measure of counterfactual effect with a quantale-based
weakness penalty, via: “A causes B” if there exists a mechanism M such that

weakness(M ) is low    and   ∆A→B (M ) = P (B | M ∧ A) − P (B | M ∧ ¬A) > ε,

for some effect threshold ε.
    In Bennett’s view, on the other hand, causality arises as “causal identities” –
subsets of intervention statements that survive observation – which an organism
constructs by w-maxing under two preconditions:

   • Incentive: the cause must afford some survival advantage.
   • Representation: the organism’s abstraction layer must be rich enough to
     express that identity.

Objects, properties, and even consciousness emerge from the hierarchy of these
learned causal identities.
    To recover Bennett’s view of causality within WWCC, we can articulate
an organism layer Lorg . Mechanisms now lie in the state space WLorg , and
weakness is computed via the associated quantale QLorg :

                                weakness Lorg (M ).

   One then defines a survival-weighted effect measure

                ∆val (A → B) = U (B) P (B | A) − P (B | ¬A) ,
                                                           


where U (B) is the organism’s utility for outcome B.
   One can then impose two preconditions:
   • M must be definable in WLorg .

   • ∆val (A → B) > δ for some threshold δ.
Bennett’s causal identities are those mechanisms solving

                  min{weakness Lorg (M ) | ∆val (A → B) > δ}.
                   M




                                       155
14.3     Reformulating Causal Learning in PLN Logic within
         Hyperon
So how can we connect all this to practical causal learning AI systems?
    Causal learning in Hyperon’s PLN framework means teaching a system to
discover and represent –if-then– relationships in the world. For example, a
mobile robot might learn that ”pressing a lever causes a door to open,” while a
bioinformatics application might learn that ”gene A influences the expression of
gene B.” In both cases, we want the machine to find a simple set of mechanisms
(physical or biological processes) that explain why observing event A makes
event B much more likely than if A had not occurred. In PLN we represent
beliefs as weighted truth values attached to logical links, and we use inference
rules to update those beliefs. By combining our theory of weakness (which biases
us toward simpler explanations) with probabilistic measures of ”how much A
raises the chance of B,” we can guide the learning process so that the system
prefers concise, strong causal hypotheses. In what follows, we first outline this
approach formally, then give two concrete examples: one where a robot learns a
causal rule about a switch and a light, and another where a system infers causal
relations among genes in a gene expression network.
    We can formalize this as follows:

  1. Weakness Algebra. We assume a finite set of candidate mechanisms
     M = {m1 , . . . , mk }. Each mechanism mi can be represented as a PLN
     rule or small network of links in AtomSpace. We assign each mechanism a
     complexity cost w(mi ) ≥ 0, e.g. equal to the length (in bits) of its symbolic
     description. For any subset M 0 ⊆ M , the total weakness is
                                            X
                               w(M 0 ) =         w(m).
                                                m∈M 0

       A lower w means a simpler, -weaker- explanation.
  2. Probabilistic Counterfactual Strength. Let A and B be two event
     predicates (e.g. PressSwitch and LightOn). We define

       P (B | A; M 0 ) = estimated probability that B occurs given A, under mechanisms M 0 ,

       and similarly
                                       P (B | ¬A; M 0 ).
       The counterfactual strength of A on B under M 0 is

                       δM 0 (A, B) = P (B | A; M 0 ) − P (B | ¬A; M 0 ).

       In PLN, P (·) is derived from reasoning over weighted links that represent
       observed event co-occurrences and mechanistic relations in data.
  3. Causal Hypothesis Ordering. We consider candidate pairs (M 0 , P ),
     where M 0 is a set of mechanisms and P is a -’simple– probability estimator


                                          156
     (e.g. a count-based model encoded as a small PLN subnetwork). Define
     the product pair
                          (w(M 0 ) + w(P ), δM 0 (A, B)).
     We order these pairs by first comparing δ (larger is better), and breaking
     ties by comparing w (smaller is better). Formally,
                                                                      
          (w1 , δ1 ) Prec (w2 , δ2 ) ⇐⇒  δ1 > δ2 ∨ δ1 = δ2 ∧ w1 < w2 .

     The best causal explanation for A − B is the maximal pair under Prec.
  4. Embedding in PLN. We represent each candidate mechanism m as a
     small PLN subnetwork: for instance, if m is ”PressSwitch causes Cur-
     rFlow,” we represent that as an ImplicationLink or a set of correlated
     Inheritance and Evaluation links with initial truth values derived from
     data. The probability model P is encoded via PLN’s statistical aggrega-
     tion functions (e.g. counting how often links co-occur in a training cor-
     pus). We then apply PLN’s forward or backward chaining rules to com-
     pute P (B | A; M 0 ) and P (B | ¬A; M 0 ) for each M 0 . Finally, we compute
     δM 0 (A, B) and compare weakness w(M 0 ) + w(P ) to select the top hypoth-
     esis.

  5. Causality Theorem. Let H be the set of all candidate   causal hypotheses
     (M 0 , P). Then the hypothesis that maximizes δM 0 (A, B), − (w(M 0 ) +
                                                     

     w(P )) under lexicographic ordering yields the simplest, strongest causal
     rule. More precisely:
     Theorem 19 (PLN Causal Hypothesis Selection). Let A, B be event pred-
     icates, H = {(M 0 , P )} candidate hypotheses, and define

                   φ(M 0 , P ) = δM 0 (A, B), − (w(M 0 ) + w(P )) .
                                                                 


     Under the lexicographic order that compares δ first (higher is better) then
     −w (higher is better), the maximal φ(M ∗ , P ∗ ) corresponds to the causal
     explanation that yields the greatest gain in B’s probability from A while
     using the simplest mechanisms and probability model.

     Sketch. If there were another pair (M 0 , P 0 ) with strictly larger δ, it would
     precede (M ∗ , P ∗ ) by definition, so (M ∗ , P ∗ ) cannot be maximal unless its δ
     is maximal. If two hypotheses share the same δ, then the pair with smaller
     w ranks higher. Hence the chosen (M ∗ , P ∗ ) is the unique maximizer.

   To give a practical sense of these concepts we now explore some toy ”thought
experiment” style examples.




                                        157
Example 1: Robot Learning Causal Rule for a Switch and a Light
Scenario. A domestic robot observes boolean sensor events –PressSwitch(t)–
and –LightOn(t’)–, where t0 occurs shortly after t if the switch was pressed.
The robot’s goal is to learn a causal rule –PressSwitch causes LightOn– using a
minimal set of mechanisms.

  1. The robot records ground observations in AtomSpace as:

     (eval (pred "PressSwitch") (list (time t))) and (eval (pred "LightOn") (list (time t’)

  2. Candidate mechanisms:
          • m1 : Mechanical linkage rule—When the switch is pressed, it closes a
            circuit, causing current flow.– Encoded as a small PLN subnetwork:
                   (ImplicationLink
                     (and
                       (eval (pred "PressSwitch") (list $t))
                       (eval (pred "CircuitClosed") (list $t)) )
                     (eval (pred "CurrFlow") (list $t)) )

            plus
                   (ImplicationLink
                     (eval (pred "CurrFlow") (list $t))
                     (eval (pred "LightOn") (list $t’)) )

          • m2 : Direct light logic rule—Pressing the switch directly toggles a
            relay that turns on the light.– A simpler single-link rule:
                   (ImplicationLink
                     (eval (pred "PressSwitch") (list $t))
                     (eval (pred "LightOn")    (list $t’)) )


  3. Complexity costs (weakness):

                              w(m1 ) = 3,    w(m2 ) = 1,

     reflecting that m2 is simpler (one rule) than m1 (two chained rules).
  4. Probability model P is -’simple– if it counts how often –PressSwitch– and
     –LightOn– co-occur in a small time window. Let w(P ) = 1.
  5. Compute empirical probabilities via PLN match counts:

     P (LightOn | PressSwitch; m2 ) = 0.9,    P (LightOn | ¬PressSwitch; m2 ) = 0.1,

     so
                          δm2 = 0.8,   w(m2 , P ) = 1 + 1 = 2.


                                       158
     For m1 , suppose the robot’s data gives

     P (LightOn | PressSwitch; m1 ) = 0.85,     P (LightOn | ¬PressSwitch; m1 ) = 0.05,

     so
                         δm1 = 0.80,   w(m1 , P ) = 3 + 1 = 4.

  6. Compare the pairs:

                     (w, δ)m2 = (2, 0.80),    (w, δ)m1 = (4, 0.80).

     Since both give the same δ = 0.80, we pick the one with smaller w, which
     is m2 . Thus the robot learns –PressSwitch − LightOn– using the simple
     relay rule.

Example 2: Learning Causal Relations in a Gene Expression Network
Scenario. We have time-series gene expression levels for genes A, B, C, etc.
We want to learn that Gene A upregulates Gene B under a simple regulatory
mechanism.

  1. Observations recorded in AtomSpace:

           (EvalLink
             (pred "HighExpr")
             (list (concept "GeneA") (Time_t)) )
           (EvalLink
             (pred "HighExpr")
             (list (concept "GeneB") (Time_{t+dt})) )


     whenever A is highly expressed at time t and B is highly expressed at
     time t + dt.

  2. Candidate mechanisms:
          • m1 : Direct activation—Gene A produces a transcription factor that
            binds B’s promoter.– Represented by a PLN subnetwork:
                   (ImplicationLink
                     (EvalLink
                       (pred "HighExpr")
                       (list (concept "GeneA") $t))
                     (EvalLink
                       (pred "Activates")
                       (list (concept "GeneA") (concept "GeneB"))) )


            plus


                                       159
                (ImplicationLink
                  (EvalLink
                    (pred "Activates")
                    (list (concept "GeneA") (concept "GeneB")))
                  (EvalLink
                    (pred "HighExpr")
                    (list (concept "GeneB") $t+dt)) )

       • m2 : Indirect co-expression—Gene A and Gene B share a common
         upstream regulator gene C.– This involves two ImplicationLinks via
         gene C:
                (ImplicationLink
                  (EvalLink
                    (pred "HighExpr")
                    (list (concept "GeneA") $t))
                  (EvalLink
                    (pred "HighExpr")
                    (list (concept "GeneC") $t+dt1)) )
                (ImplicationLink
                  (EvalLink
                    (pred "HighExpr")
                    (list (concept "GeneC") $t+dt1))
                  (EvalLink
                    (pred "HighExpr")
                    (list (concept "GeneB") $t+dt2)) )


3. Complexity costs:
                            w(m1 ) = 2,    w(m2 ) = 3,
  because m2 has two chained implications rather than one direct link.
4. Probability model P counts how often –A high at t– is followed by –B
   high at t + dt.– Let w(P ) = 1.
5. Empirical estimates from data (via PLN backward chaining):

  P (HighExpr(B) | HighExpr(A); m1 ) = 0.70,       P (HighExpr(B) | ¬HighExpr(A); m1 ) = 0.30,

  so
                       δm1 = 0.40,   w(m1 , P ) = 2 + 1 = 3.
  For m2 , suppose

  P (HighExpr(B) | HighExpr(A); m2 ) = 0.65,       P (HighExpr(B) | ¬HighExpr(A); m2 ) = 0.25,

  so
                       δm2 = 0.40,   w(m2 , P ) = 3 + 1 = 4.


                                     160
  6. Compare:
                       (w, δ)m1 = (3, 0.40),       (w, δ)m2 = (4, 0.40).
       Both mechanisms give the same counterfactual strength (0.40), but m1 is
       simpler (weakness 3 versus 4). Hence the system concludes that ”Gene A
       upregulates Gene B” via the direct activation mechanism m1 .

14.4     Disentangled Representations as a Special Case of
         Clear Representations
We now apply this notion of causality to help move toward resolution of some
fundamental issues in learning theory, related to the properties of representa-
tions within neural nets and other learning networks, and how these properties
connect with the generalization performance of these networks.
    In modern deep learning, we often hear about entangled versus disentangled
representations inside neural networks. Informally, a representation is disentan-
gled if each internal neuron or latent dimension cleanly corresponds to a single
underlying factor of variation in the data-for example, one neuron tracks object
rotation, another tracks color, and so on. In contrast, an entangled represen-
tation mixes multiple factors into each neuron, so it is hard to say why the
network produced a certain output O given input history I by looking at its
internal state. A beautiful elaboration of these ideas has been given by Ken
Stanley in his paper on ”Fractured Entangled Representations” [KCLS25].
    We suggest it may be helpful to introduce a more general notion of a clear
representation for any discrete dynamical system: when the system outputs O
after receiving inputs I, there ought to be a concise, easy-to-determine causal
explanation of why O resulted from I. Under this definition, the standard
idea of disentangled representations in neural networks turns out to be just
one example. If each latent dimension corresponds to a unique causal factor,
then explaining the output in terms of that dimension is straightforward. If
instead factors are jumbled, we no longer have a concise causal account. Thus
disentangled networks are precisely those whose internal representations are
clear in our broader sense.
    The interpretation of causality in terms of weakness has obvious implications
here.

Formal Treatment Let us begin by defining a general discrete dynamical
system, then state what it means to have a clear representation, and finally
show how disentangled neural representations fit this mold.
  1. Discrete Dynamical System. Consider a system with:
         • A finite set of internal state variables S = {s1 , . . . , sn }.
         • A set of input histories
                                     I = { i0 , i1 , . . . , iT −1 },
           where each it is taken from a finite input alphabet I.


                                           161
     • A deterministic update rule

                                  F : I × Rn −→ Rn ,

        so that if at time step t the inputs up to time t have led to internal
        state s(t) ∈ Rn , then upon receiving input it the system updates to
                                                      
                                s(t + 1) = F it , s(t) .

     • An output function
                                    G : Rn −→ O,
        where O is a finite set of possible outputs. Thus, after processing the
        input sequence I up to time T , the system’s internal state is
                                                                        
                      s(T ) = F iT −1 , F (. . . , F (i0 , s(0)) . . . ) ,

        and the final output is
                                               
                                    O = G s(T ) .

2. Clear Representation. A representation (S, F, G) is called clear if, for
   every possible input history I and resulting output O = G(s(T )), there
   exists a simple causal explanation of why O follows from I via intermediate
   state s(T ). Formally, we require:

     • A causal explanation is a logical construct in a suitable language (e.g.
       CMMC will do) that shows how specific state components sk1 , sk2 , . . .
       of s(T ) determine O.
     • Simple can be interpreted to mean the explanation’s description
       length is bounded by a small constant β... or better yet that its
       weakness is bounded from above.

  Equivalently, what this means is that there exists a small set of indices
  K ⊆ {1, . . . , n} such that knowing {sk (T ) : k ∈ K} suffices to predict O,
  and the rules describing how sk values cause O are simple.
  Roughly speaking: (S, F, G) is clear if O depends only on a small, easily
  identified subset of the internal dimensions, and the mapping from those
  dimensions to O is itself simple.
3. Neural Network Representations. A feedforward neural network or
   a recurrent network can be viewed as a special case of the above system:
     • The internal state s(t) ∈ Rn is the vector of activations in a selected
       layer at time t (for recurrent networks) or the final layer (for feedfor-
       ward).
     • The update rule F is given by matrix multiplications and activation
       functions (e.g. tanh or ReLU) that process each input sequentially.


                                     162
      • The output function G might be a softmax or argmax layer that
        produces a discrete label or decision.
4. Entangled vs. Disentangled. In the deep learning literature, a repre-
   sentation is said to be disentangled if each latent coordinate sk abstracts
   a single generative factor or concept. Formally, if the true data-generating
   process has latent factors Z = {z1 , . . . , zm }, a disentangled network learns
   a representation function

                                   R : I T −→ Rn

   such that each sk = Rk (I) depends primarily on one zj , and is invariant
   to others. By contrast, an entangled representation has each sk mixing
   multiple zj s.
5. Disentangled as Clear. We now argue that a disentangled representa-
   tion is a special case of a clear representation:

    (a) Suppose there are m true causal factors z1 , . . . , zm that determine the
        final output O. A disentangled network ensures there exist indices
        k1 , . . . , km such that
                                                 
                             skj (T ) = fj zj (I) , j = 1, . . . , m,

        where each fj is a simple mapping from factor zj to activation skj ,
        and all other coordinates carry no additional information.
    (b) Because O is a deterministic function of the latent factors z1 , . . . , zm ,
        there is a simple mapping

                                      H : Rm −→ O,

        so that                                             
                                O = H z1 (I), . . . , zm (I) .
        Composing these two steps, we write

                       O = H f1−1 (sk1 (T )), . . . , fm
                                                       −1
                                                                    
                                                          (skm (T )) ,

        or equivalently
                                                                 
                           O = Gsimple sk1 (T ), . . . , skm (T ) ,

        where Gsimple is the composed simple map.
    (c) In this scenario, the concise causal explanation of O given input I
        is: –Because sk1 (T ) = f1 (z1 (I)), ..., skm (T ) = fm (zm (I)), and O =
        H(z1 (I), . . . , zm (I)), hence O = Gsimple (sk1 (T ), . . . , skm (T )).– The
        total description length (weakness) of this explanation is small if
        each fj , H, and the inverse functions fj−1 are simple.


                                       163
      (d) Thus the network’s representation is clear : only the m coordinates
          {skj } matter for predicting O, and the mapping from (sk1 , . . . , skm )
          to O is simple. Concretely, the set K = {k1 , . . . , km } is small, and the
          explanation involves only those m neurons plus a simple combining
          rule Gsimple .

     Therefore, any disentangled representation in the usual sense satisfies the
     definition of a clear representation: there exists a small subset of latent
     dimensions that suffice to explain the output concisely.
  6. Entangled as Unclear. Conversely, suppose the network’s internal state
     s(T ) is entangled-each coordinate sk (T ) depends on multiple generative
     factors in a convoluted way. Then to explain O from s(T ), one typically
     needs to consult a large subset of coordinates and perform a complex
     nonlinear combination. Formally, no small subset KSubset{1, . . . , n} of
