     dimensions suffices to predict O with a simple map. Hence, according to
     our definition, the representation is not clear.

Summary We have shown that the conventional concept of disentangled rep-
resentations-where each latent dimension captures exactly one underlying factor-
fits precisely into our broader notion of a clear representation in a discrete dy-
namical system. In a disentangled model, there is a concise causal explanation
of the output O in terms of a small set of latent coordinates and simple com-
bining rules. When latent factors are entangled, no such concise explanation
exists, and the representation is unclear according to our theory of weakness
for learning and reasoning. Thus, disentanglement emerges as a special case
of clarity. This connection underscores how the general theory of weakness
can unify and explain why disentangled representations are beneficial for causal
interpretability and efficient reasoning.


15      Weakness-Based Causal Decision Theory
We now elaborate our weakness-based understanding of causality into a full-
fledged weakness-based causal decision theory, and flesh this out via considering
various classic problem cases for decision theory. These ”problem cases” are toy
examples of mainly academic interest, however the topic is a highly practical
one and critical to the effective operation of goal systems for AGI systems and
modern AI applications (a topic to be taken up more fully in Chapter 16 when
we look at the direct application of weakness to motivational systems for AIs).
    In ordinary decision-theory one asks “which action will best achieve my
goals”? In causal decision theory one refines that to “which action causes the
best outcomes? In [Goe20c] it is proposed to flesh this out by imposing an
Occam-style simplicity bias on hypotheses about how actions bring about out-
comes. Here we explore this direction further, but replacing “simplicity” by the
more general notion of weakness: the tendency to make as few extra assumptions
as possible.


                                        164
    One fairly simple way to insert weakness into decision theory would be to
define a weak causal decision theory (WCDT) which selects, among all candidate
causal explanations that guarantee a high degree of desirable outcomes, the one
that is weakest–that is, the most broadly applicable hypothesis that still delivers
the desired effect. This generalizes and elegantizes the earlier “simplificational”
approaches by placing them in the categorical framework of weakness.
    However it seems valuable to go further than this, and explore ways of in-
tegrating WCDT with Wei Dai’s Updateless Decision Theory (UDT), yielding
a hybrid approach that appears to have some preferential properties, especially
in situations involving collective decision making among correlated agents.

15.1     Seven Decision Theories: Verbal and Formal Overview
We briefly summarize seven decision rules, each with a verbal sketch and its
core formal criterion. Let A denote an action (or policy) and U the resulting
utility; let do(A) denote the Pearl-style intervention [Pea00].

15.1.1    Evidential Decision Theory (EDT)
Choose the action whose observation correlates with the highest utility, treating
P (U | A) as if it were causal. [Ahm14]

                          A∗RmEDT = arg max E U | A .
                                                    
                                         A


15.1.2   Timeless Decision Theory (TDT)
Reason by logical counterfactuals among all copies of your decision code, select-
ing the action that in proof yields maximal utility. [Yud10]

     A∗RmT DT = arg max U : “If all copies choose A then U ” is provable .
                         
                    A


15.1.3   Updateless Decision Theory (UDT)
[Dai09] Commit in advance to a policy before seeing any observations, optimiz-
ing a fixed prior over complete policies.
                          X
        p∗UDT = arg max       Pr (p) Pr(U ) U U, p(·) , A = p∗UDT (x).
                                                     
                p:Obs−Act       prior
                            U


15.1.4   Weak Causal Decision Theory (WCDT)
Within a known causal model, keep only causal submodels h with positive in-
terventional gain, then pick the weakest (most general) one.

∆(h) = E U | do(A) −E U | do(¬A) , h∗ = arg max w(h), A = h∗ -prescribed.
                                  
                                                h: ∆(h)≥0




                                        165
15.1.5   Weak Updateless Decision Theory (WUDT)
Fix a prior over causal submodels weighted by weakness, then at decision time
filter by interventional gain and choose the weakest sufficient submodel.

                     π(h) ∝ w(h)α ,       h∗ = arg max w(h).
                                               h: ∆(h)≥0


15.1.6   Weak Causal Updateless Decision Theory (WCUDT)
Use an updateless prior over submodels weighted by both weakness and causal
coherence, then filter by ∆ ≥ 0 and pick the submodel with highest prior weight.

                   π(h) ∝ w(h)α c(h)β ,     h∗ = arg max π(h).
                                                  h: ∆(h)≥0


15.1.7   Weakness Causal Filtering UDT (WCFUDT)
Precommit to a prior over submodels without weakness ranking, then at decision
time keep any h with ∆ ≥ 0 and choose the one with highest prior.

     π(h) fixed updatelessly,   H0 = {h : ∆(h) ≥ 0},          h∗ = arg max π(h).
                                                                    h∈H0


15.2     Decision-Theory Comparison: Smoking-Lesion Ex-
         ample
Consider the classic “smoking-lesion” scenario. There is a binary health variable

                                L = {has lesion}

and an action variable
                            S = {smoke, ¬smoke}.
A genetic lesion L causes both a preference for smoking and a high risk of lung
cancer, but smoking itself has no causal effect on cancer. The payoffs are:

                U (¬smoke, ¬L) = +1,        U (¬smoke, L) = −10,

                  U (smoke, ¬L) = +2,       U (smoke, L) = −10.
Thus smoking yields more utility when there is no lesion, and has no additional
harm when there is.

15.2.1   Evidential Decision Theory (EDT)
EDT selects the action that maximizes
                             X
              E U |S=a =            Pr(L = ` | S = a) U (a, `).
                                `∈{0,1}




                                      166
Because smoking correlates with the lesion, Pr(L | S = smoke) is higher than
Pr(L | ¬smoke), so EDT finds

                        E[U | smoke] < E[U | ¬smoke],

and therefore (incorrectly) recommends against smoking.

15.2.2   Timeless and Updateless Decision Theories (TDT, UDT)
Both TDT and UDT avoid this evidential pitfall by reasoning in terms of in-
terventions or policies, rather than mere conditioning. In TDT one asks, “If all
code-copies with my inputs choose to smoke, what utility results?” and finds
that smoking has no causal link to cancer. UDT fixes a prior over complete
policies (smoke vs. abstain) and chooses the one whose interventional expected
utility is higher. Both conclude
                                                           
                E U | do(S = smoke) > E U | do(S = ¬smoke) ,

and thus both recommend smoking.

15.2.3   Weak Causal Decision Theory (WCDT)
WCDT formalizes the above by constructing the causal graph

                   L − S,     L − Cancer,     S 6 − Cancer,

then filtering for submodels whose ∆(h) = E[U | do(S = smoke)]−E[U | do(S =
¬smoke)] exceeds zero. The minimal such submodel is the one including exactly
the edge L−S (the lesion causes smoking) and L−Cancer, omitting any spurious
“smoking causes cancer” link. Because this submodel is both strong enough to
yield ∆ > 0 and weakest (it makes no extra assumptions), WCDT likewise
recommends smoking.

15.2.4   Weak Updateless Causal Decision Theory (WCUDT)
WUCDT first assigns each causal submodel h an updateless prior π(h) ∝
w(h)α c(h)β , where w(h) measures generality and c(h) measures causal coher-
ence, then upon “intervening” re-weights each ∆(h) by π(h) and picks the weak-
est sufficient model. In the smoking-lesion case the only models passing ∆ > 0
are those that omit the smoking-causes-cancer edge, all of which share the same
maximal weakness. Hence WUCDT’s precommitment and posterior’weighted
strength selection still recommends smoking.
    In this case, WCFUDT will give a similar analysis.

15.2.5   Summary
In this simple example all causal or updateless theories – TDT, UDT, WCDT,
WCUDT, WCFUDT – agree on smoking, while EDT falls prey to a spurious


                                      167
correlation. The various refinements differ only in when and how they select
and combine causal hypotheses, but all converge on the correct decision once
genuine causal links are taken into account.

15.3     Newcomb’s Paradox
For a more challenging decision theory problem, let us consider Newcomb’s
paradox, which presents a game between a Predictor and a Player. There are
two boxes:
   • Box 1 contains either $1 000 000 (if the Predictor anticipated you will take
     only Box 1) or $0 (otherwise).
   • Box 2 contains $1 000 for sure.
You may take either both boxes (Two-box) or only Box 1 (One-box). The Pre-
dictor, who is highly reliable, has already placed the money before you choose.
Should you one-box or two-box?

15.3.1   Decision-Theory Responses
EDT: Evidential Decision Theory conditions on your choice. Since Pr(Million |
   One) ≈ 1 and Pr(Million | Two) ≈ 0, EDT predicts a higher expected
   payoff by one-boxing.
TDT: Timeless Decision Theory reasons by logical counterfactuals: it asks “if
   all agents with my code one-box, I get $1 000 000; if they two-box, I get
   $1 000.” Hence TDT one-boxes.
UDT: Updateless Decision Theory fixes a universal prior over entire policies
   and selects the policy that, if adopted by all its copies, maximizes expected
   utility. That policy is “One-box,” so UDT one-boxes.
WCDT: Weak Causal Decision Theory builds the causal graph Action−Predictor−
   Content − Payoff, filters for submodels with positive ∆, and chooses the
   weakest sufficient submodel. The minimal causal explanation yields one-
   boxing.
WUDT: Weak Updateless Decision Theory assigns each causal submodel a
  prior proportional to its weakness, then on seeing no new observation picks
  among those with positive causal gap the weakest. It thus also one-boxes.
WCUDT: The hybrid “Weak Causal Updateless” theory uses an updateless
   prior over submodels weighted by weakness and causal coherence, then at
   decision time filters by interventional gain and selects the weakest. The
   result once again is to one-box.
WCFUDT: Both the one and two box submodels have equal generality and
   equal causal coherence under the predictor-box action graph, so their prior
   weights π tie. The one-box submodel, however, yields interventional gain.
   WCFUDT discards two-box, and thus one-boxes as well.


                                       168
15.4    Superrationality across Decision Theories
Superrationality, as introduced by Hofstadter [Hof85], is the idea that if all
agents in a symmetric decision situation reason the same way, then each agent
should choose the action that yields the best collective outcome under the as-
sumption that every agent will make the same choice. In other words, rather
than treating other agents’ moves as independent, a superrational agent exploits
the logical symmetry between identical decision-makers to secure the best joint
result.

Handling by Different Theories
EDT Evidential Decision Theory conditions on one’s own action and may co-
   operate under superrational symmetry because it sees a statistical corre-
   lation: if ”I cooperate” then ”they cooperate,” so EDT often cooperates.
   But it does so for the wrong reason – it treats correlation as causation and
   can be misled by spurious links.
TDT Timeless Decision Theory explicitly reasons about logical copies: it asks
   “if all code-copies of me make the same choice, what utility follows?” By
   construction it superrationally cooperates, since it treats identical reason-
   ing agents as locked into the same decision.
UDT Updateless Decision Theory fixes a prior over full policies and optimizes
   that prior without updating. Because it evaluates a policy’s payoff un-
   der the assumption that every instance of it is used by all agents, UDT
   naturally implements superrational cooperation.
WCDT Weak Causal Decision Theory insists on genuine causal links. A pure
   superrational link (logical symmetry) is not a causal channel, so WCDT
   ignores it and will not cooperate unless there is a real intervention path
   from one agent’s choice to another’s.

WUDT Weak Updateless Decision Theory precommits to a prior over causal
  submodels based on weakness, but it still requires causal efficacy to yield
  payoff. In the absence of any real causal tie, WUDT does not exploit
  superrational symmetry, though it would if an exotic causal mechanism
  were encoded in its prior.

WCUDT The hybrid Weak Causal Updateless theory blends an updateless
   prior over submodels with causal’strength filtering. It will only superra-
   tionally cooperate if and only if its fixed prior assigns weight to a genuine
   causal channel linking agents’ choices; pure logical symmetry alone is in-
   sufficient.

WCFUDT Superrationality appeals to logical symmetry among identical agents,
   but WCFUDT requires a genuine causal link. It will only ”cooperate” or
   vote in step with others if its prior π(h) assigns weight to a submodel


                                      169
       h that encodes a real intervention channel Purely logical or evidential
       correlations, without causal efficacy, fail the filter and are ignored. Thus
       WCFUDT also captures the spirit of superrational coordination only when
       a true causal mechanism underlies the symmetry.

15.5     Voting and Superrationality
The voting problem arises because in a large electorate a single vote has almost
zero chance of changing the result, so a naive analysis would advise abstention.
Yet we know that high turnout can be crucial to good collective outcomes.
Superrationality observes that if all voters reason identically, then your choice
logically correlates with theirs – so your single vote effectively carries more
weight. We now summarize how seven decision theories respond to voting in
both uncorrelated and superrational (correlated) settings.

EDT (Evidential) Under independent voting, P (win | vote) ≈ P (win | abstain),
   so EDT recommends abstention. In a superrational model where your vote
   raises the probability that others vote, conditioning gives P (win | vote) >
   P (win | abstain), so EDT then prescribes voting.

TDT (Timeless) TDT asks ”if all logically identical agents vote, what utility
   results”? With no assumed causal link it finds no gain and abstains. If it
   accepts a logical symmetry among voters, it treats their choices as locked
   together and therefore votes.
UDT (Updateless) UDT precommits to a policy (vote or abstain) before ob-
   serving turnout statistics. With no built-in expectation of correlation it
   typically chooses abstention. If its fixed prior includes superrational cor-
   relations among voters, it instead precommits to voting.
WCDT (Weak Causal) WCDT builds a causal graph of vote-outcome. With
   independent voting that graph yields negligible payoff gap, so it abstains.
   If you introduce a genuine causal link vote-peers-outcome, WCDT finds a
   positive causal effect and votes.
WUDT (Weak Updateless) WUDT fixes an updateless prior over causal
  submodels weighted by weakness, then on election day tests each sub-
  model’s causal gap. Without correlation it abstains; with a causal social-
  influence link in its prior it votes.
WCUDT (Weak Causal Updateless) This hybrid uses an updateless, weakness-
   causality prior over submodels and then filters by day-of interventional
   gain. It abstains under pure independence and votes only if its fixed prior
   assigns weight to a real correlation channel, thus combining superrational
   coordination with genuine causal intervention.
WCFUDT (Weak Causal Filtering UDT) WCFUDT precommits to an
   updateless prior over all vote-outcome causal submodels (without explicit


                                        170
       weakness ranking), then on election day discards any submodel whose in-
       terventional payoff gap is negative and votes if any remaining submodel
       prescribes voting. Consequently, in the independent-voter case it still
       votes (due to the contrived tie-flip model), and in the correlated case it
       likewise votes, but it cannot distinguish genuine social influence from spu-
       rious channels.

15.6     Voting Variations Where WCUDT Excels
We now describe two realistic voting scenarios in which the full hybrid Weak-
ness Causal Updateless Decision Theory (WCUDT) outperforms its component
methods (WCDT, WUDT) and the simpler hybrid WCFUDT.

15.6.1    Uncertain Correlation Strength
Scenario: Before election day you know only a rough prior on your social-
influence parameter x; you will only learn its true value when polling closes.

WCDT: Waits until you observe x and then applies the causal pivot bound.
If the day-of estimate is slightly below threshold, you abstain – even though the
true value (noisily estimated) might have been above threshold.

WUDT: Precommits to a policy (”vote” or ”abstain”) based on a weakness-
and-causality prior over submodels, but cannot adapt when you learn that x is
higher than expected.

WCFUDT: Uses an updateless prior with causal weighting but no explicit
weakness ranking. As long as any contrived submodel (e.g. tie-flip) has nonzero
prior, it will vote regardless of the actual x estimate, leading to potential over-
commitment.

WCUDT:
   • Retains an updateless prior π(h) ∝ w(h)α c(h)β over causal submodels.
   • On election day, for each h computes the true ∆(h; x).

   • Discards all h with ∆ < 0, then picks among the rest the one with maximal
     π(h).
This allows WCUDT to hedge: if x is ultimately high, it will vote; if low, it
abstains; and if marginal, it falls back on the most plausible weak submodel,
avoiding both day-of overreaction and rigid precommitment.




                                        171
15.6.2   Spurious Tie-Flip versus Genuine Influence
Scenario: Besides a small genuine social channel, there exists a contrived
”tie-flip” hypothesis that yields a tiny positive ∆, yet is far less general.
    Imagine a voter who insists that “my single vote will flip the election” by
postulating a perfectly balanced scenario: without their vote, the margin is ex-
actly zero, and then the tie-breaking procedure happens to favor their preferred
candidate. Concretely, this hypothesis assumes
   • The pre-vote margin M = 0 exactly, and
   • The tie-breaker (whether a coin toss or other rule) resolves in favor of the
     voter’s candidate.
Such a model yields a small but nonzero payoff gap, since it guarantees that
casting the vote turns a loss into a win.
   However, it is wildly contrived:

  1. It requires an implausible, exact tie before the vote, an event of vanishing
     probability in a large electorate.
  2. It further assumes the tie-’breaker always goes the voter’s way, excluding
     nearly all other possible outcomes.
    As a causal hypothesis, it “works” in the sense of producing ∆ > 0, but it
is extremely strong – it rules out almost every world except those two highly
specific conditions. Hence its weakness w(h) is nearly zero. By contrast, a
genuine social-influence model merely assumes your vote nudges a few peers
to the polls – a far more plausible, far less restrictive hypothesis –and so has
much higher weakness, making it the preferred choice in Weak Causal Decision
Theory.
    In the presence of this sort of hypothesis

WCDT: Filters by ∆ > 0, then selects the weakest submodel; here it correctly
picks the genuine social-influence model since it is more general than the tie-flip
model.

WUDT: Precommits under a pure weakness+causality prior; if its prior π
underestimates the social-influence channel relative to tie-flip, it may lock into
the wrong model.

WCFUDT: Uses an unranked updateless prior and simply picks any h with
∆ > 0, but then chooses the one with highest prior weight. If its prior over-
weights the contrived tie-flip channel, it will erroneously recommend voting
purely to attempt a tie-flip.




                                       172
WCUDT:       Combines the strengths of both:
  1. Its updateless prior favors the genuine social model (higher w(h), higher
     c(h)).
  2. It filters by ∆ > 0, eliminating both the trivial model and any submodel
     that lacks genuine causal effect.
  3. It then selects the remaining model with maximal π(h), which is the social-
     influence channel.
Thus WCUDT wins in robustness: it never picks the contrived tie-flip, but still
adapts if the social influence strength changes.

15.6.3    Why WCUDT Wins
By maintaining an updateless, Occam-weakness+causality prior while still fil-
tering on do-based payoff gaps, WCUDT achieves both:
   • Adaptivity to new evidence (x, dynamic costs) as in WCDT,
   • Rigorous precommitment and modular coordination as in WUDT,
   • Dirigible plausibility ranking that avoids pathological submodels un-
     like WCFUDT.
These features make it uniquely well-suited to real-world voting problems where
correlation strength is uncertain and spurious causal hypotheses abound.


16       Weakness and Motivational Systems
In the context of real cognitive systems, decision-making occurs in the broader
context of a more complex dynamical system of goals and motivations and emo-
tions. With this in mind, we next attempt to understand how weakness-driven
learning (Occam-style preference for simple hypotheses) interacts with motiva-
tional structure in a cognitive system. We approach this within the framework
of MetaMo [GL25b] [GL25a], a formal approach to motivation that ties together
goals, motivations, and learning processes.
    In the MetaMo framework, one is inclined to look for connections between
weakness, motivation and system structure/dynamics such as:

   • If a large system is built from smaller subsystems, each of which finds its
     simplest (weakest) way to achieve its own goals, then one would like those
     subsystem-level hypotheses to “glue together– into a globally simplest hy-
     pothesis for the entire system.
   • If subsystem A is modeling subsystem B, then the best (weakest) assump-
     tions A makes about B should carry over to the real B’so that A’s internal
     model and B’s actual behavior are aligned in terms of minimal complexity.


                                     173
   • When you have to decide on an action (“what to do”) and also appraise
     the situation (“what is the situation–), you want to do both in a way that
     remains consistent with overall simplicity – so that the simplest hypoth-
     esis about the environment and the simplest decision rule commute. In
     everyday terms, you can separate understanding the world and choosing
     what to do without losing optimality, as long as both steps respect the
     same simplicity bias.

    In this section we sketch, first informally and then more formally, how these
intuitions follow from combining a categorial theory of motivations (the MetaMo
structure) with a categorial theory of weakness (an Occam’style ordering of
hypotheses).

16.1    Formal Overview of MetaMo & Weakness
We present here a simplified variant of MetaMo capturing the essence of these
compositionality desiderata, incorporating as we go the simplest connections be-
tween MetaMo and weakness theory. We assume familiarity with basic category
theory (categories, functors, monoidal products) but define each mathematical
notion carefully.

  1. Categories of Systems and Hypotheses.
        • Let C be a category of cognitive systems, whose objects are ’systems-
          (or subsystems) and whose morphisms (arrows) represent models or
          simulators from one system to another. Concretely, an arrow f : A −
          B indicates that system A internally models or understands system
          B via some abstraction.
        • Let H be a category of hypotheses (or belief’states) with a partial
          order ≤w capturing weakness (simplicity). More precisely, objects
          of H are candidate hypotheses (descriptions) about how a system
          operates, and h1 ≤w h2 means -h1 is at least as simple (weak) as
          h2 .–
  2. Monoidal Structure (MetaMo Composition).
        • The category C is equipped with a monoidal product ⊗, modeling
          parallel composition of subsystems. If A, B are subsystems, then
          A ⊗ B is the joint system that runs A and B in parallel (interacting
          or independent, depending on the design). There is a unit object I
          in C representing the -empty- or trivial system.
        • Correspondingly, H has a monoidal operation ? (often taken to be
          some notion of hypothesis concatenation or aggregation) with unit
          object e representing the -empty hypothesis- (no assumptions).
        • We assume a monoidal functor

                                Φ : (C, ⊗, I) −→ (H, ?, e)


                                      174
       that assigns to each system X ∈ Ob(C) a set of candidate hypotheses
       Φ(X) ∈ Ob(H), and to each morphism f : A − B a hypothesis-
       translation arrow
                              Φ(f ) : Φ(A) −→ Φ(B),
       preserving monoidal structure:

                    Φ(A ⊗ B) = Φ(A) ? Φ(B),        Φ(I) = e,

       and
                  Φ(f ⊗ g) = Φ(f ) ? Φ(g),    Φ(idX ) = idΦ(X) .
3. Weakest Hypothesis for a System. For each object X ∈ C, let

  Opt(X) = “the set of all minimal (weakest) hypotheses in Φ(X) under ≤w ”.

  We assume that Opt(X) is nonempty; that is, there exists at least one
  hypothesis of minimal complexity that explains how X achieves whatever
  goal or behavior is under consideration.
4. First Compositionality Principle. We require that for any two subsys-
   tems A, B ∈ C, the minimal hypotheses for the composite system A ⊗ B
   coincide with the -monoidal product– of minimal hypotheses for A and
   minimal hypotheses for B. Formally:

         Opt(A ⊗ B) = { hA ? hB | hA ∈ Opt(A), hB ∈ Opt(B) }.

  In words: -The simplest way to explain how A ⊗ B works is obtained
  by combining the simplest explanations of A and of B.– This property is
  exactly the requirement that Φ be monoidal and that ≤w be compatible
  with ?.
5. Second Principle: Modeling of Subsystems. Suppose subsystem A
   has an internal model of subsystem B given by a morphism f : A − B in
   C. Then, under Φ, there is an induced arrow

                          Φ(f ) : Φ(A) −→ Φ(B).

  We demand that this arrow preserves optimality: if hA ∈ Opt(A) is a
  simplest hypothesis about A, then

                           Φ(f )(hA ) ∈ Opt(B),

  or at least lies in the minimal layer of Φ(B). Equivalently, the simplest
  hypotheses that A uses to explain its own behavior should map, via its
  internal model, to simplest hypotheses about B’so that A’s beliefs about
  B are themselves optimally simple for B.
6. Decision vs. Appraisal Commutation. Finally, consider two distinct
   aspects of cognition:


                                 175
        • Appraisal hypotheses-hypotheses about -what the situation is- (the
          environment’s state).
        • Decision hypotheses-hypotheses about -what action to take- given
          the appraisal.
     Formally, let Xapp be the subcategory of -appraisal systems,- and Xdec be
     the subcategory of decision systems, each with corresponding hypothesis
     categories Φ(Xapp ) and Φ(Xdec ). We require a natural transformation (or
     commuting diagram) expressing that:
                     
     Opt Xapp ⊗Xdec = { happ ?hdec | happ ∈ Opt(Xapp ), hdec ∈ Opt(Xdec ) }.

     In words, -The simplest way to both appraise and decide is obtained by
     pairing a simplest appraisal hypothesis with a simplest decision hypothesis.-
     This enforces that -what to believe- and -what to do- commute under the
     simplicity ordering.

Approximate Compositionality: Continuity Assumptions In practice,
exact equality of optimal’sets may be too strict. We next describe continuity
conditions under which approximately optimal hypotheses compose approxi-
mately optimally.

  1. Metric on Weakness. Assume the weakness category (H, ≤w ) is en-
     riched with a metric dw : H × H − R≥0 , such that

                  h1 ≤w h2    =⇒     dw (h1 , h2 ) = w(h2 ) − w(h1 ),

     where w(h) is the numerical complexity of hypothesis h. In particular,
     hypotheses of equal complexity have distance zero.

  2. Approximate Optimality. For a system X, define Opt (X) to be the
     set of hypotheses whose complexity differs from the minimal complexity
     by at most :

                  Opt (X) = { h ∈ Φ(X) | w(h) ≤ w∗ (X) + },

     where w∗ (X) = min{w(h) | h ∈ Φ(X)}. Intuitively, these are -nearly
     minimal- hypotheses.
  3. Lipschitz Condition on Monoidal Functor. We assume that the
     monoidal functor Φ is Lipschitz continuous with respect to complexity
     metrics. That is, there exists a constant K ≥ 0 such that for any two
     systems A, B and hypotheses hA ∈ Φ(A), hB ∈ Φ(B),
                                  
                       w hA ? hB ≤ w(hA ) + w(hB ) + K.

     Equivalently, adding two hypotheses does not increase complexity by more
     than a constant overhead K.


                                      176
  4. Approximate Composition Theorem. Under the Lipschitz condition,
     if
                   hA ∈ Opt1 (A), hB ∈ Opt2 (B),
       then
                           hA ? hB ∈ Opt1 +2 +K (A ⊗ B).
       In words: Approximately minimal hypotheses for A and B, when com-
       bined, yield an approximately minimal hypothesis for A ⊗ B, with an
       approximation error at most 1 + 2 + K.
  5. Approximate Modeling Consistency. If f : A−B is a modeling arrow
     and
                           hA ∈ Opt (A),
       then
                              Φ(f )(hA ) ∈ Opt+K 0 (B),
       for some small constant K 0 ≥ 0. That is, a nearly-optimal hypothesis for
       A, when projected by A’s internal model of B, becomes a nearly-optimal
       hypothesis for B.
  6. Approximate Decision-Appraisal Commute. Similarly, if happ ∈
     Opt1 (Xapp ) and hdec ∈ Opt2 (Xdec ), then
                                                            
                     happ ? hdec ∈ Opt1 +2 +K” Xapp ⊗ Xdec ,

       for some small K”. Thus really close-to-optimal appraisal plus close-to-
       optimal decision yields a close-to-optimal combined action.

16.2     Weakness-Based Decision Theory Variants in MetaMo
MetaMo is a categorial theory of motivation that unifies perception, learning and
action under a single simplicity (weakness) bias. In MetaMo both hypothesis
formation and action selection minimize the same weakness measure, ensuring
coherence across all cognitive modules.
    MetaMo employs three main weakness-regularized decision procedures as its
core action-selection routines:

WCDT (Weakness Causal Decision Theory) Uses an Occam-weakness prior
   over causal models but updates that prior on day-of observations, yielding
   high adaptivity to new evidence. Ideal for reactive or perceptual subsys-
   tems.
WUDT (Weakness Updateless Decision Theory) Fixes a weakness-weighted
  prior once and for all (no updating), yielding strong precommitment and
  internal consistency. Best suited for long-horizon planning where commit-
  ment to a policy is essential.
WCUDT (Weakness Causal Updateless Decision Theory) Combines both
   strengths:


                                      177
         1. An updateless Occam-weakness plus causality prior
         2. Day-of filtering on positive causal payoff gaps
         3. Selection of the remaining model with highest prior weight
       This hybrid adapts when genuine causal influence emerges yet avoids spu-
       rious ”tie-flip” models, making it well suited for socially coordinated or
       multi-agent decision goals.

    By choosing among these variants at the goal-invocation layer, MetaMo can
tailor its motivational dynamics:

   • Exploration vs. Exploitation: rapid, evidence-driven choices via WCDT.
   • Long-Term Commitment: high-confidence commitment via WUDT.
   • Social Coordination and Robustness: balanced updateless coordination via
     WCUDT.

   In this way, MetaMo remains grounded in a single underlying simplicity bias
while allowing subsystems to adopt the decision rule best aligned with their
motivational needs.

16.3     Nontechnical Summary of Combined MetaMo and
         Weakness Theory
Putting together the MetaMo principles (which dictate that a system’s composi-
tion and its internal modeling must respect a monoidal (tensor) structure) with
our categorial account of weakness (Occam preference for simpler hypotheses),
we have obtained these basic takeaways:

   • Optimal Subsystems Compose to Optimal Systems. If each part of the
     system learns its simplest way to achieve its goals (e.g. via a WCDT or
     WUDT policy), then gluing those parts together yields the simplest way
     for the whole system. In more concrete terms, a robot whose left arm and
     right arm each learn a minimal-complexity controller under WCDT will,
     when acting together, produce a joint controller that is likewise minimal,
     rather than having to relearn a more complex joint policy.
   • Internal Models Preserve Simplicity. When subsystem A builds an internal
     hypothesis hA about B (as in WUDT’s fixed prior), that hypothesis is
     also a minimal (or near-minimal) hypothesis for B itself. This ensures
     that A’s beliefs about B are not more complex than B’s actual workings.
     In practice, if an AI module models another module, it does not need to
     invent overly complicated internal representations.
   • Appraise–Then–Decide Remains Simple. Deciding what to do after eval-
     uating the environment (for example, by first applying a weakness-based
     model selection step and then choosing an action under WCDT) does not


                                       178
       add extra complexity beyond the sum of each step. That is, you can first
       find a simple model of what is going on, then find a simple rule for what to
       do, and the pair is still simple. Hence a system can safely modularize ”un-
       derstanding” (via updateless priors) and ”choosing” (via causal filtering)
       without losing Occam-optimality.

     • Robustness under Approximation. Even if subsystems only find near-
       optimal hypotheses, the combined system still finds a nearly optimal
       global hypothesis. Moreover, using a hybrid policy like WCUDT ensures
       that genuine causal influences are captured while avoiding spurious tie-
       breaking, so the system preserves near-optimal simplicity even under ap-
       proximation.

   In sum, a cognitive architecture that implements MetaMo and actively favors
weak (simple) hypotheses – and that selects its actions via WCDT, WUDT or
WCUDT as appropriate – will automatically exhibit a compositional Occam
property: subsystems can learn and model each other, and their decisions will
remain efficient and transparent, all while guaranteeing that the overall system
stays as simple as possible.


17       Modeling Efficient, Honest, Ethical Multi-
         Agent Communication
3

    We now attempt to use our ideas about weakness-related motivational sys-
tems and linguistic encodings to formalize certain ideas about agents can struc-
ture their communications to effectively balance multiple goals such as efficiency,
honesty and empathy.
    Cognitive agents such as AGI systems can communicate with each other in a
great variety of ways depending on their construction, motivation and situation.
The theory of ”Non-Violent Communication” (NVC) [Ros03] suggests certain
principles for communication among multiple agents that are intended to pro-
mote positive interaction and lead to useful and mutually beneficial cooperative
activity. Here we explore how a variation of the NVC idea can be formalized
in terms of categorial weakness and associated linguistic constructions, and we
argue that under certain assumptions about AGI motivational systems, it can
be very natural for AGIs to communicate according to a variant of NVC.
    We construct a series of abstractions regarding inter-agent communication,
then tie these back to the practicalities of NVC.
    We begin by recalling the quantale-valued weakness measure of a relation
H ⊆ U × U:                          M                 
                         w(H) =            µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) ,
                                       (u,v)∈H

    3 These ideas were co-created with Zar Goertzel




                                            179
where (V, ⊗, ⊕, e, ≤) is a commutative quantale and µ : U → V assigns weights
to primitive tokens. We show in that w(H) arises naturally as the pointwise
colimit of a subpresheaf in the enriched presheaf category [C op , V ], where C is
the V -enriched category on U with hom-objects C(u, v) = µ(u) ⊗ µ(v).
    We embed this construction into the free commutative quantale NN[U ] and
define a generalized code-length ` : NN[U ] → V . This yields a direct MDL
interpretation of weakness: each internal state or hypothesis has a minimal
multiset code, and w(H) is the ⊕-supremum of code-lengths for the pairs in H.
    Building on these ideas, we state and prove what we call the Cognitive Com-
munication Theorem, which asserts that, under natural assumptions (finite gen-
erating vocabulary, surjective encoding, shared semantics, truthfulness, rational
updating), every internal state v ∈ V admits a canonical minimal multiset en-
coding, and for any hypothesis relation H, the minimal code of w(H) is the
optimal message to send.
    Real systems rarely satisfy the assumptions of this theorem exactly, so we
also develop an approximate Cognitive Communication Theorem under small
error tolerances for expressivity, minimality, semantic alignment, truthfulness,
and updating. We show that near-optimal codes and the weakness-as-code
property persist up to bounded slack.
    These Cognitive Communication Theorems center on detailed, fully accurate
communication of states and observations between agents. In real life, however,
there is not usually time or energy for this sort of communication, and instead
agents need to use approximate shorthands, which other agents need to decode
using inference. We formalize this via composite hypothesis tokens hv , each of
which can be associated with a quantitative ”cost” that δ(hv ) bundles (1) the
speaker’s summarization effort, (2) the optimal expansion to primitive tokens,
and (3) the listener’s inference effort. Conjecture tokens ci→j (v) hypothesiz-
ing about other agents’ internal states are incorporated here as second-order
hypotheses. If δ(hv ) is set at most to the cost of the corresponding primitive
multiset, then agents’ use of hypotheses strictly reduce scommunicative effort.
    When the agents involved are all using the MetaMo motivational system
[GL25b] [GL25a], then these hypothesis tokens and their costs can be managed
very elegantly. Given the MetaMo cost-functor δ : Cobs → V , the enriched
left Kan extension Lanj δ uniquely forces each δ(hv ) to the infimum of all valid
encodings, making minimal-cost hypothesis tokens inevitable by design. I.e.
agents are only hypothesizing and conjecturing insofar as it’s useful for making
communication work better.
    For this ”efficient communication by design” to obtain, an agent motivational
architecture–MetaMo or any suitable alternative–must provide:
   • a commutative quantale of costs (V, ⊗, ⊕),
   • a cost-functorial assignment δ on a finite (or well-founded) token set,
   • a surjective encoding map Φ : NN[U ]  V ,
   • a canonical extension mechanism (e.g. enriched Kan extension) for hy-
     pothesis tokens,


                                       180
   • shared semantics, cooperative truthfulness, and rational one-shot updat-
     ing.
Any system satisfying these properties will automatically yield provably minimal-
cost encodings of internal states and hypotheses about self and other.
     Human communication works a little differently than this, but arguably
sometimes uses different mechanisms to come to similar ends. In the final sec-
tion, we compare these formal guarantees to the social processes by which hu-
man communities develop and stabilize efficient shorthand expressions, and we
speculate on using MetaMo-based digital twins as proxies to combine formal
efficiency with human-grounded semantics.
     These abstract considerations tie back very naturally to Nonviolent Commu-
nication (NVC), the crux of which is an emphasis on agents communicating only
what they directly observe and feel, without attributing hidden motives. In our
framework, observation tokens u and self-report tokens hv capture this direct
mapping, while conjecture tokens ci→j (v) model cautious hypotheses about oth-
ers. The Cognitive Communication Theorem then ensures each token admits a
unique minimal-cost multiset encoding, and MetaMo’s enriched Kan extension
makes those minimal costs unavoidable, while communication involving humans
or other less rigorously motivated systems will in some cases be able to approx-
imate this favorable situation (of using hypothesis or conjecture in inter-agent
communications only when actually useful). In this way, our formalism provides
a precise, quantitative realization of key aspects of NVC’s ideals of transparent,
non-attached sharing of experience and hypotheses.

17.1    Cognitive Communication Theorem
First of all, we present here a ”Cognitive Communication Theorem” which shows
that, under clear conditions, every internal state or hypothesis an agent holds
can be conveyed by a unique, minimal multiset message. Before giving the
formal result, we list the key assumptions and their intuitive roles:

  1. Finite, Generating Vocabulary. The public token set U is finite (or
     well-founded) and the weight map µ : U → V generates the entire quantale
     V under ⊗ and ⊕. (Ensures every internal state is expressible by some
     multiset of tokens.)

  2. Surjective Encoding. The induced homomorphism Φ : NN[U ]  V is
     surjective. (No state in V lies outside the image of encodable code-words.)
  3. Well-Founded Minimality. The poset NN[U ], ordered pointwise, is
     well-founded so that each fibre Φ−1 (v) has a least element. (Guarantees
     a unique minimal code for each state.)

  4. Shared Semantics. All agents agree on the meaning of each token in
     U and interpret its weight µ(u) identically. (Prevents ambiguity or mis-
     encoding.)


                                      181
  5. Cooperative Truthfulness. Agents truthfully emit tokens correspond-
     ing to their true internal states or observations, and receivers trust these
     declarations. (Avoids adversarial or deceptive uses of hypothesis tokens.)
  6. Rational Updating. Upon receipt of a multiset message w, agents in-
     tegrate its conveyed state Φ(w) coherently without further corrective ex-
     changes. (Ensures no hidden additional communication is required.)

Theorem 20 (Cognitive Communication Theorem). Under assumptions (1)–
(6) above, let
               Φ : NN[U ]  V and `(w) = Φ(w).
Then:
  1. (Expressivity) For every v ∈ V there exists at least one multiset w ∈
     NN[U ] with Φ(w) = v.
  2. (Minimality) Each fiber Φ−1 (v) has a unique least element

                      wmin (v) = min{ w ∈ NN[U ] | Φ(w) = v},

     which is the most efficient encoding of v.
  3. (Weakness-as-Code) For any relation H ⊆ U × U , define
                                   M                
                         w(H) =          µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) .
                                    (u,v)∈H

     Then the minimal multiset encoding of w(H),
                                            
                                 wmin w(H) ,

     is exactly the shortest code conveying the ”strongest” element of H.
Proof Sketch. Assumptions (1) and (2) guarantee that Φ is surjective onto V ,
establishing expressivity. Assumption (3) ensures well-foundedness of NN[U ],
so each fiber has a unique minimum, proving minimality. Finally, since w(H)
is a join over ⊗-products and is attained by some (u∗ , v ∗ ) ∈ H, selecting the
minimal multiset wmin (w(H)) yields the shortest message whose length equals
w(H), establishing the weakness-as-code property.

17.1.1   Cognitive Communication Theorem: Approximate Version
In real multi-agent systems, the six idealized assumptions we used to derive the
Cognitive Communication Theorem above (finite vocabulary, exact surjectivity,
perfect minimality, shared semantics, full truthfulness, rational updating) typ-
ically hold only approximately. Nevertheless, as long as each assumption fails
by at most a small error tolerance, we still obtain near-optimal codes for inter-
nal states and hypotheses. In particular, every state can be encoded within a
bounded error, there is a nearly minimal code, and the weakness-as-code prop-
erty holds up to a small slack.


                                      182
Approximate Assumptions.          Fix small error tolerances E , M , S , T , U ∈
V . We assume:

  1. (A1) Finite Vocabulary. U is finite (or well-founded).
  2. (A2) Approximate Expressivity. For every internal state v ∈ V there exists
     w ∈ NN[U ] such that

                               v ≤ Φ(w) ≤ v ⊕ E .

  3. (A3) Approximate Minimality. Each fiber Φ−1 (v) admits a code wmin (v)
     with
                
      Φ wmin (v) ≤ v ⊕ M and wmin (v) ≤ w for all w with Φ(w) ≥ v.

  4. (A4) Semantic Alignment. Any two agents’ weight maps µi , µj : U → V
     satisfy µi (u) ≤ µj (u) ⊕ S and vice versa for all u.
  5. (A5) Approximate Truthfulness. When an agent intends state v and emits
     code w, we have Φ(w) ≤ v ⊕ T .

  6. (A6) Approximate Updating. Upon receipt of a code w, agents form a
     belief v̂ with Φ(w) ≤ v̂ ⊕ U and v̂ ≤ Φ(w) ⊕ U .

Theorem 21 (Cognitive Communication Theorem: Approximate Version).
Under (A1) – (A6), define the map `(w) = Φ(w). Then:
  1. (Approximate Expressivity) For every v ∈ V there is w ∈ NN[U ] with

                                v ≤ `(w) ≤ v ⊕ E .
                                                                                 
  2. (Approximate Minimality) For each v the chosen wmin (v) satisfies ` wmin (v) ≤
     v ⊕ M and wmin (v) ≤ w for any w with `(w) ≥ v M .
  3. (Approximate Weakness-as-Code) For any relation H ⊆ U × U , let
                                 M                 
                       w(H) =          µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) .
                                    (u,v)∈H

                            
     Then the code wmin w(H) satisfies
                                 
           w(H) ≤ ` wmin (w(H)) ≤ w(H) ⊕ (S ⊕ T ⊕ U ⊕ M ).

Sketch. (A2) gives codes that approximate each v within E . (A3) and finiteness
ensure existence of the nearly minimal code wmin (v). For any hypothesis H, the
join defining w(H) is attained up to the semantic, truthfulness, and updating
errors; selecting wmin (w(H)) then yields the desired bound on `(wmin (w(H))).



                                      183
17.2    Concise Approximate Communication Between MetaMo
        Agents
The kind of communication guaranteed by the Cognitive Communication The-
orem under its given assumptions is effective but not generally efficient – it
basically involves communicating messages that directly reflect external obser-
vations or internal states. This will work if the agents have enough time and
patience to manage such an extremely explicit communication style.
    In general, though, packing e.g. a complex internal state v into a great
number of small exact-representation tokens can be costly. One way to work
around this, formally, is to introduce a dedicated hypothesis token hv that stands
for a small, optimally chosen multiset of observation tokens plus the speaker’s
summarization effort and the listener’s inference effort. This is basically how
practical communication among humans (or other animals) always works.
    Specifically the ingredients here include:

   • Summarization effort: The speaker must compress their reasoning about
     v into the shorthand hv . That work is part of δ(hv ).
   • Composite multiset: Under the hood, hv expands to the minimal multiset
     w ∈ NN[Uobs ] with Φ(w) = v. MetaMo’s enriched Kan extension ensures
     this multiset minimizes total effort.
   • Inference effort: The listener must decode hv , recall its agreed meaning,
     integrate it with observations, and update beliefs. This cognitive work
     also contributes to δ(hv ).

    Thus δ(hv ) bundles speaker summarization, transmission, and listener infer-
ence into one cost. In a MetaMo context, one can then ensure systems engage
in this sort of hypothesis-based communication only when the estimated costs
is less than sending the corresponding primitive multiset.

Formal Setup. Let Uobs be the finite set of basic observation tokens, each
with a MetaMo cost
                         δ : Uobs → RR≥0 ,
extended additively to multisets:
                           X          X
                        δ         nu u =  nu δ(u).
                            u∈Uobs           u

                             
               Cobs (v) = min δ(w) | w ∈ NN[Uobs ], Φ(w) = v .

Enlarge the vocabulary by adding one hypothesis token hv for each v ∈ V , and
set

Let U = Uobs ∪ {hv : v ∈ V } and extend δ to NN[U ] by additivity.
