                                         203
18.3.5    Wu Wei as Quantale-Geodesic Flow
The Schrödinger bridge quantale QSB defined above formalizes a notion of rep-
resentational weakness: the cost of transporting a semantic distribution through
time while resisting entropic or dynamic divergence.
    In this setting, each interpolating path (ρ, v) is interpreted as a morphism
in the enriched category. The geodesic condition,

                            c(x, y) ⊗ d(y, z) = d(x, z),

now expresses the fact that the effort of progressing from x to z is minimized
precisely by passing through y when (x → y) and (y → z) are each segments of
a Schrödinger bridge.
   Thus, quantum evolution corresponds to following a path in configuration
space that minimizes semantic effort over the entire trajectory, under constraints
imposed by both physical structure (via v and V ) and representational diver-
gence (via KL divergence).

18.3.6    Reinterpreting the Principle of Least Action
Traditionally, the quantum principle of least action is stated as:
                                          Z t1
               δS[ψ] = 0, with S[ψ] =          hψ, i~∂t ψ − Ĥψidt.
                                              t0

    In the hydrodynamic formulation, this becomes a constraint on the tra-
jectory of (ρ, v) that minimizes the total action. Under our reformulation, this
same evolution arises by minimizing the Schrödinger bridge functional WSB [ρ, v].
Hence, quantum evolution naturally follows the weakness-geodesic in the quan-
tale of representational effort.

18.3.7    Conclusion
The dynamics of quantum systems, viewed through the framework of QSB , in-
stantiate the principle of wú wéi in its most literal sense: evolution that unfolds
by following the minimal path of effort through an informational and physical
landscape. This reframing preserves all empirical predictions of quantum me-
chanics while offering a new conceptual insight–that the apparent spontaneity
and elegance of quantum motion can be seen as the structural realization of
effortless action.
    The formulation presented does not alter the physical content of quantum
mechanics, but recasts its mathematical structure through a conceptual lens
that emphasizes alignment, minimal disturbance, and semantic economy.
    To sum up in more depth, what we have attempted here is:

   • A geometric reinterpretation of quantum evolution. By formulat-
     ing quantum dynamics as a geodesic in a quantale of entropic transport
     costs, we present a new way of understanding quantum motion as a path of


                                        204
     minimal representational and semantic effort. This reframing builds upon
     the Schrödinger bridge formalism, which naturally unites kinetic cost and
     information-theoretic divergence into a single functional.
   • A formal model of wú wéi in physical systems. Wú wéi has often
     been invoked metaphorically to describe spontaneous or harmonious dy-
     namics in natural systems. Here, we offer a precise mathematical model
     of this principle: action as geodesic motion in a quantale whose elements
     encode semantic transformation costs. The system flows in the direction
     that requires the least deviation from its internal structure and background
     evolution.

   • Integration with semantic and cognitive theory. The Schrödinger
     bridge cost functionals are widely used in machine learning, information
     geometry, and probabilistic inference. By interpreting quantum dynamics
     within this same framework, we suggest a unification of physical and cog-
     nitive models under the principle of representational efficiency. Quantum
     evolution becomes a special case of a more general theory of structurally
     constrained transformation.
   • Extension of the quantale theory of weakness. Our proposal gener-
     alizes earlier work in which quantales of representational weakness mod-
     eled symbolic and algorithmic effort. The Schrödinger bridge quantale
     extends this idea to smooth, continuous probabilistic systems, opening a
     path toward unifying discrete and continuous notions of simplicity, effort,
     and semantic flow.

    Putting these pieces together, we have sketched out here a bridge between
physical theory and conceptual philosophy, grounded in a rigorous geometric and
categorical formalism. By understanding quantum evolution as motion along
a Schrödinger bridge geodesic, we situate the behavior of fundamental systems
within a broader space of meaning: one in which the path of least informational
resistance corresponds to action that is spontaneous, efficient, and harmonized
with structure.
    In this sense, we suggest, wú wéi can be considered as not merely a deep
poetic metaphor, but also mathematically realizable principle: a special case of
a deep pattern that unites the dynamics of motion, thought, and change.


19     A Weakness-Based Model of ”Broad Appeal”
       in Aesthetics
One issue that arises frequency in the application of AI methods to creative
arts is the difficulty of formulating ”aesthetic quality” as a goal for an AI sys-
tem to pursue as it explores different potential creations. Toward this end, we
present here a simple model of ”aesthetic quality” via articulating a variation of
compression-based theories of aesthetics in the context of weakness measures.


                                       205
19.1    A Brief History of Mathematical Theories of Aes-
        thetics
The attempt to capture the core of aesthetics in mathematical formulations has
a significant history, for instance:

Fechner’s Psychophysics (1860s) Gustav Fechner introduced a quantita-
tive approach to beauty by relating stimulus intensity to subjective sensation,
laying groundwork for empirical aesthetics.

The Golden Ratio (Late 19th-Early 20th C.) Artists        √
                                                                and mathemati-
                                                        1+ 5
cians observed the aesthetic appeal of the ratio ϕ = 2 in nature and art;
speculation on its universality influenced later formal models.

Birkhoff ’s Aesthetic Measure (1933)           George Birkhoff proposed

                                         O
                                   M=      ,
                                         C
where M is aesthetic measure, O is the number of ”distinctive” elements (order)
and C is complexity, initiating explicit formulas for beauty.
                                                                P
Information Theory (1940s-1950s) Shannon’s entropy H = − i pi log pi
was applied to pattern and surprise in art, suggesting that moderate unpre-
dictability maximizes aesthetic interest.

 Algorithmic Complexity (1960s-1970s) Solomonoff, Kolmogorov and
Chaitin formalized description length K(x) of a string x, inspiring the view
that beauty correlates with low Kolmogorov complexity relative to prior expo-
sure.

Compression and Curiosity Models (1990s-2000s) Schmidhuber’s frame-
work [Sch97] treats aesthetic reward as compression gain

                          r(x) ∝ Lold (x) − Lnew (x),

emphasizing learning progress.

Recent Directions (2010s-Present)

   • Hybrid Models: Combining MDL with human preference distributions.
   • Deep-Learning Metrics: Neural aesthetic predictors trained on large image
     datasets.




                                     206
19.2     Schmidhuber’s Compression Theory of Aesthetics
Let a data sequence (e.g. an artwork, sensory input) be denoted by x. A com-
pression algorithm (or model) C assigns a code length LC (x). Schmidhuber’s
core ideas are:

   • Beauty as Learnable Regularity. An input x is beautiful if the ob-
     server’s internal compressor can find a shorter description of it than pre-
     viously:
                          ∆LC = Lold          new
                                   C (x) − LC (x) > 0.

   • Aesthetic Reward. The subjective reward r for perceiving x is propor-
     tional to the compression gain:

                                  r(x) ∝ ∆LC (x).

   • Curiosity and Boredom. Positive reward arises from discovering new
     compressible structure; when no further compression gain is possible, re-
     ward vanishes and one becomes bored.
   • Temporal Dynamics. In an ongoing sequence x1:t , the instantaneous
     aesthetic value can be measured by the second derivative of code length:
                                                                  
                   rt ≈ LC (x1:t−1 ) − 2 LC (x1:t ) + LC (x1:t+1 ) .

Looks’ Critique Moshe Looks [Loo10] identified vulnerabilities in raw compression-
based reward schemes:

   • PRNG-Seed Exploit. Arbitrary pseudorandom bit-strings can yield
     spurious ”compression gain” if the compressor is unconstrained, thus gam-
     ing the reward.
   • Unbounded Search-Space. Without a fixed, finite code-vocabulary,
     the compressor can invent ad hoc codewords, breaking the intended link
     between ”beauty” and genuinely simple regularities.
   • Need for Fixing Both Cost and Language. To avoid gaming, one
     must:
       1. Restrict to a finite, prefix-free codebook L.
       2. Use a fixed MDL cost measure within that codebook.

19.3    Weakness-Based Aesthetic Framework
Incorporating the essence of Schmidhuber’s aesthetic theory plus the insights
from Moshe Looks’s critique, present a categorical ”aesthetic-weakness” frame-
work in which compression-based beauty arises via the Bennett-MDL connec-
tion:


                                      207
1. Aesthetic MDL via a Finite Vocabulary
   Aesthetic Language L. Fix a finite, prefix-free ”aesthetic vocabulary” L
   (e.g. the set of musical motifs, color primitives, melodic transformations)
   that is dead-code-eliminated.
   MDL Complexity. For any artwork a ∈ A, let

                   CL (a) = min{|p| | p ∈ L∗ , p generates a}.

  By Bennett’s thesis, within any such prefix-free language L, the weakness
  of the subobject {a} obeys

                               wL (a) ∝ 2− CL (a) ,

  up to a constant normalization factor.
2. Blending MDL-Weakness with Mind-Average
   Rather than setting
                       app(m, a) ≡ 2− CL (a) ,
  (which would define beauty as simplicity), we instead combine two sources
  of weakness:

     • Structural weakness wL (a) from MDL in L.
     • Perspective weakness wM (a) from averaging human appreciation:
                                               
                       wM (a) = Em∼µ app(m, a) .

  A simple unifying form is to take a product prior:

                  w(a) = wL (a)β × wM (a) 1−β ,       0 ≤ β ≤ 1.

  Here β lets you interpolate between pure MDL-weakness and pure population-
  average weakness.
3. Surprise and Temporal Aesthetics
   For a sequence a1:n , define

                     w(a1:n ) = wL (a1:n )β × wM (a1:n ) 1−β .

  Then the per-step change

                           ∆w = w(a1:n+1 ) − w(a1:n )

  captures ”surprising fulfillment” when ∆w > 0 (new structure is both
  MDL-cheap and widely appreciated), and ”tension” when ∆w < 0.
4. Categorical Weakness Perspective
   Working again in the quantale (V, ⊗, ⊕) = ([0, 1], ×, +), we can view each
   of


                                    208
         • the subobject HL ⊆ A (the ”MDL-weakness relation”),
         • the subobject HM ⊆ M × A (the ”appreciation relation”),
       as inducing enriched-category weakness measures. The combined prior
       w(a) is then simply the tensor product of those two weakness maps in the
       category of enriched presheaves.

  5. Why This Resolves Looks’s Objection
     Looks’s PRNG-seed counterexample shows that raw compression progress
     can be gamed by arbitrary pseudorandom structures unless one fixes both
     (a) the cost measure (MDL in L) and (b) the search-space (finite, dead-
     code-free). By:

         • Restricting to a finite, prefix-free aesthetic vocabulary L,
         • Using Bennett’s equivalence to identify wL (a) ∼ 2− CL (a) ,
         • Combining it multiplicatively with human-average weakness wM (a),
       we guarantee that:

         • Pathological pseudo-compression (e.g. PRNG seeds) carry zero or
           exponentially small wL .
         • Genuine aesthetic richness (patterns humans actually like) get boosted
           both by being compressible in L and by having high wM .

  6. Summary
     A refined ”weakness-based” aesthetics theory:
         • Leverages MDL via Bennett’s prefix-free result, not by redefining
           appreciation as simplicity.
         • Maintains the original categorical notion of weakness averaged over
           minds.
         • Unifies compression-, appreciation-, and surprise-based accounts of
           beauty in a single, pluggable framework.

19.4     Neural-symbolic Music Generation via Blended Aes-
         thetic Fitness
To illustrate how these ideas might play out in a particular context, we elaborate
how they might be used to guide aesthetics-driven automated music generation.

19.4.1    System Overview
   • Symbolic MDL Component. A prefix-free music grammar L of prim-
     itives and operators. For any candidate piece a, define

                                   CL (a) = min∗ |p|.
                                             p∈L



                                       209
   • Neural Appreciation Component. A neural model N trained on a
     target corpus to predict an appreciation score
                               app(a) = N (a) ∈ [0, 1].

   • Blended Aesthetic Fitness. Define
                       wL (a) = 2− CL (a) ,   wN (a) = app(a),
     and combine via
                    fitness(a) = wL (a)β wN (a) 1−β ,     0 ≤ β ≤ 1.

19.4.2   Symbolic MDL Engine
  1. Define primitives (motifs, chords, rhythms) and operators (transpose, in-
     vert, concatenate).
  2. Assign prefix-free bit-codes to each symbol.
  3. Parse or synthesize a to find minimal code p, yielding CL (a).

19.4.3   Neural Appreciation Model
   • Choose architecture (e.g. Transformer over MIDI tokens, CNN over pi-
     anoroll).
   • Train on labeled corpus to minimize prediction error for human ratings.
   • Use N (a) at inference for app(a).

19.4.4   Evolutionary Programming with EDA
  1. Initialize population P of random programs p ∈ L∗ .
  2. For each p, render ap , compute fitness(ap ).
  3. Select top N , fit a probabilistic model D over their code-sequences.
  4. Sample new population P 0 ∼ D.
  5. Repeat until convergence or target quality achieved.

19.4.5   Extensions
   • Reinforcement Learning: Treat generation as a sequential decision
     process with reward = fitness(a).
   • MCTS: Build compositions operator-by-operator, using UCT guided by
     fitness.
   • Hierarchical Generation: First evolve high-level form (e.g. chord pro-
     gression), then refine details.


                                      210
20     Measuring the Integrated-Information Aspect
       of Consciousness via Weakness
Integrated Information Theory (IIT), proposed by Giulio Tononi [Ton04], seeks
to quantify consciousness via the scalar Φ, which measures how much informa-
tion a system generates as a unified whole beyond its parts. Formally, for a
system S in state s,                              
                          Φ(S) = min I(S) − Iπ (S) ,
                                   π

where I(S) is the mutual information between past and future states of S, Iπ (S)
is the same for the system partitioned according to π, and the minimum is taken
over all bipartitions (the Minimum Information Partition, MIP).
    Criticisms leveled against this approach to consciousness include:

   • Computational Intractability. Exact evaluation of Φ requires search-
     ing over exponentially many partitions and computing high-dimensional
     probability distributions.

   • Ambiguous Partitioning. Different choices of system elements or time’scales
     yield widely varying Φ values, undermining robustness.
   • Empirical Validation. Correlations between Φ and consciousness levels
     remain limited and sometimes inconsistent across experimental paradigms.

   • Theoretical Assumptions. IIT’s axiomatic derivation (e.g. existence,
     composition, exclusion) has been challenged as conceptually arbitrary
     rather than empirically mandated.
   • Conceptual Inadequacy. IIT is what many consciousness theorists
     would call a ”potential correlate of consciousness” rather than, as Tononi
     sometimes argues, the essence of consciousness itself. Tononi somewhat
     bypasses Chalmers’ ”hard problem of consciousness” – connecting subjec-
     tive experience with objective observations – in a way that some consider
     a feature and others a bug

 Φ as an Interesting Correlate While Φ offers an intriguing quantitative
correlate of certain conscious states – capturing integration and differentiation
of neural activity – my own perspective (as presented e.g. in [Goe14]) is that it
should be viewed as one among many metrics (e.g. perturbational complexity,
neural synchrony, global workspace measures). Its computational and concep-
tual limitations suggest that Φ is not a fundamental definition of consciousness,
but rather a useful tool for probing aspects of integrated information in complex
systems.




                                       211
20.1     Weakness-Based Integrated Information
20.1.1    Clarity via Weakness
Let a system S exhibit behavior B. An explanation E is a causal model mapping
interventions on S to outcomes B. Define the weakness w(E) ∈ (0, 1] as our
simplicity measure of E. The clarity of E is
                             Cl(E) = − log w(E).

20.1.2    Whole vs. Part Explanations
Let
                             ES = arg max w(E)
                                        E∈ES
be the clearest explanation of B using the whole system. For any partition
π = {P1 , . . . , Pk } of S’s parts, let
                            EPj = arg max w(E)
                                        E∈EPj

be the clearest explanation of B using only part Pj . Then the total clarity of
the part-based explanations is
                                       k
                                       X           
                             Cl(π) =         Cl EPj .
                                       j=1


20.1.3    Defining Weakness-Based Integrated Information
Define integrated information for partition π by
                          Φw (π) = Cl(π) − Cl(ES ).
Analogous to Tononi’s minimum information partition (MIP), set
                             Φw (S) = min Φw (π).
                                         π

A large positive Φw (S)  0 indicates that the whole-system explanation is
significantly clearer (simpler) than any explanation constructed by explaining
the parts separately.

20.2     Recovering Tononi’s Φ as a Special Case of Weakness-
         Based Integrated Information
Our weakness-based framework assigns to any causal explanation E a weakness
w(E) ∈ (0, 1], with greater weakness corresponding to less simplicity. To recover
Tononi’s measure in this context, we begin with the assumption that we can
define the clarity of E by
                             Cl(E) = − log w(E),
so that more simple explanations (smaller w) have higher clarity.


                                       212
20.2.1     Choosing Weakness via Mutual Information
To connect with Tononi’s integrated information, we then make the special-case
choice

                                 winfo (E) = 2− I(E) ,
where I(E) denotes the mutual information captured by explanation E (e.g.
between past and future states of the system). Under this definition, we can
conceive clarity as
                      Cl(E) = − log 2−I(E) = I(E).
                                            

Thus, the clarity of any explanation becomes exactly its mutual information.

A Fuller Concept of Clarity Now, above we introduced a richer, causal
clarity that rewards not only simplicity but also counterfactual causal strength.
I.e. writing

            s(E) = causal strength of E,        w(E) = weakness of E,

we defined the causal clarity as, for example,
                          s(E)
         Clcausal (E) =           or   Clcausal (E) = log s(E) − log w(E).
                          w(E)
In this framework, the Integrated-Information clarity ClΦ is recovered as the
special case s(E) = 1 (or a constant) for all explanations:

        ClΦ (E) = log(1) − log w(E) = − log w(E) = Clcausal (E) s(E)=1 .

Thus:
   • Simplicity-only clarity ClΦ measures how compact an explanation is,
     ignoring its causal efficacy.
   • Causal clarity Clcausal balances Occam simplicity against the actual
     strength of the causal link.
   For reconstituting Tononi’s Φ one can ignore these subtleties and stick with
mutual information, but we shall see that taking fuller account of the natural
causality yields a potentially improved version of Φ. But for starters let’s stick
with Tononi-style clarity.

20.2.2     Whole-system vs. Part-Based Clarity
Let S be the full system in state s. In Tononi’s approach, the best explanation
using the entire system achieves clarity

                                  Cl(ES ) = I(S),

where I(S) is the mutual information of the whole.


                                         213
   Now consider a partition π = {P1 , . . . , Pk } of S into disjoint parts. Each
part Pj has its own optimal explanation EPj with clarity

                                 Cl(EPj ) = I(Pj ).

Since these part-explanations are combined in the simplest additive way, the
total clarity for the partition is
                                 k
                                 X                  k
                                                    X
                       Cl(π) =         Cl(EPj ) =         I(Pj ).
                                 j=1                j=1


20.2.3    Weakness-Based Integrated Information
We define weakness-based integrated information for partition π, Tononi-style,
as the difference between whole-system clarity and part-based clarity:
                                                              k
                                                              X
                Φw (π) = Cl(ES ) − Cl(π) = I(S) −                   I(Pj ).
                                                              j=1

Finally, by taking the minimum over all possible partitions (the analog of
Tononi’s MIP), we recover:
                                            X        
            Φw (S) = min Φw (π) = min I(S) −    I(Pj ) = Φ(S),
                       π                π
                                                      j

which is precisely Tononi’s original Φ.
   This derivation shows that Tononi’s Φ arises when one defines weakness in
terms of mutual information. Our general weakness-based framework, however,
permits other choices of w(E) grounded in different simplicity measures, yielding
a more flexible and theoretically robust family of ”Φ-like” metrics.

20.3     Causal Φ
We have seen that Tononi’s Φ-clarity is the special case of causal clarity when
causal strength is uniform. One can define an improved integrated information
metric by replacing ClΦ with Clcausal in the usual partition-vs-whole difference:
                                               X
                Φcausal = Clcausal (Ewhole ) −     Clcausal (EP ).
                                               parts P

This generalizes Tononi’s original Φ by rewarding not only simplicity but also
genuine causal coherence of the integrated explanation.

20.4     Observer-Relative Integrated Information and Quan-
         tum Graphtropy
So far, following Tononi, we have been considering integrated information Φ
as an objective measure of how much a system’s joint states fail to factorize


                                         214
across a bipartition. Here we define a perspectival Φ – denoted ΦO (S) – which
quantifies how much integrated information system S exhibits for a particular
observer O. We then show how this observer-relative Φ connects to quantum Φ,
distinction graphs and graphtropy, and discuss applications in quantum biology
and future quantum AI.

20.4.1    Defining Perspectival Φ
Let S be a system partitioned into subsystems A and B. Observer O is char-
acterized by a (possibly weak or coarse-grained) measurement map fO on the
state space of S, inducing distinction graphs:

                                 GA    B    AB
                                  O , GO , GO ,

where nodes represent outcome-classes and edges indicate pairs of outcomes that
O cannot reliably distinguish. Denote by h(G) ∈ [0, 1] the graphtropy (fraction
of distinguished pairs) of graph G. Then we define

                   ΦO (S) = h GA            B           AB
                                                         
                                  O + h GO − h GO            .

Equivalently, in terms of weakness w(G) = 1 − h(G),

                    ΦO (S) = w GAB   − w GA         B
                                                   
                                 O          O + w GO .

Thus ΦO measures the excess missing distinctions that appear only when O
views A and B jointly.

20.5      Relation to Perspectival-Causal Φ
The ”perspectival ΦO ” defined via graphtropy is in fact an instance of our
general causal Φ when one chooses a particular causal-strength function.
    To see the connection, observe that letting each explanation EX for subsys-
tem X correspond to the distinction-graph GX O , and choosing the causal-strength
factor
                        s(EX ) = 1 − h(GX             X
                                           O ) = w(GO ),

we have

    Clcausal (EX ) = log s(EX ) − log w(EX ) = log w(GX            X
                                                      O ) − log w(GO ) = 0.

This degenerate choice gives zero clarity for each part, but the whole explanation
EAB may have strictly positive causal clarity if its graphtropy differs from the
sum of the parts. More meaningfully, one can choose

                                 s(EX ) = h(GX
                                             O)

so that causal clarity becomes

                 Clcausal (EX ) = log h(GX                X
                                                             
                                         O ) − log 1 − h(GO ) .


                                       215
Plugging into the general Φcausal definition yields
                                              X 
  Φcausal = log h(GAB              AB
                                                    log h(GX            X
                                                                         
                    O ) − log w(GO ) −                     O ) − log w(GO ) ,
                                             X∈{A,B}

which up-weights integrated distinctions relative to the parts. In the limit of
small weakness (high distinction), this reduces to the linear combination of
graphtropies defining ΦO .

Summary Thus the perspectival graphtropy-based ΦO is a special case of our
general causal-Φ framework, corresponding to particular choices of the causal
strength function s(E). More elaborate causal-clarity measures (combining both
h and w nonlinearly) will yield refined versions of ΦO that more sharply distin-
guish genuine multi-part causal integration.

20.5.1   Connection to Quantum Integrated Information
In quantum IIT one typically uses von Neumann entropies and mutual informa-
tion on ρAB . To make Φ operationally perspectival, let O implement a POVM
{Ei } on HA and {Fj } on HB . Then:

   • The ”distinction weight” between outcomes i and i0 in A is Tr(ρA Ei Ei0 ),
     and similarly for B.
   • One builds quantum distinction graphs GA    B    AB
                                            O , GO , GO  whose edges link
     sufficiently overlapping effects.

   • The resulting graphtropies hA    B    AB
                                 Q , hQ , hQ  feed into ΦO (S) = hA    B
                                                                  Q + hQ −
     hAB
      Q , yielding a measurement-based quantum Φ that respects O’s actual
     measurement capabilities.

    In the limit of a tomographically complete POVM and fine-grained distinc-
tion threshold, ΦO (S) converges to the standard quantum IIT Φ.

20.5.2   Relation to Distinction Graphs and Graphtropy
Graphtropy h(G) quantifies the fraction of distinctions an observer makes among
pairs of states. Weakness w(G) = 1 − h(G) is the fraction of pairs left indistinct.
Perspectival ΦO is thus the difference between the joint weakness and the sum
of the parts’ weaknesses:

                    ΦO (S) = w(GAB             A         B
                                                           
                                  O ) − w(GO ) + w(GO ) .

This directly ties integrated information to the observer-indexed notion of ”miss-
ing distinctions” in a unified categorical framework.




                                       216
20.5.3    Potential Applications
The distinction between standard and perspectival integrated information may
seem a bit obscure, but there are practical cases where this will make a huge
difference.
    In quantum biology settings – e.g. photosynthetic complexes or avian magne-
toreceptors, but there are almost surely many further examples not yet charted
out, including potentially some closer to the core of human cognition – the ”ob-
server” is the protein environment or reaction center, implementing a coarse
POVM on the excitonic Hilbert space. Computing ΦO via graphtropy could
reveal how ”integrated” the quantum coherence is from the perspective of the
actual biological detector.
    Similarly, for an AI agent built on quantum hardware – as will almost surely
be the case a decade or so from now – the agent’s internal measurement devices
define its distinction graphs. A perspectival ΦO then quantifies the system’s
self-awareness or integrated information relative to its own sensors and control
channels, guiding architectural choices for consciousness-like processing.
    —
    By making the measurement lens O explicit and using graphtropy, we obtain
a fully perspectival yet operationally quantum extension of integrated informa-
tion, applicable to both biological and artificial quantum systems.


Part IV
Information & Pattern
21       McBride Derivatives of Weakness and Gra-
         dient Descent
In this chapter we build out a little more mathematical tooling regarding cate-
gorial weakness measures – explaining how to do ”differential calculus” style AI
using weakness, via introducing the McBride derivative [McB01], an abstract
categorial variation on the traditional differential-calculus concept.

21.1     McBride Derivative of Weakness
Conceptually, the McBride derivative of a polynomial functor captures the type
of ”one-hole contexts” in its values. The beautiful thing here is that this deriva-
tive satisfies the same core algebraic identities as the standard derivative in the
calculus of numerical functions [McB01].
    Since the weakness measure is a polynomial-style functor
                                         M
                             w(H) =            φ(u, v),
                                       (u,v)∈H




                                       217
we can define its derivative to record, for each link in the relation, the residual
context obtained by removing that link.

Definition of the Derivative Context Functor Given a relation H ⊆
U × U , define the weakness-derivative context set
                               a                          
                   Dw (H) =            (u, v), H \ {(u, v)} .
                              (u,v)∈H


Each element of Dw (H) is a pair consisting of a ”hole” (u, v) and the remaining
context H \ {(u, v)}. Thus Dw generalizes the zipper construction to weakness
subobjects.

Weighted Detail Coefficients as Derivative Values We further decorate
each context by the removed link’s weight φ(u, v). Define the detail-derivative
map                                                
             dw : Dw (H) −→ V , dw (u, v), C = φ(u, v).
When aggregated via the quantale join ⊕, this recovers the original weakness:
                  M                 M
                        dw (c) =          φ(u, v) = w(H).
                   c∈Dw (H)             (u,v)∈H


General vs. Domain-Specific Instantiations The above construction is
fully general for any quantale-enriched subobject functor. In particular:

   • Next-Token Prediction: Let H be the set of observed (i − 1)-gram con-
     texts extended by one token. Then Dw (H) enumerates all pairs (c, t)
     with context-token ”holes” and the remaining pairs. The derivative value
     φ(c, t) = Pemp (c, t) gives the probability mass of each one-hole event.

   • Humanoid Robotics: Let H be the kernel relation of a state-abstraction
     φi . Then Dw (H) lists each abstracted link (s, t) removed, together with
     the coarser context. The derivative value φ(s, t) can be chosen as the
     occupancy weight π ∗ (s)π ∗ (t) or the Bellman-error contribution of that
     transition.

Interpretation and Use The McBride derivative of weakness yields a first-
order sensitivity analysis: each one-hole context shows how the global weakness
would change if a single link were removed. Aggregating these contexts and
weights can guide greedy refinement (by picking holes with largest φ(u, v)) or
support higher-order transforms (by iterating the derivative to obtain ”two-
hole” contexts).
   Among other applications, this derivative construction opens avenues for:

   • Zipper-style traversals of relational models, enabling local edits and incre-
     mental updates.


                                         218
   • Higher-order pattern mining, where repeated derivatives reveal multi-link
     interactions.
   • Formal sensitivity measures in quantum or probabilistic settings, by track-
     ing how each measurement context contributes to overall weakness.

21.2    Gradient Descent for Weak Models via McBride Deriva-
        tive
One of the valuable things we can do with derivatives is optimized functions
via gradient descent. Suppose, for instance, we are seeking models that cover
observed data while remaining as simple (weak) as possible. By introducing
a parametrized family of ”soft” models and using the McBride derivative–
enumerating one-hole contexts–as a sensitivity measure, we can apply gradient
descent to balance data fidelity against weakness.

Parameterized Soft Models Let the dataset be a relation D ⊆ U × U .
Define a family of soft inclusion probabilities

                      pθ (u, v) ∈ [0, 1]   for each (u, v) ∈ U × U,

where θ ∈ Θ are model parameters. The ”hard” model is the support of pθ
above a threshold, but we optimize in the continuous regime.

Objective Function We combine a fidelity term `(θ) that penalizes failure
to explain data with a weakness regularizer:
                 X                 2           X
         `(θ) =        1 − pθ (u, v) ,    w(θ) =   φ(u, v) pθ (u, v),
                   (u,v)∈D                               (u,v)


where φ(u, v) = µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) is the weight of link (u, v). The total loss is

                                 L(θ) = `(θ) + λ w(θ).

McBride Derivative as Gradient The McBride derivative identifies each
link’s contribution to weakness. Differentiating,
            X                                      X            
∇θ w(θ) =       φ(u, v) ∇θ pθ (u, v), ∇θ `(θ) = −2    1−pθ (u, v) ∇θ pθ (u, v).
           (u,v)                                       (u,v)∈D

Thus
                              ∇θ L = ∇θ `(θ) + λ ∇θ w(θ),
which drives parameters to explain data while minimizing aggregate link weights.




                                            219
Abstract vs. Continuous Smoothness In practical settings, θ may lie in
Rd and pθ be a sigmoid or softmax, yielding standard gradients and backpropa-
gation. Alternatively, one can remain in a purely categorical framework by en-
dowing the parameter space Θ with a differential-category or tangent-category
structure. There, the McBride derivative arises as a natural transformation
DF : Θ → T Θ, and ”gradient descent” is implemented via the category’s tan-
gent operations rather than numeric updates.

Algorithmic Sketch
  1. Initialize θ (either in Rd or an abstract parameter object).
  2. Repeat until convergence:
        • Compute ∇θ ` and ∇θ w via McBride derivative.
        • Update θ ← θ − η ∇θ L (or apply tangent-category step).
  3. Extract a discrete weak model by thresholding or taking the coKleisli
     counit.
  This approach provides a principled, differentiable path to discovering weak
models across both neural and symbolic domains.

21.3    Differentiable Proof Search via McBride Derivative
Conceptually, we treat a proof as a ”weak model” of a dataset: it must cover
observed facts while remaining as simple (weak) as possible. By endowing each
inference step with a continuous activation parameter, we obtain a soft proof
whose coverage and simplicity are both differentiable. We then apply gradi-
ent descent on a composite loss that balances data fidelity against a weakness
penalty, using the McBride derivative to compute exact link-wise sensitivities.

Soft Proof Parameterization Let the dataset be a set of fact pairs

                                  D ⊆ U × U.

A candidate proof comprises m inference steps, indexed by i = 1, . . . , m. Intro-
duce parameters
                         θ = (θ1 , . . . , θm ) ∈ [0, 1]m
so that step i is ”active” with strength θi . Define the soft coverage probability
for each fact (u, v) by
                                             Y
                    pθ (u, v) = 1 −                    (1 − θi ),
                                    i:(u,v) supported by i

which approaches 1 whenever any supporting step is active.



                                       220
Loss Function We combine a fidelity term that penalizes uncovered data
with a weakness regularizer:
                                                                    m
                         X              2                         X
              `(θ) =        1 − pθ (u, v) ,                w(θ) =         θ i φi ,
                       (u,v)∈D                                      i=1


where φi > 0 measures the complexity of step i. The total loss is

                                 L(θ) = `(θ) + λ w(θ).

McBride Derivative as Gradient The McBride derivative enumerates one-
hole contexts, yielding the gradient contributions from each step:
       ∂`                 X                               Y                  ∂w
           = −2                           1 − pθ (u, v)      (1 − θj ),            = φi .
       ∂θi                                                                     ∂θi
                  (u,v)∈D: i∈supp(u,v)                     j6=i


Thus
                                  ∇θ L = ∇θ ` + λ φ,
which drives activation of proof steps that most improve coverage per unit com-
plexity.

Continuous vs. Categorical Smoothness In practice, θ may reside in Rm
with pθ implemented by sigmoids or softmax, allowing standard backpropaga-
tion. Alternatively, one can work in a purely categorical setting by choosing Θ
as a differential category (or tangent category) and interpreting the McBride
derivative as a natural transformation DF : Θ → T Θ. Updates then proceed
via tangent-category operations rather than numeric subtraction.

Algorithmic Sketch
  1. Initialize θ uniformly small.

  2. Repeat until convergence:
        • Compute ∇θ ` and ∇θ w via the McBride derivative.
        • Update θ ← θ − η ∇θ L (or apply the tangent step).
  3. Extract a discrete proof by selecting steps with θi above a threshold.

   This framework yields a principled, differentiable proof search that finds
weak models of data by following the McBride gradient toward simple yet com-
prehensive explanations.




                                            221
21.4     Gradient Descent for Neural-Symbolic Hierarchical
         Weak Models
Because we are not yet far enough out on a speculative limb here, it seems nat-
ural to take the ”differential calculus of weakness” one step further and explore
how information geometry and natural gradients might fit into the picture. It
would seem the use of gradients on the symbolic side gives interesting poten-
tials for fusing together neural and symbolic learning more closely. For instance,
in a sequence prediction context, one might seek to train a hybrid model that
combines a symbolic multiresolution weakness transform with a neural Wave-
Former, so that both the symbolic abstractions and the neural DWT subbands
are learned jointly to explain data with minimal complexity. We introduce
continuous parameters for both the symbolic and neural sides and derive a uni-
fied gradient descent that uses the McBride derivative to guide updates in the
symbolic hierarchy and standard backpropagation for the neural network.

Model Parameterization Let the input dataset be D. We construct:
   • A symbolic chain of soft subobjects D0sym ⊇ D1sym ⊇ · · · ⊇ DN     sym
                                                                            , each
                                                              sym
     governed by parameters α = (α1 , . . . , αN ). We write pi (u, v; αi ) ∈ [0, 1]
     for the soft inclusion of link (u, v) at level i.
   • A neural WaveFormer with parameters β, which applies a discrete wavelet
     transform to features, processes low- and high-frequency bands via multi-
     head attention, and reconstructs via an inverse DWT.

  1. Symbolic fidelity and weakness:

     squared reconstruction:
                                 N

21.4.1   Natural Gradient Search via McBride Derivative and Infor-
         mation Geometry
Conceptually, natural gradient methods adjust parameters along the steepest
descent direction measured not in Euclidean space but in a Riemannian metric
that reflects the model’s sensitivity. By combining the McBride derivative of
the weakness functor with an information-geometric metric on the parameter
manifold, we obtain ”Occam-aware” natural gradients that respect both data
fidelity and simplicity.

Parameterized Weak Models and Loss Let D be the dataset and pθ (u, v) ∈
[0, 1] a soft model of links (u, v), governed by parameters θ ∈ Θ. Define the com-
posite loss
                          X                         X
                L(θ) =          [1 − pθ (u, v)]2 +λ   φ(u, v) pθ (u, v),
                        (u,v)∈D                      (u,v)
                        |         {z             }   |             {z      }
                                  `(θ)                         w(θ)

where φ(u, v) = µ(u) ⊗ µ(v) weights each link. Its gradient is
                  X                                X
       ∇i L = −2      1 − pθ (u, v) ∂i pθ (u, v) + λ   φ(u, v) ∂i pθ (u, v),
                    D                                        u,v

and the McBride derivative identifies each ∂i pθ (u, v) term.


                                             223
Fisher-Metric Natural Gradient If pθ defines a probability distribution
over links, we can endow Θ with the Fisher information metric
                          X
                Gij (θ) =   pθ (u, v) ∂i log pθ (u, v) ∂j log pθ (u, v).
                           u,v

The natural gradient update is

                         θ(t+1) = θ(t) − η G(θ)−1 ∇L(θ).

This rescales the McBride-derived gradient by the local curvature of the model
manifold, prioritizing moves that change the model less in information-theoretic
terms.

Wasserstein-Metric Natural Gradient Alternatively, if pθ lives on a dis-
crete space with an underlying cost metric d((u, v), (u0 , v 0 )), one can equip Θ
with the 2-Wasserstein metric tensor [CL20]. Its approximate Riemannian met-
ric is               XX
                           W e(u,v) , e(u0 ,v0 ) ∂i pθ (u, v) ∂j pθ (u0 , v 0 ),
                                                
           Gij (θ) =
                     u,v u0 ,v 0

where W is the discrete 2-Wasserstein inner product. The corresponding natural
gradient step
                        θ(t+1) = θ(t) − η G(θ)−1 ∇L(θ)
yields updates that move pθ along geodesics in probability-mass transport, re-
specting the ”mass-movement” cost structure.

Implementation Notes Both variants require efficient computation of G(θ)−1 ∇L.
In practice one uses low-rank approximations or Krylov solvers for Fisher matri-
ces, and Sinkhorn-based approximations for Wasserstein metrics. The McBride
derivative supplies the exact ∂i pθ factors needed for either geometry, unifying
Occam-driven simplicity with principled natural-gradient optimization.

21.4.2   Curvature in the One-Hole-Context Manifold
It’s perhaps worth pausing a moment to take a conceptual view of some of this
crunchy math. In particular, what are we actually doing when we’re taking
curvature in the space of data structures, building onto the McBride derivative
and its interpretation of derivatives as sets of one-hole contexts?
    Conceptually, curvature measures how the sensitivity of weakness to single-
link edits (the McBride derivative) itself changes as we move through the model
space. Just as the Hessian in classical optimization captures second-order effects
of gradient variation, curvature in the one-hole-context manifold captures non-
additive interactions between different ”holes” (links).




                                       224
Second-Order Sensitivity Let pθ (u, v) be our soft inclusion probability and
write the McBride derivative as the vector of partials ∂i pθ (u, v). The curvature
tensor
                                        ∂ 2 w(θ)
                                 Hijk =
                                        ∂θj ∂θi
measures how the contribution of hole (ui , vi ) to weakness changes when we also
perturb parameters in direction j. Large entries Hijk indicate that removing
one link dramatically alters the importance of another.

Flat vs. High Curvature If curvature is near zero, the one-hole contexts
behave independently and first-order McBride updates remain accurate over a
region. High curvature signals strong interactions: targeting one link shifts the
sensitivity landscape for others, necessitating smaller steps or geometry-aware
updates.

Implications for Update Rules Regions of high curvature warn against
naive Euclidean gradient steps. By measuring curvature in the same parameter
manifold as the McBride derivative, we know when to switch from first-order
McBride descent to natural-gradient or trust-region methods that respect the
underlying geometry of link-context interactions.


22     Quantale Pattern Theory
The cognitive theory presented in [Goe21a] and earlier works, and instantiated
concretely in the Hyperon AGI design, is founded on the notion that one can
get a lot of mileage from viewing cognitive structure and dynamics in terms of
patterns. A formal definition of pattern has been presented in [Goe20b] and
elsewhere and has been used as motivation for multiple aspects of the Hyperon
system. Here we port this definition of pattern into the quantale setting and
show that, in this context, it can very conveniently be interrogated regarding
its properties, and then applied directly in various practical cases.

22.1    Quantale Patterns
Let (V, ⊗, ⊕, e) be a commutative quantale and X a universe of ”elements.” A
V -predicate is a function
                                m : X → V.
Given a valuation ϕ : X → V , the weakness of m is defined by joining over all
points:                           M               
                       wϕ (m) =       ϕ(x) ⊗ m(x) .
                                    x∈X


Composite Weakness         Fix two predicates y, z : X → V . Define their combi-
national weakness by
                          h(y, z) = wϕ (y) ⊗ wϕ (z).


                                       225
Pattern Intensity Since V is a quantale, it admits a residuated implication
( satisfying
                   a ⊗ b ≤ c ⇐⇒ b ≤ a ( c.
We then set the pattern intensity of the pair (y, z) in the context m to be
                          Iy,z (m) = h(y, z) ( wϕ (m).
We declare (y, z) to be a pattern in m exactly when
                                  Iy,z (m) ≥ e.

Relation to Simplicity-Theory Patterns This definition parallels the core
”pattern intensity” construction in the Compositional Simplicity Measure (CoSM)
framework [Goe20b], where one compares a baseline simplicity measure σ1 (m)
against a composite cost h12 (y, z) to form
                                        σ1 (m) − h12 (y, z)
                        I(y, z | m) =                       ,
                                             σ1 (m)
declaring a pattern when this ratio is positive . In our quantale-enriched version,
simplicity measures are replaced by weakness values in V , and the fraction is
captured by the quantale implication ( .

22.2     Emergent Patterns in a Quantale Framework
Let (V, ⊗, ⊕, e) be a commutative quantale, X a universe, and ϕ : X → V a
valuation. For any V -predicates y, z, m : X → V define:
                      M               
            wϕ (p) =      ϕ(x) ⊗ p(x) , h(y, z) = wϕ (y) ⊗ wϕ (z).
                      x∈X


Pattern Intensity The intensity of a candidate pattern (y, z) in context m
is
                    Iy,z (m) = h(y, z) ( wϕ (m),
where ( is the quantale-residuation.
   Likewise, the individual intensities are
           Iy (m) = wϕ (y) ( wϕ (m),        Iz (m) = wϕ (z) ( wϕ (m).

Synergy and Emergence Define the synergy measure
                                        
              σy,z (m) = Iy (m) ⊗ Iz (m) ( Iy,z (m).
We say that (y, z) is an emergent pattern in m precisely if
                                  σy,z (m) ≥ e,
i.e. when the composite pattern intensity Iy,z (m) exceeds what would be pre-
dicted by the individual intensities Iy (m) and Iz (m) alone.


                                        226
Interpretation     This captures the idea that a pattern (y, z) is emergent in m
when
                            Iy,z (m) 6≤ Iy (m) ⊗ Iz (m),
so that knowing y and z together yields strictly more ”weakness-explaining
power” than combining their separate contributions.

         Patterns

                   Iy,z (m) = Iz,y (m),         Iy,y (m) = Iy (m).

               Iy,z (m) ⊗ h(y, z) ≤ w(m) =⇒ Iy,z (m) ≤ w(m),

6. Emergence Criterion Define the synergy measure
                                         

    These identities show that pattern intensities and emergent patterns sat-
isfy elegant algebraic laws–monotonicity, symmetry, idempotence, residuation-
of-joins and tensors, and a crisp emergence criterion–in any commutative quan-

Interpretation

   • Individual meme intensities Iyq and Izr grow exponentially, reflecting viral
     replication.
   • Emergent synergy σyq ,zr decays exponentially: the co-occurrence of two
     specific memes is most informative early on, then becomes diluted as the
     overall meme pool explodes.

   • This formalism can guide strategies for meme detection and intervention:
     target combined patterns when σ is high to catch emerging trends, then
     switch to individual tracking as memes go mainstream.

22.5.3   Structuring a Meme Detection and Intervention System via
         Quantale Weakness
We work in the commutative quantale

                    V = ([0, ∞), ×, +),      ϕ(p) = 1 ∀p ∈ M,

where M is the set of tracked memes.

Meme Representation and State
   • Universe of memes: a (growing) set M of probabilistic programs.

   • Memetic strength: for each p ∈ M , let

                 mp (t) = estimated frequency of meme p at time t.

   • Early ”viral” growth assumption:

                              ṁp (t) = k mp (t),   k > 0.



                                       233
Core Weakness and Pattern Intensities
   • Total memetic weakness:
                                        X
                             W (t) =          mp (t),    Ẇ = k W.
                                        p∈M


   • Single-meme intensity: for any meme q,

                                         W (t)
                              Iq (t) =         = W (t) ∝ ekt .
                                          1

   • Joint-meme synergy: for two memes q, r,

                       W (t)                              Iq,r (t)       1
          Iq,r (t) =         = W (t),      σq,r (t) =                 =       ∝ e−kt .
                       1·1                              Iq (t) Ir (t)   W (t)

Detection Logic
   • Emergence threshold: choose τ > 0. Flag (q, r) emergent when

                            σq,r (t) > τ      ⇐⇒        W (t) < 1/τ.

   • Priority ranking: sort co-meme pairs by σq,r (t) (highest first).

Intervention Strategies
   • Early-warning alerts: trigger human or automated review when σq,r > τ .
   • Resource allocation: allocate monitoring resources to pairs with largest
     |σ̇q,r (t)|.
   • Adaptive filtering: shift from joint-pattern detection (σ) to single-meme
     volume (Iq ) as W (t) grows.

System Architecture
   • Data ingestion: real-time streams → pattern-matching → update mp (t).
   • Quantale-weakness engine: compute W (t), Iq (t), σq,r (t) on sliding win-
     dows.
   • Alert API: publish emergent patterns to dashboards or moderation pipelines.
   • Feedback loop: human labels adjust τ , seed new memes, refine pattern
     definitions.
    This design leverages the quantale-weakness and pattern-calculus to time
alerts precisely at the leading edge of meme outbreaks, allocate resources dy-
namically, and provide a principled transition as memes become widespread.

                                          235
23       Formulating Cognitive Synergy in Terms of
         Weakness
Contemporary cognitive architectures often organize knowledge into multiple
distinct memory stores: for example, a procedural-knowledge store (programs
in a domain’specific language), a declarative-knowledge store (logical state-
ments), a perceptual-knowledge store (images, video clips with algebraic com-
position), and an attentional-knowledge store (attention distributions over con-
cepts). Each such store can be modeled as a category
                              Mi ,    i = 1, . . . , n,
whose objects are memory elements and whose morphisms represent transfor-
mations (e.g. inference steps, perceptual mappings, memory lookups) endowed
with quantitative costs or reliabilities.
    ”Cognitive synergy” refers to the observation that in most complex cogni-
tive systems, carrying out sophisticated cognitive operations involves intensive
interoperation between the different memory stores and associated transforma-
tions – so that the cognitive processes associated with the different stores are
frequently exchanging intermediate representations, rather than interacting like
black boxes exchanging only final conclusions.

23.1     Weakness-Based Formalization of Cognitive Synergy
Let {Mi }ni=1 be categories (each enriched in a commutative quantale (Q, ≤
, ⊗, e)) representing distinct memory stores of a cognitive system. There is a
monoidal product
                                       On
                                 M =       Mi
                                         i=1
describing their joint use, and a category B of observable behaviors.
   A weakness functor
                                  W : C op − Q
assigns to each causal explanation E (an object or morphism in a suitable cat-
egory C) a value W (E) ∈ Q. Its clarity is
          Cl(E) = − log W (E)        (using a chosen logarithm on Q) .

23.1.1    Best Explanations over Subsets of Stores
For each subset S ⊆ {1, . . . , n}, let
                        O
               MS =            Mi and ES = arg max W (E)
                                                          E∈EMS
                        i∈S

be the clearest explanation of a given behavior b ∈ B using only the stores in S.
Denote its clarity by
                      Cl(S) = Cl(ES ) = − log W (ES ) .


                                       236
23.1.2    Cognitive Synergy Measure
Define cognitive synergy as the advantage in clarity gained by using all stores
jointly rather than any partition thereof. Let Π be the set of all partitions of
{1, . . . , n}. Then
                            h                     X      i
                     Σ = min Cl({1, . . . , n}) −   Cl(S) .
                           π∈Π
                                                        S∈π

If Σ > 0, the system achieves its clearest (lowest-weakness) explanations only
by synergistically combining multiple memory stores.

23.1.3    Theorem: Lax Monoidality Implies Synergy
Theorem. If W is lax monoidal, then for any non-trivial partition π,
                      O                                     X
    W (E{1,...,n} ) ≥   W (ES ) =⇒ Cl({1, . . . , n}) ≤         Cl(S),
                          S∈π                                        S∈π

so Σ ≥ 0. Strict inequality in the lax-monoidality law yields Σ > 0, i.e. genuine
cognitive synergy.
    This construction shows how ”cognitive synergy” emerges when the joint
use of diverse memory stores yields strictly simpler (more clear) causal explana-
tions than any separate-store combination. It generalizes our Φ-style integrated-
information measures to a multi=store, enriched-category setting.

23.2     Relationship Between Cognitive Synergy and Φw
Let {Mi }ni=1 be our n memory-store categories, and write
                                             n
                                             O
                                     M =           Mi
                                             i=1

for their joint category. Fix a lax-monoidal weakness functor
                  W : C op − (Q, ≤, ⊗, e),     Cl(E) = − log W (E).
For any subset S ⊆ {1, . . . , n}, let
                  ES = arg max W (E),               Cl(S) = Cl(ES ).
                                E∈EMS

We define
                                                    n
                                                    X
                        Σ = Cl({1, . . . , n}) −          Cl({i}),
                                                    i=1
the cognitive synergy measured by comparing the joint system to the partition
into single-store components. Meanwhile, our integrated information is
                               h                    X       i
                  Φw (M) = min Cl({1, . . . , n}) −   Cl(S) ,
                                 π

where π ranges over all partitions of {1, . . . , n}.

23.3     Theorem
                                    Φw (M) ≤ Σ.
