By definition of the minimum,
                               h                     X      i
                   Φw (M) = min Cl({1, . . . , n}) −   Cl(S) .
                                π
                                                         S∈π
                                                                      
In particular, one such partition is the singleton partition πsingle = {1}, {2}, . . . , {n} .
Hence
                                    X                                      n
                                                                           X
Φw (M) ≤ Cl({1, . . . , n}) −               Cl(S) = Cl({1, . . . , n}) −         Cl({i}) = Σ.
                                S∈πsingle                                  i=1



23.4.1    Corollaries and Discussion
    • If Φw (M) > 0, then necessarily Σ > 0. Thus positive integrated informa-
      tion implies positive cognitive synergy.
    • Equality Φw (M) = Σ holds exactly when the minimum information par-
      tition (MIP) is the singleton partition, i.e. no coarser partition yields a
      smaller clarity difference.
    • In general, Σ may exceed Φw , since other partitions (grouping some mem-
      ory stores together) might yield an even larger gap between joint and
      part-wise clarity.

    This result situates cognitive synergy –defined via the natural ”singletons”
partition – as an upper bound on the fully minimized integrated information
Φw , clarifying their precise relationship.
    To understand this fully on a conceptual level: Think of Σ as the synergy you
get when you ”cut” the system exactly along its natural memory-store modules
–asking:

       How much explanatory power is lost if each module (vision, logic,
       procedural, etc.) is forced to explain itself in isolation?
    By contrast, Φ asks:
       What is the single worst way to slice the system – potentially subdi-
       viding modules further – so as to destroy its joint explanatory power
       as much as possible?




                                            238
    Σ (module-synergy) measures how poorly the system performs when each
memory store goes it alone. If isolating the standard modules makes explana-
tions much clumsier, those modules clearly need to work together.
    Φ (integrated information) goes further: it examines every possible partition
of the stores – including finer splits of the natural modules – and selects the cut
that inflicts the greatest explanatory loss. That worst-case cut defines Φ.
    Because no partition can damage explanatory power less than the worst-case
partition, we always have
                                       Φ ≤ Σ.
In practice, however, some finer partitions (subdividing a module into submod-
ules) may be even more disruptive, so one often finds Σ > Φ. Only when the
natural modular split is indeed the most damaging does Σ coincide with Φ,
and in that special case positive module-synergy (Σ > 0) guarantees positive
integrated information (Φ > 0).


24     A Multiresolutional Weakness Transform
We now describe a somewhat more general sort of weakness-based AI tool – a
multiresolution decomposition of relational data or models that mirrors the clas-
sic wavelet transform, but leveraging weakness as the core pattern recognition
and synthesis modality.
    Conceptually, one constructs a hierarchy of increasingly fine simplifications
(“approximations”) of the original dataset, and at each scale one extracts the
“detail” – the residual structure not captured at the coarser level. Just as
wavelets decompose a signal into low- and high-frequency bands, our transform
peels off layers of “weakness” (highest generality) before refining to capture
more specific patterns.
    We believe this may be a quite effective method for structuring weakness-
based AI processing in a variety of contexts – basically any setting where the
environments and tasks involved have a suitable hierarchical structure (a notion
we formalize a bit below). We flesh out potential applications here a bit in the
context of transformer neural net style prediction and humanoid robotics.

24.1     Multiresolution Weakness Transform
At level 0 we choose a very simple model D0 , a maximally weak subobject of the
dataset D. This could be, for instance, a single-cluster partition, a low-order
polynomial fit, or a trivial relation preserving only the most obvious links. The
residual relation
                                  R0 = D − D0
captures everything omitted by the coarse model. We record its ”detail coeffi-
cient” as the weakness of the residual:
                                        M
                    c0 = w(R0 ) =          µ(x) ⊗ µ(y) ,
                                       (x,y)∈R0


                                       239
where (V, ⊗, ⊕) is the chosen quantale and µ assigns weights to objects.
    For the next scale, we select a model D1 ⊆ D0 of strictly lower weakness,
refining the approximation. The new residual

                                  R1 = D0 → D1

yields coefficient
                                           M
                      c1 = w(R1 ) =                 µ(x) ⊗ µ(y) .
                                         (x,y)∈R1

Iterating this process produces a chain

                           D ⊇ D0 ⊇ D1 ⊇ ... ⊇ DN

and detail coefficients {c0 , c1 , . . . , cN }. Reconstruction follows by reuniting the
coarsest model with all details:
                                              −1
                                           [ N[
                               D = DN                 Ri .
                                                i=0

    In practice, one implements this transform by a greedy selection at each scale:
choose Di to minimize weakness subject to an error tolerance i , then compute
the residual Ri and its coefficient. The error tolerance enforces that states or
data points merged by the coarser model remain approximately interchangeable
with respect to the task (e.g. next-token likelihood or control value).
    This framework applies in any domain admitting a quantale-enriched sub-
object lattice. For next-token prediction, one may let Di be the set of n-gram
contexts of order i, µ(c, t) = Pemp (c, t), and ⊕ ordinary addition. In humanoid
robotics, Di arises from a state abstraction φi : S − Ai , with µ(s) proportional
to occupancy or reward weight. Each residual coefficient then quantifies how
much modeling mass is added by refining the abstraction.
    Efficient implementation on the MORK back-end exploits its PathMap alge-
bra: each Di is indexed by a path label (“refinedBy i”), residuals are computed
via fast bitmap differences, and detail coefficients via parallel quantale folds
over changed atoms. As a result, the multiresolution weakness transform runs
in time nearly linear in the size of incremental updates rather than in the size
of the full dataset.
    This multiscale decomposition provides both interpretability – by yielding
human-readable models at each scale – and computational savings – by focus-
ing expensive inference or control on the most informative residuals. It thus
generalizes wavelet-like analysis to the realm of categorical simplicity.

24.2     Multiresolution Weakness Transform and Mind-World
         Correspondence
There is no guarantee that this sort of hierarchical approach to decomposing
weakness will be useful in any particular domain. However in practice one


                                          240
suspects it often will be valuable, because real problems in the physical and
human world often due tend to have the needed sort of hierarchical structure.
    One can abstract this observation a bit: Conceptually, a multiresolution
weakness transform only yields useful simplifications when the enviro fnment it-
self admits a natural hierarchy of abstractions that align with the agent’s goals.
Framing this in terms of the ”Mind-World Correspondence” framework sketched
in Chapter ?? above, we may say an AI’s internal model hierarchy must mir-
ror the world’s hierarchical structure relative to the reward or value function;
otherwise, each successive layer in the transform merely discards relevant infor-
mation. For instance, in navigation tasks the world decomposes into regions,
subregions, and local features; in language modeling it decomposes into n-gram
contexts of increasing order. When such hierarchies exist, peeling off layers of
maximal generality (weakness) at each scale both compresses the model and
preserves goal-relevant distinctions.
    Formally, suppose the environment is modeled as an MDP

                                  M = (S, A, P, R, γ)

with state set S, action set A, transition kernel P (s0 | s, a), reward R, and
discount γ. Let Q∗ (s, a) be the optimal action-value function. We choose a
commutative quantale (V, ⊗, ⊕) and a valuation µ : S − V (e.g. stationary
occupancy or reward magnitude).
    We then define a chain of state abstractions

                          φ0 , φ1 , . . . , φN   ,    φi : S − Ai

with corresponding kernel relations

           Di = {(s, t) | φi (s) = φi (t)} ,         D0 = S × S ,    DN = ∆S

and require a weakness bound
                   M
        w(Di ) =        µ(s) ⊗ µ(t) ≤ αi ,                  α0 ≥ α1 ≥ · · · ≥ αN .
                  (s,t)∈Di

To ensure each abstraction respects the goal, impose the goal-relevance condition

            φi (s) = φi (t) =⇒ Q∗ (s, a) − Q∗ (t, a) ≤ εi            ∀a ∈ A,

so that lumped states remain approximately interchangeable.
    The multiresolution transform can then e.g. be constructed greedily:
  1. Level 0: choose the coarsest φ0 satisfying w(D0 ) ≤ α0 and ε0 .
  2. Detail 0: set R0 = D0 \ D1 , record coefficient
                                          M
                        c0 = w(R0 ) =           µ(s) ⊗ µ(t).
                                                 (s,t)∈R0



                                             241
   3. Level i: refine to φi so that w(Di ) ≤ αi and εi , compute
                               Ri = Di−1 \ Di ,         ci = w(Ri ).

   4. Iterate until αN ≈ 0 and εN ≈ 0.
Reconstruction proceeds as
                                                   −1
                                                  N[
                               D0 = DN ∪                 Ri ,
                                                  i=0

analogous to the inverse wavelet transform.
    We will argue below that this formalism applies equally to next-token pre-
diction – where Di are i-gram contexts and µ(c, t) = Pemp (c, t) – and to mobile
humanoid robotics – where φi are state-abstraction maps and µ(s) reflects oc-
cupancy or reward. In each case, the hierarchical structure must align with
task-relevant distinctions, ensuring that each layer of the transform yields both
compression and fidelity with respect to the AI’s goals.

24.3     Greedy Pattern-Mining Heuristic for Multiresolution
         Weakness Transform
Conceptually, the Hyperon pattern-mining pipeline [GT25] provides a natural
template for a greedy multiresolution decomposition. At each scale one (a) pro-
poses simple candidate models, (b) scores their ”detail” by measuring residual
weakness, (c) selects the most informative pattern, and (d) refines it to gener-
ate finer candidates. Iterating these steps yields a tower of models and detail
coefficients analogous to wavelet low- and high-frequency bands.

Stage 0: Atomic Model Extraction Run the equivalent of the get-links
step to enumerate simplest subobjects {Mi0 }, e.g. single-edge patterns or one-
clause relations. Each Mi0 ⊆ D is a maximally weak candidate capturing one
basic relationship.

Stage 1: Detail Scoring         For each Mi0 , compute its residual
                                   Ri0 = D \ Mi0
and its ”detail coefficient”
                                              M
                      δi0 = w Ri0
                                    
                                        =               µ(x) ⊗ µ(y).
                                            (x,y)∈Ri0

This quantifies how much structure is omitted by Mi0 , akin to surprisingness.

Stage 2: Greedy Selection and Residual Update Choose the pattern
Mi0∗ with largest δi0∗ . Record coefficient δ 0 = δi0∗ and update the dataset residual
                                  D1 = D \ Mi0∗ .


                                            242
Stage 3: Candidate Refinement Generate next-level candidates {Mj1 } by
combining Mi0∗ with additional structure (e.g. pairwise joins, conjunctions).
Each Mj1 ⊆ D1 has lower weakness than its parent.

Stage 4: Iterate For each level k = 1, 2, . . .:
                             
  1. Compute δjk = w Dk \ Mjk for each candidate.

  2. Select Mjk∗ maximizing δjk .

  3. Record detail δ k and set Dk+1 = Dk \ Mjk∗ .

  4. Refine Mjk∗ to generate {M`k+1 }.
Stop when residual weakness is below a threshold. The multiresolution trans-
form is then
             D = Mi0∗ ∪ Mj1∗ ∪ ... ∪ DN ,       {δ 0 , δ 1 , . . . , δ N −1 }.

Algorithmic Implementation Expressed in MeTta-IL, each level fuses the
filter, difference, map (to compute µ ⊗ µ) and fold (to compute ⊕) into
a single fused traversal. This avoids materializing intermediate subgraphs and
runs in time proportional to new atoms per level rather than the full data size.
    This greedy pattern-mining adaptation thus yields an efficient, interpretable
multiresolution weakness transform, selecting the most informative simplifica-
tions at each scale.

24.4    Efficient Implementation via PathMap in MORK
Conceptually, the multiresolution weakness transform decomposes a dataset into
a hierarchy of subobjects and residuals, computing a ”detail coefficient” at each
scale. To run this pipeline at large scale, MORK’s PathMap back end compiles
the entire transform into a small number of path-algebra expressions that ex-
ecute as a single fused graph traversal. PathMap indexes all paths of interest
in advance, so subobject extraction, residual differencing, and quantale folding
become constant-time bitmap operations per graph atom rather than repeated
full scans.
    First, each approximation level Di is represented by a path expression such
as
   P_i = dataset ~> ("refinedBy" : i)+
which matches all atoms reachable by one or more edges labeled ”refinedBy:i”.
PathMap builds an index for Pi mapping each atom to a bit in a compressed
bitmap. Extracting Di then costs a single bitmap lookup per atom.
   Next, the residual relation
                                Ri = Di−1 \ Di
is computed by a bitmap difference:

                                      243
   R_i_bitmap = PathMap.index(P_{i-1})
              AND NOT PathMap.index(P_i)
This vectorized difference uses one machine-word instruction per bitmap word,
independent of the number of atoms.
   Finally, the detail coefficient
                                   M
                             ci =      µ(x) ⊗ µ(y)
                                   (x,y)∈Ri

is computed by iterating only over bits set in R i bitmap and performing parallel
quantale operations. PathMap provides a fast iterator of changed atoms, so each
µ(x) ⊗ µ(y) is fetched from an incremental cache and combined via a native ⊕
primitive.
    By expressing the full multiresolution pipeline as a single PathMap plan:
   PLAN = dataset
        ~> (("refinedBy":0)+ MINUS ("refinedBy":1)+)
        ~> (("refinedBy":1)+ MINUS ("refinedBy":2)+)
        ...
MORK fuses all filters, differences, and folds into one traversal that touches
each edge exactly once. Incremental updates to data or thresholds only replan
affected path segments and recompute local bitmaps and coefficients, achieving
update costs proportional to the change set rather than the full graph size. This
mechanism underlies the same near-real-time performance that makes wavelet-
CNNs and WaveFormers efficient in MORK [F1R25].

24.5     Formalizing Detail Coefficients via Residual Weak-
         ness
Conceptually, each detail coefficient measures the “information mass” omitted
by a coarser model and captured by the next finer model. Just as a wavelet
detail coefficient quantifies high-frequency content, the residual-weakness coeffi-
cient quantifies how much relational structure remains when a maximally weak
subobject is refined.
    Formally, let
                                D0 ⊇ D1 ⊇ · · · ⊇ DN
be a chain of subobjects in a V -enriched category, where D0 is the raw dataset
and DN the finest model. Define the residual at level i by
                                  Ri = Di−1 \ Di ,
and then the detail coefficient
                                          M
                     ci = w(Ri ) =                 µ(x) ⊗ µ(y),
                                        (x,y)∈Ri

where (V, ⊗, ⊕) is the chosen quantale and µ the object valuation. This construc-
tion is completely general, but practical instantiations require domain-specific
choices of Di and µ.


                                         244
Textual Next-Token Prediction Choose Di to be the set of observed context-
token pairs explained by an i-gram model (or by the first i layers of a trans-
former). Let
                   µ(c, t) = Pemp (c) Pemp (t | c) = Pemp (c, t) ,
the empirical joint probability of context c and token t. Then
                                              X
                    Ri = Di−1 \ Di , ci =          Pemp (c, t),
                                                   (c,t)∈Ri

which equals the total probability mass not captured by the coarser i-gram.

Mobile Humanoid Robotics Let each abstraction φi : S − Ai induce the
kernel relation
                  Di = {(s, t) | φi (s) = φi (t)}.
Choose µ(s) proportional to the long-run state-occupancy π ∗ (s) under the op-
timal policy (or to reward magnitude). Then
                                              X
                Ri = ker φi−1 \ ker φi , ci =      π ∗ (s) π ∗ (t),
                                                     (s,t)∈Ri

measuring how much “behavioral mass” the refinement adds. Alternative for-
mulations use expected Bellman-error reductions in place of occupancy weights.
    In both domains, ci = w(Ri ) serves as a wavelet-like detail coefficient, guid-
ing multiresolution analysis by quantifying the residual complexity at each scale.

24.6     Orthogonality via Adjoint Functors and Approximate
         Bi-orthogonality
Conceptually, orthogonality in a multiresolution transform ensures that analysis
(decomposition) and synthesis (reconstruction) operators form exact inverses up
to scale, so that no information is lost or duplicated when moving between levels.
Categorically, this corresponds to an adjoint pair of functors between successive
enrichment levels: the analysis functor projects a coarse model to a finer one,
while the synthesis functor reconstructs the coarse model from the fine one.
Exact adjunction yields perfect bi-orthogonality; in practice, we often settle for
approximate adjunction enforced by reconstruction losses.

General Formalization Let Ci−1 ⊇ Ci be two full subcategories of a V -
enriched category, representing coarser and finer models. Define
           Fi : Ci−1 − Ci     (analysis),         Gi : Ci − Ci−1   (synthesis),
and require an adjunction
       Fi a Gi   ⇐⇒         Ci (Fi X, Y ) ∼
                                          = Ci−1 (X, Gi Y ) ∀ X ∈ Ci−1 , Y ∈ Ci .
The unit η : X → Gi Fi X and counit  : Fi Gi Y → Y witness perfect bi-
orthogonality. When quantale weights obey η and  up to small error, we have
approximate adjunction.


                                            245
24.7     Approximate Bi-Orthogonality and Neural Models
Next-Token Prediction In this sort of use-case, Ci−1 is the category of (i −
1)-gram models and Ci the category of i-gram (or i-layer transformer) models.
Analysis Fi fits an i-gram model Pi by minimizing

                        KL(Pemp |Pi ) + λ KL(Pi |Pi−1 ),

while synthesis Gi marginalizes Pi to recover Pi−1 . Jointly training Fi and Gi
with additional reconstruction terms
                                                          
                     KL Pi−1 |Gi Fi Pi−1 + KL Pi |Fi Gi Pi
     enforces approximate unit/counit identities. Empirically, techniques like dis-
tillation, layer pruning, and interpolation smoothing implement similar approx-
imate adjoints in transformers and n-gram interpolations.

Mobile Humanoid Robotics Let φi−1 and φi be successive state abstrac-
tions S − Ai−1 and S − Ai . Analysis Fi splits macro-states in φi−1 to minimize
Bellman residual subject to a weakness bound:

                     arg min kT φi−1 − T φi k + λ w(ker φi ).
                          φi

Synthesis Gi merges micro-states in φi whose Q-values differ by less than a
threshold, recovering φi−1 . Adding reconstruction and dynamics-consistency
losses
              Es |ψi (φi (s)) − s| + Es,a |T (s, a) − ψi (T 0 (φi (s), a))|
enforces ψi ◦ phii ≈ id and approximate adjunction. Hierarchical RL and
autoencoder abstractions already approximate this in practice.

24.7.1   Viability of Approximate Bi-orthogonality
Exact adjunction is generally infeasible due to model misspecification and noise.
However, by casting analysis and synthesis as dual networks (or encoder/decoder
pairs) and training with reconstruction-style losses and quantale regularizers,
both NLP and robotics domains achieve effective bi-orthogonal behavior. The
trade-off parameter λ controls the balance between simplification (weakness)
and reconstruction fidelity, yielding practical multiresolution transforms that
focus computation where it is most informative.

24.8     Factor-Graph Realization of the Multiresolution Weak-
         ness Transform
The multiresolution weakness transform can be implemented directly as a factor
graph over a chain of quantales. Concretely:




                                       246
  1. Quantale chain. Let

                            Q0 ⊆ Q1 ⊆ · · · ⊆ QN = Q

       be the same nested lattices used in the transform
  2. Variables and factors.
         • For each data object or model component x, introduce a variable
           node vx .
         • For each candidate subobject Di or residual Ri , introduce a factor
           node fi whose potential
                             N φfi over the relevant variables computes the
           weakness w(Ri ) = (x,y)∈Ri µ(x) ⊗ µ(y)
  3. Coarse-to-fine inference.
         • Projection: at level k, replace each factor potential φfi ∈ Qk+1 by
           its join-map projection into Qk .
         • Coarse inference: run one pass of belief-propagation on the graph
           over Qk to compute approximate marginals.
         • Detail selection: select the ”hottest” factors (those whose marginals
           exceed αk ), and restore their full-precision potentials in Qk+1 .
  4. Greedy refinement loop. Iterate for k = 0, . . . , N − 1: project to Qk ,
     infer, select hot factors, and refine them– exactly mirroring the greedy
     multiresolution pipeline described above.
  5. Efficiency and interpretability. This factor-graph view:
         • Shares substructures (repeated Ri patterns) via common factor nodes.
         • Performs each projection and residual update via local join and tensor
           operations.
         • Achieves near-linear incremental cost by only visiting newly refined
           factors.
         • Produces human-readable ”models” at each scale (the coarse factor
           graph at Qk ).

    Thus the classic multiresolution weakness transform is a special case of
quantale-valued factor-graph inference, inheriting its parallelism, incremental-
ity, and multigrid-style efficiency.

24.9     Multiresolution Weakness Control Heuristic for PLN
         Background Inference
Multiresolution factor graphs can be used, among other purposes, for a different
sort of PLN inference control than we considered in Chapter ?? above – con-
tinuous, wide-ranging ”background inference” over a large knowledge base. For


                                      247
this case, we leverage a multiresolution strategy in which we maintain multiple
versions of our Factor Graph at different ”precision” levels in a quantale chain:

                       Q0 ⊆ Q1 ⊆ · · · ⊆ QN = Qtruth
   Each Qk encodes a different resolution of Simple Truth Values (e.g. low-
precision to high-precision confidence). We then apply the following control
heuristic:

  1. Projection to coarse resolution. For each factor φf in the full graph
     over Qk+1 , compute a coarse approximation
                                (k)
                                         _
                              φf =              φf
                                         Qk+1 →Qk

     via the quantale join-map.
  2. Coarse-level inference. Run a single pass of belief-propagation on the
     reduced graph over Qk to compute approximate marginals for every ex-
     pression. This quickly filters out factors whose contribution is below a
     threshold at this scale.
  3. Selective refinement. Identify the subset of factors
                          (k)
                        Fhot = { f : VarMarginalk (f ) ≥ τk }

     whose coarse-level uncertainty exceeds a preset threshold τk . For each
          (k)
     f ∈ Fhot , restore its full precision potential in Qk+1 and re-compute local
     messages.
  4. Iterate through levels. Repeat steps (1)-(3) for k = 0, . . . , N − 1, cas-
     cading hot-spot refinements upward. At the finest level QN one recovers
     high-confidence marginals only where needed.

   This yields a ”multigrid-style” inference loop that:

   • Exploits coarse approximations to eliminate low-impact factors early,
   • Focuses high-precision computation on a small subset of the graph,
   • Runs in time proportional to the number of newly refined atoms per level
     rather than the full graph size,
   • Provides both interpretability at each scale and computational savings by
     peeling off large blocks of ’ structure.




                                      248
24.10     Symbolic WaveFormer via Multiresolution Weak-
          ness Transform
Conceptually, the WaveFormer architecture applies a discrete wavelet transform
(DWT) to neural feature maps, splitting them into low-frequency (coarse) and
high-frequency (detail) subbands, processes each band with self-attention, then
recombines via an inverse DWT (IDWT). We can augment this pipeline with
a parallel symbolic DWT built from multiresolution weakness models, yielding
a hybrid ”Symbolic WaveFormer” that injects Occam-driven structure into the
neural multiscale attention.
   First, at each scale i we define:
   • A symbolic approximation Di ⊆ Di−1 , e.g. an i-gram model or i-layer
     transformer truncation.
   • A residual Ri = Di−1 \ Di with detail coefficient
                                        M
                      ci = w(Ri ) =            µ(x) ⊗ µ(y).
                                         (x,y)∈Ri


These play the role of low- and high-frequency symbolic channels. We embed
Di and Ri as auxiliary token streams parallel to the neural DWT subbands.
   Technically, each Symbolic WaveFormer block proceeds as follows:
  1. Symbolic DWT: Compute Di and Ri via multiresolution weakness trans-
     form.
  2. low-freq Attention: Apply a lightweight symbolic attention or predic-
     tion on Di , yielding coarse context embeddings αi .
  3. Detail Gating: Use ci to compute a gating scalar gi = σ(β ci ), where σ
     is a squashing function and β a learned gain.
  4. Neural WaveFormer: Run standard WaveFormer self-attention on low-
     and high-frequency neural DWT subbands, adding gi as a bias to high-
     frequency attention logits.
  5. Symbolic IDWT: Map symbolic embeddings αi and gated detail tokens
     back to token-level space via a symbolic reconstruction function.
  6. Neural IDWT: Apply the neural IDWT to recombine subbands into the
     next layer’s input.
   This hybridization yields several benefits:
   • Interpretability: Coarse predictions αi are human-readable n-gram or
     grammar summaries.
   • Efficiency: Neural attention focuses on high-frequency regions where sym-
     bolic detail ci signals missing mass.


                                      249
   • Consistency: An auxiliary reconstruction loss
                                    
               KL Pemp |P̂sym+neural + γ IDW T (Di , Ri ) − Di−1
      enforces approximate bi-orthogonality between symbolic and neural trans-
      forms.
    By treating the multiresolution weakness coefficients ci and subobjects Di , Ri
as symbolic DWT components, and coupling them via gating and reconstruction
losses to the neural WaveFormer’s DWT streams, we obtain a unified neural-
symbolic multiscale transformer that marries Occam-guided abstraction with
state-of-the-art wavelet attention.

24.11     Neural-Symbolic Multiscale Correspondence in Hu-
          manoid Robotics
While the details would get hairier, in principle a similar approach could work
for intensively multimodal use-cases – let’s consider humanoid robotics as our
working example. Conceptually, environments for mobile humanoid robots often
admit natural hierarchies of spatial and task structure (for example, building −
room − furniture − handle). A neural-symbolic correspondence could leverage
this by running two parallel streams at each resolution: a symbolic abstraction
channel that plans over coarse macro-states, and a neural residual channel that
refines actions where the symbolic plan is uncertain.

Multiscale Symbolic and Neural Channels At level i, define a symbolic
abstraction map
                           φ i : S − Ai
that clusters raw states S into a small set of macro-states Ai . The coarse
channel uses a lightweight symbolic planner (for example, value iteration or
graph search) on the abstract MDP (Ai , A, Pi , Ri ) to compute abstract Q-values
Qi (α, a). The neural channel employs a residual policy πi (a | s, α) – a small
neural network that takes the full state s together with the abstract state α =
φi (s) and outputs fine-grained control actions.

Gating and Fusion To combine channels, compute a confidence score
                     ∆i (s) = max Qi (α, a) − min Qi (α, a)
                                 a                a

and map it to a gating factor gi (s) ∈ [0, 1] via a simple monotonic function (for
example, a sigmoid). The final action at resolution i is then
                   (
                     arg maxa Qi (α, a), with probability 1 − gi (s),
          ai (s) =
                     πi (a | s, α),          with probability gi (s).

This ensures that when the abstract planner is confident (∆i large), the symbolic
action is used, and when it is uncertain, the neural residual corrects or refines.


                                       250
Approximate Bi-orthogonality via Reconstruction Losses To enforce
consistency between symbolic and neural representations, train the abstraction
φi and a decoder ψi : Ai → S jointly with losses

       Lrec = Es∼S kψi (φi (s)) − sk + Es,a T (s, a) → ψi (Ti0 (φi (s), a)) ,
                                                                         


where T and Ti0 are the true and abstract transition operators. The first term
enforces ψi ◦ φi ≈ idS , while the second enforces that planning in the abstract
MDP lifts back to near-optimal real transitions. A regularizer w(ker φi ) penal-
izes abstraction complexity, balancing compression with fidelity.

Hierarchical Iteration and Planning By stacking these modules for i =
0, . . . , N , the robot executes a coarse-to-fine control loop:

        s0 → φ0 (s0 ) → a0 → s1 → · · ·     then   φ1 (s1 ) → a1 → s2 → · · ·

Each level uses the residual of the previous level’s plan as its input, analogous
to multiresolution detail in a wavelet transform. This neural-symbolic corre-
spondence yields interpretable plans, efficient computation, and robustness to
unmodeled dynamics by confining neural learning to high-frequency ”detail”
corrections.
