25     Evolving Weak Models: A Schema Theorem
We turn now from probabilistic reasoning and discriminatory learning to evolu-
tionary learning.
    Another interesting case of interplay between weakness and AI learning
methods arises when one looks at evolutionary learning with a weakness fit-
ness function – i.e. evolving weak models. If one sets things up right, one can
obtain some rather crisp theoretical results about this sort of weakness-guided
evolution.
    We will give here a significantly strengthened version of the classical GA
schema theorem [Hol75], specialized to CoDD programs in Elegant Normal
Form (ENF) with fitness equal to weakness w(h). In this setting, monoidal
composition of CoDD subprograms perfectly preserves and adds weakness, so
crossover is building-block-preserving. As a result, we obtain an almost exact re-
currence for the expected number of schema instances and a transparent bound
on convergence toward the weakest (max-weakness) explanation. The concept
is similar to the classic schema theorem, but there is a lot less mess around.




                                          251
25.1     A Categorical Schema Theorem for Weakness in CoDD-
         Based GAs
Setup.
   • Let H be the set of all ENF-normalized CoDD programs.
   • Define fitness F (h) = w(h), the categorical weakness of program h.
   • A schema s is any connected sub-DAG of CoDD combinators. We write
     h ⊇ s if s appears as a subgraph of h.
   • Selection is roulette-wheel proportionate to F (h).
   • Crossover picks two parents h1 , h2 , chooses a cut-edge in each, and swaps
     the corresponding sub-DAGs; since CoDD is monoidal and ENF is canon-
     ical, the offspring’s weakness is
                                                                                
     w(hRmchild ) = w(hRmpref ix )+w(hRmgraf t ) = w(h1 )+w(h2 )− w(h1 s)+w(h2 s) ,
       so any schema s present in both parents survives intact in both offspring.
   • Mutation edits one combinator node at random, reducing weakness by at
     most ∆max .
Theorem 22 (CoDD Weakness Schema Theorem). Let ms (t) be the expected
number of individuals in generation t containing schema s. Denote the population-
mean weakness by w(t) = N1 h w(h). Then under selection and crossover alone
                            P
(mutation rate µ = 0),
                                                 ws
                           ms (t + 1) = ms (t)       ,
                                                w(t)
where ws is the average weakness of all programs containing s. If mutation at
rate µ can disrupt s in at most δ(s) places, then
                                          ws
                     ms (t + 1) ≥ ms (t)       (1 − µ δ(s)).
                                          w(t)
Sketch. (1) Selection+Crossover. Because CoDD’s monoidal ENF ensures that
no recombination ever splits a connected sub-DAG s, every selected copy of s in
the parent generation yields exactly two offspring copies. Thus the only effect
on ms is the change in total population size under proportionate selection:
                               P
                                 h⊇s w(h)                  ws
                ms (t + 1) = 2 P           ms (t) = ms (t)      ,
                                  h w(h)                   w(t)
after renormalizing to a fixed population of size N .
    (2) Mutation. A mutation hits one of the δ(s) nodes of s with probability
δ(s)/|h|, and only those events can remove s. Thus with mutation rate µ,
                                          ws
                      ms (t + 1) ≥ ms (t)      (1 − µ δ(s)).
                                          w(t)



                                       252
Corollary (Convergence toward Max-Weakness). Suppose there is a
unique global maximally weak program h∗ . If µ and crossover ensure every
other schema s 6= h∗ has ws < w(h∗ ), then repeated application of the above
recurrence drives mh∗ (t) − N exponentially fast in t.

Discussion. Because CoDD’s ENF representation makes every schema a true
building block with additive weakness under monoidal recombination, the usual
schema disruption terms vanish. This yields an exact expected-value update
under crossover and a simple mutation penalty, guaranteeing rapid convergence
to the weakest explanation in expectation.
    And thus, as one says ... ”Bob’s your uncle”...! 4


26      Evolutionary Dynamics as Optimal-Transport
        Geodesics
As a related perspective on evolutionary dynamics and weakness, we argue here
that, under the usual infinite-population and small-variance limits, common evo-
lutionary processes (e.g. mutation-selection dynamics, evolutionary algorithms
with recombination, and Estimation-of-Distribution Algorithms) admit a de-
scription as gradient flows in the space of probability distributions equipped
with a Wasserstein-type metric. As a consequence, their trajectories follow
optimal-transport geodesics. In the case where evolution is aimed at the learn-
ing of weak models, we can say that a maximal-local-weakness trajectory is
being followed on the path to learning maximally weak models.

26.1      Evolution as Optimal Transport
Consider an evolving population whose genotype distribution at time t is ρt (x)
on a continuous trait space X ⊂ Rn . Under selection by fitness f (x) and
(Gaussian) mutation with small variance σ 2 , the infinite-population limit yields
the Fokker-Planck PDE:
                                                2
                        ∂t ρt = ∇· ρt ∇V + σ2 ∆ρt ,
                                         
                                                                               (6)

where V (x) = −β −1 log f (x) (for selection intensity β > 0). It is well known
(Jordan-Kinderlehrer-Otto, 1998) that (6) is the 2-Wasserstein gradient flow of
the free-energy functional

                                           σ2
                       Z                      Z
                F[ρ] =     V (x) ρ(x) dx +       ρ(x) log ρ(x) dx
                        X                   2 X

in the space P(X) endowed with the Wasserstein-2 metric W2 . That is,

                                  ∂t ρt = −gradW2 F[ρt ].
   4 In less informal terms: yes, this actually packages up the conceptual notion of the schema

theorem very nicely and with relatively little mess compared to prior formulations.


                                             253
Theorem 23. Under selection-mutation dynamics (6), the curve t 7→ ρt is the
unique W2 -geodesic steepest-descent path for F. In particular, for any 0 ≤ s <
t ≤ T,                              Z        t
                       W2 (ρs , ρt ) =           ∂t ρu               du,
                                         s               Tρu P, W2

and (ρt )t∈[0,T ] solves the Jordan-Kinderlehrer-Otto (JKO) minimizing-movement
scheme.

Proof Sketch.     The JKO theorem shows that successive time-discretizations
                                  n                      o
                   ρk+1 = arg min 2τ1
                                      W22 (ρ, ρk ) + F[ρ]
                                ρ∈P(X)


converge as τ → 0 to the continuous-time solution of (6), which is therefore the
W2 -gradient flow of F . Standard results in metric-gradient-flow theory then
imply the geodesic property above.

26.1.1   Extension to Recombination and EDAs
Recombination (crossover) can be modeled by additional operators that mix
parental distributions. In the infinite-population limit, these also admit con-
tinuum descriptions (e.g. as nonlocal redistribution operators). Under mild
assumptions, the combined selection-mutation-recombination process remains a
gradient flow of an appropriately modified free-energy functional in a Wasserstein-
like metric (or in a mixture of Wasserstein and information-geometric metrics).
    Estimation-of-Distribution Algorithms (EDAs) replace the full distribution
ρt by a parametric family {ρθ } and update parameters θ via maximum-likelihood
or moment-matching rules. In the limit of infinitesimal step-sizes and large
samples, the parameter-flow is a gradient flow on the statistical manifold, which
can also be interpreted as an approximate Wasserstein geodesic under a pullback
metric .

26.1.2   Evolving Weak Models
If one interprets the ”fitness” f (x) as inversely related to a representational-
weakness cost c(x), then evolution seeks genotypes of minimal weakness. The
above theorem shows that, in the large-population/continuous limit, the weak-
model distribution evolves along optimal transport paths in the weakness quan-
tale, thus instantiating wu wei : the population flows with minimal cumulative
weakness to reach more robust genotypes.
   In summary, a broad class of evolutionary and EDA processes can be rigor-
ously understood as geodesic flows in an optimal-transport metric space. This
provides a unifying theorem linking evolutionary computation, representational
simplicity, and the principle of effortless action.




                                             254
26.2     Evolutionary Learning Converges to Weakness-AIXI
         Geodesics
Expanding this line of thinking regarding evolution and geodesics a bit fur-
ther, we can also show that a broad class of evolutionary algorithms (EAs) and
estimation-of-distribution algorithms (EDAs) that learn ”weak” models to guide
action will – in the infinite-population, small-variation, and small-learning-rate
limits – converge to the same entropic-transport geodesic flows as weakness-
AIXI. This establishes that evolutionary learning of weak models manifests the
wu wei principle in the quantale of representational effort.

26.2.1    Setup and Assumptions
Let HH be a hypothesis class and ρt ∈ ∆(HH) the population distribution at
(continuous) time t. Suppose the algorithm at each step:

  1. Selects or weights hypotheses h ∈ HH proportional to

                             ρt (h) exp − 1 w(h) f (h),
                                                 

       where w : HH → Q is a weakness prior, f (h) is a fitness score, and  > 0
       is an entropy regularizer.
  2. Applies infinitesimal variation (mutation, crossover, or parametric update)
     that induces a velocity field v(h, t) satisfying

                                  ∂t ρ + ∇· (ρ v) = 0.

  3. Optionally refits a parametric model to the new population via maximum-
     likelihood or KL-minimization, with learning rate η → 0.

   Under the joint limit

              population size → ∞,    variation scale → 0,   η → 0,

the population flow ρ(t) satisfies:
Theorem 24. Let

               FF[ρ] = −EEρ [log f (h)] + EEρ [ w(h) ] +  KL(ρkp),

where p(h, t) is the prior predictive under the current model. Then ρ(t) is the
gradient flow of FF in the Wasserstein-2 metric with entropic regularization,
i.e. it minimizes the Schrödinger-bridge cost
                        Z TZ                                        
           WSB [ρ, v] =         ρ(h, t) 21 kv(h, t)k2 +  log ρ(h,t)
                                                              p(h,t) dh dt,
                        0   HH

subject to ∂t ρ + ∇· (ρv) = 0. Equivalently, ρ(t) is a geodesic in the quantale of
weakness costs.


                                       255
Sketch.    • By standard results, infinite-population, small-variation EAs con-
     verge to a replicator-mutator PDE, which is the W2 -gradient flow of
     EEρ [w(h)] − EEρ [log f (h)].
   • Adding an entropy bonus  KL(ρkp) yields an entropically-regularized gra-
     dient flow.

   • The Jordan-Kinderlehrer-Otto scheme then shows that the continuous-
     time limit follows the Schrödinger-bridge variational principle.
   • Hence the population distribution evolves along minimal-effort geodesics
     in the Schrödinger-bridge quantale.


26.2.2    Parallel with Quantum Evolution
The same Schrödinger-bridge cost appears in the hydrodynamic formulation
of quantum mechanics, with  = ~ and p(x, t) the free-diffusion prior. Thus
evolutionary learning of weak models and quantum evolution both follow the wu
wei principle: geodesic flow with minimal representational effort in a common
quantale.

26.3     Resource-Efficient Evolutionary Approximation of Wú
         Wéi Dynamics
Evolutionary algorithms (EAs) and estimation-of-distribution algorithms (EDAs)
can approximate the entropic-transport geodesic flows of weakness-AIXI – hence
instantiate wu wei dynamics – provided certain conditions hold:

26.4     Key Conditions for Approximation
  1. Large Population, Small Variation. The population size N must
     be large enough that the empirical distribution ρ̂t concentrates
                                                             √         around
     the true density ρt (sampling error δ scales like O(1/ N )). Variation
     operators
        √      (mutation step-size or crossover perturbation) should scale σ ∼
     O( ∆t) so the discrete update approximates a diffusion-drift process.
  2. Smooth Free-Energy Landscape. The free-energy functional

                  FF[ρ] = −EEρ [log f (h)] + EEρ [w(h)] +  KL(ρkp)

       must have Lipschitz-continuous gradient in the Wasserstein geometry, en-
       suring stable convergence of gradient-flow approximations.
  3. Expressive Model Families for EDAs. When using an EDA, the
     parametric family {ρθ } (e.g. factored or graphical models) must capture
     the main modes of ρt with fitting error O(δ). This keeps the KL-projection
     ρ̂t+1 ≈ arg minθ KL(ρ̂t+1 kρθ ) accurate.


                                      256
  4. Entropy-Regularization vs. Selection Pressure. The regularization
     weight  must balance exploration and exploitation. If   1, strong
     selection collapses diversity; if   1, the flow fails to track the free-energy
     descent.
  5. Sufficient Computational Budget. To achieve kρ(T ) − ρ∗ k ≤ δ, one
     needs on the order of
                                           1
                                               poly dim HH, 1
                                                               
                              N ×T &         2
                                           δ
       fitness evaluations. Recombination accelerates convergence by mixing de-
       pendencies nonlocally.

   Under these conditions, the EA or EDA population flow ρ(t) closely tracks
the Schrodinger-bridge geodesic
                              Z TZ                                     
                                                      1    2        ρ
               WSB [ρ, v] =             ρ(h, t)       2 kvk +  log p       dh dt,
                               0   HH

thus approximating the optimal-transport wu wei dynamics.

26.5     Evolutionary Strategies for AGI and Human-Like Cog-
         nition
For an AGI aiming at effective human-like cognition in typical tasks – language
understanding, motor control, creative problem-solving – evolution can be an
especially resource-effective approach when:

   • High-Dimensional, Structured Domains. Human tasks often involve
     rich combinatorial structure (grammar, spatial layouts, causal models).
     Evolutionary recombination naturally exploits modularity and reuses sub-
     structures, approximating semantic transport along minimal-effort paths.
   • Nonconvex Objective Landscapes. Many cognitive goals (e.g. novel
     idea generation) present multiple local optima. Population-based search
     maintains diversity, enabling exploration of alternative geodesics in the
     belief space.
   • Implicit Weakness Priors. Human bias priors (simple grammar rules,
     perceptual heuristics) act like weakness costs. Evolutionary variation
     learns and refines these priors over generations, steering the AGI along
     minimal-weakness inference paths.

   • Online Adaptation and Continual Learning. Real-time tasks de-
     mand rapid adaptation. Evolutionary dynamics – when implemented with
     fast EDA updates – can approximate belief-flow geodesics on the fly, al-
     lowing the AGI to update its internal models with minimal disruption.



                                           257
   • Hardware Parallelism. Modern accelerators enable massively paral-
     lel fitness evaluations. This resource can be leveraged to sample large
     populations, satisfying the large-N condition and yielding high-fidelity
     approximations to the continuous geodesic.

   Thus, evolutionary learning provides a practical and biologically-inspired
mechanism for AGI to follow wu wei in complex, human-oriented domains –
evolving weak models and actions along near-optimal transport paths in the
quantale of representational effort.


27     Homomorphic Encryption of the Search for
       Weak Models via Polynomial Functors
In [Goe25b] I have outlined an approach to homomorphic encryption of a broad
class of computer programs via polynomial functor operations. Here we explore
how this may be applied to weakness-based AI operations, by taking a particular
example as a case in point: a genetic algorithm being used to find weak models.

27.1     Quantale as a Ring-Object in Polynomial Functors
Let (V, ⊗, ⊕) be a commutative quantale. Define the polynomial functor
                                         G
                             PV (X) =       X
                                           v∈V

in the category of polynomial endofunctors on Set. Under Day-convolution and
coproduct,
                  PV ⊗ PV ∼= PV ⊗V ,     PV ⊕ PV ∼ = PV ⊕V .
Performing group-completion in Poly yields a genuine ring-object PeV , whose
addition and multiplication recover ⊕ and ⊗.

27.2     Encoding Candidate Models
A dataset X is fixed. A candidate model µ is a function X → V . We embed µ
into PeV by sending each x ∈ X to the summand labelled by µ(x). This yields
a polynomial-functor morphism

                             Fµ : X −→ PeV (X).

27.3     Encryption via BNF Quotients
To encrypt the ring-object PeV , pick a secret Bounded Natural Functor Φ ∈ B
and form the quotient functor           
                                     PeV GΦ



                                     258
where GΦ is the equivalence subfunctor induced by Φ. This hides each summand
µ(x) among equivalent classes. Encrypting Fµ yields
                                                            
              E\c Fµ = Fµ /GΦ ∈ Hom X, (PeV /GΦ )(X) .

27.4    Homomorphic GA Operations
All GA steps are carried out on encrypted functors E\c(Fµ ):

Fitness Evaluation Weakness fitness is
                            M              
                    w(µ) =       ϕ(x) ⊗ µ(x) .
                                   x∈X

Homomorphically, one composes and adds the corresponding summands in PeV /GΦ ,
using the fact that inference-like steps lift to the quotient. The server computes
                                  M                        
                      E\c w(µ) =           ϕ(x) ⊗ E\c(µ(x))
                                   x∈X

via coproducts and Day-convolutions in Poly.

Crossover and Mutation Given two encrypted models E\c(Fµ1 ) and E\c(Fµ2 ),
homomorphic crossover is implemented as a natural transformation
                                                            
            Crossover : E\c(Fµ1 ) ⊕ E\c(Fµ2 ) −→ E\c Fµchild ,
and homomorphic mutation is another functor-level transformation. Both op-
erate entirely within the quotient PeV /GΦ , preserving the encrypted structure.

 Selection and Iteration Since direct comparison of encrypted weaknesses
is infeasible, the server returns the encrypted fitness values to the client, who
decrypts, selects the top candidates, re-encrypts them (using the same Φ), and
sends them back. The server then applies homomorphic GA operators to pro-
duce the next generation of encrypted models.

27.5    Security and Correctness
The hardness of recovering Φ from PeV /GΦ is the BNF Distinguishing Problem,
analogous to subgroup-decision assumptions in bilinear-group schemes. All ho-
momorphic operations correspond exactly to the intended quantale ⊕, ⊗ on
plaintext models, thanks to the functorial lifting of operations to quotients.

27.6    Quantum Homomorphic GA over the Quantum Op-
        erator Quantale
Following the pattern of [Goe25a] we now show how the ideas of the previous
section might be used to encrypt a quantum-computing-based GA optimizing a
quantum-algebra weakness fitness function, in a homomorphically quantum-safe
way.


                                      259
Quantum Operator Quantale Let QChan be the dagger-compact category
                                                          n
whose objects are finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces H ' C2 and whose mor-
phisms are CPTP maps Φ : D(H) → D(K). We form a commutative quantale
on each hom-set by

Φ ⊕ Ψ := direct-sum of channels,       Φ ⊗ Ψ := tensor (or composition) of channels.

Weakness as Fitness Fix an ensemble prior ϕ on input states ρ. For a
candidate quantum circuit C : D(Hin ) → D(Hout ) define
                                M                   
                     w(C) =             ϕ(ρ) ⊗ C(ρ) ,
                                ρ∈supp(ϕ)

and then scalarize via the trace map Tr : ⊕ 7→ R to obtain a real-valued GA
fitness.

Quantum Ring-Object via Superfunctors Replace classical polynomial
functors by quantum polynomial functors
                                   M
                         F (X) =       Ai ⊗ X ⊗ni ,
                                       i

and perform group-completion in the category of endofunctors on QChan to
obtain a ring-object PeQ .

Secret Quotient: Bounded Natural Superfunctor Choose a secret BNSF
Ψ = {ΨH } satisfying

                   Φ ◦ ΨH = ΨK ◦ Φ           and   kΨH k ≤ λ.

Form the quotient of PeQ by the equivalence ρ ∼ ΨH (ρ). Encrypt each density
operator ρ as the MLWE ciphertext pair

                 ( ρ , ΨH (ρ) ) 7→ (c0 , c1 ) = (A s + e, ρ − s),

so that every natural transformation (including lifted CPTP gates) acts homo-
morphically on ciphertexts.

Encrypted GA Workflow
  1. Population Encoding: Each individual is a circuit C, encoded as a quan-
     tum superfunctor FC and encrypted via BNSF+MLWE to E\c(C) ∈
     PeQ /GΨ .
  2. Fitness Evaluation: Server applies the lifted natural transformation
                         ϕ⊗−    M                    Tr
                    FC −−−−→       (ϕ(ρ) ⊗ C(ρ)) −−→ scalar,
                                              
     entirely on ciphertexts to yield E\c w(C) .


                                       260
  3. Selection: Ciphertext fitness values are returned to the client, who de-
     crypts, selects top candidates, re-encrypts them, and sends them back.
  4. Crossover & Mutation: Server applies homomorphic combinators (Day
     convolution, coproducts, superfunctor merges) to produce offspring E\c(Cchild ).
  5. Iteration: Repeat evaluation, selection, and genetic operators until con-
     vergence.

Security and Correctness
   • MLWE hardness ensures quantum-safe confidentiality of circuits, priors,
     and fitness.
   • Naturality of CPTP maps and MLWE quotients guarantees that all quan-
     tale operations (⊕, ⊗) lift correctly to the encrypted domain.
   • No server-side decryption or comparison is needed beyond returning en-
     crypted fitness scalars for client-side selection.
    This construction yields a fully homomorphic, post-quantum secure genetic
algorithm whose fitness function is the quantale-weakness of candidate quantum
circuits, all performed inside the quantum ring-object of encrypted superfunc-
tors.


28     Hierarchical Hypervector Embeddings
Embedding symbolic structures in continuous spaces has become a cornerstone
of modern AI, enabling neural models to leverage rich relational and functional
information. Two of the more interesting instantiations of this paradigms are:

   • Type-based hierarchical named functional embeddings, proposed in [Ke25],
     assign each concept a concatenation of semantically meaningful sub-vectors
     drawn from its position in a type ontology. Each sub-vector (e.g. Demand Subject,
     Demand Object, Effect) encodes precisely what a concept requires or pro-
     vides, ensuring interpretability and leveraging inheritance across ancestor
     types.
   • Modular compositional hypervector representations [SF14b] use fixed-length
     integer vectors partitioned into modules, with algebraic binding and bundling
     operations that support true compositionality and noise-tolerant retrieval.
     Roles and fillers are bound via permutation-convolution and aggregated
     via summation, producing robust, fixed-dimensional embeddings.

   Each of these innovative approaches has pluses and minuses. While hi-
erarchical named vectors excel at semantic clarity, their concatenation-based
composition scales poorly as ontologies deepen and lacks principled unbinding
operations. Conversely, hypervector methods offer powerful binding/unbinding


                                     261
and efficient similarity search but rely on uniform, unnamed modules, missing
the fine-grained functional distinctions of type-based embeddings.
    In this chapter, we present a Hierarchical Modular Hypervector framework
that unites the strengths of both approaches. By slicing a fixed-length hyper-
vector into named modules aligned with each ontology type and functional role,
we retain semantic interpretability while gaining the algebraic compositional-
ity, fixed dimensionality, and noise robustness of vector symbolic architectures.
Using the running example of

                          Deliver( Pizza, Customer ),

we demonstrate how each sub-structure is represented, composed, and queried,
and we highlight key advantages:
  1. True inverse binding to recover subjects, objects, and effects.
  2. Fixed-size embeddings independent of hierarchy depth.

  3. Robustness to partial corruption and efficient nearest-neighbor retrieval.
  4. Seamless extension of the type hierarchy without vector re-concatenation.
   We argue this integrated approach paves the way for interpretable, scalable,
and resilient embeddings in tasks requiring both precise semantic roles and
powerful compositional reasoning. The HMH framework may be of quite general
use, however we develop it here in large part in the context of our work on the
PRIMUS cognitive architecture being implemented within the Hyperon AGI
framework [GBD+ 23].
   We then flesh out the particulars of the approach via a number of directions:

   • Advanced Semantic Pattern Matching. We show how inverse bind-
     ing and modular masking enable complex queries–such as extracting ob-
     jects, filtering by actor and time, and comparing multi-step processes.

   • Hybrid Symbolic/Vector Architecture. We propose cross-linking
     MORK’s AtomSpace (symbolic layer) with an external ANN index (vector
     layer) for sublinear semantic search.
   • Algorithmic Particulars. We cover calibrated QA-Emb and SensePO-
     LAR value elicitation, binding operator choices (FFT vs. XOR), mod-
     ule allocation heuristics, scalability and multi-inheritance strategies, opti-
     mized unbinding for nested queries, and dynamic updates under evolving
     ontologies.
   • Theoretical Guarantees. We provide formal bounds on information
     capacity, reconstruction error under noise, binding/unbinding accuracy,
     enhanced guarantees for complex query strategies, and similarity preser-
     vation.



                                       262
    Next, to illustrate the concrete use of the HMH framework for embedded
cognition, particularly in the Hyperon/PRIMUS context [GBD+ 23], we explore
applications to probabilistic inference guidance and cross-modal memory re-
trieval.
    We show how HMH may be used to guide PLN inference control, in a rel-
atively subtle way wherein the hierarchical ontology behind the embeddings is
used to minimize independence assumption errors on the PLN side. By for-
malizing a quantale morphism from the hypervector algebra (bundling as join,
binding as meet) into the PLN truth-value quantale, we establish that nearly or-
thogonal role-filler hypervectors map to approximately independent truth values
in PLN. This correspondence not only provides a principled way to translate hy-
pervector difference scores into calibrated inference weights, but–via a Lipschitz-
style error-reduction theorem–guarantees that crosstalk in the embedding space
bounds the approximation error in logical conjunctions and disjunctions.
    To guide proof search in Probabilistic Logic Networks using our hypervector
framework, we define a separation score that quantifies how cleanly each se-
mantic slot within an embedding is isolated from every other. Formulated as a
quantale weakness measure, this score is maximized in hypervector space to find
embeddings whose components interfere minimally. A morphism then carries
these high-separation embeddings into the PLN product-quantale as inference
rules with maximal reliability (i.e. minimal independence-assumption error). By
shifting the search for reliable proofs into continuous hypervector optimization,
we exploit efficient vector operations and approximate-NN retrieval to discover
and rank high-confidence rules without exhaustive logical enumeration.
    Finally, we use the HMH framework to unify episodic, procedural, and
declarative memory in the PRIMUS cognitive architecture. High-level schemas
for events and procedures remain in AtomSpace, while detailed perceptual traces
and motor patterns are stored externally and linked via named hypervector
slots. Retrieval proceeds by symbolic pre-filtering constrained by the declara-
tive ontology in AtomSpace, followed by ontology-guided approximate-nearest-
neighbor search over the hypervector store, yielding efficient and context-aware
recall of past experiences or invocation of learned skills. This cognitive syn-
ergy illustrates the power of a shared ontology to orchestrate memory access
across diverse modules, pointing toward a cohesive architecture for integrated
symbolic-neural cognition.

28.1     Review of Our Ingredients
28.1.1   Hierarchical Embedding Vectors
Type-based hierarchical embeddings assign each concept a concatenation of
named sub-vectors drawn from its type and all ancestor types. Each sub-vector
encodes a semantic ”function” – e.g. as an illustrative example, for an action,
this might include its Demand Subject, Demand Object, and Effect.
    For instance we could have:
   • Type hierarchy: Relation → Action → PhysicalAction → Deliver.


                                       263
   • Module allocation:

               vDeliver = vRelation k vAction k vPhysicalAction k vDeliverType ,

       where k denotes vector concatenation.
   • Named sub-vectors:
                                                                        
                vDeliverType = vDemand Subject , vDemand Object , vEffect .

   • Example values:

                   vDemand Subject · fRobot > vDemand Subject · fHuman ,

       ensuring Robot is a better actor for Deliver.

28.1.2    Snaider’s Modular Compositional Hypervectors
Hyperdimensional computing represents symbols as fixed-length integer vec-
tors and composes structure via binding and bundling. Snaider goes beyond
standard hypervector embedding by building modularity into the mechanism,
enabling flexible compositionality:

   • Module partitioning: A hypervector h ∈ ZD is split into equal-sized
     modules M1 , . . . , MK .
   • Binding: Role-filler pairs are bound via a permutation-convolution oper-
     ator ⊗.
   • Bundling: Multiple bindings are summed (with possible binarization) to
     yield a composite vector.
   • Example:

       hDeliver = (RoleSub ⊗hRobot ) + (RoleObj ⊗hPizza ) + (RoleEff ⊗hMoved ),

       capturing the structure of Deliver(Robot,Pizza).

28.2     Integration: Hierarchical Modular Hypervectors
Our key proposal here is to combine the hierarchy of named sub-vectors with
the algebra of hypervectors.
  1. Per-type modules: Allocate a distinct hypervector module Mt for each
     type t in the ontology, inheriting ancestor modules by index overlap.
  2. Named functional slots: Within each module Mt , reserve sub-slots for
     roles as appropriate, e.g. Demand Subject, Demand Object, Effect, etc.
  3. Value elicitation: For each slot (t, s), elicit a scalar vt,s ∈ [0, 1] (e.g. via
     QA prompts), then scale a random seed vector rt,s by vt,s .


                                          264
  4. Binding and bundling: Bind role tokens to filler vectors within the
     same slot and bundle across slots and types:
                        M
        hDeliver =                   (RoleSub ⊗(vt,Sub rt,Sub ))
                   t∈{Rel,Act,Phys,Del}
                                                                              
                   + (RoleObj ⊗(vt,Obj rt,Obj )) + (RoleEff ⊗(vt,Eff rt,Eff )) ,

     where ⊕ denotes bundling across modules.
   Why do it this way?

28.2.1   Advantages over Standard Hierarchical Vector Embeddings
The proposed Hierarchical Modular Hypervector (HMH) approach improves
upon concatenation-based hierarchical embeddings in several key ways:

   • True Compositionality: Binding and unbinding operations allow exact
     recovery of subjects, objects, effects, and other roles, rather than relying
     solely on dot-product matching of concatenated slices.
   • Fixed Dimensionality: The overall hypervector size remains constant
     regardless of hierarchy depth, avoiding the linear growth in vector length
     that occurs when concatenating one sub-vector per ancestor type.
   • Noise Robustness: Distributed representations degrade gracefully un-
     der corruption or partial loss, whereas errors in any single concatenated
     float slice directly distort similarity scores.
   • Efficient Similarity Search: Fixed-length binary or integer vectors sup-
     port locality-sensitive hashing and hardware-accelerated approximate-NN,
     outperforming brute-force inner-product scans over high-dimensional float
     arrays.
   • Modular Ontology Updates: Adding or removing a type requires only
     allocation or deletion of its module, without re-concatenating or recom-
     puting existing embeddings.
   • Named, Interpretable Slots: Each vector slice is tied to a semantic
     role (e.g. Demand Object, Effect), simplifying debugging and targeted
     queries compared to anonymous uniform modules.
   • Cross-Component Queries: Unbinding enables flexible pattern match-
     ing across multiple steps or roles (e.g. comparing prepared vs. delivered
     items) within a single embedding, which is cumbersome with flat concate-
     nation.
   • Analogical and Hierarchical Generalization: Standard hypervector
     arithmetic (addition, binding, permutation) supports analogical inference
     (e.g. type-role analogies) and automatically inherits common semantics
     from ancestor types.


                                          265
28.2.2   Hierarchical Enhancements over Modular Compositional Hy-
         pervectors
The addition of a type hierarchy and named functional slots, as we do in HMH,
also brings several improvements over Snaider’s original uniform-module hyper-
vector scheme:

   • Type-Specific Modules: Instead of splitting the vector into anonymous
     equal-sized modules, we allocate each ontology type its own module. An-
     cestor types inherit their modules automatically, enabling shared seman-
     tics without duplicating vector content.
   • Named Functional Slots: Within each type module, sub-slots such
     as Demand Subject, Demand Object, and Effect carry explicit semantic
     roles. This contrasts with uniform modules, where roles must be inferred
     or manually aligned.
   • Semantic Inheritance and Generalization: A concept inherits all
     modules from its supertypes, so common properties need only be stored
     once at a higher level. New subtypes automatically gain relevant slots
     without retraining existing vectors.

   • Compact Fixed Dimensionality: By reusing modules via indexed
     binding rather than concatenating every ancestor’s entire vector, the over-
     all dimensionality remains bounded even as the hierarchy deepens.
   • Enhanced Interpretability: Each slice of the hypervector has a clear,
     human-readable meaning. This makes debugging, visualization, and role-
     based queries straightforward compared to opaque uniform partitions.
   • Dynamic Ontology Updates: Adding or removing a type only requires
     allocating or deleting its module and slot links–no need to reconstruct or
     resize existing embeddings.

28.3     Advanced Semantic Pattern Matching
To flesh out the concept, consider a transaction embedding

                       Htx = Horder ⊕ Hprepare ⊕ Hdeliver ,

where, for example,

                      Hdeliver = (RoleSub ⊗ HDriver )
                                + (RoleObj ⊗ HItem )
                                + (RoleRec ⊗ HCustomer )
                                + (RoleTime ⊗ HTimeStamp )
                                + (RoleLoc ⊗ HAddress ) .



                                       266
28.3.1   Extracting the Delivered Object
To determine which object was delivered, unbind the object role:

                            Hobj ≈ Htx ⊗ RoleObj−1 .

A nearest-neighbor search between Hobj and known item hypervectors reveals
if a pizza was delivered.

28.3.2   Filtering Afternoon Pizza Deliveries by Alice
Apply multiple unbinding operations on the same Htx :

                           Hobj ≈ Htx ⊗ RoleObj−1 ,
                           Hsub ≈ Htx ⊗ RoleSub−1 ,
                          Htime ≈ Htx ⊗ RoleTime−1 .

Matching

             Hobj ≈ HPizza ,   Hsub ≈ HAlice ,    Htime ≈ HAfternoon

yields all afternoon pizza deliveries by Alice.

28.3.3   Comparing Prepared vs. Delivered Items
Define
                           Hpd = Hprepare ⊕ Hdeliver .
Using modular indexing, isolate the prepare and deliver object slots separately:

Hitem,prepare ≈ maskprepare Hpd ⊗RoleObj−1 , Hitem,deliver ≈ maskdeliver Hpd ⊗RoleObj−1 .
                                                                                


High similarity between these two vectors indicates that the prepared item
matches the delivered item.
    These queries leverage true inverse binding, modular masking, and fixed-
length algebraic operations–capabilities absent in concatenation-based hierar-
chical embeddings.

28.4     Hybrid Symbolic/Vector Architecture
We now muse a bit on how to implement this sort of embedding in the infras-
tructure of an AI system.
    Modern AI systems often split symbolic reasoning from vector operations;
e.g. in the Hyperon architecture this might look like

   • Symbolic Layer (MORK AtomSpace): Stores ontology, type hierar-
     chy, transaction metadata, and graph patterns. Each concept atom holds
     a small VectorID instead of a full hypervector.


                                       267
   • Vector Layer (ANN Index): Stores fixed-length Hierarchical Modular
     Hypervectors (HMH) keyed by VectorID. Provides fast binding, unbind-
     ing, and approximate-nearest-neighbor search.
   • Cross-Linking: AtomSpace atoms link to vector store entries via VectorID.
     Queries fetch IDs from the graph and delegate vector operations to the
     ANN index.

28.4.1   Approximate Nearest-Neighbor (ANN) Query
The ANN query to be utilized here retrieves the top-k hypervectors most similar
to a query vector hq using a sublinear search index rather than a full scan.

   • Index structures: Hierarchical Navigable Small World (HNSW), In-
     verted File with Product Quantization (IVF-PQ), or Locality-Sensitive
     Hashing (LSH).
   • Similarity metric: Cosine similarity or dot-product between hypervec-
     tors.
   • Query operation:

                     { hi1 , . . . , hik } = ANN index.query(hq , k)

   • Performance: Sublinear time in the number of stored vectors, often
     O(log N ) or better.

Advantages of HMH+ANN over Concatenation-Based Embeddings
Although both the HMH and simplistic concatenation approach ultimately rely
on dot-product (or cosine) similarity, the Hierarchical Modular Hypervector
(HMH) combined with an approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) index offers
key benefits over simple concatenation of hierarchical vectors:

  1. Sublinear, hardware-accelerated search: HMH hypervectors stored
     in an ANN structure (e.g. HNSW, IVF-PQ, LSH) can be queried in
     O(log N ) or better, using bit-parallel or GPU routines. In contrast, concatenation-
     based vectors demand a full O(N · D) inner-product scan.
  2. Exact role extraction: Binding and unbinding operations permit direct
     recovery of embedded subjects, objects, or effects. Concatenated slices
     only yield similarity scores and cannot invert to retrieve the original filler
     vectors.

  3. Fixed, bounded dimensionality: Module reuse via indexed slots keeps
     every HMH hypervector at the same size D, regardless of ontology depth.
     Simple concatenation grows vector length linearly with each added ances-
     tor type.



                                       268
  4. Graceful noise tolerance: Distributed hypervector components tolerate
     random bit or value corruption with smooth degradation in similarity. Any
     error in a concatenated float slice fully skews that dimension’s dot-product
     contribution.
  5. Dynamic module updates: Introducing or removing a type involves
     only adding or deleting its module in the index. Concatenation-based
     embeddings require re-concatenating or re-padding every existing vector
     to reflect ontology changes.

28.4.2   Concrete Hybrid Query Workflow
To illustrate how this might work, consider the following series of steps:
   Goal: Find all afternoon pizza deliveries by Alice.

   Symbolic Pre-filtering       Use AtomSpace pattern queries to narrow can-
didates:

; match transactions where Recipient = Alice
Match: TransactionLink ?tx, RoleRec "Alice"
Collect: VectorIDList = [ "tx1", "tx7", ... ]

   Vector-Store ANN Query In the vector store, unbind the object slot
and search for Pizza among the filtered IDs:

                    query.target = unbind(HPizza , RoleObj)

ANN_query({
  filter_ids: VectorIDList,
  target_embedding: query.target,
  top_k: 10
}) -> [ "tx7", "tx1", ... ]

   Time-Slot Filtering       For each candidate ID, unbind the time slot and
compare to Afternoon:

                       Htime ≈ unbind(HtxID , RoleTime)

final_ids = []
for id in results:
  h = fetch_vector(id)
  if similarity(unbind(h, RoleTime), H_Afternoon) > theta:
    final_ids.append(id)




                                      269
   Remap to Symbolic Atoms Convert the remaining VectorIDs back to
their AtomSpace transaction atoms:
Answer = [ Atom("tx7"), Atom("tx1"), ... ]
   This pipeline leverages:

   • Graph-pattern matching in MORK to apply structural constraints.
   • Fast ANN search and binding/unbinding in the vector store for semantic
     filtering.
   • Seamless remapping between symbolic atoms and vector entries.

28.5     Further Algorithmic Details
Making this sort of approach work effectively involves a number of low-level
algorithmic choices. Here we address some of these in a moderately fine-grained
way. To an extent, though, the right way to work these details will only be
determined via experimentation with applications, and some things might end
up optimally being done differently for data drawn from different domains.

28.5.1    Detailed Value Elicitation via QA-Emb and SensePOLAR
The step ”elicit a scalar vt,s ∈ [0, 1] via QA prompts” indicated above is not
entirely trivial, and potentially requires (at least) two specialized models rather
than generic LLM calls. One pipeline to do this would be:

   • QA-Emb Model: A transformer fine-tuned on binary question-answer
     pairs.

         – Input: ”Does Deliver require a human subject?”
         – Output: Probability p ∈ [0, 1] that the answer is ”yes.”
         – Use: Assign vt,Demand Subject = p.
   • SensePOLAR Model: A dual-embedding network trained on antony-
     mous feature pairs (e.g. Big-Small).
         – Input: A feature pair and context, e.g. ”Edible - Inedible” for Item.
         – Output: Scalar q ∈ [0, 1] representing degree toward the first feature.
         – Use: Assign vt,Edible = q.

   Elicitation Pipeline
  1. Dimension selection: For each named slot s (e.g. Demand Object, Ef-
     fect), list its natural-language description dt,s .
  2. Model assignment:


                                        270
         • If dt,s is binary (yes/no), use QA-Emb.
         • If dt,s is a polar feature, use SensePOLAR.
  3. Prompt construction: Formulate a clear QA prompt or feature-pair
     input.

  4. Inference: Run the prompt through the chosen model, obtaining scalar
     v̂t,s .
  5. Calibration: Apply temperature scaling or isotonic regression on a held-
     out calibration set to ensure v̂t,s is well-calibrated in [0, 1].

   Implementation Considerations
   • Batching: Group prompts to amortize model load.

   • Caching: Store frequent (t, s) responses to avoid redundant inference.
   • Fallback: If a model’s confidence is low (< 0.5), default to a prior or
     request human annotation.
   This explicit pipeline recognizes the complexity of value elicitation and en-
sures consistent, calibrated scalars for every hierarchical slot. It could of course
be designed and engineered in a variety of different ways and the above is just
an initial suggestion.

28.5.2   Binding Operator Specification
The binding operator ⊗ was not fully specified above. It could be implemented
as permutation-convolution on real-valued vectors. We give a few comments on
design choices and their impact:

    Circular Convolution Binding Given two length-D vectors r (role) and
f (filler), define
                                    D−1
                                    X
                    (r ⊗ f )[k] =         r[i] · f [(k − i) mod D].
                                    i=0

This uses a cyclic shift of f for each component of r. It is associative and
invertible via deconvolution in the Fourier domain:
                                                           F(h)
                      fˆ = r−1 ⊗ h        ⇐⇒     F(fˆ) =        .
                                                           F(r)

Implementation via FFT yields O(D log D) cost per binding or unbinding.




                                           271
   Permutation Pattern Choices The permutation of indices in the con-
volution can be any fixed bijection π : {0, . . . , D−1} → {0, . . . , D−1}. Common
choices:
   • Fixed cyclic shift: π(k) = (k + 1) mod D. Simple, FFT-friendly.

   • Random permutation: π drawn once uniformly. Maximizes orthogo-
     nality between roles but requires explicit storage of π.
   • Block-wise rotation: Partition indices into blocks and rotate within
     each block, balancing locality and orthogonality.
Choice of π affects:

   • Orthogonality: Random permutations yield lower cross-talk between dif-
     ferent role-filler pairs.
   • Computational cost: Simple cyclic shifts leverage FFT; arbitrary π may
     require O(D) permutation before convolution.

   • Hardware mapping: Block or cyclic patterns map efficiently to SIMD and
     GPU kernels.

   Binary Hypervector Binding For binary or bipolar (±1) hypervectors,
one can use bit-wise XOR as a fast binding:

                                r ⊗ f = r XOR f,

with ⊗ invertible by the same XOR. This runs in O(D/64) on 64-bit words.

   Performance Trade-offs

   • FFT binding: D log D time, high precision, supports real vectors.
   • XOR binding: O(D) bit-parallel time, low memory, only for binary vec-
     tors.
   • Direct permutation: O(D) time, flexible π, moderate memory for stor-
     ing π.
    In practice, a fixed cyclic shift with FFT provides a robust default. For
applications demanding extreme speed or binary representations, XOR binding
is preferable. Random or block permutations can be adopted when reduced
interference is critical.




                                       272
28.5.3   Module Allocation under Fixed Dimensionality
Another thing we have not fully addressed above is how to determine optimal
module sizes for each type. With a fixed total dimensionality D, how do you
allocate bits across potentially hundreds of types in a deep ontology?
    Let us assume a fixed total dimensionality D to be partitioned across T types
in the ontology. Denote by dt the number of dimensions (or ”bits”) allocated
to type t, subject to
                            X T
                                 dt = D,      dt ≥ 0.
                            t=1

Determining the optimal {dt } is a constrained resource-allocation problem. We
outline several practical approaches:

  1. Uniform allocation: Set
                                            
                                         D
                                    dt =         ∀t.
                                         T

     Simple but ignores differences in type importance or usage frequency.

  2. Frequency-based allocation: Estimate a usage frequency ft (e.g. rela-
     tive occurrence in the corpus) and allocate
                                                
                                            ft
                                 dt = D P          .
                                            u fu

     Types appearing more often receive more capacity.
  3. Information-theoretic allocation: Compute an entropy or mutual-
     information metric Ht for each type (e.g. variance of its slot activations)
     and set                                         
                                                 Ht
                         dt ∝ Ht , dt = D P             .
                                                 u Hu
     Allocates more dimensions to types with richer or more variable semantics.

  4. Downstream-driven optimization: Define a validation loss L({dt }) on
     a held-out task (e.g. accuracy of role-matching) and solve
                                              X
                            min L({dt }) s.t.    dt = D.
                            {dt }
                                                 t

     Relax dt to real values, apply gradient-based or Bayesian optimization,
     then round to integers.
  5. Progressive pruning and growth: Start with a coarse allocation (e.g.
     uniform), monitor slot-level utilization during training, and iteratively re-
     assign dimensions from under-utilized types to over-utilized ones.



                                      273
  6. Hybrid heuristics: Combine the above, for example by allocating a
     base δ dimensions to every type and distributing the remaining D − δT
     according to frequency or entropy.

    In practice, a two-stage pipeline–heuristic initialization followed by light
downstream tuning–often balances simplicity and performance without incur-
ring excessive optimization cost.

28.5.4   Scalability and Module Allocation Constraints
When the ontology grows deep or broad, and types may inherit from multiple
parents, fixed module sizes and reserved sub-slots can become limiting. A few
practical strategies suggest themselves:

   • Module sharing and hashing: Rather than allocating a distinct con-
     tiguous block for every type, use a modulo or hash-based indexing scheme
     to map each type’s slots into a fixed pool of dimensions. This bounds total
     dimensionality while still isolating semantic roles via controlled collisions.
   • Lazy or on-demand slot instantiation: Reserve only minimal default
     capacity for every type (e.g. a small base dmin ) and allocate additional
     dimensions at runtime when a type’s slot utilization exceeds a threshold.
     Unused or low-variance slots can be reclaimed or repurposed.
   • Multiple inheritance via module composition: For a subtype S
     inheriting from parents P1 , . . . , Pk , define its module embedding as the
     element-wise sum or bundle of its parents’ modules plus its own delta:

                            HS = HP1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ HPk ⊕ H∆ ,

     avoiding full duplication of each parent’s dimensions.
   • Slot pruning and compression: Monitor each slot’s activation variance
     or mutual information. Low-utility sub-slots (e.g. rarely used demands or
     effects) can be pruned or quantized to fewer bits, freeing capacity for more
     critical slots.
   • Hierarchical bit allocation heuristics: Use a top-down budget: allo-
     cate more dimensions to higher-level types (covering many subtypes) and
     progressively fewer to leaf types. A simple rule is
                                              1
                                  dt ∝                ,
                                         1 + depth(t)
     ensuring deeper types share majority capacity with their ancestors.
   • Hybrid static-dynamic optimization: Initialize with a heuristic split
     (e.g. frequency or entropy based), then refine via light downstream tuning
     or BayesianPoptimization over a validation task, respecting the global sum
     constraint t dt = D.


                                         274
28.5.5   Optimal Binding/Unbinding Strategies for Complex Queries
Complex queries often involve nested or multiple role-filler bindings in a single
hypervector. The following are examples of strategies that may potentially
improve accuracy and efficiency when extracting fillers:
  1. Sequential Unbinding by Role Specificity: For a composite
                                           n
                                           M
                                      h=         (ri ⊗ fi ),
                                           i=1

     unbind in order of least interference (e.g. rarest or highest-dimension roles
     first):                                                
                                            M
                          fˆk ≈ r−1 ⊗ h ⊕
                                  k              (− ri ⊗ fi ) .
                                                  i<k

     Removing already-extracted components reduces crosstalk.
  2. Hierarchical Masking of Modules: If each role ri occupies a distinct
     module Mi ⊂ {1, . . . , D}, mask out other modules before unbinding:

                                  h|Mk ⊗ rk−1 −→ fˆk .
     This confines error to the module of interest.
  3. Pseudo-Inverse Convolution: For circular convolution binding, per-
     form unbinding in the Fourier domain:
                                 H(ω)
                      F̂ (ω) =              =⇒          fˆk = F −1 [F̂ (ω)].
                                 Rk (ω)
     Using FFTs accelerates deconvolution for large D.
  4. Joint Unbinding for Multi-Role Queries: To extract a combination
     of roles {ra , rb } simultaneously, compute

                         fˆa,b ≈ (ra ⊗ rb )−1 ⊗ h = rb−1 ⊗ ra−1 ⊗ h .
                                                                   

     This recovers fillers bound to both roles in one pass.
  5. Least-Squares Deconvolution: When noise or interference is high,
     solve
                                           2
                          min h − (rk ⊗ x) 2
                                      x
     by gradient descent or conjugate-gradient methods to find the best ap-
     proximate filler x.
  6. Learned Inverse Operators: Train a small neural decoder gk for each
     role:
                              gk (h) ≈ fk ,
     using supervised pairs (h, fk ). This compensates for systematic binding
     distortions.


                                           275
  7. Caching and Reuse of Inverses: Precompute ri−1 or its FFT repre-
     sentation and reuse across queries to minimize per-query cost.
  8. Thresholded Similarity Checks: After unbinding fˆk , compare to a
     candidate set {fj } and accept only those with sim(fˆk , fj ) > θ, rejecting
     low-confidence extractions.

   These strategies–masking, ordering, domain-specific inverses, optimization,
and learned decoders–together seem likely to yield robust, accurate unbinding
even for deeply nested or highly entangled hypervector queries.

28.5.6   Dynamic Updates under Evolving Ontologies
When the type hierarchy changes–new types appear or existing relations restructure–
you must both reallocate module capacity and migrate stored embeddings. We
describe here some reasonable-looking practical strategies.

Module Rebalancing Let the total dimension budget be D. When a new
type t0 is added, its required dimensions dt0 must come from the existing pool:
                                   X
                                        dt = D.
                                t∈T ∪{t0 }

Possible rebalancing schemes:
   • Reserved buffer: Maintain a small unassigned buffer B (B  D) of dimen-
     sions. On each new type, allocate from B until exhausted, then trigger a
     full rebalance.

   • Proportional shrinkage: Reduce each dt by a factor α < 1:
                                                    X
                     dt ← α dt    ∀t ∈ T, dt0 = D −       dt .
                                                         t∈T

                                                                            P
   • Usage-driven reallocation: Monitor slot utilization ut and solve min   t   dt −
         2            P
     β ut subject to     dt = D, rounding to integers.

Embedding Migration Depending on what is stored, one might migrate
existing concept embeddings as follows:

  1. Regeneration from Seeds & Values: If you have recorded the slot-
     level scalars vt,s and seed vectors rt,s , simply recompute:
                                     MM                   
                                h=               vt,s rt,s .
                                     t∈T 0 s∈St

     This fully reflects the new dt allocation without approximation.


                                       276
  2. Linear Projection: When only old hypervectors hold are available, learn
                                          0
     or compute a projection matrix P ∈ RD ×D mapping old to new space:

                                    hnew = P hold .

       P can be initialized to preserve original module subspace projections and
       identity-map new slots to zero.
  3. Buffer-Zone Rollout: Pre-allocate a ”rollout” zone of dimensions R
     where new modules map. Existing vectors keep their original first D − R
     dims, and only the rollout dims change. Once all legacy vectors have been
     accessed and updated to the new scheme, reclaim any unused rollout dims.
  4. Versioned Coexistence: Maintain both old and new embeddings in
     parallel for a transition period. Route queries for concepts of version v to
     the corresponding embedding. Gradually deprecate the old version once
     downstream systems adapt.

Restructuring Inheritance If a type S changes parents from {P1 , P2 } to
{P10 , P20 }, update its module by:
                                            
                            HS ← HP10 ⊕ HP20 ⊕ H∆S ,

where H∆S is its own delta-module. Existing HS can be adjusted by subtracting
the old parents and adding the new ones via unbinding and rebinding.
   These techniques ensure that as your ontology evolves, module capacities
remain balanced and all concept embeddings stay consistent with minimal re-
computation.

28.6     Theoretical Guarantees
Next, we outline several formal aspects of the Hierarchical Modular Hypervector
(HMH) scheme. These results are relatively simple and sketchy and could be
fleshed out much further with effort. The basic gist is to show that HMH embed-
dings have provable capacity, robust noise-tolerance, reliable binding/unbinding
in high dimensions, and accurate preservation of similarity relationships. This
is ”standard hypervector stuff” and we are essentially just affirming that indeed
it applies here.

28.6.1    Information Capacity Bounds
Let each hypervector have length D with i.i.d. Rademacher (±1) components.
The dot-product of two random vectors has mean 0 and variance D. To store
M distinct items and distinguish them with similarity threshold θ and failure
probability δ, a Chernoff bound yields the requirement
                                   2 ln M + ln(2/δ)
                            D ≥                     .
                                          θ2

                                      277
28.6.2   Reconstruction Error under Noise
If each component of a stored hypervector is flipped independently with prob-
ability , the expected similarity to the clean vector is
                                E[sim] = 1 − 2 ,
and the standard deviation is
                                     r
                                         4  (1 − )
                                σ≈                   .
                                              D
The probability of confusing two noisy vectors decays exponentially in D.

28.6.3   Binding/Unbinding Accuracy
Use random orthogonal role vectors {ri } for binding via circular convolution (or
XOR in the binary case). For a bound vector
                                   h = r ⊗ f,
the unbinding fˆ = r−1 ⊗ h recovers f with expected mean-squared error
                                                  √
                      E kf − fˆk22 = O √DD = O( D),
                                          

which is negligible compared to the signal magnitude O(D) as D grows.

         gies
By leveraging module masking, ordered unbinding, and optimized deconvolu-
tion, we can derive tighter bounds on capacity and extraction error beyond the
basic Rademacher-vector analysis.

28.6.5   Similarity Preservation under Binding
A key property of the permutation-convolution (or XOR) binding operator is
that it approximately preserves the inner-product (or cosine) similarities of the
original vectors:

   • Random orthogonality: If the role vector r is drawn at random and
     normalized, then for any two filler vectors a, b ∈ RD ,
                                             
                            E hr ⊗ a, r ⊗ bi = ha, bi,
                                   √
      up to vanishing variance O(1/ D). Thus the expected similarity is exactly
      preserved.




                                         279
   • Circular convolution: Circular convolution implements a unitary (up
     to scaling) transform in the Fourier domain. Writing ⊗ as convolution,

                             r ⊗ a = F −1 F(r) · F(a) ,
                                                     


       we have
                                hr ⊗ a, r ⊗ bi = ha, bi,
       provided krk = 1, since the Fourier transform is unitary.

   • Binary XOR binding: For bipolar {±1} vectors and Hamming similar-
     ity,
                    simH (r ⊕ a, r ⊕ b) = simH (a, b),
       because XOR with a fixed pattern is a bijection on bit patterns.
   • Empirical robustness: In practice, binding adds√only minimal noise:
     the variance of hr ⊗ a, r ⊗ bi around ha, bi is O(1/ D), which vanishes
     for large D.

    Hence, binding does not distort the semantic similarity structure of the orig-
inal type-based embeddings, enabling both accurate matching and reliable un-
binding in downstream queries.

28.7     Guiding PLN Inference via Hierarchical Modular Hy-
         pervector Embeddings
Now we verge into less standard territory – exploring how these hypervector em-
beddings may be used to guide probabilistic logic inference within the PRIMUS
and Hyperon framework.
    Goertzel et al. [GDD+ 20] investigate how vector differences computed from
DeepWalk embeddings of Atomspace nodes align with ”intensional differences”
in Probabilistic Logic Networks (PLN). They show that the numerical vector
pointing from a premise node to a conclusion node often approximates the log-
ical difference in properties between those hypergraph nodes, suggesting that
vector arithmetic can guide uncertain inference control. In their Bio-Atomspace
experiments, 100-dimensional DeepWalk vectors proved to reflect semantic dis-
tinctions encoded in biological ontologies. However, these embeddings lack ex-
plicit semantic roles and hierarchical structure.
    By adopting Hierarchical Modular Hypervectors we can realize the same
conceptual idea in a more elegant and effective way. The core idea is to
view hypervector operations–bundling (vector addition or XOR) and binding
(permutation-convolution or XOR)–as algebraic joins and meets in a ”hyper-
vector quantale.” We then define a mapping into the PLN truth-value quantale,
where logical OR and AND correspond to the same algebraic structure. Under
this morphism, combining two hypervectors via bundling becomes taking the
uncertain OR of their mapped truth values, and binding a role and filler vector
corresponds to uncertain AND.


                                       280
    By choosing role vectors that are nearly orthogonal, we ensure that unrelated
bindings interfere minimally in the hypervector space. The quantale morphism
carries this low-interference property into the PLN domain as near-independence
of truth values, thereby justifying the use of standard PLN deduction and con-
junction rules with reduced approximation error. In practice, one can compute a
”difference” hypervector between premises and conclusions, map it through the
morphism, and obtain a suggestive truth value for guiding or ranking candidate
inferences.

28.7.1   Quantale Morphism from Hypervector Space to PLN Truth-
         Value Quantale
Let (Qh , ⊕h , ⊗h , ≤h ) be the hypervector quantale, where ⊕h is vector bundling
(e.g. element-wise addition or XOR), ⊗h is binding (permutation-convolution or
XOR), and ≤h is the similarity order (e.g. cosine similarity induces a preorder).
Let (Qp , ∨p , ∧p , ≤p ) be the PLN truth-value quantale, where ∨p and ∧p are the
uncertain OR/AND operations on simple-TV truth values.
    We define a quantale morphism

                                 φ : Qh −→ Qp

satisfying

         φ(h1 ⊕h h2 ) = φ(h1 ) ∨p φ(h2 ),     φ(r ⊗h f ) = φ(r) ∧p φ(f ),

and monotonicity h1 ≤h h2 =⇒ φ(h1 ) ≤p φ(h2 ).

28.7.2   Orthogonality → Independence
When two role-filler pairs (ri , fi ) produce hypervectors ri ⊗h fi that are nearly
orthogonal, their similarity hri ⊗ fi , rj ⊗ fj i ≈ 0 for i 6= j. Under φ, this maps
to φ(ri ⊗ fi ) ∧p φ(rj ⊗ fj ) ≈ 0, i.e. logical independence in PLN, reducing the
error of the independence assumption in uncertain AND/OR formulas.

Formal Analysis: Orthogonality Implies Probabilistic Independence
To flesh out the above (conceptually fairly obvious) correspondence, we model
hypervectors as binary indicator vectors and truth-values as simple proportions.
This yields an exact correspondence between orthogonality (disjoint support)
and probabilistic independence under OR/AND.
Theorem 25. Let h1 , h2 ∈ {0, 1}D be two binary hypervectors with disjoint
support:
                         ∀ i : h1 [i] · h2 [i] = 0.
Define the truth-value map
                                              D
                                         1 X
                               φ(h) =          h[i],
                                         D i=1


                                        281
and probabilistic OR/AND in the PLN quantale by

                        p ∨p q = p + q − p q,    p ∧p q = p q.

Then:

         φ(h1 + h2 ) = φ(h1 ) ∨p φ(h2 ),     φ(h1 ⊗h h2 ) = φ(h1 ) ∧p φ(h2 ),

where + is element-wise addition (with no clipping) and ⊗h is bit-wise AND.
   Hence disjoint (orthogonal) hypervectors map to independent truth-values
under ∨p and ∧p .
Proof. Since h1 , h2 ∈ {0, 1}D and h1 [i]h2 [i] = 0, their element-wise sum h1 + h2
has
        (h1 + h2 )[i] = h1 [i] + h2 [i], (h1 ⊗h h2 )[i] = h1 [i] · h2 [i] = 0.
Therefore
                               1 X                
               φ(h1 + h2 ) =       h1 [i] + h2 [i] = φ(h1 ) + φ(h2 ).
                               D i

On the other hand,

                  φ(h1 ) ∨p φ(h2 ) = φ(h1 ) + φ(h2 ) − φ(h1 ) φ(h2 ).

But since h1 , h2 have disjoint support, φ(h1 ) φ(h2 ) counts the fraction of coor-
dinates where both are 1, which is zero. Hence

                  φ(h1 + h2 ) = φ(h1 ) + φ(h2 ) = φ(h1 ) ∨p φ(h2 ).

Similarly,
                     1 X                
    φ(h1 ⊗h h2 ) =       h1 [i] · h2 [i] = 0 = φ(h1 ) φ(h2 ) = φ(h1 ) ∧p φ(h2 ).
                     D i

This establishes the claimed equalities, showing that perfect orthogonality in the
hypervector space induces exact probabilistic independence in the PLN truth-
value quantale.

28.7.3     Guiding Inference
Given premises with embedding hprem and a candidate conclusion embedding
hconc , consider the ”difference” vector ∆h = hconc ⊕h (− hprem ). We compute
τ = φ(∆h ) ∈ Qp as a suggestive truth value for an intensional inference rule.
Larger τ indicates higher confidence that the corresponding PLN inference step
follows the ontology’s orthogonal directions.

Detailed Example: Guiding PLN Inference via Hypervector Differ-
ences We illustrate how to use Hierarchical Modular Hypervectors (HMH) to
suggest a new PLN inference rule, using the running example of Deliver(Pizza, Customer)
and the candidate conclusion Possess(Customer, Pizza).


                                           282
   Hypervector Definitions Let

Hdeliver = (RoleSub ⊗ HRobot ) ⊕ (RoleObj ⊗ HPizza ) ⊕ (RoleRec ⊗ HCustomer ),

           Hpossess = (RoleSub ⊗ HCustomer ) ⊕ (RoleObj ⊗ HPizza ).

   Difference Hypervector Compute the ”difference” embedding
                                             
                    ∆ = Hpossess ⊕ − Hdeliver .

Intuitively, ∆ highlights the semantic shift from delivering to possessing.

   Quantale Morphism and Suggested Truth Apply the quantale mor-
phism φ to map ∆ into a PLN truth value:

                               τ = φ(∆) ∈ [0, 1].

For example, if τ = 0.92, we take this as the suggested strength of the inference:

   Deliver(Pizza, Customer) =⇒ Possess(Customer, Pizza)         with truth τ.

   Incorporating into PLN In PLN, an implication rule

                         R:A ⇒ B        with weight wR

is applied to a known fact A of truth tA to derive B with truth

                                tB = tA ∧p wR ,

where ∧p is the uncertain-AND in the PLN quantale. Here we set wR = τ .
Thus, given
                        tdeliver = 0.85, wR = 0.92,
we infer
                tpossess = 0.85 ∧p 0.92 = min(0.85, 0.92) = 0.85
(or another ∧p formula per the chosen PLN semantics).

   Benefits

   • The hypervector difference ∆ captures semantic shifts in the ontology.
   • The morphism φ translates that shift into a calibrated truth value τ .
   • PLN uses τ to weight and rank candidate inference rules, focusing search
     on high-confidence steps.

This workflow generalizes to multi-premise or multi-step inferences by bundling
multiple premise hypervectors before differencing and mapping through φ.


                                       283
28.7.4        Error Reduction
Let h be the crosstalk error due to non-orthogonality in Qh . Because φ pre-
serves joins and meets, the resulting PLN inference error p satisfies

                                          p ≤ C  h ,

for some constant C depending on quantale norms. Thus by designing the
ontology so its role modules are mutually orthogonal in Qh , we guarantee smaller
independence-assumption errors in the guided PLN inference sequence.
    This morphism formalizes how hierarchical modular hypervector structure
can transfer to more accurate, embedding-guided PLN reasoning.

Formal Error Reduction Theorem The observation above regarding error
reduction may be fleshed out further as follows:
Theorem 26 (Error Reduction under Quantale Morphism). Let (Qh , ⊕h , ⊗h , ≤h
) be the hypervector quantale and (Qp , ∨p , ∧p , ≤p ) the PLN truth-value quantale.
Suppose
                                 φ : Qh → Qp
is a quantale morphism satisfying

                φ(x ⊕h y) = φ(x) ∨p φ(y),        φ(x ⊗h y) = φ(x) ∧p φ(y),

and is L-Lipschitz with respect to similarity metrics dh on Qh and dp on Qp :
                               
              dp φ(h1 ), φ(h2 ) ≤ L · dh (h1 , h2 ) ∀ h1 , h2 ∈ Qh .

Define the hypervector crosstalk error
                                                         
                          h = max dh ri ⊗h fi , rj ⊗h fj ,
                                   i6=j

and the corresponding PLN inference error
                                                             
                       p = max dp φ(ri ⊗h fi ), φ(rj ⊗h fj ) .
                            i6=j

Then
                                          p ≤ L h .
Sketch of Proof. By the Lipschitz property of φ,
                                                                    
p = max dp φ(ri ⊗h fi ), φ(rj ⊗h fj ) ≤ L max dh ri ⊗h fi , rj ⊗h fj = L h .
       i6=j                                          i6=j

Since φ preserves ⊗h as ∧p , orthogonality (small dh ) maps to logical inde-
pendence (small dp ), directly bounding the PLN inference error by the scaled
hypervector crosstalk error.



                                              284
28.8     Separation Score Correspondence and Hypervector-
         Driven Search for Reliable Proofs
This section introduces a separation score that quantifies how cleanly the se-
mantic components of a hypervector embedding are separated (i.e. how little
they interfere), and shows how maximizing this score in hypervector space di-
rectly yields inference rules in PLN with maximal reliability, in the sense of
minimal dependence on unjustified independence assumptions. We define the
separation score as a normalized sum of pairwise non-interaction terms, then
use a quantale morphism to carry it into the PLN product-quantale, where it
measures minimal independence-assumption error.
    This is interesting in itself, and also as a study in the flexibility of the
quantale weakness framework itself.

28.8.1    Hypervector Separation Score
For a Hierarchical Modular Hypervector embedding, let each named slot si carry
weight vi ∈ [0, 1]. Define the raw crosstalk:
                                                      
                                X                     n
                      wh =            vi vj , Wmax =     .
                                                      2
                                1≤i<j≤n

We then introduce the separation score
                                        wh        1 X
                       Sh = 1 −             = 1 − n    vi vj ,
                                       Wmax       2 i<j


so that Sh = 1 when all slots are perfectly orthogonal (vi vj = 0) and decreases
as interference grows. We seek embeddings that maximize Sh .

28.8.2    PLN Reliability Score
Consider a PLN rule with premise truth-values t1 , . . . , tn ∈ [0, 1]. Define
                     X                              wp        1 X
           wp =              ti tj ,    Sp = 1 −        = 1 − n    ti tj .
                                                   Wmax       2 i<j
                   1≤i<j≤n

Here Sp measures the rule’s reliability: higher Sp indicates fewer implicit inde-
pendence assumptions and thus broader applicability.

28.8.3    Quantale Morphism and Score Equivalence
Introduce a quantale morphism φ mapping hypervector slot weights to PLN
truth-values:
                                               P          P
           φ(vi ) = ti , φ(vi vj ) = ti tj , φ  i<j vi vj = i<j ti tj .




                                            285
Since φ(Wmax ) = Wmax , it follows that
                1 X                   1   X            φ(wh )
     Sp = 1 −           ti tj = 1 −     φ    vi vj = 1 −        = Sh .
              Wmax i<j              Wmax i<j             Wmax

Thus any embedding that maximizes the hypervector separation score Sh pro-
duces a PLN rule with exactly the same maximal reliability score Sp .
    As well as a useful result in itself, this is an interesting case study in the se-
mantics of weakness and generalization. The raw crosstalk is a formal weakness
measure, so that a large raw crosstalk implies strong generalization in a certain
sense: strong ability to generalize to predict the dependency between entities.
However this sort of generalization is actually anticorrelated with robust gen-
eralization at the level of PLN inferences on the entities in question. None of
this is surprising, it just requires careful thinking; the practical semantics of
quantale weakness obviously depends on the quantale in question.

28.8.4    Hypervector-Driven Proof Search
Proof discovery can then proceed by maximizing Sh :
   1. Parametrize slot weights vi (θ) for a candidate conclusion embedding.
   2. Compute Sh (θ) = 1 − n1
                                 P
                                       v (θ) vj (θ).
                            ( 2 ) i<j i
   3. Optimize θ∗ = arg maxθ Sh (θ) using gradient methods or heuristic search.
   4. Map the optimized weights vi (θ∗ )
   5. Execute the PLN inference using the rule with generality score Sp = Sh ,
      ensuring minimal independence-assumption error.
    By shifting search into continuous hypervector space and maximizing the
separation score, we leverage efficient vector operations and ANN retrieval to
find the most general, confident inference rules in PLN without exhaustive log-
ical enumeration.

Concrete Example: Multi-Premise Separation-Driven Rule Discovery
We show how maximizing a separation score among multiple candidate premises
in hypervector space leads to selecting the most reliable inference rule in PLN.

   Candidate Premises Two potential explanations for Possess(Customer, Pizza):
                     P1 : Deliver(Pizza, Customer),         t1 = 0.9,
                     P2 : Pay(Customer, Invoice),         t2 = 0.8.

    Hypervector Embeddings and Slot Weights Each premise Pi has an
HMH embedding Hi . We assign a slot-weight vi ∈ [0, 1] proportional to its
cosine similarity with the conclusion embedding:
         v1 = ρ( Hdeliver , Hpossess ) = 0.8,    v2 = ρ( Hpay , Hpossess ) = 0.5.


                                           286
   Separation Score Objective With two slots, raw crosstalk is

                            wh = v1 v2 = 0.8 × 0.5 = 0.40,

and the separation-weakness sum-of-products is

                          ws = 1 − v1 v2 = 0.60,      Wmax = 1.

Thus the hypervector separation score is
                                          ws

                                         Wmax
To allow blending, introduce a ∈ [0, 1]:
                                                                                

We seek a∗ = arg max Sh (a).
                     a


   Optimizing Separation
                                           
              Sh (a) = 1 − 0.8 a 0.5 (1 − a) = 1 − 0.4 a (1 − a).

This is maximized at a = 0 or a = 1, giving Sh = 1. We compare the two
pure-premise options.

   Mapping to PLN Reliability Score Under the quantale morphism
