We interpret Sp as the reliability score of the rule (higher = more reliable).
Both rules have Sp = 1, so we break ties by original premise strengths ti .

   Final Inference         Select the rule with higher ti . For P1 ,

       Deliver ⇒ Possess,       reliability = 1,     tpossess = min(0.9, 1) = 0.9.

For P2 , tpossess = min(0.8, 1) = 0.8. Thus the ”Deliver” rule wins, yielding a
final conclusion confidence of 0.9.
    By maximizing the separation score in hypervector space, we automatically
identify and rank the most reliable PLN inference rule without exhaustive logical
enumeration.




                                          287
28.9     Bridging Episodic, Procedural, and Declarative Mem-
         ory
In the PRIMUS cognitive architecture, three memory systems–declarative, episodic,
and procedural–play complementary roles. By leveraging a shared declarative
ontology and Hierarchical Modular Hypervectors (HMH), we can achieve elegant
cross-module retrieval via ANN lookups.
    We describe idea here in a fairly schematic way; actual implementations will
involve further particulars.

28.9.1   Memory Storage Architectures
   • Declarative Memory (AtomSpace): Facts, concepts, and relations are
     stored explicitly as Atoms in MORK’s AtomSpace. Each Atom carries a
     VectorID linking to its HMH embedding in the vector store.
   • Procedural Memory (Hybrid): High-level procedure schemas (e.g.
     ”MakePizza”) reside as sequences of ActionLinks and RoleLinks in Atom-
     Space. Only the low-level motor patterns (e.g. precise joint trajectories)
     are stored externally as raw time-series data or motor-hypervectors.
   • Episodic Memory (Hybrid): Episode skeletons–temporal and causal
     structure of events–are encoded as subgraph patterns in AtomSpace. De-
     tailed perceptual data (images, sounds, sensor streams) remain in special-
     ized media stores, referenced by PerceptLinks from the episode atoms.

28.9.2   Hypervector Encoding and Cross-Module Links
Each memory trace obtains an HMH embedding that binds its declarative
schema to its sensory or motor components:

             Hepisode = Hschema ⊕ (RolePercept ⊗ HperceptData ),
             Hprocedure = Hschema ⊕ (RoleMotor ⊗ HmotorData ).
    Here Hschema is the concatenated module for the episode or procedure type
(inherited from the declarative ontology), while the RolePercept and RoleMotor
slots link to external data hypervectors.

28.9.3   ANN-Based Retrieval with Ontology Guidance
  1. Cue Encoding: A retrieval cue (e.g. a partial scene description or de-
     sired skill) is mapped to an HMH query vector hq via the same ontology
     modules.
  2. Symbolic Pre-filtering: AtomSpace pattern queries narrow candidates
     by high-level constraints (e.g. ”episodes involving Pizza” or ”procedures
     requiring Oven”).



                                     288
  3. Vector Search: The filtered VectorIDs feed into an ANN index over
     HMH embeddings. Sublinear search returns the top-k matching episode
     or procedure embeddings.
  4. Reconstruction: Retrieved hypervectors unbind via ⊗−1 to recover linked
     perceptual or motor data, then remap back to AtomSpace for further sym-
     bolic reasoning or replay.

28.9.4   Cognitive Synergy
Using the declarative ontology to structure HMH embeddings yields a unified
retrieval mechanism:

   • Efficiency: Ontology-based pre-filtering reduces the ANN search space
     dramatically.
   • Precision: Named slots ensure that only the relevant perceptual or motor
     component is matched and recovered.

   • Flexibility: The same cue can trigger both episodic recall and procedu-
     ral invocation, depending on which module slots dominate the similarity
     score.

   This tight integration of symbolic schemas and vector-based associations
enables seamless memory lookup across declarative, episodic, and procedural
systems, embodying a powerful, ontology-grounded cognitive synergy.

28.10     Conclusion
We have introduced the Hierarchical Modular Hypervector (HMH) framework,
which combines type-based hierarchical named functional embeddings with mod-
ular compositional hypervectors to yield fixed’size, interpretable, noise-tolerant
representations. We demonstrated advanced semantic pattern matching via
binding and unbinding, a hybrid symbolic/vector architecture cross-linking Atom-
Space with an ANN index, detailed algorithmic components, and formal theo-
retical guarantees on capacity, noise robustness, and binding/unbinding accu-
racy. We then showed how a quantale morphism links hypervector orthogonal-
ity to logical independence in PLN, including a normalized separation-weakness
sum-of-products measure whose maximization yields high-reliability inference
rules, and illustrated how HMH embeddings can bridge episodic, procedural,
and declarative memory in PRIMUS.
    Beyond PLN guidance, the hierarchical hypervector quantale appears to
merit further exploration as a general substrate for embedding-based cognitive
operation control throughout the PRIMUS architecture:

   • Evolutionary Search (MOSES): Quantale joins and meets can inform pro-
     gram evolution fitness by measuring semantic module interactions.



                                      289

   • Decision-Making (MetaMo): Separation scores may be used to guide se-
     lection of action’schema hypervectors with minimal interference between
     competing heuristics.
   • Predictive Coding: Role-filler quantale operations support rapid hypothe-
     sis generation and pruning via vector’space projections.
   • Memory Systems Integration: As we have shown, the same ontology-
     aligned modules orchestrate retrieval across declarative, episodic, and pro-
     cedural memory using ANN lookups. This orchestration may be fleshed
     out further via deeper models of memory modules, and extended further
     e.g. to memory stores focused on sensation, action and attention.
    In sum, the HMH quantale not only unifies symbolic and vector representa-
tions but also enables principled, embedding-driven guidance of diverse cognitive
processes in PRIMUS – a potentially powerful tool helping pave the way toward
a cohesive, scalable, and interpretable AI system.


29     Chaining Weaknesses Together: Predictive
       Coding with Information Geometry for Deep
       Kernel Learning
In this section we walk through a somewhat more elaborate example of the
application of weakness to AI, involving the chaining together of three sorts of
weakness associated with three different interoperating AI algorithms: SVM,
and predictive coding neural nets used for learning a kernel function for an
SVM, and information geometry used for guiding learning in the predictive
coding neural net.
    To understand why this combination makes sense, recall that practical uti-
lization of SVM depends heavily on choosing the right kernel function. The
old-style approach was to manually choose a kernel function appropriate for
one’s application and then adjust its parameters empirically, e.g. by a grid
search. These days, however, it’s been recognized that one can get more robust



                                      290
results via learning a whole kernel function, e.g. in the form of a deep neural
network.
    This is known as ”deep kernel learning” [WHSX16] – a technique that com-
bines the representational power of deep neural networks with the theoretical
strengths of kernel methods. In essence, one uses a neural network to learn a
mapping of inputs into a feature space, and then applies a kernel (e.g. Gaussian)
in that learned feature space to perform tasks such as classification or regression.
    Most work on deep kernel learning so far has involved learning kernel func-
tions using the backpropagation algorithm. However, it is also interesting to
think about alternatives such as predictive coding – a biologically inspired frame-
work in which each layer of a network attempts to predict the activity of the
layer below, and the resulting prediction errors drive both inference (computing
latent representations) and learning (updating network weights). This local,
error-driven scheme avoids backpropagation’s global credit assignment and can
lead to more stable and interpretable learning dynamics.
    Information geometry adds another layer of refinement to neural learning
by recognizing that probability distributions (and the parameters that define
them) lie on a curved manifold rather than in a flat Euclidean space. The
natural gradient [CL20] accounts for this curvature by preconditioning gradient
updates with the inverse of the Fisher information matrix. As a result, updates
follow the steepest descent direction relative to the manifold’s geometry, often
leading to faster convergence and more stable learning. Information-geometry-
enhanced predictive coding could provide a particularly powerful approach to
deep kernel learning.

29.0.1   Deep Kernel Learning
A foundational concept for SVM theory is the:
Definition 6 (Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS)). Let XX ⊂ RRd be
an input space. A function k : XX × XX − RR is a positive definite kernel if for
any finite set {xi }ni=1 ⊂ XX, the Gram matrix

                                  Kij = k(xi , xj )

is positive semidefinite. Associated to k is a Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space
HHk of functions f : XX − RR such that:
  1. For each x ∈ XX, the function k(·, x) ∈ HHk .
  2. For all f ∈ HHk and x ∈ XX, the reproducing property holds:

                                 f (x) = hf, k(·, x)iHHk .

   This lets us define a
Definition 7 (Deep Kernel). Let φθ : XX − RRp be a feature mapping im-
plemented by a neural network with parameters θ. Given a base kernel k0 :


                                        291
RRp × RRp − RR (e.g. Gaussian exp(−kφ − φ0 k2 /σ 2 )), the deep kernel is de-
fined as
                     kθ (x, x0 ) = k0 φθ (x), φθ (x0 ) .
                                                      

This defines a kernel on XX whose geometry and similarity measure adapt to
data via θ.
   A supervised learning task (e.g. classification) with labels yi ∈ {−1, +1}
proceeds by solving in HHkθ a regularized risk minimization, for instance the
SVM primal problem:
                                                    n
                                    1              X
                                      kf k2HHk + C
                                                                         
                     min                               ` yi , f (xi ) + b ,
               f ∈HHkθ , b∈RR       2         θ
                                                   i=1
              where `(y, t) = max{0, 1 − y t} is the hinge loss.
One solves this by kernel methods once θ is fixed; simultaneously, one can update
θ to improve generalization.

29.0.2   Predictive Coding Framework
We briefly review the basics of one sort of predictive coding neural network,
which we suspect can be helpful for deep kernel learning.
   Predictive coding, in one simple form, posits that each layer in a hierarchy
both generates predictions of the layer below and receives prediction errors from
that layer. Let us index layers by ` = 1, . . . , L, where layer 0 is the input. For
each layer `:
   • Let r` ∈ RRn` be the activation vector or latent representation at layer `.
   • Let W` ∈ RRn` ×n`−1 and b` ∈ RRn` be the feedforward weights and biases
     mapping from layer ` − 1 to `.
   • Define the generative prediction for layer ` − 1 as
                              r̂`−1 = g W`− pr` + b` ,
                                                     

      where g is an activation function (e.g. identity or nonlinear).
   • The prediction error at layer ` − 1 is
                                           e`−1 = r`−1 − r̂`−1 .

   Inference proceeds by adjusting r` to minimize the total error:
                                           L
                                           X 1
                                                     k r`−1 − g W`− pr` + b` k22 .
                                                                            
               Einf (r1 , . . . , rL ) =
                                                 2
                                           `=1

                                            ∂Einf

                                             ∂r`

minimize prediction error over data:

29.0.3   Information Geometry and Natural Gradient
Information geometry treats a parametric family of probability distributions
{p(y | x; θ)} as a Riemannian manifold. The Fisher information matrix F (θ)
defines a metric tensor on this manifold:
                              h                                      i
                 F (θ) = EEx,y ∇θ ln p(y | x; θ) ∇θ ln p(y | x; θ)− p .

Definition 8 (Natural Gradient). The natural gradient of an objective L(θ) is
defined as
                           ˜ θ L = F (θ)−1 ∇θ L,
                           ∇
where ∇θ L is the ordinary Euclidean gradient. An update step using natural
gradient is
                     θ ← θ−η∇ ˜ θ L = θ − η F (θ)−1 ∇θ L.

This corresponds to steepest descent on the curved information manifold.
    In practice, one approximates F (θ) (e.g. by a block-diagonal or low-rank
estimate) to make inversion feasible.
    One can also do predictive coding using other metrics than Fisher, e.g. the
Wasserstein optimal-transport metric. The math is different and there may be
practical advantages in various contexts, but the basic concept is roughly the
same.

29.0.4   Combining Predictive Coding and Natural Gradient in Deep
         Kernel Learning
We now integrate the above elements into a cohesive algorithm for deep kernel
learning.

Joint Learning Objective Our goal is to learn θ so as to minimize the
SVM’s generalization error. We define a surrogate objective J(θ) proportional
to the SVM dual objective (or its regularized estimate). For simplicity, consider
the SVM margin in feature space:
                                                         n
                         yi fα∗ ,θ (xi )                 X
         γ(θ) = min                       , where w(θ) =     αi∗ yi φθ (xi ).
               i=1,...,n    kw(θ)k2                      i=1

We wish to maximize γ(θ) (i.e. find a weak classifier with large margin). Equiv-
alently, we minimize
                                 L(θ) = − γ(θ).
This objective depends on θ both through φθ (x) in the kernel and through α∗ ,
which itself depends on θ. In practice, one approximates α∗ as fixed in each
update, yielding a bi-level optimization.




                                             294
Natural Gradient Update for θ We interpret θ as parameters of the predic-
tive coding model that defines a probability distribution over features or latent
variables given data. Let p(r | x; θ) be the implicit distribution over top-layer
activations r = rL (x). The Fisher information matrix is
                                    h                                     i
        F (θ) = EEx∼DD EEr∼p(·|x;θ) ∇θ ln p(r | x; θ) ∇θ ln p(r | x; θ)− p .

We compute the gradient of L(θ), ∇θ L, by differentiating the SVM margin with
respect to φθ and then backpropagating through the predictive coding inference.
Using the natural gradient, the update is

                           θ ← θ − ηθ F (θ)−1 ∇θ L(θ),

where ηθ > 0 is a learning rate. This ensures that the update respects the
underlying statistical manifold of the predictive coding model, leading to more
stable adaptation of the deep kernel.

Summary of Advantages             Why might one want to do things this way?

   • Local, biologically plausible inference: Predictive coding layers ad-
     just their activations r` via local prediction-error minimization, avoiding
     backpropagation’s global pass.
   • Manifold-corrected weight updates: Natural gradient uses F (θ) to
     precondition ∇θ L, ensuring parameter steps follow the steepest descent
     on the probability manifold rather than Euclidean space.
   • Large-margin deep kernel: By maximizing γ(θ), we ensure the learned
     kernel yields a weak (large-margin) classifier, which in turn reduces VC
     dimension and improves generalization.
   • Faster convergence and robustness: Combining predictive coding’s
     rapid error correction with natural gradient’s curvature-aware steps yields
     efficient learning of θ.

29.1     Three Kinds of Weakness: An Elegant Synergy
This section has gone fairly deep down a particular rabbit hole regarding deep
kernel function learning – let us now emerge back up the surface and take a
high level view. What led us to explore this rabbit hold was an intuition that
combining predictive coding with information geometry for deep kernel learning
offers a principled route to learning kernels that yield large-margin classifiers.
Predictive coding provides fast, local inference of layer activations, ensuring
that feature representations adapt rapidly to data. Information geometry, via
natural gradient updates, ensures that kernel parameters θ move in a way that
respects the underlying statistical structure, leading to robust, stable, and effi-
cient convergence. The resulting deep kernel yields an SVM classifier with high
weakness (large margin) and provable generalization bounds, thereby uniting
neural, probabilistic, and geometric perspectives in a unified learning frame-
work.
    What we would like to point out now, by way of summary, is that this
synergy has an elegant formal underpinning. We now show how three ostensibly
different ”weakness” criteria–entropy-based in predictive coding (PC), Fisher-
metric based in information geometry (IG), and margin-based in SVMs–fit into
a single categorical weakness framework via different quantale enrichments and
monoidal composition.


                                         296
 Predictive-Coding Weakness In predictive coding one maintains a gener-
ative model pθ (z, x) and infers latent activations z given input x by minimizing
prediction error. A high-entropy posterior q(z | x) means the model remains
uncertain–i.e. it is weak about which latent causes generated x.
    Equip the latent-space category with the quantale

                        VPC = [0, 1],    ⊗ = ×,        ⊕ = +,

and measure µPC (x) by the Shannon entropy
                                      X
             wPC (x) = H q(z | x) = −     q(z | x) log q(z | x).
                                                 z

Then wPC (x) is exactly the categorical weakness of the event ”latent explanation
under q for x,” viewed in the product space (z, z 0 ).

  Information-Geometric Weakness Natural-gradient updates move pa-
rameters θ along geodesics of (in the simplest case) the Fisher information met-
ric, thereby changing the model as little as possible in distributional terms. A
small Fisher-distance step is a weak change of hypothesis.
    On the parameter manifold we use the quantale

                     VIG = [0, ∞],      ⊗ = min,       ⊕ = sup,

and define for any candidate update ∆θ
                                         1                      −1
                    wIG (∆θ) = p                       = ∆θ F .
                                   ∆θ− pF (θ) ∆θ

where F (θ) is the Fisher matrix. Maximizing wIG yields the natural-gradient
direction of least informational strength.

 Margin (SVM) Weakness An SVM decision boundary that maximizes the
geometric margin is the weakest separation: it excludes the smallest neighbor-
hood of counter-examples while still classifying the data correctly.
   As before, take

                    VSVM = [0, ∞],      ⊗ = min,        ⊕ = sup,

and for a hyperplane w, x 7→ sign(wT x) define

                                                 yi wT xi
                            wSVM (w) = inf                ,
                                             i      |w|

the usual hard-margin. Maximizing wSVM yields the largest-weakness (max-
margin) classifier.




                                        297
 Monoidal Composition of Weakness Each stage in this process – repre-
sentation, parameter update, and classifier – fibers into its own enriched cate-
gory with weakness measure wPC , wIG , wSVM . We form the composite quantale

                          Vtotal = VPC ⊗ VIG ⊗ VSVM ,

with ⊗ acting component-wise and ⊕ taking the joint supremum. Then the
overall weakness is

                  wtotal = wPC (x) ⊗ wIG (∆θ) ⊗ wSVM (w).

Choosing updates and kernels that maximize wtotal yields:
   • High-entropy latent representations (PC),
   • Minimal informational change in parameters (IG),
   • Large geometric margin (SVM).
    In sum: By viewing each weakness as a morphism weight in a monoidal cate-
gory, we see formally how neural (predictive-coding), probabilistic (Fisher), and
geometric (margin) criteria chain together: each stage adds a causal subobject
whose weakness is measured in its own quantale, and their monoidal product
yields a single objective that unites all three perspectives.

29.2     Monoidal Composition of Weakness and Unified Hy-
         perparameter Tuning
In this section we show how viewing the three weakness measures – predictive-
coding weakness (wPC ), information-geometric weakness (wIG ), and SVM-margin
weakness (wSVM ) – as a single monoidal composite

                       wcomb = wPC ⊗ wIG ⊗ wSVM

yields both conceptual clarity and practical benefits for hyperparameter tuning
across the entire deep-kernel/SVM apparatus.

29.2.1    Single Scalar Objective for Search
By optimizing wcomb directly, one replaces separate searches over PC inference
rates, Fisher damping factors, and SVM regularization constants with a sin-
gle unified goal. Raising wcomb guarantees non-degradation in any component
weakness due to the commutativity and monotonicity of ⊗.

29.2.2    Evolutionary Tuning with Convergence Bounds
Treat hyperparameter configurations as individuals in an evolutionary algorithm
with fitness wcomb . The CoDD Weakness then ensures that, under monoidal
crossover and selection proportional to weakness, schema instances evolve ex-
ponentially fast toward maximal weakness.


                                      298
29.2.3    Gradient-Based PC Hyperparameter Updates
Predictive-coding learning rules can be shown to minimize the PC weakness
term directly. Hence one can apply standard gradient optimizers on PC rate
parameters (inference learning rate, weight-update rate, lateral strength) with
respect to the composite weakness loss,

                   ∇η wcomb = ∇η wPC ⊗ wIG ⊗ wSVM ,

allowing online adaptation of PC hyperparameters in lockstep with IG and SVM
components.

29.2.4    Geometry-Aware Learning-Rate Scheduling
The IG term’s Fisher-metric foundation yields learning-rate invariance to reparametriza-
tions. By tying the IG step-size αIG and the PC inference rate αPC so that
their contributions to wcomb remain balanced, one avoids imbalance where one
modality dominates the joint objective.
    The overall argument here would be that monoidal composition of weakness
not only unifies neural, probabilistic, and geometric perspectives but also en-
ables provably convergent, efficient, and elegant hyperparameter tuning across
the full deep-kernel and SVM learning pipeline.

29.2.5   MDL versus Quantale-Weakness for Unified Hyperparameter
         Tuning
One would be justified to wonder: Why not recast the monoidal-composition
tuning framework in MDL terms rather than dealing with weakness– i.e. measure
the total code length of:
  1. the predictive-coding inference schedule and parameters,
  2. the Fisher-metric step sizes,
  3. the final SVM weight vector,

and then minimize the sum of those three code-lengths.
   However, the weakness formalism offers several practical advantages:

  1. Algebraic Simplicity. Weakness lives in a commutative quantale (V, ⊗, ⊕),
     so
                       wcomb = wPC ⊗ wIG ⊗ wSVM
     is automatically associative, commutative, and monotonic. An MDL ap-
     proach would require designing a joint universal code for three distinct
     “languages” (PC schedules, manifold steps, SVM weights) and proving
     that bit-counts compose cleanly.




                                      299
  2. Continuous Gradients. Quantale-enriched weakness yields a smooth
     penalty term with closed-form gradients for all continuous hyperparame-
     ters. True MDL code lengths are integer bit-counts and non-differentiable;
     smoothing them via Shannon-Fano approximations essentially recreates a
     continuous regularizer equivalent to a weakness penalty.
  3. Unified Scale and Interpretability. Each weakness term wPC , wIG , wSVM
     lives in the same algebraic domain V , so one can directly compare con-
     tributions and know that improving any single term raises the composite
     score. MDL bits for parameters, errors, and margins may require addi-
     tional normalization weights to balance across scales.
     In summary, although MDL provides a principled complexity-versus-fit trade-
off, the quantale-weakness framework embeds that trade-off in a leaner algebraic
structure, yields smooth gradients, and inherits provable convergence properties
for unified hyperparameter tuning.


30     Combining SVM and PLN for Inference Con-
       trol
We now connect some dots between SVMs and PLN, providing an additional
example of the combination of weakness-guided AI methods leveraging weakness
defined over different quantales.
    We have reviewed above one of the problems arising in PLN inference control:
choosing which inference rule or “rewrite” to apply at each step so as to reach
a conclusion efficiently. In other words, we need a heuristic that tells us:
   • Which candidate inference step has the best chance of leading to a valid
     conclusion with minimal work,
   • And which such step remains “weak” enough (i.e. broadly applicable) to
     avoid overcommitting prematurely.
    Support Vector Machines (SVMs), as also reviewed above, provide a prin-
cipled way to rank candidates by learning a decision boundary in a feature
space, effectively estimating whether a given rewrite will be low-cost and high-
weakness. Concretely, we can:
  1. Construct a feature vector for each possible rewrite in the current PLN
     state (for example, features that capture how “tight” the rewrite’s con-
     straints are, how many subgoals it produces, and so on).
  2. Train an SVM to predict which rewrites historically led to efficient, gen-
     eralizable inference-thus learning a notion of “weakness” (large margin)
     and “low cost.”
  3. At inference time, for each candidate rewrite we compute its feature vector
     and use the SVM’s output to choose the rewrite that lies furthest on the
     “desirable” side of the margin.


                                      300
    This hybrid approach leverages PLN’s probabilistic logic to generate train-
ing labels (which rewrites succeeded quickly and generally), while using SVM’s
margin-maximization to generalize and guide future inference. Thus PLN in-
forms the SVM, and the SVM accelerates PLN’s search, resulting in markedly
faster inference overall (or at least that’s the idea!).
    We now review this approach in more detail.

30.1     PLN Inference and Rewrite Candidates
We begin by recalling the PLN inference setting. Let

                               SSS = {PLN states}

be the set of all possible inference states in PLN. Each state s ∈ SSS consists
of:
   • A set of goals or subgoals to prove, each represented by a logical formula
     or probability distribution.
   • A set of known facts or intermediate conclusions, with associated proba-
     bilities.
At state s, there is a finite set of candidate rewrites

                                    RRs ⊆ RR,

where RR is the global set of all inference rules in the PLN system. Each
rewrite r ∈ RRs transforms s into a new state s0 = r(s) by invoking one or
more inference rules (e.g. probabilistic deduction, induction, abduction).
   In this context, we then wish to learn a predictor that scores each candidate
rewrite r in state s according to a combination of:
   • Cost, denoted c(s, r), measuring (or estimating) the expected number of
     additional inference steps (or time) needed after applying r.
   • Weakness, denoted w(s, r), measuring how broadly applicable (general)
     the resulting state r(s) remains.
    Rather than requiring exact values c(s, r) and w(s, r), we represent each pair
(s, r) by a feature vector

                φ(s, r) = φ1 (s, r), φ2 (s, r), . . . , φd (s, r) ∈ RRd ,
                                                                 

where each coordinate is a real-valued feature, for example:
  1. φ1 (s, r): the number of new subgoals generated by applying r.
  2. φ2 (s, r): the average prior probability of newly introduced facts in r(s).
  3. φ3 (s, r): a measure of rule specificity (e.g. number of literal constraints in
     r’s pattern).


                                        301
   4. φ4 (s, r): the incremental change in the GSLT-graph diameter or ’spread-
      after r is applied.
   5. φ5 (s, r): a count of how many previously-unseen predicates appear in r(s).
   6. . . .

We assume φ is chosen so that higher values of certain linear combinations
correspond to lower cost and higher weakness.

30.2          SVM for PLN Inference Control: Linear Case
We now formulate the SVM learning problem involved in PLN inference control.
For simplicity we will initially assume a straightforward linear SVM but we will
then show things work basically the same if one uses a learned deep kernel as
outlined above.
   We gather a training set
                                                                                     N
                      DD =       ( φ(si , ri1 ), yi1), . . . , (φ(si , rimi ), yimi ) i=1 ,

where for each historical inference state si :
   • {ri1 , . . . , ri,mi } = RRsi are the candidate rewrites at si .

   • For each rij , we assign a label yij ∈ {−1, +1}, where +1 indicates that
     rewrite rij was preferred (i.e. led to low cost and high weakness), and −1
     indicates it was not preferred.
Definition 9 (Linear SVM). A linear SVM seeks a weight vector w ∈ RRd and
bias b ∈ RR that solve:
                       1           X
              min        kwk22 + C     ξij ,
              w,b,ξ    2           i,j

                             yij wT φ(si , rij ) + b
                                                         
              subject to                                     ≥ 1 − ξij ,      ξij ≥ 0, ∀i, j.

Here ξij are slack variables, and C > 0 is a regularization parameter. The
solution (w∗ , b∗ ) yields a decision function

                           h(s, r) = sgn w∗−p φ(s, r) + b∗ ,
                                                          


which returns +1 for -preferable- rewrites.
Remark. The SVM objective 21 kwk22 enforces a large margin between preferred
and nonpreferred rewrite examples. Thus SVM is learning, in effect, a notion of
weakness (large margin implies broad generality) and low cost (since we label
examples according to observed efficiency).




                                                      302
Inference Control with the SVM Predictor At run time, for a new PLN
state s, we:
  1. Enumerate the candidate rewrites RRs = {r1 , . . . , rm }.
  2. Compute φ(s, rj ) for each j = 1, . . . , m.
  3. Evaluate the SVM score

                       fj = w∗−p φ(s, rj ) + b∗ ,      j = 1, . . . , m.

  4. Select
                                   r∗ = arg max fj ,
                                           j=1,...,m

     that is, the rewrite with the highest SVM output, which corresponds to
     large margin (weakness) and low cost (as per training).
  5. Apply r∗ to transition to the next state s0 = r∗ (s). Observe the actual
     cost and weakness outcome, and add (φ(s, r∗ ), +1) to the training set
     if it performed well, or (φ(s, r∗ ), −1) if it did not-thus enabling online
     refinement.

PLN/SVM Integration Loop              Formally, the combined algorithm we pro-
pose here proceeds as follows:

  1. Initialization:
        • Start with an empty or bootstrap training set DD.
        • Solve the initial SVM on DD, obtaining (w∗ , b∗ ).
        • Set the current PLN state s0 to the initial query (e.g. -Explain data
          set D-).
  2. Iterative Inference: For t = 0, 1, 2, . . .:
      (a) Candidate Generation: Let RRst ⊆ RR be the set of applicable
          rewrites at state st .
      (b) Feature Computation: For each r ∈ RRst , compute φt (r) = φ(st , r).
      (c) SVM Scoring: Compute

                           ft (r) = w∗−p φt (r) + b∗ ,      ∀ r ∈ RRst .

      (d) Selection: Choose
                                     rt∗ = arg max ft (r).
                                            r∈RRst

      (e) Application: Apply rt∗ , obtaining new state

                                        st+1 = rt∗ (st ).


                                        303
       (f) Observation: Measure the actual cost ct of reaching a partial con-
           clusion from st+1 , and the actual weakness wt (e.g. margin-based
           generality). Define a label:
                         (               
                           +1, if ct , wt meets performance criteria,
                    yt =
                           −1, otherwise.

       (g) Training Update: Append φt (rt∗ ), yt to DD. Periodically (e.g. every
                                                

           K steps), retrain the SVM on the updated DD to obtain a new
           (w∗ , b∗ ).
       (h) Termination: If st+1 satisfies the inference goal (e.g. success in prov-
           ing or sufficiently high posterior probability), halt.

   This approach yields the following interesting properties:

Margin Guarantees By construction, the SVM maximizes the margin be-
tween positive and negative examples in the feature space. A larger margin
implies better generalization from DD to unseen states. In practice, this means
that the SVM will robustly predict which rewrites are likely to be cost-effective
and weak enough, even for novel PLN states.

Convergence of Inference Control Under mild assumptions on the diver-
sity of training examples and the expressiveness of φ, the online-updated SVM
will converge to a stable decision boundary. Consequently, the inference control
policy converges to selecting near-optimal rewrites in the long run. A formal
statement can be derived from standard online learning bounds (e.g. perceptron
or stochastic SVM convergence guarantees).

Feature Design and Weakness Encoding Choosing the feature map φ is
crucial. To encode weakness, one might include features such as:
   • The support of a rewrite-how many data points or partial proofs it matches.
   • A margin-like estimate, e.g. the distance between most restrictive con-
     straints in the resulting subgoal.

   • The entropy of the posterior distribution induced by the rewrite.
These capture how broad or narrow the rewrite’s effect is, enabling the SVM to
approximate ”weakness” in a linear feature space.

30.3     SVM for PLN Inference Control: Nonlinear Case
We now repeat the above analysis for the SVM learning problem in its nonlinear
(kernel) form, focusing on the differences from the linear case presented above.

                                     ψθ : (s, r) 7−→ RD ,
e.g. the activations of a neural network with weights θ. Define the associated

new PLN state s:

Margin Guarantees All margin-based generalization bounds extend to RKHS
margins: a larger margin in the feature space ψθ still implies better control over
generalization error, provided one regularizes kψθ k via R(θ).

Feature Design Now ψθ (s, r) can be a deep encoder of both symbolic and
sub-symbolic aspects of the rewrite, letting the SVM kernel adaptively learn a
nonlinear notion of ”weakness+cost” that goes beyond any fixed linear feature
map.

30.4     Categorical Integration of SVM and PLN Weakness:
         Linear Case
We now explain how our proposed hybridization of SVM and PLN emerges
naturally from the categorial approach to weakness. We model the two forms
of weakness as functors out of their native categories, then combine them in a
product category and equip the result with a ”weakest-first” ordering.

Base Categories and Weakness Functors
   • Clin : objects (V, H), a real vector space V with hyperplane H; morphisms
     are affine maps preserving the hyperplane.
                                                                                 1
                    Wlin : (Clin )op −→ (R≥0 , ≤),           Wlin (V, H) =          ,
                                                                               γ(H)

       where γ(H) is the SVM margin.
   • Cprob : objects (Ω, F), a sample space Ω with ”proof events” F; morphisms
     are measure-preserving maps.
                                                                     [ 
          Wprob : (Cprob )op −→ ([0, 1], ≤), Wprob (Ω, F) = 1 − Pr     F .




                                               306
Product Category and Combined Weakness Form the product

                             Ccomb = Clin × Cprob ,

with projection functors π1 and π2 . Define

           Wcomb = µ ◦ Wlin × Wprob : (Ccomb )op −→ (R≥0 , ≤),
                                       


where µ(x, y) = x · y (or min(x, y), etc.) gives a single weakness measure.

Commutative Diagram
                             π1              Wlin
                 Ccomb              Clin              (R≥0 , ≤)

                  π2                                      µ



                  Cprob            Wprob
                                                      ([0, 1], ≤)

Here the square commutes once both (R≥0 , ≤) and ([0, 1], ≤) are identified via
the embedding [0, 1] ,→ R≥0 , and µ fuses the two weakness values.

 Weakest-First Queue in Ccomb Controlling PLN inference with SVM now
amounts to running the usual ”weakest-first” priority queue in the combined
category Ccomb , ordered by Wcomb . Concretely:
                                                            
   1. Each candidate proof state is an object (V, H), (Ω, F) ∈ Ccomb .
  2. Compute its combined weakness
                                                           
                 Wcomb (V, H), (Ω, F) = µ 1/γ(H), 1 − Pr(∪F) .

  3. Insert into a priority queue keyed by this scalar; always expand the object
     with minimal Wcomb – the weakest in the combined sense.

   This construction is elegant because:
   • Modularity: the SVM margin and probabilistic coverage remain defined
     in their native categories.
   • Compositionality: new forms of weakness (e.g. logical, topological) can
     be integrated by forming larger product categories and extending µ.
   • Uniform inference: the proof search algorithm need only compare real
     numbers Wcomb , unifying margin-based and probabilistic heuristics under
     one ordering.




                                      307
30.5     Categorical Extension to Kernel SVM: Nonlinear Case
All of the constructions for the linear SVM carry over verbatim once we observe
that any kernel SVM is a linear SVM in its RKHS. We simply replace the
”linear” category by a ”kernel” category:
   • Define                                   
                                   Ckern =        (HKθ , H) ,
       where HKθ is the RKHS induced by the deep kernel Kθ , and H ⊂ HKθ
       is an affine hyperplane. Morphisms are affine maps preserving the hyper-
       plane.
   • The margin-functor becomes
                                                                                 1
           Wkern : (Ckern )op −→ (RR≥0 , ≤),              Wkern (HKθ , H) =           ,
                                                                              γKθ (H)
       where γKθ (H) is the RKHS margin of H.
   We then form the same product category and combined weakness:
                                                                  
           Ccomb = Ckern × Cprob ,       Wcomb = µ ◦ Wkern × Wprob ,
with µ(x, y) = x · y (or min(x, y), etc.).

Commutative diagram
                              π1                  Wkern
                    Ccomb             Ckern                 (RR≥0 , ≤)

                    π2                                           µ


                    Cprob             Wprob
                                                             ([0, 1], ≤)

Once we identify [0, 1] with its image in RR≥0 , the square commutes and
                                                               
           Wcomb (HKθ , H), (Ω, FF) = µ γK 1(H) , 1 − Pr(∪FF) .
                                      
                                                      θ



Weakest-first queue Inference
                                   control proceeds by inserting each candidate
object (HKθ , H), (Ω, FF) into a priority queue keyed by Wcomb , and always
expanding the object with smallest Wcomb . This is exactly the same ”weakest-

31      Weakness-Based Formalization of Concept Blend-
        ing
Returning to the theme of applying weakness to various aspects of learning and
reasoning, we now explain how the information-theoretic approach to concept
blending presented in [Goe25c] can naturally be expressed in terms of weakness:


                                         308
31.1      Categorical Formulation
Let C be a category of entities (concepts, artifacts, etc.), enriched over a com-
mutative quantale (Q, ≤, ⊗, e). A weakness functor

                            W : C op −→ (Q, ≤, ⊗, e)

assigns to each morphism f : P − E (which we interpret as the instantiation of
”property” P in entity E) a weakness W (f ) ∈ Q. Higher W (f ) means ”harder
to explain,” lower W (f ) means ”simpler/stronger” membership.
    Fix two source objects A, B ∈ C and a candidate blend C ∈ C. Let { xi :
Pi − C}mi=1 be a chosen finite set of salient properties of C. For each i, form the
induced morphisms
                          fi : Pi −→ A, gi : Pi −→ B
(using whatever universal constructions or mediating maps the category pro-
vides). Then define:

Blend-Membership Weakness
                                   1 X  m                        
            µblend (C; A, B) = min e,       max{ W (fi ), W (gi )} .
                                      m i=1

Emergent Weakness
                                 
  µemergence (xi ; A, B, C) = max W (xi : Pi − C) − max{W (fi ), W (gi )}, 0 ,

and weight by
                           wi = min{W (fi ), W (gi )}.

High-Quality Blend
                               1 X  m                              
           µhq (C; A, B) = min e,       wi µemergence (xi ; A, B, C) .
                                  m i=1

31.2     Choices of Weakness Functor
Below are four instances of W landing in Q = [0, 1] with ⊗ = × and e = 1:

  1. Probabilistic Weakness For each property-instantiation morphism f :
     P − E, let                                      
                   Wprob (f ) = Pr f (P ) = Em∼µ 1f (P ) .
                                    m∼µ

  2. Mutual-Information Weakness If I(xi ; E) is the mutual information
     (in bits) between property xi and entity E, set

                                 WMI (f ) = 2− I(xi ;E) .


                                       309
   3. Linear (SVM-Margin) Weakness Given a trained binary SVM classi-
      fier for ”xi ∈ E” with signed margin γ(f ), define

                         Wsvm (f ) = exp −κ γ(f )2 , κ > 0.

      P to projector Pi , let
                                                       √            √ 2

31.3     Example: Blending Bat and Man
We take sources A = Bat, B = Man, and three candidate blends:

C1 = {flight, detective skills},   C2 = {echolocation, altruism},        C3 = {flight, echolocation}.

Here m = 2 for each blend, with properties P1 , P2 varying by Ci .
Weakness Values for Each Measure:
  -prule                     flight                                                   detective
  Measure             Bat                          Man                       Bat                   Man

Qualitative Interpretation
   • Probabilistic Weakness: The blend C2 (echolocation, altruism) has the
     lowest average weakness (0.775), since echolocation is strongly associated
     with bats (0.70) and altruism with humans (0.85). Thus µblend favors
     combining one signature trait of each source.
   • Mutual-Information Weakness: C2 again wins (0.754) because both echolo-
     cation and altruism carry substantial mutual information with their re-
     spective sources, yielding low algorithmic weakness.


                                        310
   • SVM-Margin Weakness: Here C1 (flight, detective skills) is best (0.781),
     as the classifier most confidently separates flight and detective-ability fea-
     tures, giving a low margin-based weakness for that pair.
   • Quantum Fidelity Weakness: Again C1 wins (0.500), since the quantum-
     state fidelity for flight and detective skills in the ”Bat” and ”Man” density
     matrices is highest, leading to lower quantum weakness.
Thus, depending on whether one measures ”weakness” via raw probability,
information-theoretic simplicity, classification margin, or quantum fidelity, dif-
ferent candidate blends emerge as the strongest. This illustrates the flexibility
of the categorical weakness-based blending framework.

         tum Weakness

                          A = Piano,          B = Violin.

             C1 = Electric Piano      (rich harmonics, long sustain),
