31.4.4     Divergent Rankings
   • SVM-Margin Weakness: µblend (C2 ) = 0.60 < 0.75 = µblend (C1 ), so
     the SVM-based measure prefers the Synthesized Hybrid C2 (it lies closer
     to the linear decision boundary between Piano and Violin features).
   • Quantum Fidelity Weakness: µblend (C1 ) = 0.30 < 0.55 = µblend (C2 ),
     so the quantum measure prefers the Electric Piano C1 (it retains higher
     fidelity to the classical Piano and Violin states).

Qualitative Insight. The SVM margin treats features as linearly separa-
ble attributes and favors blends that lie near the boundary (mixed features).
Quantum fidelity, by contrast, respects the full state-space structure (including
coherence) and rewards blends that maintain high overlap with each parent’so
it prefers a blend that is individually closer to both sources.
    This illustrates how quantum weakness can rank blends differently from
classical margin-based measures.
    One can get even more interesting blends by extending weakness beyond the
quantum domain to quaternions or octonions but we will leave this for another
paper!




                                         312
32       Predictive Coding as Weakness-Minimizing
         Learning
The theoretical results of [PMZM25] indicate that predictive coding (PC) based
neural net training can be especially valuable for learning minimum-description-
length models. Here we extend and improve these results via moving from MDL
to weakness, and we explore how this direction of thought can be used to improve
the attention mechanism in transformer neural nets, including purely neural and
symbolic waveformers.

32.1     Initial Predictive Coding Architecture
In this section we specify a precise predictive-coding-based neural architec-
ture and update rule-set, which we will use for convergence and weakness-
minimization analysis. For simplicity, we adopt the canonical hierarchical predictive-
coding model of Rao and Ballard [RB99], implemented as a deep feed-forward
network with local error units. The conceptual conclusions apply beyond this
particular architecture but we need to specify something concrete to get sim-
ple, tractable results. A little later we will extend this simple model to include
lateral connections.

32.1.1    Network Structure
Let the network have L + 1 layers indexed by l = 0, 1, . . . , L, where layer 0 is
the input and layer L the topmost representation. For each input example n,
denote
                (n)                  (n)
               x0 = input datan , xl ∈ Rdl (l = 1, . . . , L)
the activation vectors at each layer. Each layer l maintains two sets of variables:
   • A representation vector xl .
   • A prediction error vector l of the same dimension.
The generative (top-down) mapping from layer l to layer l − 1 is given by
                                   x̂l−1 = fl (Wl xl ),
where Wl ∈ Rdl−1 ×dl are the feed-forward weights (shared between bottom-up
and top-down paths) and fl is a smooth activation (e.g. tanh or ReLU).

32.1.2    Inference (Error Propagation)
At each time step t of the inference phase, activations and errors are updated
to minimize local prediction errors. For each layer l = 1, . . . , L, we compute:
       l−1 = xl−1 − fl (Wl xl )     and xl ← xl + ηx WlT fl0 (Wl xl ) l−1 ,
where fl0 is the elementwise derivative, and ηx > 0 is a small inference learning
rate. The update for xl uses the local prediction error l−1 and the transpose
weights, implementing a local backprop of error.


                                          313
32.1.3   Learning (Weight Update)
After the inference phase has approximately equilibrated the xl and l , the
weights Wl are updated to reduce prediction error and network weakness:
                         h               i
             ∆Wl = − ηw fl0 (Wl xl ) l−1 xTl − ηw λ ∂Wl w(HW ),

where
                      HW = {(i, j) | (Wl )ij 6= 0 for some l}
is the neuron-connection relation, w(HW ) its quantale weakness, and ∂Wl w(HW )
the McBride derivative providing the per-weight sensitivity. Here ηw > 0 is the
weight-learning rate and λ ≥ 0 trades off prediction-error fidelity against sim-
plicity.

32.1.4   Summary of Update Cycle
Each predictive-coding iteration consists of:

  1. Inference sweep: for l = 1, . . . , L, update (xl , l−1 ) as above until conver-
     gence.
  2. Learning sweep: for l = 1, . . . , L, update Wl via the combined prediction-
     error and weakness gradient.

Under mild smoothness and boundedness assumptions on fl and the quantale
operations, this alternating scheme provably decreases the composite loss
                                 X (n)
                       J(W, x) =    kl−1 k2 + λ w(HW ),
                                   l,n

and converges to a blockwise stationary point.

32.2     PC as Descent on a Weakness-Incorporating Objec-
         tive
We show that the standard predictive coding (PC) algorithm in neural networks,
as outlined above, can be viewed as performing block-coordinate descent on
a composite objective that combines prediction-error fidelity with a quantale-
based weakness penalty. This interpretation bypasses the usual route through
minimum description length (MDL) and ties PC directly to our categorical
notion of simplicity.

32.2.1   Network Weakness Measure
Let D be a dataset and consider a feed-forward network with L layers. Denote
                                                              (n)
by θ = (θ1 , . . . , θL ) the layer-wise weight matrices, by xl the activation at




                                         314
layer l for example n, and by fl (θl , xl−1 ) the forward map at layer l. We view
the network’s connectivity as a relation

                 Hθ = {(i, j) | θl,ij 6= 0 for some l} ⊆ N × N,

where N is the set of all neurons. Assign to each neuron i a valuation µ(i)
(for instance, its average activation), and – as a first simple pass– define the
weakness of the network by
                                      M
                          w(Hθ ) =         µ(i) ⊗ µ(j),
                                         (i,j)∈Hθ

with (V, ⊗, ⊕) the chosen quantale.

32.2.2   Composite Loss Function
As a first simple stab in the right direction, we can introduce a combined loss
                         L X
                                     (n)              (n)
                         X
               J(θ) =              kxl     − fl (θl , xl−1 )k2 + λ w(Hθ ),
                         l=1   n

where the first term is the usual sum of squared prediction errors and the second
term penalizes network complexity via weakness.

32.2.3   Leveraging Local Weakness
The above mode for incorporating weakness into PC networks ”works” but has
one serious problem; it violates the PC principle of locality. In the predictive-
coding architecture, penalizing the complexity of the entire network at once can
obscure local credit assignment. Instead, it may be better to regularize only
the active subgraph of connections used in each prediction step. Concretely, let
Ht ⊆ Hθ be the set of synapses and neurons participating in the update at time
t. We replace the global penalty

                                           λ w(Hθ )

with the local penalty                              M
                               λ w(Ht ) = λ                φ(e),
                                                    e∈Ht

where φ(e) is the weight of edge e in the weakness quantale.

Local Loss Formulation Unrolling across timesteps and layers, the com-
bined loss becomes
                      X (t)                      2
                                             (t)
                                                     X       
           L(theta) =   xl−1 − fl (thetal , xl ) + λ   w Ht,l ,
                         t,l                                       t,l

where Ht,l is the active subgraph at layer l and time t.


                                             315
Benefits of Local Weakness
   • Locally Biased Credit Assignment. Each weight update is regularized
     only by the complexity of the subgraph that actually generated the error.
   • Scalability. Computing w(Ht,l ) requires work proportional to the active
     subgraph size, and yields interpretable, per-layer complexity diagnostics.

Implementation in MeTTa/MORK In practice, Ht,l can be defined as
all neurons whose activations or errors exceed a small threshold at layer l, or
simply the receptive-field subgraph. MORK’s PathMap indices allow efficient
extraction of Ht,l , and MeTTa’s quantale-fold routines compute w(Ht,l ) on the
fly. During weight update for layer l, we backpropagate both the prediction-
error gradient and                             
                               λ ∇thetal w Ht,l ,
affecting only parameters in the active neighborhood.
    By focusing the Occam penalty on local neighborhoods, we ensure each up-
date applies the right simplicity bias to the subnetwork that actually contributed
to the prediction, improving both learning fidelity and regularization efficiency.

32.2.4   Predictive Coding Updates as Coordinate Descent
A single PC iteration alternates:
  1. Inference step: update activations {xl } to reduce prediction error,
                                                          
                         xl ← xl − ηx xl − fl (θl , xl−1 ) .

  2. Learning step: update weights θl by

            ∆θl = −ηθ l fl0 (θl , xl−1 ) x>
                                        
                                           l−1 ,    l = xl − fl (θl , xl−1 ).

Under mild smoothness assumptions on fl , each of these substeps decreases
J(θ) if λ is chosen so that the weight update aligns with a descent direction
for w(Hθ ). Thus PC implements an inexact block-coordinate descent on the
composite objective J.

32.2.5   Choice of Neuron Valuation in Weakness-Based Predictive
         Coding
According to the above scheme, the quantale requires a valuation map

                              µ : {neurons} → V

that assigns each neuron a nonnegative ”weight” reflecting its importance. Mul-
tiple criteria guide this choice, e.g.:



                                       316
   • Reflective of Usage. Neurons that contribute more to data reconstruc-
     tion should receive higher valuation.
   • Smoothness. The valuation should vary gradually with small changes in
     activity to avoid brittle weakness scores.

   • Computational Efficiency. It must be updateable online with minimal
     overhead during inference.
   • Semantic Interpretability. The measure should admit a clear proba-
     bilistic or geometric meaning for downstream modules.

Average Activation as a Baseline Average activation over a sliding window
is proposed as an initial rough stab to start with, as on first impression it seems
to meet all four criteria:

   • Usage. A neuron with higher mean activation carries more predictive load.
   • Smoothness. The running mean filters out spikes and noise, yielding stable
     valuations.
   • Efficiency. A single add/multiply per neuron per time-step maintains the
     running mean incrementally.
   • Interpretability. It matches classical notions of empirical occupancy or
     duty-cycle in information geometry.

Alternative Measures More complex options would include:

   • Activation Variance or Entropy, to focus on unpredictability but requiring
     two-pass or higher storage.

   • Mutual Information, measuring predictive power but demanding density
     estimation.
   • Graph-Centrality, valuing neurons by connectivity but incurring global
     graph computations.

For initial experiments, average activation seems like the simplest viable default:
it is trivial to implement, seems like it will produce nondegenerate weakness
signals on simple tasks, and feels like it should interact smoothly with McBride-
driven updates, yielding rapid convergence without oscillation. Experimentation
will tell a fuller story!




                                       317
32.2.6    McBride Derivative and Local Sensitivity
The McBride derivative of the weakness functor enumerates one-hole contexts:
for each connection (i, j) ∈ Hθ , it associates the residual context Hθ \ {(i, j)}
and weight φ(i, j) = µ(i) ⊗ µ(j). This provides the local sensitivity of w(Hθ )
to adding or removing each connection. In the learning step, the gradient w.r.t.
individual weight θl,ij includes a term

                        ∂                       ∂
                             w(Hθ ) = φ(i, j)       1θ 6=0 ,
                      ∂θl,ij                  ∂θl,ij l,ij

which guides synaptic updates to prune or reinforce connections according to
their contribution to network weakness.

32.2.7    Convergence and Implications
Standard results on alternating minimization (to be elaborated below) imply:

   • Monotonicity: PC substeps yield J(θ(t+1) ) ≤ J(θ(t) ).
   • Blockwise stationarity: limit points θast satisfy ∂θl J(θast ) 3 0 for each
     layer l.
Hence PC emerges as a principled weakness-guided learning algorithm. This
unifies energy-based neural learning with the categorical notion of weakness,
suggesting new hybrid schemes in which McBride-derived sensitivities directly
inform synaptic plasticity.

32.3     Convergence Guarantees for Alternating Minimiza-
         tion
Let us now dig into how the ”alternating minimization” arguments can actually
work here.
    Conceptually, predictive coding alternates between two coordinate-descent
steps–updating activations to reduce prediction error, and updating weights
to reduce prediction error plus a weakness penalty. Under standard regularity
assumptions, each substep decreases the overall objective, and any accumulation
point of the sequence of iterates is blockwise stationary.

Assumptions

  1. Each per-layer prediction-error term

                        El (θl , xl , xl−1 ) = k xl − fl (θl , xl−1 )k2

       is continuously differentiable and has Lipschitz-continuous gradients in
       both θl and xl .



                                         318
  2. The weakness penalty
                                                    M
                                 w(Hθ ) =                 µ(i) ⊗ µ(j)
                                               (i,j)∈Hθ

      is nonnegative and lower-bounded.
  3. The composite objective
                                     L X
                                                        (n)   (n)
                                     X
                         J(θ, x) =             El (θl , xl , xl−1 ) + λ w(Hθ )
                                     l=1   n

      is coercive or level-bounded in θ for fixed x.

Monotonic Decrease Let (θ(t) , x(t) ) denote the parameters and activations
at iteration t. One full PC sweep performs:
               1                                                                     1
         x(t+ 2 ) = arg min J(θ(t) , x),            θ(t+1) = arg min J(θ, x(t+ 2 ) ).
                            x                                         θ

By construction,
                   1                                            1                       1 
    J θ(t) , x(t+ 2 )    ≤ J θ(t) , x(t) ,      J θ(t+1) , x(t+ 2 )       ≤ J θ(t) , x(t+ 2 ) .
                                        

Hence each full iteration satisfies

                            J θ(t+1) , x(t+1)       ≤ J θ(t) , x(t) ,
                                                                  

establishing monotonic convergence of the objective values.

Blockwise Stationarity Assume that the sequence (θ(t) , x(t) ) has an accu-
mulation point (θast , xast ). By continuity and the optimality of each substep,
one shows
                0 ∈ ∂x J(θast , xast ),     0 ∈ ∂θ J(θast , xast ),
where ∂ denotes the limiting subdifferential (for nonsmooth w) or ordinary
gradient. In particular, for each layer l,

                    0 ∈ ∂θl J(θast , xast ),        0 ∈ ∂xl J(θast , xast ).

Thus no single block update can further decrease the objective, i.e. (θast , xast )
is blockwise stationary.

Discussion These results justify predictive coding as a weakness-guided alter-
nating minimization: each PC substep strictly reduces the composite loss, and
the algorithm converges (in objective value) to a point satisfying first-order op-
timality conditions in each coordinate block. Extensions to inexact or stochastic
updates follow by standard analyses of block coordinate proximal descent.


                                               319
32.4     Lateral Connections in Predictive Coding
Let us now consider enhancing our basic predictive coding neural set up by
adding lateral connections within each layer. Conceptually, adding lateral in-
teractions within each layer of a predictive-coding network allows units to share
context, reduce redundancy, and stabilize inference. Lateral links can imple-
ment competition, cooperation, or attractor dynamics, all of which help drive
down the combined prediction-error plus weakness objective more efficiently.

Benefits of Lateral Connections
   • Error decorrelation: Inhibitory laterals suppress redundant activations,
     reducing overlap in residuals and lowering total weakness.

   • Contextual completion: Excitatory laterals enable partial patterns to re-
     cruit neighbors, filling in missing structure and shrinking residual error.
   • Attractor stability: Auto-associative lateral networks clean noisy activa-
     tions, yielding more stable fixed points and faster convergence.

32.4.1   Learning Rules for Lateral Weights
We learn lateral weights Lij by including their effect on the composite loss

                         J = kk2 + λ w(Hff ∪ Hlat ),

where Hff and Hlat are the feed-forward and lateral link relations.

Hebbian-Style Update
                                              ∂
                       ∆Lij ∝ xi xj − α           w(Hlat ),
                                             ∂Lij

so that co-activated units strengthen their connection unless its contribution to
weakness exceeds a threshold.

Hopfield-Style Update       Treat the lateral net as an auto-associative energy

                              Elat (x) = − 21 x> L x,

and learn L by gradient descent on

                       Jlat = Ex k(x)k2 + λ w(Hlat ),
                                       


using the McBride derivative to compute ∂w/∂Lij .




                                       320
Integration into Predictive Coding Each PC iteration with lateral learn-
ing proceeds:
1. Inference:
   for t = 1..T do
     x <- x + eta_x*(W^T*epsilon + L*x - x)
     epsilon <- bottom_up_error(x)
2. Learning:
   for each layer l do
     W <- W - eta_w*(dE/dW + lambda*d w(H_ff)/dW)
     L <- L - eta_l*(dE/dL + lambda*d w(H_lat)/dL)

where dE/dL is the gradient of the prediction-error after lateral settling, and
dw(H)/dL is given by the McBride derivative.

Expected Outcomes
   • Faster error reduction: Lateral completion captures predictable structure
     in one step.
   • Sparser codes: Competitive laterals prune redundant activity, lowering
     weakness.
   • Robust inference: Attractor dynamics mitigate noise, improving conver-
     gence and stability.
    By learning lateral connections under the same weakness-minimizing ob-
jective, predictive coding becomes a fully integrated framework in which both
feed-forward and lateral architectures are optimized for simplicity and accuracy.

32.4.2   Formal Result on Lateral Connections Improving Conver-
         gence
We now give a precise statement and proof showing that adding positive-definite
lateral connections strictly accelerates convergence of the predictive-coding in-
ference step.

Demonstration Under Linear Assumptions We begin with a simplified
demonstration that holds under linearized inference dynamics and suitable spec-
tral assumptions,
Theorem 27. Consider a single layer with linear generative model

                                   x̂ = W x,

prediction error  = xdata − x̂, and inference update

                          x ← x + ηx (W T  − L x),


                                      321
where W T W and L are symmetric positive semidefinite, and 0 < ηx < 2/kW T W +
Lk. Let

            Mno lat = I − ηx W T W,        Mlat = I − ηx (W T W + L).

If L is nonzero positive semidefinite then

                              ρ(Mlat ) < ρ(Mno lat ),

where ρ(·) denotes the spectral radius. Consequently, the inference iteration
converges faster with lateral matrix L than without.
Proof. Since W T W and L commute (they are jointly diagonalizable by assump-
tion), the eigenvalues of Mno lat are

                      λno
                       i = 1 − η x σi ,    σi ∈ spec(W T W ),

and those of Mlat are

                     λlat
                      i = 1 − ηx (σi + `i ),        `i ∈ spec(L).

Because L is positive semidefinite and nonzero, for each i we have `i ≥ 0 and
at least one `j > 0. Hence

                |λlat                                     no
                  i | = 1 − ηx (σi + `i ) < 1 − ηx σi = |λi |,

for each i (using 0 < ηx < 2/kW T W + Lk to keep all eigenvalues in (−1, 1)). It
follows that

                 ρ(Mlat ) = max |λlat         no
                                  i | < max |λi | = ρ(Mno lat ),
                              i                 i

proving the claim.

Remark This result shows that, at least in the linear regime, adding a sym-
metric positive-definite lateral matrix L yields a strictly smaller contraction fac-
tor for the inference dynamics. More generally, nonlinear activation functions
and noncommuting matrices complicate the analysis, but the same principle–
lateral connections act as a preconditioner–carries over and motivates their in-
clusion whenever one seeks faster error reduction under the combined prediction-
error plus weakness objective.

Nonlinear Convergence via Contraction under Lipschitz Conditions
We now explore going beyond the linear case – which makes things a little
messier but not qualitatively different. Conceptually, even in the presence of
nonlinear activation functions, inference with lateral connections can be shown
to converge faster by viewing the update as a fixed-point iteration and apply-
ing the Banach contraction principle. We only require mild assumptions on
smoothness and boundedness of the nonlinearities, avoiding case-by-case anal-
yses of specific nonlinearities and their implications.


                                          322
   Setup and Assumptions Consider a single layer with activation vector
x ∈ Rd , data input xdata , weight matrix W ∈ Rd×d , and lateral weight matrix
L ∈ Rd×d . The generative prediction is

                                   x̂ = f (W x),

where f : Rd → Rd acts coordinate-wise with derivative f 0 (z) satisfying

                       0 ≤ m ≤ f 0 (z) ≤ M         ∀z ∈ R.

We assume:

  1. W T W and L are symmetric positive semidefinite and commute.
  2. The step-size ηx > 0 satisfies
                                               2
                             ηx <                        .
                                      M 2 kW T W k + kLk

   Inference Map and Jacobian The inference update with lateral term is

              F (x) = x + ηx W T (xdata − f (W x)) − L x .
                                                        


Its Jacobian at x is

                DF (x) = I − ηx L − ηx W T diag f 0 (W x) W,
                                                         


where diag(f 0 (W x)) is a diagonal matrix with entries in [m, M ].

  Contraction Constant          Since W T diag(f 0 (W x))W  m W T W and 
M W T W , we have

               kDF (x)k ≤ 1 − ηx λmin (L) − ηx m λmin (W T W ),

where λmin denotes the smallest nonzero eigenvalue. By assumption on ηx , the
right-hand side is strictly less than 1. Moreover, because DF (x) is uniformly
bounded in norm by a constant ρ < 1 for all x, F is a contraction on Rd .

    Convergence and Acceleration By the Banach fixed-point theorem, the
iteration x(t+1) = F (x(t) ) converges exponentially fast to the unique fixed point
x̄. Comparing to the case L = 0, the contraction constant

                   ρ(L) = 1 − ηx λmin (L) + m λmin (W T W )
                                                              


is strictly smaller for any nonzero L. Hence lateral connections accelerate con-
vergence of the nonlinear inference dynamics under these general Lipschitz and
spectral assumptions.



                                        323
    Discussion This argument cleanly extends the linear spectral-radius proof
to nonlinear activations, replacing W T W by its lower bound m W T W and show-
ing that any positive-definite L further reduces the contraction rate. It holds
without specifying particular forms of f , relying only on its uniform derivative
bounds, and applies layer by layer in a deep network.


33     The Role of Commutativity in Continual Learn-
       ing in Predictive Coding Networks
One issue commonly observed in neural nets applied in a continuous or lifelong
learning manner is ”catastrophic forgetting” – it can be counterintuitively hard
to get neural nets to keep on learning and keep on acquiring new knowledge and
memories while gracefully rather than messily and suddenly letting its memory
of older thing fade as it reaches capacity limits. Adding lateral activations
to PC networks–implementing competition or inhibition within layers–can help
somewhat, improving feature disentanglement and stability in continual learning
scenarios. However, naively interleaving inference and lateral updates can lead
to order-dependent weight trajectories and catastrophic interference.
    In this subsection we explore a principled potential remedy to these issues
based on a commutativity design principle, proposed by Daniel MacDonald
[Mac25] but fleshed out here in a more general and mathematical way. The
core idea here is to require that the quantized update actions for PC infer-
ence and for lateral inhibition lie in a commutative subquantale. Under this
assumption, we prove here that (i) inference and lateral updates commute on
the network’s parameter module, (ii) the associated enriched-category of lay-
ers has all relevant diagrams strictly commuting, and (iii) the parameter space
cleanly factorizes into feedforward and lateral submodules, yielding disentangled
learning.
    We then show how the same quantale-enriched formalism applies to PC-
driven reinforcement learning within the MetaMo motivational framework. By
identifying sensation and decision updates as commuting quantale actions, we
obtain commuting appraisal and decision functors, exactly matching MetaMo’s
desideratum. We conclude with a discussion of failure modes when commuta-
tivity is absent, illustrating risks in both image classification and social-context
RL.

33.1     Simple Predictive-Coding Classifier with Lateral Ac-
         tivations
First we outline a simple reference PC network, to use for initial exploration of
our ideas on commutativity.
    We consider a feedforward network for image classification with L hidden
layers. At each layer ` = 1, . . . , L we maintain:
   • A latent activation vector z` ∈ Rn` ,


                                        324
   • A feedforward prediction ẑ` = f` (z`−1 ; W` ), where z0 is the input image
     and W` are the layer weights,
   • A lateral prediction z̃` = V` z` , where V` ∈ Rn` ×n` implements lateral
     inhibition.
   Define the total energy over latents z1:L and label y by
                   L
                   X 1                  2       1          2   1                   2
   E(z1:L , y) =             z` − ẑ`       +     z` − z̃`   +   y − g(zL ; WL+1 ) ,
                         2                      2              2
                   `=1

where g(·; WL+1 ) is the final classification readout. Inference is performed by
iterating, for each `,
                                     h                      i
                       z` ← z` − α (z` − ẑ` ) + (z` − z̃` ) ,

and similarly updating y. Learning then updates
                                 T
                                      , ∆V` = η z` − z̃` z`T .
                                                        
               ∆W` = η z` − ẑ` z`−1

33.2     Commutativity Principle in Continual Learning
The design principle of commutativity demands that inference updates (driven
by reconstruction error) and lateral updates (driven by inhibition error) can be
applied in either order without affecting the final state. Concretely, if we denote
by qinf the update action corresponding to feedforward inference and by qlat the
action for lateral inference, commutativity requires
                                                         
                         qinf ? qlat ? θ = qlat ? qinf ? θ ,

where θ = (W1:L , V1:L ) are the parameters. This ensures that alternating infer-
ence and lateral steps in arbitrary sequences does not introduce order-dependent
drift, a key property for stable continual learning.

33.2.1   Quantale-Enriched Formalization
Let us formalize this commutativity principle in a more abstract way, so as to
more thoroughly understand its implications.
   Let Q = (R≥0 ∪ {∞}, min, +) be the commutative quantale of energy levels,
and let Θ be the Q-module of all network parameters under the action

                                        q ? θ = θ + ∆θ(q),

where ∆θ(q) is the parameter change induced by an update of energy q. In
particular we identify
                           qinf ∈ Q, qlat ∈ Q,
and require qinf ⊗ qlat = qlat ⊗ qinf in the commutative subquantale of valid
updates.


                                                325
   Organize the L layers as a small Q-enriched category L with objects 1, . . . , L
and hom-objects

                  L(`, `) = e,   L(`, ` + 1) = qinf ,        L(`, `) = qlat ,

so that each square
                                              qinf
                                          `          `+1
                                   qlat               qlat

                                          `   qinf   `+1

commutes strictly in Q. Finally, decompose Θ as a symmetric-monoidal tensor
of submodules
                               Θ ∼= W ⊗Q V,
where W are feedforward weights and V are lateral weights. Updates from qinf
act only on W , and updates from qlat act only on V .
    Given this setup, we can then draw conclusions such as:
Theorem 28. In the above PC-based classifier with lateral activations, if

   1. the inference and lateral update energies qinf , qlat ∈ Q commute,
   2. all hom-objects in the layer category L associated to inference and lateral
      edges lie in the commutative subquantale,
   3. the parameter module Θ factorizes as W ⊗Q V with disjoint update actions,

then
   • inference and lateral updates commute on Θ,
   • all enriched-category diagrams for layer transitions commute strictly,
   • the factorization yields disentangled learning of feedforward and lateral
     parameters.
Proof. Commutativity of qinf and qlat implies
                                                                                
      qinf ? qlat ? θ = (qinf ⊗ qlat ) ? θ = (qlat ⊗ qinf ) ? θ = qlat ? qinf ? θ .

Strict commutation of each square in L follows because the corresponding hom-
objects multiply in Q in either order to the same result. Finally, since qinf
acts only on the W factor and qlat acts only on the V factor in the tensor
product, their updates never interfere, yielding a clean disentanglement of the
two parameter subspaces.




                                               326
33.2.2   MetaMo Motivational Framework and Commutativity in PC-
         RL Networks
We now show that a PC-driven RL network whose inference and learning up-
dates commute in the quantale sense instantiates MetaMo commutativity at the
category level.
                               W
Theorem 29. Let Q = (Q, ⊗, ) be a commutative quantale of energy costs,
and let Θ be a Q-module of network parameters. Identify two distinguished
updates:
                                qsens , qdec ∈ Q
corresponding respectively to predictive-coding inference (“sensation”) and RL
reward-driven learning (“decision”). Suppose

                           qsens ⊗ qdec = qdec ⊗ qsens

so that sensation and decision updates commute on Θ. Define two endofunctors
on the category S of network states:

                    App(θ) = qsens ? θ,      Dec(θ) = qdec ? θ.

Then
                            App ◦ Dec = Dec ◦ App
as endofunctors on S. Conversely, if qsens and qdec do not commute, then App ◦
Dec 6= Dec ◦ App.
Proof. For any network state θ ∈ Θ, the composite actions satisfy
                                             
            (App ◦ Dec)(θ) = qsens ? qdec ? θ = (qsens ⊗ qdec ) ? θ,

and                                           
             (Dec ◦ App)(θ) = qdec ? qsens ? θ = (qdec ⊗ qsens ) ? θ.
Commutativity qsens ⊗ qdec = qdec ⊗ qsens implies the two composites coincide
on every object, hence the functors are equal. If the updates do not commute,
then the two composite actions differ for some θ, so the functors differ.

33.2.3   When Commutativity Fails
If predictive-coding inference and RL updates do not commute, then appraisal
and decision no longer commute in the MetaMo sense. This can lead to practical
failures, such as:

Image Classification A non-commutative PC-classifier may yield different
parameter trajectories depending on the scheduling of inference and reward
updates. For example, applying a classification-error correction before cleaning
up hidden activations can lead to weights that overfit spurious noise. The result
is unstable accuracy and greater sensitivity to update order.


                                       327
Social-Context RL In a complex social environment, decision updates en-
code learned policies from reward signals, while inference updates integrate new
observations of other agents. If these updates do not commute, then learning
a policy before fully inferring new social cues can misalign the agent’s beliefs,
causing erratic or unsafe actions. Conversely, over-inference before policy ad-
justment can slow learning or entrench outdated models of other agents. Lack
of commutativity thus risks both poor adaptation and unintended social conse-
quences.

33.2.4   Commutativity Regularization in Predictive-Coding Networks
We have argued strenuously for the value of commutativity in PC networks.
However, that doesn’t mean commutativity is necessarily easy to achieve. De-
signing a predictive-coding network so that inference and lateral (or learning)
updates commute by construction often requires exact algebraic structures or
dual projection steps, which can be computationally expensive and difficult to
scale. As MacDonald points out a lightweight commutativity regularizer pro-
vides a practical alternative: it softly penalizes order-dependent dynamics, en-
couraging updates to become order-independent without enforcing rigid con-
straints.

MacDonald’s Basic Commutativity Regularizer MacDonald proposes
adding to the loss a term that measures the difference between two update
operators applied in opposite orders. If U and V are the inference and lateral
update maps on parameters θ, then
                                                     2
                     RMac (θ) = 21 U V (θ) − V U (θ) .
                                          


Minimizing this term alongside the original objective drives U ◦ V ≈ V ◦ U ,
reducing interference in continual learning.

Fisher Information Geometry Regularizer One can go even further in
this direction using an information geometry approach.
    In a Fisher-information geometry view, U and V induce natural-gradient
vector fields XU and XV on parameter space with metric G(θ). Commutativity
is equivalent to
                               [ XU , XV ](θ) = 0,
where [·, ·] is the Lie bracket. Define the Fisher regularizer

                  RF (θ) = 12 [ XU , XV ](θ), [ XU , XV ](θ) G(θ) ,

with hv, wiG = v T G w. Adding λ RF encourages the two natural-gradient flows
to commute.




                                        328
Wasserstein Information Geometry Regularizer Alternatively, one can
view updates as gradient flows in the space of parameter distributions under
the 2-Wasserstein metric. Let ΦU and ΦV be the JKO-step operators for the
two energies. Form the two compositions

                  pU V = ΦV ΦU (p) , pV U = ΦU ΦV (p) .
                                                       


Define the Wasserstein regularizer

                           RW (p) = 21 W22 pU V , pV U .
                                                      


Minimizing λ RW alongside the inference and lateral objectives aligns the two
flows in the optimal-transport geometry, promoting order-independent updates.

33.3    Causal Weakness-Augmented Transformer Attention
The connection of PC neural learning with weakness is broad in applicability,
and can be deployed in the context of many different neural architectures aimed
at various different objectives. As a particular and potentially useful example,
we explore how to apply it in a transformer context.
    Conceptually, we wish to bias transformer self-attention toward past tokens
that admit a simple, predictive (causal) relationship to the current token. We
define a causal weakness score between each context position i and target po-
sition t by fitting a tiny ”weak” model and measuring its residual weakness.
This score is then converted into a causal salience prior, which modulates the
transformer’s attention logits.

Causal Weakness Score For each pair of positions (i, t), let xi be the em-
bedding at position i and xt the target embedding. Fit a simple predictor (e.g.
                                    (i)
a linear model or one-layer MLP) x̂t = g(xi ; φi ), and define the residual set

                 Ri→t = {(c, xt ) | g(xi ; φi ) fails to predict xt }.

Assign a valuation µ on events (e.g. token frequency or embedding norm) and
measure                            M
                       wi→t =             µ(c) ⊗ µ(xt ),
                                  (c,xt )∈Ri→t

the weakness of the residual.

Causal Salience Prior Invert weakness to a salience score
                                      
                   si→t = exp −γ wi→t ,      γ > 0.

Low residual weakness (strong simple predictor) yields high si→t .




                                         329
Attention Logit Modulation In a standard Transformer layer, the attention
logits are
                                       qtT ki
                               Ai,t = √       .
                                           d
We augment this with the causal salience prior:

                           A0i,t = Ai,t + α log si→t ,

where α ≥ 0 controls the strength of the causal bias. The attention weights
become                           X
                                    softmaxi A0i,t vi .
                                                  
                     Attn(xt ) =
                                    i


End-to-End Training All parameters–including the weak-model parameters
{φi }, the transformer weights, and the bias scale α–are trained jointly to min-
imize the overall language-modeling (or prediction) loss. Optionally one adds
a complexity penalty on the weak models, for example an `1 norm on φi , to
enforce simplicity directly.

Extensions
                                                 h
   • Multi-head causal priors: Compute distinct wi→t and shi→t per attention
     head h.
   • Cross-attention: Apply the same causal weakness modulation when at-
     tending over encoder tokens in an encoder-decoder model.
   By integrating a quantale-based causal weakness score into the attention
mechanism, the model ”pays attention” preferentially to those past tokens that
support a simple causal explanation for the current prediction.

33.4    Integrating Causal Weakness into WaveFormer
A variation on the above ideas would be to integrate weakness-oriented PC
learning into the Waveformer wavelet-based transformer architecture. Concep-
tually, WaveFormer applies a discrete wavelet transform (DWT) to layer acti-
vations, processes low-frequency (coarse) and high-frequency (detail) subbands
via self-attention, then recombines via an inverse DWT (IDWT). To bias at-
tention toward causally important tokens, we introduce a causal-weakness prior
in parallel with the symbolic multiresolution transform and inject it into the
WaveFormer’s attention of both subbands.

Expected Benefits
   • Causal focus in both subbands: Low-frequency attention prioritizes tokens
     that provide simple global context; high-frequency attention amplifies de-
     tail tokens with strong simple causal links.
   • Interpretable multi-scale causality: Salience priors at each scale reveal
     which coarse or fine patterns drive predictions.
   • Efficiency and accuracy: By gating attention with causal weakness, the
     WaveFormer can allocate computation to genuinely predictive features and
     reduce spurious high-frequency noise.

                                          332
33.6    Weakness-AIXI via Predictive Coding and Reinforce-
        ment Learning
A different – and less immediately practical – sort of application of weakness-
integrated PC neural nets is to provide a twist on our above ideas about Univer-
sal AI. We can replace AIXItl ’s Solomonoff-like mixture over computable models
with a single predictive-coding network that embodies an Occam bias via weak-
ness regularization, and use that network for both world-model learning and
policy search.

Predictive-Coding World Model Let Pθ (ht+1 | ht , at ) be a predictive-
coding network that, given history ht and action at , predicts the next observa-
tion and reward. Train it online by minimizing
                             X                2               
                Lmodel (θ) =      ot+1 − ôt+1 + λ w ker Pθ ,
                                t

where w(ker Pθ ) is the quantale-folded weakness of the network’s connection
relation (computed via the McBride derivative).

Weakness-Regularized Planning At decision time, generate candidate ac-
tion sequences at:t+H and simulate trajectories using Pθ . Score each sequence
by
                         H
                         X                                       
           V (at:t+H ) =   rt+k − β w ker Pθ on simulated data ,
                        k=1

and select the sequence with maximal V , executing its first action. This is an
Occam-weighted expectimax in a single learned model.

Policy Extraction via Predictive Coding RL Augment Pθ with a policy
head πφ (a | h) and train (θ, φ) jointly on
                                 H
                                hX                           i            
         Lpolicy (φ) = −Ea∼πφ             rt+k − β w(ker Pθ ) + γ w ker πφ ,
                                    k=1

so that action proposals minimize complexity as well as maximize reward.

Relation to AIXI

   • AIXI averages value across all models weighted by 2−K(M ) ; our predictive-
     coding network is the analog of a single model M whose prior arises from
     the weakness penalty.
   • Planning with a weakness penalty on imagined trajectories implements
     the equivalent of an Occam-weighted expectimax within Pθ .



                                            333
Implementation Sketch
  1. Model update: alternate predictive-coding inference (activations) with
     weight update on Lmodel (θ).
  2. Action selection: sample action sequences, evaluate V (at:t+H ), pick best.

  3. Policy learning (optional): update φ by gradient descent on Lpolicy (φ).
    This yields a practical, differentiable approximation to AIXI where the role
of the Solomonoff prior is played by a quantale-based weakness regularizer.

33.6.1   Convergence Conditions for Predictive-Coding WAIXI and
         Hybrid Architectures
What would be general characteristics of environments and reward functions
under which this predictive-coding WAIXI agent might be expected to work
especially well – e.g. to converge relatively rapidly to an optimal or near-optimal
policy?

Favorable Environment Properties             It seems rapid convergence is most
likely when the environment satisfies:
   • Low Descriptive Complexity. The true transition and reward dynamics
     admit a compact predictive-coding model. A small network with a strong
     weakness penalty can capture the structure with few samples.
   • Fast Mixing / Limited Memory. Future observations depend only on a
     short history (low Markov order). Bounded receptive fields suffice for
     accurate prediction, reducing sample requirements.

   • Dense, Smooth Reward. Frequent reward feedback that varies smoothly
     with state/action changes yields informative gradients for both model
     learning and planning under the weakness-regularized objective.
   • Stationarity and Ergodicity. The environment’s statistics remain stable
     over time and all relevant states are eventually visited, ensuring that em-
     pirical averages converge to true expectations under the Occam-biased
     loss.
   • Alignment of Predictability and Reward. High-reward regions coincide
     with low-weakness (highly predictable) structure. The Occam bias then
     guides the agent to focus on those regions first, accelerating policy im-
     provement.




                                       334
Hybrid WAIXI with Evolutionary Programming In domains where one
or more of the above conditions fail–e.g. highly non-stationary, sparse-reward,
or algorithmically random environments–a pure predictive-coding WAIXI may
learn too slowly or overfit noise. To address this, a hybrid architecture can be
employed:
   • Predictive-Coding WAIXI Module: As before, learns a weakness-
     regularized world model and performs Occam-weighted planning for re-
     gions of the environment that are amenable to compact modeling.
   • EDA/Evolutionary Module: Runs an estimation-of-distribution algo-
     rithm (EDA) or genetic search over policy/program spaces, guided by the
     same weakness prior when evaluating candidate controllers. This module
     excels at exploring highly irregular, low-predictability regions.
   • Meta-Controller Arbitration: A higher-level MeTTa routine monitors
     model confidence and weakness gradients to allocate control:
        – Use WAIXI planning when the predictive model achieves low residual
          weakness and high confidence.
        – Switch to EDA search when residual weakness remains high or reward
          is too sparse for reliable gradient signals.
   This hybrid scheme combines the sample-efficiency of weakness-AIXI in well-
behaved subdomains with the exploration power of evolutionary methods in
challenging regions, yielding a more universally robust AGI agent.

33.7     Conditional Weakness Regularization with Background
         Knowledge
In many settings we wish to regularize a predictive-coding network not only by
its internal weakness w(Hθ ), but also by how its structure aligns with an external
knowledge base. We consider two paradigms for presenting that knowledge:

Option A: Knowledge as Predictive Desirability Function                In this case
the knowledge base is given by a fixed function
                                                 
                           d(θ) = Desirability θ
which scores any candidate network parameterization θ according to how de-
sirable or plausible the subnetwork is, based on its unregularized performance.
We then define a conditional weakness penalty
                                   M                    
                      wA (Hθ ) =           ϕ(i, j) ⊗ d(θ) ,
                                  (i,j)∈Hθ

so that each synapse (i, j) is down-weighted when d(θ) is low. The PC learning
objective becomes
                            X
                      L=        kpred-errk2 + λ wA (Hθ ) .
                            t,l


                                       335
Option B: Weak Meta-Model of Capability Here the knowledge base
itself is a small ’ predictive model B with parameters β. It takes a summary
s(θ) of the main network’s structure and predicts its expected performance:

                                  B : s(θ) 7→ b(θ) .

We train B by minimizing its own composite loss
                   X                         2
              LB =      B(s(θi )) − perf(θi ) + λB wB (Hβ ),
                       i

where wB (Hβ ) is the quantale-weakness penalty on B’s graph. Once trained,
B defines a background score b(θ) = B(s(θ)), and we set
                                  M                  
                      wB (Hθ ) =        ϕ(i, j) ⊗ b(θ) .
                                    (i,j)∈Hθ

The PC-net then minimizes
                        X
                 LPC =    kpred-errk2 + λPC wB (Hθ ).
                            t,l


Elegance and Synergy of Option B               Option B leverages weakness through-
out both learning stages:
   • The meta-model B is itself trained with a quantale-weakness penalty
     wB (Hβ ), yielding a concise, high-weakness predictor of network capability.
   • The main PC-net uses b(θ) from B as a conditional valuation in its own
     weakness penalty wB (Hθ ), focusing regularization on synapses that B
     deems implausible.
   • Both levels share the same commutative, associative quantale structure, so
     their penalties compose cleanly and admit smooth, closed-form gradients.

   • One can even optimize β and θ jointly in a single composite objective,
     allowing B to adapt as the PC-net learns, and the PC-net to follow B’s
     updated guidance.
   • The CoDD Weakness Schema Theorem extends to this two-tier system,
     providing provable convergence guarantees under evolutionary or hybrid
     search.
   Thus Option B delivers a fully compositional, algebraically elegant, and
provably convergent framework in which weakness drives both the meta-model
induction and the PC-net regularization.

34.1     Quantale-Valued Stochastic Processes for Risk-Normalized
         Returns
Stochastic processes like Brownian motions and Levy processes are commonly
used to model financial time series. However these often fail to model the
characteristics of real financial systems, especially in cases such as emerging
markets or cryptocurrencies. This brings up the question: Can we use some
generalization of stochastic processes over these financial quantales to create


                                                338
more flexible versions of these sort of stochastic processes for generalizing time
series?
    We work in the financial quantale
                                                              p(x)
                  V = ([0, ∞), ⊗ = ×, ⊕ = +),      ϕ(x) =          ,
                                                             r(x)γ
so that for any payoff function m : X → [0, ∞) the weakness
                                       X
                       W (t) = w m(t) =      ϕ(x) mx (t)
                                             x∈X

gives the risk-normalized aggregate at time t.

V -Valued Random Variables and Noise A V -valued random variable Z is
an ordinary nonnegative real random variable, but we interpret its moments and
convolutions via the quantale operations. In particular a quantale-Levy noise
L(t) can be defined by its characteristic exponent in V :
                                                 
                  E exp(−u L(t)) = exp − t Ψ(u) , u ≥ 0,
with Ψ(u) chosen to model heavy tails or jumps.

Quantale-Geometric Brownian Motion (QGBM)                     Generalize the classi-
cal SDE
                      dS = µ S dt + σ S dW
to the quantale setting by replacing addition of increments with ⊕ and multi-
plication with ⊗. We write in differential form
                                                    
                  dS(t) = µ ⊗ S(t) dt ⊕ σ ⊗ S(t) dQ(t),
where dQ(t) is an increment of a quantale-Levy process (e.g. a compound-
Poisson or stable noise). Equivalently, in integral form
                                         Z t
                                                        
             S(t) = S(0) ⊗ exp⊗ µ t ⊕           σ ⊗ S(s) ⊕ dQ(s).
                                              0
Here exp⊗ is the quantale-exponential (i.e. real exp).

                                X
                 W (t) = w m(t) =       ϕ(x) mx (t),
                                             x∈X

and suppose each instrument mx (t) follows its own QGBM:
                                                  
                 dmx = µx ⊗ mx dt ⊕ σx ⊗ mx dQx (t),
with possibly correlated noises dQx . Then by linearity of ⊕
             X               X                     X
      dW =      ϕ(x) dmx =        ϕ(x) µx mx dt ⊕        ϕ(x) σx mx dQx .
              x                 x                        x
This V -valued SDE captures portfolio-level drift and heavy-tailed shock aggre-

                                       339
Quantale-OU Process for Mean Reversion A mean-reverting ”Ornstein-
Uhlenbeck” style process in V is
                                   
                       dX = κ θ ( X dt ⊕ σ dQ(t),

where θ ( X = (X/θ) is the residuated ”ratio” in V . This yields mean-
reversion toward level θ with jumps or heavy tails.

Flexible Calibration and Features
   • Heavy tails: choose Ψ(u) ∼ uα (0 < α < 2) to model stable-law incre-
     ments.
   • Volatility clustering: let σx depend on a quantale-OU process on past
     |dQx |.
   • q
     Risk coupling: encode cross-instrument correlation by letting dQx = ρxy dL0 +
         1 − ρ2xy dLx .

Benefits This quantale-valued framework allows
   • direct aggregation of heavy-tailed shocks via ⊕,
   • compounding via ⊗ that respects risk-normalized weights,
   • flexible calibrations by choosing quantale-Levy exponents,
   • consistent portfolio-level SDEs preserving the quantale algebra.
Such models can better capture jump clustering, volatility regimes, and extreme
events in emerging or crypto markets than classical Brownian or Levy models.

34.2     Quantale-Valued Stochastic Models for Cryptocur-
         rency Time Series
To make the above more concrete, we flesh out some speculative possible details
of how to apply these modeling methods to a practical use-case, cryptocurrency
time series.

34.2.3   Quantale-Lévy Increments dQx (t)
For each asset x we model jumps and heavy tails via a Lévy process Qx (t) whose
Laplace transform in V is

                    E e−u Qx (t) = exp − t Ψx (u) , u ≥ 0,
                                               


where the characteristic exponent Ψx (u) is calibrated from data:
   • Fit the empirical tail of |mx (tk )| to a power law Pr[|mx | > y] ≈ cx y −αx ,
     yielding tail index αx ∈ (0, 2).
   • Then choose a stable-law exponent Ψx (u) = Cx uαx or a compound-
     Poisson form Ψx (u) = λx 1 − φx (u) where φx is the jump-size Laplace
     transform.
An infinitesimal increment dQx (t) is the quantale-Lévy noise driving jumps for
mx .

34.2.4   Quantale-GBM for Individual Assets
We posit for each x a quantale-geometric Brownian motion with jumps:
                                                    
              dmx (t) = µx ⊗ mx (t) dt ⊕ σx ⊗ mx (t) dQx (t).

Equivalently, in integral form,

   • Compute on sliding windows: W (t), portfolio entropy, mutual information
     with market regimes.
   • Detect emergent co-movement patterns via σS,T (t) = 1/W (t).

   • Trigger alerts when quantale diagnostics exceed thresholds.

34.3      Financial Quantale Based Attention Allocation
The Economic Attention Allocation (ECAN) subsystem of the Hyperon design
applies economic principles to the allocation of attention in an AGI system’s
memory metagraph. Here we explore how this system might be upgraded to
leverage the more sophisticated approach to finance embodied in a financial
quantale.




                                            342
34.4     A Quantale-ECAN for Risk-Normalized Financial At-
         tention
We begin by simplistically adapting the Economic Attention Network (ECAN)
of [PISG09] to our financial quantale
                                            
                V = [0, ∞), ⊗ = ×, ⊕ = + , e = 1, 0 = 0,

with risk-normalized weights ϕx = px /rxγ on assets x ∈ X.

States: STI and LTI as Quantale Values For each asset x at time t we
maintain
                       STIx (t), LTIx (t) ∈ V,
interpreted as short-term and long-term ”importance-currencies.”

Rent Generation        Each asset x generates a ”rent” from its STI:

                    rentx (t) = ρrent ⊗ STIx (t),     ρrent ∈ V,

analogous to interest on importance funds.

 Importance Diffusion A fraction ρspread ∈ V of each   P asset’s STI is re-
distributed to others via a normalized adjacency wx→y ( y wx→y = e):
                                              M                         
 STIy (t + ∆) = STIy (t) ⊗ ( e     ρspread ) ⊕   ρspread ⊗ wx→y ⊗ STIx (t) .
                                                x∈X

Here e   ρ is the (residuation) ”complement” of ρ in V .

Long-Term Importance Update A fraction ρLTI ∈ V of each rent is added
to LTI:                                               
             LTIx (t + ∆) = LTIx (t) ⊕ ρLTI ⊗ rentx (t) .

Attentional Focus At each step, rank assets by STIx (t) in the quantale order
and select the top K as the Attentional Focus.

Risk-Weighted Stimulus         When new market data arrives (e.g. a price shock
sx ), we stimulate STI by
                                              
                          STIx = STIx ⊕ ϕx ⊗ sx ,

so that high-return, low-risk assets receive proportionally larger attention boosts.




                                        343
Interpretation and Use
   • Assets in the Attentional Focus have the greatest STI and thus attract
     portfolio rebalancing or risk-management actions.
   • The rent/diffusion dynamics spreads attention from highly ”active” assets
     to their neighbors in the asset-graph (e.g. sector or correlation network).
   • LTI accumulates persistent importance, guiding long-horizon investment
     decisions.
   • Risk-normalized stimulation ϕx sx ensures that attention aligns with both
     return potential and risk exposure.

    This Quantale-ECAN framework thus provides a principled, algebraic mechanism–
fully expressed in the (×, +) financial quantale–to allocate ”attention-capital”
across assets, blending short-term market shocks with long-term sectoral influ-
ence.

                                          345
Cognitive Interpretation
   • HSTI and HLTI measure breadth of a cognitive system’s short-term focus
     versus long-term priorities.
   • Mutual information I(STI; LTI) captures alignment between rapid atten-
     tional shifts and stable importance beliefs.
   • Pattern intensity ISSTI (resp. ISLTI ) measures average attention (impor-
     tance) per asset in cluster S.
   • Synergy σS reveals when a cluster attracts disproportionate combined at-
     tention and importance–i.e. an emergent thematic focus or cognitive ”hot-
     spot.”
    These quantale-information and pattern-theory diagnostics provide a rich,
algebraic description of how a cognitive system allocates attention over assets
(or concepts) both in the moment and over time, and how new, unexpected
clusters of focus can emerge.

34.6     Attention as an Emergent Strange Attractor via McBride
         Pattern-Dynamics
The next natural step in this line of investigation is to ask: Can one use the
notion of McBride-differential equations on patterns on the STI/LTI quantale to
articulate the notion of the attentional-focus as an emergent strange attractor
of the ECAN dynamics? That is: Can we present ECAN’s ongoing updating of
attention values as a nonlinear dynamic in which the strange/chaotic attractor
of attentional focus emerges?
    We show here how the ECAN update laws for short-term importance (STI)
and long-term importance (LTI) can be given a continuous-time, McBride-
derivative form whose nonlinear coupling admits a strange (chaotic) attractor,
interpreted as the emergent attentional focus.

34.6.1   Continuous-Time ECAN via McBride Derivatives
Recall the discrete ECAN update from above:
                                        M
  STIx (t + ∆) = (STIx (t) ⊗ (e ρs )) ⊕   ρs ⊗ wy→x ⊗ STIy (t) ⊕ ϕx sx (t),
                                            y

                 LTIx (t + ∆) = LTIx (t) ⊕ ρL ⊗ (ρr ⊗ STIx (t)).
Define McBride derivatives by

 ˙ x (t) = lim STIx (t + ∆) − STIx (t) ,
STI                                          ˙ x (t) = lim LTIx (t + ∆) − LTIx (t) .
                                            LTI
           ∆→0           ∆                             ∆→0           ∆

Using residuation and linearization of ⊕, ⊗ near the identity, one obtains the
ODEs                                 X
                 ˙ x = − λ STIx + ρs
               STI                      wy→x STIy + ϕx sx (t),
                                            y

                                  LTI
where λ arises from (e     ρs ) ≈ (1 − ρs ).

34.6.2   Pattern-Intensity Coordinates
Choose a small collection of candidate clusters Si ⊂ X, i = 1, 2, 3. Define their
pattern intensities
                            X                               X
        IiSTI (t) = |Si | (   STIx (t), IiLTI (t) = |Si | (    LTIx (t).
                           x∈Si                                     x∈Si


Let Pi (t) = IiSTI (t) ⊗ IiLTI (t). By McBride differentiation,
                                  X                           X
               I˙iSTI = |Si | (        ˙ x , I˙iLTI = |Si | (
                                      STI                        ˙ x.
                                                                LTI

                                  X X            X

                                     y   x∈Si                     x∈Si

34.6.3    Reduced Nonlinear System and Chaos
Focus on the three-dimensional system in the pattern-intensities:

            I˙i = −a Ii + b Pi + ui (t),    Ṗi = c Ii − d Pi ,     i = 1, 2, 3,

where Pi = IiSTI ⊗ IiLTI and ui (t) is external stimulus. For suitable parameters
(a, b, c, d) this is a well-known Lorenz-like system admitting a strange attractor
in (I1 , I2 , I3 )-space.

34.6.4   Emergent Strange Attractor as Attentional Focus
The chaotic attractor in the (I1 , I2 , I3 ) dynamics corresponds to a persistent,
structured locus in pattern-intensity space–i.e. the cognitive system’s attentional
focus emerges not as a fixed point but as a strange attractor. Trajectories on
this attractor represent continual exploration of a repertoire of patterns, with
sensitivity to stimuli sx (t) yet bounded by the attractor’s geometry.




                                           347
34.6.5    Interpretation
   • The McBride-derived ODEs translate discrete ECAN updates into a smooth,
     residuated dynamical system.
   • Pattern-intensity coordinates reduce high-dimensional ECAN to a low-
     dimensional nonlinear system.
   • Under generic coupling and feedback parameters, the resulting ODE ex-
     hibits a strange attractor, modeling sustained, non-repeating, but struc-
     tured attentional focus.

   • This accounts for cognitive phenomena like persistent exploration, sen-
     sitivity to novelty, and bounded rationality as manifestations of chaotic
     attractor dynamics in pattern space.

35       Weakness as a Unifying Bias in PRIMUS
The ideas regarding weakness and AI unfolded throughout this paper are in most
cases general in scope rather than tightly tied to any particular AGI approach or
software architecture. However, the Hyperon architecture has nonetheless been
a thread running throughout, and in this section we will do a quick re-cap of the
various ideas presented with a view toward their utility for shaping, improving
and realizing the PRIMUS 5 cognitive architecture for the Hyperon platform.
    PRIMUS [GBD+ 23] interleaves two cognitive meta-dynamics over a shared
collection of Atomspaces (largely stored in MORK instances in-RAM, and man-
aged across machines by the Distributed Atomspace (DAS)), both driven by
MeTTa scripts:
   • Goal-driven cognition: MetaMo maintains goals and invokes planning,
     inference, learning and decision routines.
   • Background cognition: Unsupervised pattern mining, concept forma-
     tion and evolution continuously enrich the Atomspace.
  5 Formerly known as CogPrime




                                      348
    The various AI processes underlying these meta-dynamics can all be viewed
in one way or another as guided by quantale-based Occam simplicity measures.
The following bullets highlight, for some of the subsystems of PRIMUS, some
of the ways it benefits from the weakness framework:

   • PLN with Weakness-Guided or SVM-Guided Inference
        – Goal-driven: MetaMo requests proofs or classifiers ranked by proba-
          bilistic or linear weakness, ensuring only the simplest high-confidence
          derivations are chosen to execute serving current goals.
        – Background: Continuous proof-mining and classifier retraining prune
          low-prominence inference paths, keeping the belief graph lean and
          reusable.
   • Logical Entropy and Graphtropy

        – Goal-driven: Concept refinement for a subgoal uses partition-weakness
          or graphtropy to split or merge categories with minimal complexity.
        – Background: Unsupervised concept discovery applies entropy-based
          weakness to identify compact clusters and distinction subgraphs.

   • Evolutionary Learning (MOSES/CoDD)
        – Goal-driven: To synthesize a controller or program, MetaMo triggers
          CoDD evolution under a Kraft-type weakness prior to drive evolution
          in rapid convergence to minimal-complexity solutions.
        – Background: Offline CoDD runs generate libraries of simple, high-
          utility codelets guided by weakness, ready for later use.
   • Neural Learners and Predictive Coding
        – Goal-driven: Decision-transformers and neural planners include McBride-
          based weakness regularizers to produce generalizable, simple policies.
          Similar combinations of methods can be used for perceptual pattern
          recognition across different modalities.
        – Background: Continuous background training of Transformers and
          WaveFormers uses weakness-driven gradient updates to compress
          representations.

   • CoDD Schema Theorem
        – Goal-driven: MetaMo relies on exponential convergence guarantees
          for weakness-guided genetic operators in time-critical synthesis.
        – Background: Unbounded background evolution exploits the same
          theorem to build extensive libraries of compact programs.

   • Clear, Disentangled Representations


                                      349
    – Goal-driven: MetaMo requests ”clear”’ subgraphs (simplicity + causal-
      strength fusions) for robust planning models.
    – Background: Disambiguation processes extract disentangled concept
      graphs to improve downstream tasks.
• Weakness-Based Decision Theories
    – Goal-driven: MetaMo invokes WCDT, WUDT or WCUDT policies
      to optimize weakness-regularized expected utility in paradoxical de-
      cisions.
    – Background: Offline simulation refines weakness priors in these poli-
      cies for faster real-time deployment.
• MetaMo Motivational Bias
    – Goal-driven: Short-term importance and confidence incorporate local
      weakness gradients to focus on under-explained subgoals.
    – Background: Anomaly detection flags high-weakness regions as can-
      didates for future goals.
• Aesthetic Generality in Creativity
    – Goal-driven: For creative subgoals (e.g. ”compose a melody”), weakness-
      based generality metrics select broad-appeal motifs.
    – Background: Continuous aesthetic evaluation populates a library of
      simple yet novel patterns.
• WAIXI and Universal Planning
    – Goal-driven: MetaMo’s AIXItl analogue uses a CoDD-weakness prior
      to bias model search toward simple predictors for real-time planning.
    – Background: Offline hypothesis enumeration under weakness priors
      builds a cached mixture of environment models.
• Controller Synthesis and Complexity
    – Goal-driven: MetaMo invokes CoDD-synthesis for control subgoals,
      obtaining minimal-complexity controllers.
    – Background: Background synthesis populates an Atomspace index
      of minimal-weakness controllers for common subtasks.
• Quantum Weakness Extensions
    – Goal-driven: When Hyperon is run on combined classical/hardware,
      MetaMo invokes quantum-WAIXI with multiperspectival weakness
      priors for quantum subgoals.
    – Background: Quantum model mining uses quantum graphtropy to
      compress operator algebras for later retrieval.


                                 350
• Galois Connection Acceleration
    – Goal-driven: MetaMo’s dynamic programming and RL leverage Galois-
      fusion of abstractions for near-real-time multi-scale planning.
    – Background: Background RL on coarse abstractions refines complex-
      ity where weakness gradients signal the need for finer detail.

• Integrated Information via Weakness
