      guided updates to compress representations and prune links.




                                351
36       Toward a Quantale Weakness Library for MeTTa
Given the applicability of quantale weakness and associated ideas to the various
cognitive algorithms in the PRIMUS cognitive architecture, it seems desirable
to implement various commutative quantales with enriching measures on them
in the MeTTa language, which lives at the core of the Hyperon software archi-
tecture being used to implement PRIMUS. One would then use the assorted
quantale operations to implement various Ai algorithms in MeTTa, making use
of tools such as weakness, quantale-specific information and pattern and emer-
gence, McBride derivatives of weakness and information and pattern, etc.. It
would be desirable to implement all this efficiently at the MeTTA-IL and MORK
level [F1R25], but also have it easily accessible to users of MeTTa at the full
MeTTa-syntax level.
    With all this in mind, in this section we very roughly outline what a quantale-
weakness MeTTa software library might look like , embodying a variety of de-
sired features.

36.0.1    A Basic Quantale-Weakness Library for MeTTa
We might organize such a library in four layers:

Core Primitives (MeTTa-IL / MORK)                Define the bare quantale opera-
tions as built-in primitives:

(module Quantales.Core
  ;; q-join : Q Q ? Q
  (prim q-join (Q a b)          ;; associative, commutative, idempotent
    ...)

  ;; q-times : Q Q ? Q
  (prim q-times (Q a b)         ;; associative, commutative, unit = 1
    ...)

  ;; q-unit : Q
  (prim q-unit (Q)              ;; the multiplicative unit
    ...)

  ;; q-resid : Q Q ? Q
  (prim q-resid (Q a b)         ;; residual for implication
    ...))

   All heavy loops over join/times compile to optimized MORK code.

Weakness Module (MeTTa Syntax) Provide user-facing macros and func-
tions:



                                       352
(module Quantales.Weakness
  ;; define a new quantale by naming its operations
  (macro define-quantale (name join times unit resid?)
    ‘(do
       (def ,name
         {:join ,join
          :times ,times
          :unit ,unit
          :resid ,resid?})
       (register-quantale ,name)))

  ;; weakness : given Q, weighting phi : X->Q, model m : X->Q
  ;; w(m) = reduce join [ times(phi(x), m(x)) | x in keys(phi) ]
  (defn weakness (Q phi m)
    (let ((j (Q :join)) (t (Q :times)))
      (reduce j
        (for x in (keys phi)
          (t (phi x) (m x))))))

  ;; alias for convenience
  (alias q-weakness weakness))


Information & Pattern Analytics Build entropy, mutual-info, pattern-
intensity on top:

(module Quantales.Information
  ;; quantale-entropy: H(m) = reduce join [ times(m(x), ln(m(x))) ]
  (defn entropy (Q m)
    (let ((j (Q :join)) (t (Q :times)))
      (reduce j
        (for x in (keys m)
          (t (m x) (ln (m x)))))))

  ;; quantale-mutual-info: I(X;Y)
  (defn mutual-info (Q mxy px py)
    (let ((j (Q :join))
          (t (Q :times))
          (r (Q :resid)))
      (reduce j
        (for (x y) in (keys mxy)
          (let ((p (mxy x y))
                (c (t (px x) (py y))))
            (t p (r p c)))))))

  ;; pattern-intensity: I_S(m) = resid( join_{x in S} m(x), join_{x} m(x) )


                                 353
    (defn pattern-intensity (Q m S)
      (let ((j (Q :join)) (r (Q :resid)))
        (r
           (reduce j (for x in S (m x)))
           (reduce j (vals m))))))

McBride Derivatives Expose partial-derivatives for sensitivity:

(module Quantales.Differentiation
  ;; partial_x weakness = phi(x)
  (defn mcbride-weakness-deriv (Q phi m x)
    (phi x))

    ;; partial_{x,y} mutual-info = resid-partial...
    (defn mcbride-mi-deriv (Q mxy px py x y)
       ...)
)

 Example Usage (Rough Sketch)        Users import and instantiate quantales,
then call analytics:

(import Quantales.Core
        Quantales.Weakness
        Quantales.Information
        Quantales.Differentiation)

;; create the [0,1] probabilistic quantale
(define-quantale prob-quantale
  Quantales.Core/q-join    ;; max
  Quantales.Core/q-times   ;; multiply
  1                        ;; unit
  nil)                     ;; no resid

;; define weight phi and model m on keys "a","b"
(def phi {"a" 0.2, "b" 0.8})
(def m   {"a" 0.5, "b" 0.3})

;; compute weakness, entropy, mutual-info
(def w (weakness prob-quantale phi m))
(def H (entropy prob-quantale m))
;; for mutual-info, supply joint mxy and marginals px,py
;; (def I (mutual-info prob-quantale mxy px py))

;; McBride derivative of weakness w.r.t. "a"
(def dw (mcbride-weakness-deriv prob-quantale phi m "a"))



                                   354
36.0.2   A Quantale Factor-Graph Sub-Library for MeTTa
As an example of how to possibly develop such libraries one step deeper, we
sketch how one might build on the above code infrastructure to make a quantale
factor-graph library in three layers.

Core Primitives (MeTTa-IL / MORK)             Define built-in primitives to ma-
nipulate graphs of variables and factors:
(module Quantales.FactorGraphs.Core
  ;; fg-create : ? Graph
  (prim fg-create   ()                        ;; allocate new empty graph
    ...)
  ;; fg-add-var : Graph Symbol Set?Q -> Graph
  (prim fg-add-var (G name domain)            ;; add variable node
    ...)
  ;; fg-add-fctr: Graph (X?Q)^n Symbol^n -> Graph
  (prim fg-add-fctr (G factor-fn var-list)    ;; add factor tying variables
    ...)
  ;; fg-infer   : Graph Symbol^m -> (Symbol -> Q)
  (prim fg-infer    (G query-vars)            ;; run belief/update
    ...))

User-Facing Syntax (MeTTa level) Provide macros and functions for ease
of use:
(module Quantales.FactorGraphs
  ;; define a new empty graph
  (macro define-graph (name)
    ‘(do (def ,name (fg-create)) ,name))

  ;; add a variable with domain in Q
  (defn add-variable (G name domain)
    (fg-add-var G name domain))

  ;; add a factor (potential) over given variables
  (defn add-factor (G factor-fn vars)
    (fg-add-fctr G factor-fn vars))

  ;; perform marginal or MAP inference
  (defn infer (G queries)
    (fg-infer G queries)))

Inference Module      Wrap common routines over commutative quantale Q:
(module Quantales.FactorGraphs.Inference
  (defn compute-marginals (Q G)


                                     355
    ;; e.g. run sum-product or max-product over Q
    ...)
  (defn belief-update (Q G evidence)
    ;; condition G on observed evidence
    ...))

CoDD Models as a Special Case of Quantale Factor-Graphs In this
sort of framework, Combinatory Decision DAGs (CoDD) can be encoded di-
rectly as factor graphs over a suitable quantale (e.g. logical or weakness quan-
tale) by treating each decision node as a variable with domain {branches} and
each combinator constraint as a factor. In MeTTa syntax:

;; 1. Instantiate a graph
(define-graph codd-graph)

;; 2. For each decision node d in the CoDD, add a variable
(add-variable codd-graph d ’(choice1 choice2 ...))

;; 3. For each combinator (e.g. K, S) impose a factor constraining
;;    child variables to match the combinator semantics
(add-factor codd-graph k-combinator-factor ’(d1 d2))

;; 4. Optionally add weakness weights or logical potentials
;;    (domain Q chosen to reflect CoDD logical entropy)

;; 5. Perform inference to extract most likely program/path
(def result (infer codd-graph ’(root-node)))

    Because all CoDD operations – pattern-memoization, SK combinators, log-
ical entropy constraints – can be expressed as quantale factors and variables,
CoDD evolution, mutation and synthesis become instances of generic factor-
graph inference and learning over Q.

36.1    Quantale-Based Crossover and Mutation for CoDD
        Models
As an example of how the nitty-gritty of AI operations might be expressed in
terms of a quantale software library, let’s consider the case of GA evolution of
CoDD models.
   Suppose each CoDD program is interpreted as a model

                                 m:X → Q

in a commutative quantale (Q, ⊗, ⊕, e). We can then define offspring models
directly via the quantale operations:



                                      356
Pointwise Join-Crossover Given two parent models m1 , m2 : X → Q, de-
fine their join-crossover

                   mchild (x) = m1 (x) ⊕ m2 (x),    x ∈ X.

This yields a model that, for each datum x, adopts the weaker (larger) of the
two parents’ predictions.

Pointwise Times-Crossover Alternatively, define

                        mchild (x) = m1 (x) ⊗ m2 (x).

This produces a model whose confidence is the intersection (strongest common
prediction) of the parents.

Masked Crossover via a Predicate Let p : X → {0, 1} ⊂ Q be a predicate
(e.g. a random mask), with complement p̄ = e ( p. Then

                       mchild = (p ⊗ m1 ) ⊕ (p̄ ⊗ m2 ),

i.e.                                               
              mchild (x) = p(x) ⊗ m1 (x) ⊕ e ( p(x) ⊗ m2 (x).
This generalizes the usual GA mask-based crossover to arbitrary quantales.

Mutation as Quantale Perturbation Given a small ”perturbation” model
δ : X → Q (e.g. drawn at random from a low-weakness distribution), define two
natural mutations:

            m+
             mut (x) = m(x) ⊕ δ(x),     m×
                                         mut (x) = m(x) ⊗ δ(x).

The ”additive” mutation m ⊕ δ relaxes the model on those inputs where δ is
large; the ”multiplicative” mutation m⊗δ sharpens it where δ has high support.

 Residual-Based Correction For a desired adjustment δ, one can also use
the residuum to ”remove” or ”correct” parts of m:

                    mcorr (x) = m(x) ⊗ (m(x) ( δ(x)).

This pulls the model’s prediction at x toward δ(x) in a residuated manner.

MeTTa-Level Definition In MeTTa pseudocode, one might write:

(defn crossover-join (Q m1 m2)
  (fn (x) ((Q :join) (m1 x) (m2 x))))

(defn crossover-mask (Q p m1 m2)
  (let ((join (Q :join)) (times (Q :times)) (resid (Q :resid)))


                                     357
    (fn (x)
      (join
        (times (p x)    (m1 x))
        (times (resid (Q :unit) (p x)) (m2 x))))))

(defn mutate-plus (Q m delta)
  (let ((join (Q :join)))
    (fn (x) (join (m x) (delta x)))))

(defn mutate-times (Q m delta)
  (let ((times (Q :times)))
    (fn (x) (times (m x) (delta x)))))

    These definitions eliminate ad-hoc tree-rewriting for crossover/mutation and
instead leverage the quantale’s built-in ⊕, ⊗, (. This yields a uniform, alge-
braic GA in which all variation operators are instances of the same underlying
quantale calculus.

36.2    MeTTa-like Pseudocode for GA Evolution of Weak
        CoDD Models
We can then give quite elegant pseudocode for a GA evolving CoDD programs
to identify weak models in data.

;;-----------------------------------------
;; 1. Import core quantale & weakness library
;;-----------------------------------------
(import Quantales.Core
        Quantales.Weakness
        Quantales.Crossover
        Quantales.Mutation)

;;-----------------------------------------
;; 2. Define the CoDD quantale
;;     - q-join implements quantale join (e.g. max/union)
;;     - q-times implements quantale product (e.g. composition/intersection)
;;     - q-unit is the unit for q-times
;;     - q-resid is the residuation (implication) operator
;;-----------------------------------------
(define-quantale codd-quantale
   Quantales.Core/q-join    ;; q-join
   Quantales.Core/q-times   ;; q-times
   Quantales.Core/q-unit
   Quantales.Core/q-resid)

;;-----------------------------------------


                                      358
;; 3. Random model generator: produce m : X -> Q
;;-----------------------------------------
(defn random-model (domain Q)
  (let ((j (Q :join)))        ;; to combine random component values
    (fn (x)
      ;; pick a random Q-element, e.g. q-unit or a small random multiple
      (Quantales.Core/q-times Q
        (Q :unit)
        (rand))))             ;; rand in Q sampled

;;-----------------------------------------
;; 4. Fitness: weakness w(m)
;;     w(m) = q-join over x of [ phi(x) q-times m(x) ]
;;-----------------------------------------
(defn fitness (m phi)
   ;; ‘weakness‘ uses q-times for phi(x)*m(x) and q-join to aggregate
   (weakness codd-quantale phi m))

;;-----------------------------------------
;; 5. Crossover & Mutation using quantale ops
;;     - join-crossover: m1 join m2 pointwise
;;     - mutate-plus:    m join delta pointwise
;;-----------------------------------------
(defn crossover (m1 m2)
   ;; uses q-join
   (crossover-join codd-quantale m1 m2))

(defn mutate (m)
  (let ((delta (random-model (domain m) codd-quantale)))
    ;; uses q-join for mutation
    (mutate-plus codd-quantale m delta)))

;;-----------------------------------------
;; 6. Initialize population of M models
;;-----------------------------------------
(defn initialize-population (domain M)
  (for i in (range M)
       (random-model domain codd-quantale)))

;;-----------------------------------------
;; 7. Select top K by weakness
;;-----------------------------------------
(defn select-top (pop phi K)
  (let ((scored (map (fn (m) [m (fitness m phi)]) pop)))
    (map first
      (take K


                               359
          (sort-by second scored :descending)))))

;;-----------------------------------------
;; 8. Reproduce next generation of size M
;;     Crossover + mutation use quantale ops
;;-----------------------------------------
(defn reproduce (parents M)
   (let ((offspring ’()))
     (while (< (length offspring) M)
       (let ((m1    (choice parents))
             (m2    (choice parents))
             (child (crossover m1 m2)))
         (push (mutate child) offspring)))
     offspring))

;;-----------------------------------------
;; 9. GA loop: evolve for G generations
;;     Selection & fitness use quantale weakness
;;-----------------------------------------
(defn evolve (domain phi M K G)
   (let ((pop (initialize-population domain M)))
     (loop ((gen 1) (population pop))
       (if (> gen G)
           population
           (let ((parents (select-top population phi K))
                 (children (reproduce parents M)))
             (recur (inc gen) children))))))

;;-----------------------------------------
;; 10. Run GA on dataset
;;-----------------------------------------
(def domain (keys dataset))
(def phi    (fn (x) ((dataset x) :phi)))
(def final-population (evolve domain phi 100 20 50))


37     Conclusion
We have developed a unified categorical theory of weakness as an Occam-style
simplicity measure, showing how quantale-enriched categories recover and gener-
alize classical notions of simplicity in various fields – from probabilistic weakness
and SVM margins to logical entropy, graphtropy, and quantum multiperspecti-
val priors.
    If you have read this treatise through from the start, it’s been a long and
somewhat winding journey through a lot of rather interesting and varied terri-
tory. We have linked weakness to description-length bounds in Combinatorial


                                        360
Distinction Dags, formulated weakness-guided inference control for Probabilistic
Logic Networks, and interpreted predictive-coding and information-geometric
updates as direct weakness minimization via the McBride derivative. We proved
a schema theorem for CoDD genetic algorithms, constructed a CoDD-weakness
analogue of AIXtl (”WAIXI”) with universality and regret-vanishing guaran-
tees, and demonstrated efficient implementation of a multiresolution weakness
transform in graph databases (e.g. MORK). Among many other things....
    Given the conceptual breadth of Occam’s Razor as a concept, and the math-
ematical generality of quantale weakness as a formal instantiation of Occam’s
Razor, the applications are both wide and deep and this book could have been
either much shorter or much longer...
    Looking ahead, several directions stand out:

   • Deepening the mathematics. Further development of the theory of
     weakness–particularly in noncommutative, multiperspectival, and categorical-
     analytic settings–can yield new insights into Occam?s Razor and its rela-
     tion to measure-theoretic and homotopical notions of complexity.
   • Hyperon AGI applications. Embedding weakness as a first-class quan-
     tale in the Atomspace/MORK store and MeTTa interpreter promises
     tighter cognitive synergy in PRIMUS, unifying symbolic, probabilistic,
     evolutionary, neural, and quantum modules under a single simplicity ob-
     jective.
   • Quantum computing and hybrid systems. The extension of weak-
     ness to operator quantales and quantum AIXItl opens paths to principled
     priors in quantum machine learning and decision making, as well as to
     new hybrid classical-quantum AGI architectures.
   • Cognitive science and consciousness. Weakness-based integrated-
     information measures and cognitive-synergy functors suggest a formal
     bridge between category-theoretic simplicity and phenomenological rich-
     ness. Developing these notions may advance mathematical models of at-
     tention, awareness, and consciousness.
   • Practical algorithms and benchmarks. Implementing and evaluat-
     ing weakness-driven predictive coding, wavelet-like decompositions, and
     natural-gradient updates across real-world tasks–ranging from language
     modeling to robotics–will test and refine the theory in grounded settings.

   By continuing to develop the mathematical, computational, and empirical
aspects of quantale weakness theory, we aim to forge new connections between
simplicity theory, cognitive science and AGI algorithmics, ultimately guiding
the construction of more efficient, interpretable, and general AI systems as well
as perhaps gaining new understanding into biological minds.

                                    366
A     Appendix: Review of CMMC and CoDD Com-
      putational Models
We review in this Appendix two computational models that play roles in the
body of the paper. Both of these have been described in previous papers but
in slightly different formal languages, and are here re-presented in a notation
and framework more immediately amenable to incorporation in the weakness
perspective.

A.1     Tbe CMMC model of Computation
Next we review a ”biological-ish” model of computation proposed in [Goe20b],
which has been used among other purposes to formalize a species of Occam’s
Razor, and which we will show later on to connect very elegantly with weakness.
    This can be thought of as a ”dynamic Lego” mode of computation, wherein
one pieces together a computational process from components by sticking them
together in the ways they allow – but unlike ordinary lego blocks, once one block
is appropriately connected to others, it can produce some output – including
the option to produce additional blocks which then get stuck onto the existing
ones in the growing structure.
    These Legos are not necessarily embedded in any sort of ambient 3D space
(though one could do it that way), so the Lego-blocks metaphor should not
be overstretched. Rather, each of the simple building blocks involved can be
thought of as a tiny “macro” that lives in a non-restrictive abstract space and
takes some inputs and produces some output (which may be a value like a num-
ber or may be another combinator), without explicitly naming or manipulating
variables. Instead of writing code that says “let x be this value and let y be
that value, then compute f(x,y)”, you simply have a collection of these little
Legos/macros (often called combinators) and you glue them together in various
ways. The result is a network of interconnected pieces. When you feed an initial
input into that network, the combinators pass messages, combine them, split
them, or ignore them, and eventually an output emerges.
    Intelligent computation can be built up from these combinator operations
in a highly flexible and self-adaptive way. Rather than thinking in terms of
variables that store numbers or symbols, you think in terms of combinators
that “already know” how to combine their inputs. This leads to a style of
programming (and of reasoning) that is very compact yet powerful: you only
needs a few primitive combinators to get started, and then every more complex
function arises by combining them. (In theory you can build everything from
a single well-chosen combinator, though in practice it’s simpler to start with a
slightly larger set than this.). One can extend this idea to richer data structures
– graphs, metagraphs, or networked representations – and still remain within
the same basic framework; basically, one builds the primitives of these data
structures (e.g. nodes and edges) into the assumed primitive combinators.
    To summarize in plain language:


                                       367
   • We replace the usual notion of variables and assignments with a collec-
     tion of self ’sufficient modules (combinators), each of which knows how to
     process its inputs.
   • Computation proceeds by repeatedly applying one module to another -for
     instance, telling combinator S to combine two other combinators to form
     a new one, or telling K to pick one of its two inputs, and so on.
   • Because each combinator is just a tiny piece of logic, when you assemble
     many of them together, you can build arbitrarily complex functions. There
     is no need for explaining how data “flows through variables”-you only need
     to specify how these small pieces interconnect.
   • The extension of this combinatory approach to handle graph-based data
     structures and self-modifying rewrite rules yields what can be thought of
     as a sort of metagraph-based ”algorithmic chemistry”, which is appealing
     for modeling highly dynamic AI systems.
    With that intuitive picture in mind, we can now turn to a more formal
discussion of the combinational model.

A.1.1   Basic Definitions
We begin by recalling some standard notions from combinatory logic. After-
wards we will indicate how to extend these ideas to more general graph-based
structures.
Definition 11 (Combinator Terms). Let C = {S, K, I, . . . } be a set of primi-
tive combinators. We define the set of terms, denoted T , as follows:
                •   : C ∈ C =⇒ C ∈ T ,
                •   : If M ∈ T and N ∈ T , then (M N ) ∈ T .
In other words, every primitive combinator is a term, and if M and N are terms
then the application of M to N , written (M N ), is also a term. We often omit
parentheses by adopting the convention that application is left-associative. Thus
XY Z stands for ((X Y ) Z).
Definition 12 (Reduction Rules). Each primitive combinator C ∈ C has an
associated rule that specifies how to reduce certain application forms. The most
common examples are:
                            K xy     −→     x,
                           S xyz     −→     x z (y z),
                              Ix     −→     x.
(note that this uses curried notation in which a term first binds the argument
immediately to its right, and then the combinator produced by this binding goes
on to bind other things). In more detail:


                                      368
   • The K-combinator extracts its first argument: (K x) y − x.
   • The S-combinator distributes its third argument to both x and y: ((S x) y) z−
     (x z) (y z).
   • The I-combinator is the identity: (I x) − x.
A reduction is any finite sequence of applying these rewrite rules until no further
rule can apply, at which point the term is in normal form.
Remark. It is a classical result that combinatory logic with just the S and K
combinators is Turing-complete. That is, any computable function on (suitably
encoded) data can be represented by some combinator term built from S and K
alone.

A.1.2    Extending Graph-Based Computation to Metagraphs
For many AI applications (especially those involving knowledge graphs, concept
networks, or self-referential rule sets), the simple tree-structured terms of classi-
cal combinatory logic can be awkwardly restrictive. It is desirable to generalize
the notion of a “term” from a rooted tree to a labeled directed graph (sometimes
called a “metagraph”). The key ideas here are:
Definition 13 (Metagraph). A metagraph G = (V, E, `V , `E ) consists of:
   • A finite set V of vertices (or nodes).
   • A set E ⊆ V × V of directed edges.
   • A vertex-labeling function `V : V − ΣV that assigns each vertex a label
     drawn from some finite alphabet ΣV .
   • An edge-labeling function `E : E − ΣE that assigns each edge a label from
     a finite alphabet ΣE .
Each vertex label might encode a primitive combinator (like S, K, or I), or it
might encode some data symbol (an integer, string, etc.). Edge labels typically
indicate the “role” of that edge in a larger computational pattern (for instance,
whether it represents a function-argument link, a type annotation, or a pointer
to a subgraph).
Definition 14 (Graph Rewrite Rule). A rewrite rule is a pair (L −→ R),
where L (the left side) and R (the right side) are both metagraphs. Intuitively:
   • L specifies a pattern to be matched somewhere in the “host” metagraph
     G.
   • R specifies the subgraph that replaces the matched portion of G.
More formally, a rewrite step applies whenever there is a graph isomorphism φ
from L into a subgraph of G. One removes the image φ(L) from G and then
inserts a (possibly renamed) copy of R, connecting R to the rest of G in the
same way that L was connected. The result is a new metagraph G0 .


                                        369
Definition 15 (Combinational Metagraph Model of Computation). One then
arrives at a ”simple combinational computational model of computation” (CMMC)
based on the following components:
  1. Atoms and Molecules: An Atom is a single labeled vertex v (with no
     edges). A Molecule is a connected metagraph.

  2. Agent State: At any moment, the agent’s state is given by a (usually
     large) metagraph G. Nodes in G can represent data, primitive combina-
     tors, inference rules, goals, etc.
  3. Reaction Rules: A finite (or countable) set R of graph rewrite rules,
     each of which matches a small subgraph of G and replaces it with a dif-
     ferent subgraph. Some of these rules may correspond to inference steps
     (“if you see pattern P , replace it by pattern Q”), others may correspond
     to learning rules (“upgrade a rule’s parameters based on error signals”),
     or goal-driven modifications (“when you reach a certain subgraph, trigger
     a new process”).

  4. Computation Dynamics: Computation proceeds by repeatedly applying
     any applicable rewrite rule from R to the current metagraph G. One may
     choose the rule to apply:
        • Non-deterministically, leading to many possible rewrite paths.
        • Stochastically, with probabilities assigned to each rule.
        • Goal-driven, selecting rules that move the metagraph toward satisfy-
          ing a particular objective.
     Over time, G evolves through a search space of metagraphs, ideally con-
     verging (or oscillating) to configurations where the agent’s computational
     or motivational goals are met.
Remark. Note that in classical combinatory logic each term is a tree of nested
applications (e.g. (S K) (K I)). In this CMMC model, those trees are replaced
by arbitrary directed graphs. Thus, one can encode not only simple function
application but also shared substructures, cyclic references, and self-modifying
rule sets. This added flexibility is crucial for modeling rich cognitive processes
such as meta-learning, self-reflection, and open-ended creativity.

A.1.3    Formal Properties
1. Expressiveness. Since classical S, K-combinatory logic is Turing-complete,
and since any tree can be seen as a special case of a graph, it follows that CMMC
is also Turing-complete. Moreover, the presence of graph rewrites allows direct
modeling of operations that are awkward in tree-based systems, such as merging
identical subterms (graph unification) or explicitly encoding cyclic dependen-
cies.



                                       370
2. Self-Modification. We may include meta-rules in the rule set R that
match and rewrite portions of R itself (since R can be encoded as a subgraph
of G). Formally, let R(G) denote the subgraph of G that encodes the current
set of rewrite rules. A meta-rule might have the form
                                                   
                            LRmrule −→ RRmrule ,
where LRmrule matches some pattern within R(G), thereby altering or extending
the agent’s own inference/learning strategies at runtime. This ability to rewrite
one’s own rules is the cornerstone of self-reflection and meta-learning.

3. Confluence and Termination. In general, graph rewrite systems need
not be confluent (different orders of applying rules lead to different final graphs)
and need not terminate (one can keep rewriting forever). For many AI appli-
cations, nontermination is acceptable (the agent runs indefinitely). Confluence
may or may not be desired: if one wants deterministic outcomes, one must
restrict R to a confluent subset. If one wants divergent creative exploration,
permitting non-confluence can be advantageous.

A.1.4    Example: Simple Graph-Based Combinator Application
To illustrate concretely how this sort of combinator computation works, consider
the following minimal metagraph G:
   • Nodes:
                              V = { S, K, X, Y, U, V },
      where S, K are combinator nodes and X, Y, U, V are data nodes.
   • Edges: Four directed edges indicating the combinator-data matches:
                    S − X,      S − Y, QQuadK − U,          K − V.
      Here each arrow C → D means -combinator C will match data node D-
      in its two-argument pattern.
In this setup:
  1. The rewrite rule for S, say RS : (PS RightarrowTS ), will match the sub-
     graph consisting of node S together with its two outgoing edges to X and
     Y . After matching, it replaces that pattern by its template TS .
  2. Likewise, K’s rule RK matches K −U and K −V and rewrites accordingly.
  3. No graphics are needed: the entire combinational application is captured
     by listing nodes and edges, and by explaining that - C → D - encodes a
     combinator-pattern match.
   This example is trivial and lacks sophisticated AI-related metagraph rewrite
operations, which would make the example substantially larger, but may be
valuable in illustrating how the basic rewrite dynamics works.


                                        371
A.1.5   Summary of Formal Treatment
To reiterate the essence of the CMMC model, then:

   • We start with primitive combinator nodes (for example S, K, I) and data
     nodes (integers, strings, etc.) all embedded in a labeled directed graph.
   • We have a (possibly dynamic) set of graph rewrite rules that specify how
     small matched subgraphs are replaced by other subgraphs. These rules
     encode:
        – Combinator reduction (e.g. S, K reductions).
        – Learning or inference steps (e.g. modifying weights or rule parame-
          ters based on local errors).
        – Goal-driven transformations (e.g. -if you reach a certain configura-
          tion, insert a new subgoal-).
        – Meta-rules that rewrite other rules, enabling self-reflection.
   • Computation is simply repeatedly apply any applicable rule to the current
     metagraph, yielding a (possibly nonterminating, possibly nondeterminis-
     tic) sequence of graph states.
   • This framework generalizes ordinary combinatory logic (which uses tree’structured
     terms) to arbitrary graphs, allowing direct representation of shared sub-
     structures, cyclic references, and self-modifying code.
   • Standard properties like Turing-completeness, confluence, and termination
     must be revisited in the context of graph rewrites, but the fundamental
     expressive power follows from the fact that tree terms embed into graphs,
     and S, K combinators are already Turing-complete.

    Putting these pieces together, we arrive at a unified, graph-based general-
ization of classical combinatory logic, enriched with the ability to rewrite its
own rewrite rules, thereby capturing powerful forms of self-organizing and self-
modifying computation.

A.2     The CoDD Model: A Specific Example of CMMC
        with Interesting Properties
The CMMC envisions a system as a graph where both data and combinators
(small rewrite modules) are nodes, and computation proceeds by matching cer-
tain subgraph patterns and rewriting them into new subgraphs. In this abstract
model:
   • Each combinator is defined by a pair (P RightarrowT ), where P is a
     pattern subgraph and T is a template for replacement.
   • When the pattern P appears in the current graph, it is replaced by T ,
     producing new data or further computational structure.


                                      372
   • Because combinator definitions themselves are stored as parts of the graph,
     the system can include meta-rules that modify combinator definitions at
     run time-enabling self-modification and learning.
    In [Goe20a] the CoDD (Combinatory and Data-Driven Dynamics) model
of computation is presented as a specific interesting instance of this general
combinational model. In CoDD:
   • Every node is labeled either by a data symbol, a combinator name, or the
     special label rule (indicating a rule encoding).
   • Each combinator C has its rewrite rule (PC RightarrowTC ) stored under
     a node labeled rule, so that matching and rewriting follow the general
     CMMC scheme.
   • Meta-rules are simply other rule-labeled nodes whose patterns match
     these combinator rule subgraphs, allowing CoDD to modify its own com-
     binator set.
   CoDD implements CMMC in a concrete way, specifying exactly how to en-
code patterns, templates, and meta-rules in a metagraph.

A.2.1   Specifying the CoDD Model
We now describe CoDD formally:
Definition 16 (CoDD Metagraph). A CoDD metagraph is again a quadruple

                               G = (V, E, `V , `E ),

with the following conventions on labels:
   • ΣV = ΣD ∪ ΣC ∪ {rule}, where:
        – ΣD is the set of data labels (e.g. numeric constants, symbolic to-
          kens).
        – ΣC is the set of combinator names (e.g. plus, mult, or higher-order
          combinators).
        – The label rule identifies a node that encodes a rewrite rule.
   • ΣE is the set of edge labels, including roles such as arg1, arg2, result,
     pattern, and template.
Definition 17 (Encoding a Combinator Rule in CoDD). For each combinator
C ∈ ΣC , CoDD stores its rewrite rule RC = (PC RightarrowTC ) as follows:
   • Create a node rC ∈ V labeled rule.
                        comb
   • Add an edge (rC −−→ vC ) where vC is a node labeled C. This attaches
     the rule to its combinator name.


                                       373
   • Under rC , attach two distinguished subgraphs: one labeled pattern whose
     structure is isomorphic to PC , and one labeled template whose structure is
     isomorphic to TC . Variables in PC are nodes labeled with question-marks
     (e.g. -x, -y).
In this way, RC is itself represented as a subgraph rooted at rC .

Definition 18 (CoDD Pattern Matching). To apply a combinator C in CoDD:
  1. Locate the node rC labeled rule and find the pattern subgraph PC under
     it.
  2. Find an injective embedding σ : PC → S ⊆ G that matches node labels
     (instantiating variables) and edge labels.
  3. Record variable bindings σ(-x) 7→ vx , etc.
If such S exists, RC applies to S.
Definition 19 (CoDD Rewrite Step). Given a match σ : PC → S:

  1. Remove all nodes and edges in S from G.
  2. Insert a copy of the template subgraph TC under rC , instantiating each
     variable node -x by σ(-x).
  3. Reconnect any edges in G that formerly pointed to nodes in S to the cor-
     responding nodes in the instantiated TC , according to edge labels in TC .
Denote this rewrite by
                                        rC
                                     G −−→ G0 .
Definition 20 (CoDD Meta-Rule). A CoDD meta-rule M is similarly encoded
as a node m ∈ V labeled rule. Its pattern subgraph PM is designed to match
another rule encoding (i.e. a subgraph rooted at some rC 0 ). When PM matches
that rule encoding, apply the rewrite to replace the matched subgraph under rC 0
by the template TM , thereby modifying the combinator C 0 ’s rule definition.
Definition 21 (CoDD Computation). A CoDD computation is any sequence
of metagraphs
                        R1     R2      R3
                    G0 −−→ G1 −−→  G2 −−→ ··· ,
where each Ri is either a combinator rule node rC or a meta-rule node m. If at
some step no rule applies, the computation halts in normal form.

A.2.2    CoDD as a Special Case of CMMC
We now list explicitly how each component of the general CMMC model is
instantiated by CoDD:

  1. Node Labels:


                                        374
        • In the general model, ΣV may include arbitrary combinator names
          and data labels.
        • CoDD specifies ΣV = ΣD ∪ ΣC ∪ {rule}. Every combinator rule is
          rooted at a rule-labeled node.
  2. Encoding of PC and TC :
        • In the abstract model, each combinator C has an associated pattern
          PC and template TC .
        • CoDD stores PC and TC as subgraphs under the node rC . Vari-
          ables in PC are explicit nodes labeled with question-marks, and the
          structure of TC indicates how matched data variables propagate.
  3. Pattern Matching:
        • The general CMMC model requires an injective graph isomorphism
          σ : PC → S ⊆ G.
        • CoDD implements exactly this: find a subgraph S ⊆ G matching the
          pattern subgraph under rC .
  4. Rewrite Step:
        • Abstractly, remove subgraph S and insert TC .
        • CoDD removes S ⊆ G and instantiates the template subgraph TC ,
          reconnecting external edges according to the labels in TC .
  5. Meta-Rules:
        • In the general model, meta-rules match and replace rule definitions.
        • CoDD’s meta-rules are nodes labeled rule whose pattern subgraphs
          match the encoding of another rC 0 . Applying a meta-rule rewrites
          that encoding-modifying combinator definitions at run time.
  6. Computation Sequence:
        • Both models describe a computation as a sequence of metagraphs
          connected by rewrite steps.
        • CoDD’s computation G0 → G1 → · · · follows the same pattern, with
          each step labeled by the node rC or m whose rule is applied.
  7. Universality:
        • CMMC can simulate Turing-complete systems if combinators are cho-
          sen appropriately (e.g. to simulate S and K combinators).
        • CoDD inherits that universality, since one can encode the same com-
          binators and their rules under rule-nodes.
    None of this tells us why CoDD might be especially interesting compared to
other instantiations of CMMC – but our burden in this section has been just to
explain what CMMC is. The relation between CoDD and weakness is covered
in Section 2.3.


                                     375
A.2.3   Universality of CoDD
As a footnote, if one wanted to construct a more detailed demontration that
CoDD has universal computational power– means it can simulate any Turing
machine – one would proceed roughly as follows (leveraging the classic tape-
based TM construct):
   • First, one encodes the Turing-machine tape, head position, and finite’state
     control as nodes and edges in a CoDD metagraph.
   • Each Turing transition becomes a single CoDD rewrite rule that updates
     the state, writes a new symbol, and moves the head.
   • By repeatedly applying these rewrite rules, CoDD mimics each step of the
     Turing machine.
Since a Turing machine can compute anything a conventional computer program
can, CoDD can also compute it. In other words, any computable function can
be implemented in CoDD by writing the appropriate metagraph and rewrite
rules.

A.2.4   Elegant Normal Form for CoDD Programs
Finally, we ask: What would an ENF for CoDD look like?
    An Elegant Normal Form (ENF) for a Combinational Decision DAG (CoDD)
program is a unique, minimal DAG representation satisfying the following con-
ditions [Hol90]:
  1. Primitive Nodes.
        • Every leaf is labeled by an Atom(a) for some atomic test or action a.
        • Every internal node is labeled Combine i(v 1,...,v n i), where asti
          is the ith combinator of arity ni and each vj is a pointer to a child
          node.
  2. DAG Structure.
        • The graph is acyclic, with a single designated root node.
        • Each node has a unique integer ID, and for every edge u − v we
          require ID(u) < ID(v).
  3. Maximal Sharing.
        • If two subgraphs rooted at nodes u and v are isomorphic in labels
          and structure, they are represented by the same node (i.e. u = v).
  4. Canonical Ordering.
        • For each combinator asti that is commutative, its child pointers
          (v1 , . . . , vni ) are sorted by ascending node ID.
        • For non-commutative combinators, the ordering of children is as in
          the original program.


                                     376
Conversion to ENF. Given any CoDD program, its ENF is obtained by:
  1. Unfold the CoDD into a tree of combinator applications plus atoms.
  2. Assign provisional node IDs by a postorder traversal.
  3. Merge identical subtrees: traverse bottom-up, replacing isomorphic sub-
     trees by a single shared node.
  4. ReID: renumber nodes so that each node’s ID is one more than the max-
     imum of its children’s IDs.
  5. Sort children of each commutative combinator by child ID.

Uniqueness. By maximal sharing, ID-ordering, and canonical argument sort-
ing, the ENF is unique for each equivalence class of CoDD programs under
renaming and associative restructuring of commutative combinators.

Benefits.
   • Guarantees a one-to-one correspondence between CoDD semantics and
     their DAG representation.
   • Enables efficient equality tests by simple pointer comparison.

   • Minimizes storage by maximal subgraph reuse.
   • Provides a clear measure of program complexity via the number of nodes.




                                     377
